Motivation: To address inconsistent evaluation protocols and overemphasis on predictive accuracy, the authors propose a unified, reproducible benchmark that includes computational efficiency metrics and open resources.

Method: Evaluate ten representative architectures spanning CNNs to Vision Transformers (ViTs) on ten heterogeneous EO change detection datasets using identical experimental protocols. Compare training from scratch vs. pre-trained weights. Assess predictive performance and computational efficiency (parameter counts, inference latency). Publicly release standardized data splits, training scripts, logs, and model checkpoints under FAIR principles.

Result: Classical architectures, when optimized for efficiency, often outperform more complex contemporary models in overall performance-efficacy, particularly Siamese U-Nets. Pre-training yields a substantial performance boost with no additional inference cost, and standardized benchmarks reveal these trends more clearly than previous, heterogeneous evaluations.

Conclusion: A standardized, open benchmark with transparent resources enables fair, comprehensive evaluation of EO change detection models regarding both accuracy and efficiency. It highlights that simple, well-configured architectures can beat state-of-the-art complexity, and that pre-training benefits generalize across datasets without incurring inference overhead.

Abstract: Change detection in Earth observation (EO) is critical for monitoring land surface transformations, yet recent research in the field is constrained by inconsistent evaluation protocols and a narrow focus on predictive accuracy without regard for computational efficiency. To address this, we present a standardized, open-source benchmark for evaluating state-of-the-art (SOTA) deep learning methods for Earth observation change detection. We conduct a comprehensive analysis of ten representative model architectures, ranging from convolutional networks (CNNs) to vision transformers (ViTs), across ten heterogeneous change detection datasets. We rigorously evaluate these models with identical experimental protocols, comparing models trained from scratch against those utilizing pre-trained weights. Furthermore, we evaluate predictive performance alongside computational efficiency, including parameter counts and inference latency. Our findings reveal that well-optimized classical architectures, such as Siamese U-Nets, frequently outperform more complex contemporary models when computational efficiency is factored in, and that pre-training consistently provides a significant performance boost with no additional inference cost. To ensure complete transparency and reproducibility, all experimental resources, including standardized data splits, training scripts, training logs, and model checkpoints are publicly available and adhere to FAIR principles (Findable, Accessible, Interoperable, and Reusable).

</details>


### [52] [Synth-JDoc: Synthesizing a Japanese Document Image Dataset for OCR with Diverse Layouts and Embedded Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28248)
*Keito Sasagawa,Shuhei Kurita,Daisuke Kawahara*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Synthesize a Japanese OCR dataset with vertical and horizontal text using HTML/CSS layouts, embedded T2I imagery, and noise to train LVLMs; shows superior improvement on vertically written Japanese text over baselines; dataset released publicly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LVLMs struggle to read vertically written Japanese text; high-quality OCR data is essential for robust document understanding. Manual data collection is costly and scaling synthetic OCR from existing images has issues like recognition errors and source image dependence.

Method: Generate multi-column document images from text using HTML/CSS to capture both vertical and horizontal writing. Insert images produced by text-to-image models into the document layout to enhance realism. Apply noise and degradation to simulate real-world conditions. Fine-tune LVLMs on this synthetic dataset and compare to baselines that use prior synthetic datasets or high-quality T2I-generated data.

Result: The synthetic dataset yielded the best improvement in LVLM performance for reading vertically written Japanese text compared to baselines. The authors publicly release the dataset and code at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: Synthetic, layout-aware OCR data creation with realistic degradation and embedded imagery is effective for enhancing LVLMs’ capability to read vertically written Japanese text; the approach offers a scalable alternative to manual data curation and traditional OCR dataset generation.

Abstract: The ability of Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs) to read text within document images is crucial, as it enables various applications such as Document Visual Question Answering. To enhance the text-reading capabilities of LVLMs, high-quality OCR datasets are essential. This need is particularly critical for Japanese documents, which often feature vertically written text alongside horizontally written text. Current LVLMs demonstrate considerably lower performance on vertically written Japanese text than on horizontally written text, necessitating specialized OCR datasets to bridge this gap. However, manually constructing OCR datasets is expensive and difficult to scale. Alternatively, constructing datasets by extracting text from existing document images using OCR models introduces challenges, such as text recognition errors and the prerequisite of sourcing document images.
  To address these issues, we construct an OCR dataset by synthesizing document images directly from text. Leveraging HTML and CSS, we generate multi-column documents that incorporate both vertical and horizontal writing styles. Furthermore, to ensure the visual realism of the documents, we embed images generated by text-to-image models within the layout. Additionally, to foster model robustness, we apply noise and degradation filters to the synthesized document images. In our experiments, we compared the performance of models fine-tuned on our synthetic dataset against baselines fine-tuned on synthetic datasets from prior work and those generated by a high-performance text-to-image model. Evaluation results demonstrate that our synthetic dataset is the most effective approach for improving LVLM performance on reading vertically written Japanese text. Our dataset and code are publicly available (https://github.com/llm-jp/synth-jdoc).

</details>


### [53] [Non-Uniform Quantisation for 3DGS Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28272)
*Bert Van hauwermeiren,Patrice Rondao Alface,Adrian Munteanu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Importance-weighted non-uniform quantisation with merging for 3D Gaussian Splatting enables state-of-the-art compression and standardisation readiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has high bitrate; need interoperable, standardised compression formats for practical deployment and interoperability within the 3DGS ecosystem.

Method: Develop a non-uniform quantisation scheme tailored to 3DGS that uses importance-weighted quantisation and eliminates post-voxelisation redundancy via importance weighted merging; claims compatibility with any point-cloud-based representation.

Result: Extensive benchmark evaluations demonstrate state-of-the-art compression performance.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme is compatible with any point-cloud-based representation and is proposed as a formal contribution to MPEG 3DGS compression standardisation activities.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has emerged as a powerful technique for novel view synthesis, yet its high bitrate requirements pose significant challenges for storage and transmission. To enable practical applications and ensure interoperability within the 3DGS ecosystem, standardised compression formats are essential. In this paper, we propose a novel non-uniform quantisation scheme specifically tailored for 3DGS models. Our approach adapts to the underlying data distribution by applying importance-weighted quantisation and eliminating post-voxelisation redundancy through importance weighted merging. Extensive evaluations on benchmark datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art compression performance. Furthermore, the proposed scheme is compatible with any point-cloud-based representation and is intended as a formal contribution to the upcoming MPEG 3DGS compression standardisation activities.

</details>


### [54] [GeoFF3D: Coordinate-Anchored Feed-Forward Reconstruction for Large-Scale UAV Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28288)
*Xiang Yang,Yongli Wang,Yunsheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoFF3D combines a coordinate-anchored, gravity-aligned model with a spatial large-scale reconstruction framework (SLRF) to enable scalable, robust 3D reconstruction for large-scale UAV mapping, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy and fast processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scale limitations of existing feed-forward 3D reconstruction for large UAV datasets: need scalable multi-chunk processing, reliable aggregation, and stable alignment (even with near-collinear trajectories).

Method: Georeferenced camera translations and optional geometric priors are used to predict poses and dense maps in a gravity-aligned Z-up frame. SLRF partitions images into spatially overlapping chunks, propagates shared-view priors, and aggregates local reconstructions hierarchically, while remaining compatible with different bounded-view models.

Result: Across nine aerial mapping blocks, F@5 improves from 0.829 (Pi3X + SLRF) to 0.877. On long UAVScenes sequences, F@5 reaches 0.848 (vs 0.687 for Pi3X + SLRF and 0.451 for the strongest SLAM/streaming baseline). Reconstructs 2,000 images in ~5 minutes.

Conclusion: GeoFF3D delivers scalable, robust large-scale UAV reconstruction with improved accuracy and speed; code available at the referenced GitHub repository.

Abstract: Existing feed-forward 3D reconstruction methods typically process a bounded number of images and recover cameras and geometry in local or internally normalized frames. Extending them to large-scale UAV mapping requires scalable multi-chunk processing and reliable aggregation, while full Sim(3) alignment can become unstable for near collinear trajectories. We present GeoFF3D, which combines a coordinate-anchored model with a spatial large-scale reconstruction framework (SLRF). The model uses georeferenced camera translations and optional geometric priors to predict camera poses and dense point maps directly in a gravity-aligned Z-up metric frame. SLRF partitions images into spatially overlapping chunks, propagates shared-view priors, and aggregates local reconstructions hierarchically, while remaining applicable to different bounded-view models. Across nine aerial mapping blocks, GeoFF3D achieves the best average reconstruction quality, improving F@5 from 0.829 for Pi3X + SLRF to 0.877. On long UAVScenes sequences, it reaches 0.848, compared with 0.687 for Pi3X + SLRF and 0.451 for the strongest evaluated SLAM/streaming baseline. GeoFF3D reconstructs 2,000 images in approximately five minutes, demonstrating scalable and robust large-scale UAV reconstruction.The code is available at https://github.com/yanxian-ll/GeoFF3D.

</details>


### [55] [FUSED: Forensic-Semantic Mixture-of-Experts for AI Inpainting Detection and Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28302)
*Anton Nuzhdin,Marcel Worring,Ivona Najdenkoska*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FUSED is a unified detector-localizer for AI-generated inpainting that fuses low-level forensic cues with high-level semantic features via a sparsely-gated Mixture-of-Experts, achieving state-of-the-art detection and localization with strong cross-generator generalization and transfer to held-out benchmarks; code is released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inpainting-based edits modify only localized image regions, while many AI-image detectors rely on global artifacts that vary across generators, leading to poor transfer under distribution shifts. A robust detector should localize the manipulated region and perform well across unseen generators.

Method: FUSED uses a sparsely-gated Mixture-of-Experts to combine low-level forensic cues with high-level semantic features. For each input, it jointly predicts an image-level manipulation score and a pixel-level mask of the inpainted area, adaptively prioritizing signals for each token. The model is evaluated on OpenSDID (cross-generator), with transfer tests to AutoSplice and CocoGlide.

Result: On OpenSDID, FUSED achieves the best average detection and localization, with the largest gains on unseen generators. The model transfers directly to AutoSplice and CocoGlide, more than doubling localization performance. Evaluations with and without the global artifact show all methods rely at least partly on the artifact, but FUSED remains strongest under both conditions.

Conclusion: FUSED provides a unified, robust approach to detecting and localizing AI-generated inpainting, demonstrating strong cross-generator generalization and transferability to held-out benchmarks; code and pretrained models are available.

Abstract: Diffusion-based inpainting models modify only a localized part of an image, while many AI-image detectors rely on global artifacts and do not localize. These artifacts vary across generators, limiting detector transfer under distribution shifts. Recent work shows that restoring the authentic pixels outside the inpainted region removes these cues and can degrade pretrained detectors. To address this, we present FUSED, a unified framework for the joint detection and localization of AI-generated inpainting. FUSED combines low-level forensic cues with high-level semantic features using a sparsely-gated Mixture-of-Experts architecture, enabling the model to adaptively prioritize the most relevant signal for each token. For each input, FUSED predicts both an image-level manipulation score and a pixel-level mask of the inpainted area. On the OpenSDID cross-generator benchmark, FUSED achieves the best average detection and localization, with the largest gains on unseen generators. The same model transfers directly to the held-out AutoSplice and CocoGlide benchmarks, more than doubling localization performance. Evaluating each held-out benchmark with and without the global generator artifact further shows that all evaluated methods, ours included, partly read the artifact as evidence of manipulation, and FUSED remains the strongest under both conditions. Code and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/AntonNuzhdin/FUSED.

</details>


### [56] [AIM: Anchor Identity Features, Then Match for Multimodal Large Language Model Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28312)
*Wonjun Lee,Jaehyuk Jang,Kangwook Ko,Hee-Seon Kim,Changick Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AIM is a two-stage method to forget identity-specific knowledge in multimodal LLMs without needing retain images at deletion time, by anchoring an identity-forgetting target with a universal visual prompt and aligning the vision encoder under a Fisher-based constraint to preserve general perception and other identities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy risks arise when MLLMs memorize identity-specific facts from fine-tuning data and deletion requests may require unlearning without access to retained images or ground-truth answers, a scenario common in real-world deployments.

Method: Two-stage approach: (1) anchor an identity-forgetting target using a universal visual prompt to encode the forgetting objective, (2) align the vision encoder to this target under a Fisher-based constraint to suppress identity knowledge while preserving non-deleted identities and general visual perception.

Result: Experiments show AIM achieves competitive identity forgetting while preserving non-deleted identities, prior knowledge, and visual perception on the same images.

Conclusion: AIM enables targeted identity unlearning in multimodal LMMs even when retain images are unavailable, balancing forgetting of identity-related information with preservation of overall visual perception and previously learned content.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can memorize identity-specific facts about people in their fine-tuning data, creating privacy risks when a person requests deletion. Existing MLLM unlearning methods often assume access to retain images or ground-truth answers during deletion, which is unrealistic in many practical scenarios. We study identity unlearning when retain images are unavailable at deletion time. Our analysis shows that identity and visual-perception questions occupy distinct regions in fine-tuned hidden states and are organized differently: identity questions cluster by person, whereas perception questions cluster by question type. This suggests that identity knowledge can be suppressed without erasing general visual perception. Building on this observation, we propose AIM, a two-stage method that anchors an identity-forgetting target with a universal visual prompt and then matches the vision encoder to that target under a Fisher-based constraint. Extensive experiments show that AIM achieves competitive identity forgetting while preserving non-deleted identities, prior knowledge, and visual perception on the same images.

</details>


### [57] [Conditional Visual Evidence Utility: State-Dependent Rank Reversals in Frozen Vision-Language Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28316)
*Yunxuan Fang,Xinhe Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Evidence importance in visual search is state-dependent; rank-order of cues can reverse with state, concentrating in overlapping-cue regimes; updating rankings after initial evidence can preserve decision utility across evaluators; supports conditional evidence-use over a single static ranking and suggests adaptive evidence selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test whether static importance scores suffice when evidence accumulates across states; quantify conditional marginal utility of color, shape, and texture evidence in a controlled compositional visual search using language-augmented models.

Method: Controlled compositional visual search exposing color, shape, and texture evidence independently; measure conditional marginal utility across acquisition states; held-out confirmation on 800 scenes using frozen OpenCLIP and SigLIP; multiple evidence-accumulation constructions and ten query wordings; assess robustness to query-wording and derangement; exploratory matched-first-action analysis to relate reranking to decision utility.

Result: Robust state-dependent rank reversals observed, concentrated in candidate-overlap regimes; reversals persist across accumulation constructions and wordings; disappear under query-scene derangement; reranking after first acquisition yields positive step-2 utility when decisions are cross-evaluated.

Conclusion: Evidence ordering is state-dependent; updating evidence ordering can preserve decision-relevant value across evaluator changes; findings motivate conditional evaluation of vision-language evidence use and provide targets for adaptive evidence-selection methods.

Abstract: Static importance scores compress visual evidence into a single ranking, but the value of remaining evidence can change after one cue has been observed. We study this possibility in controlled compositional visual search, where color, shape, and texture evidence can be independently exposed and their conditional marginal utility measured across acquisition states. In a held-out confirmation on 800 scenes, frozen OpenCLIP and SigLIP exhibit robust state-dependent rank reversals that concentrate in candidate-overlap regimes designed to induce ordering changes. The structure persists across two evidence-accumulation constructions and ten equivalent query wordings, but disappears under query-scene derangement. We also ask whether these reversals matter for decisions. In a post-confirmation exploratory matched-first-action analysis, reranking only after the first acquisition yields positive step-2 utility when decisions are selected under one evidence mode, wording, or backbone and evaluated under another. Together, these results show that evidence importance is state-dependent in this controlled setup and that updating an evidence ordering can retain decision-relevant value across evaluator changes. They motivate evaluating vision-language evidence use conditionally rather than through a single static ranking, while providing a measurable target for future adaptive evidence-selection methods.

</details>


### [58] [Abstract4D: A Large-Scale Dataset and Framework for Understanding the Visual Language of Abstract Art](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28339)
*Haowei Zhang,Yuanpei Zhao,Ji-Zhe Zhou,Mao Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces Abstract4D, the largest dataset of abstract paintings with rich metadata and multidimensional prompts; uses a hybrid human–VLM annotation pipeline; analyzes semantic structure via embedding visualizations and establishes benchmarks for classification, cross-modal retrieval, and text-to-image generation to evaluate AI perception of abstract visual language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap in AI perception and interpretation of abstract art by providing a large, richly annotated dataset and concrete tasks that capture perceptual attributes beyond object semantics.

Method: Assemble >120k abstract paintings with metadata and multidimensional prompts targeting perceptual attributes (form, color, texture, composition). Use a hybrid human–vision-language model (VLM) pipeline for quality-controlled annotations. Perform large-scale embedding visualizations to analyze semantic structure. Define benchmark tasks in classification, cross-modal retrieval, and text-to-image generation.

Result: Demonstrates that perceptual relationships organize artistic meaning within abstract art via embedding analyses. Establishes benchmark tasks that evaluate AI models’ ability to perceive and reproduce abstract visual language.

Conclusion: Abstract4D enables exploration and quantitative assessment of AI’s capacity to represent and interpret abstract art, facilitating future research in computational perception of abstract visual language.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence can classify artistic styles and synthesize images, but it still lacks a model of the visual language that gives art meaning. Abstract painting minimizes object semantics and foregrounds structural cues, making it an ideal testbed for computational perception. We introduce \textbf{Abstract4D}, the largest dataset of abstract paintings to date: more than 120,000 images paired with rich metadata and multi-dimensional prompts that capture each work's perceptual attributes---\textit{form, color, texture, and composition}. Annotations are produced by a hybrid human--VLM pipeline for quality and consistency. Using Abstract4D, we (i) analyze the semantic structure of abstract art through large-scale embedding visualization, uncovering how perceptual relationships organize artistic meaning, and (ii) establish benchmark tasks for classification, cross-modal retrieval, and text-to-image generation to evaluate how AI models perceive and reproduce abstract visual language. Together, these analyses demonstrate how Abstract4D enables both exploration and quantitative assessment of AI's ability to represent and interpret abstract art.

</details>


### [59] [Cross-Spectral Dense Correspondence for Multimodal Spectral Medical Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28341)
*Eric L. Wisotzky,Jost Triller,Simon W. Härtl,Oliver T. Bruns,Peter Eisert,Anna Hilsmann*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Sensor-agnostic cross-spectral modulation and synthetic benchmarks enable robust dense cross-spectral correspondence under spectral mismatch, improving backbones while preserving RGB performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense cross-spectral correspondence is impeded by non-overlapping spectral sensitivities causing wavelength-dependent differences and ambiguous ground truth. Traditional RGB-supervised data is insufficient for alignment across modalities; a data-augmentation protocol and benchmarks are needed to bridge the spectral gap.

Method: Introduce a sensor-agnostic cross-spectral modulation protocol with intensity input projection on established benchmarks, and a synthetic cross-spectral correspondence benchmark simulating radiometric differences. Evaluate multiple dense correspondence backbones trained under this unified protocol. Ablations include view-dependent channel selection and nonlinear radiometric transformations to assess robustness.

Result: The protocol yields substantial improvements under severe spectral mismatch across modern dense correspondence backbones, while preserving RGB benchmark performance. Ablations show complementary benefits from view-dependent channel selection and nonlinear radiometric transforms; primary limitation lies in training distribution misalignment rather than model capacity. Qualitative medical-system evaluations confirm practical relevance for spatially coherent spectral fusion in HSI workflows.

Conclusion: Data augmentation via cross-spectral modulation and synthetic radiometric differences effectively enhances cross-spectral dense correspondence. Training distribution mismatch, not architectural capacity, limits performance. The approach enables robust spectral fusion in multimodal imaging pipelines and can be extended to other cross-spectral tasks.

Abstract: Precise dense correspondence is a fundamental prerequisite for multimodal spectral imaging systems that fuse disparate wavelength ranges for subsequent analysis in medical and scientific imaging. Corresponding image points are often observed with non-overlapping spectral sensitivities, leading to wavelength-dependent contrast changes, intensity inversions, and appearance shifts for which dense ground truth is difficult to obtain and conventional RGB-based training data provides only limited supervision. We address this data gap by introducing a sensor-agnostic cross-spectral modulation protocol on established correspondence benchmarks with intensity input projection, and by proposing a synthetic cross-spectral correspondence benchmark simulating physically plausible radiometric differences. Evaluation on several modern dense correspondence backbones trained with our unified cross-spectral protocol showed substantial improvements under severe spectral mismatch while maintaining performance on standard RGB benchmarks. Ablation experiments show that view-dependent channel selection and nonlinear radiometric transformations provide complementary robustness, indicating that the primary limitation of existing models is not their structural matching capacity but the mismatch between training distribution and spectral characteristics of the target image pair. Qualitative evaluations on heterogeneous medical spectral acquisition systems demonstrate the practical relevance of the proposed training data augmentation protocol as an enabler for spatially coherent spectral fusion in HSI workflows.

</details>


### [60] [Denoising-Aware Temporal Point Cloud Completion for 3D Crop Architecture Recovery and Phenotypic Trait Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28343)
*Mrudul Mittal,Soumyashree Kar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Synthetic, temporally evolving plant point clouds (SynthCrop4D) enable benchmarking of temporal 3D reconstruction; a two-stage denoise-then-temporal completion pipeline (Adaptive Temporal PoinTr) improves recovery of self-occluded regions, validated on synthetic and Pheno4D with strong geometry and hull-volume metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lack of datasets with complete geometric ground truth across growth stages hampers development and evaluation of temporal completion methods for high-throughput phenotyping.

Method: Procedurally generated SynthCrop4D with controllable noise/occlusion and complete geometry. A two-stage pipeline: (1) denoise raw laser-scanned point clouds; (2) Adaptive Temporal PoinTr reconstructs the current stage (t) using information from the previous stage (t-1) to recover regions missing due to self-occlusion. Evaluations on SynthCrop4D and Pheno4D (tomato and maize) with and without denoising. Metrics include Chamfer Distance, F-Score, and hull-volume MAEs.

Result: Best configuration achieves Chamfer Distance 0.0061 on SynthCrop4D (Temporal PoinTr + Mamba-DG) and F-Score 0.2080 on Pheno4D (Vanilla PoinTr + Mamba-DG). Hull-volume MAEs: 0.021 (synthetic) and 0.343 (real). Demonstrates denoising benefits and utility of completed point clouds for phenotypic traits (height, canopy width, convex hull volume).

Conclusion: SynthCrop4D provides a benchmark and methodology for temporal plant reconstruction; denoising substantially enhances temporal completion, enabling accurate phenotypic trait extraction and supporting high-throughput phenotyping workflows.

Abstract: High-throughput phenotyping depends on accurate 3D reconstruction of plants across growth stages, yet the development and evaluation of temporal completion methods are limited by the lack of datasets with complete geometric ground truth. To address this challenge, we introduce SynthCrop4D, a procedurally generated synthetic dataset of temporally evolving plant point clouds that provides controllable noise, occlusion, and complete plant geometry for benchmarking reconstruction methods. Using this dataset, we evaluate a two-stage pipeline that combines spatial denoising and temporal point cloud completion. First, a denoising module removes structural artifacts from raw laser-scanned point clouds. The resulting data are then processed by an Adaptive Temporal PoinTr model that reconstructs the current growth stage (t) using information from the previous stage (t-1), enabling recovery of regions missing due to self-occlusion. We evaluate the proposed framework on both SynthCrop4D and the real-world Pheno4D dataset (tomato and maize) under settings with and without denoising. Results show that denoising substantially improves reconstruction quality, with the best configuration achieving a Chamfer Distance of 0.0061 on SynthCrop4D (Temporal PoinTr + Mamba-DG) and an F-Score of 0.2080 on Pheno4D (Vanilla PoinTr + Mamba-DG). We further demonstrate the use of completed point clouds for phenotypic trait extraction, including plant height, canopy width, and convex hull volume, obtaining hull-volume MAEs of 0.021 on synthetic data and 0.343 on real data. Together, SynthCrop4D and the proposed pipeline provide a benchmark and methodology for temporal plant reconstruction and high-throughput crop phenotyping.

</details>


### [61] [Real-Time Musculoskeletal Surrogates for Pediatric Cerebral Palsy: a Credibility Pilot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28371)
*Mohammad Arif Ul Alam*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Subject-conditioned causal neural surrogate for pediatric CP gait yields high-fidelity musculotendon length predictions with millisecond inference; shows leakage-free evaluation and a credibility framework, while revealing force modeling and epistemic uncertainty as key challenges for clinically credible digital twins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable real-time, subject-specific, credible musculoskeletal surrogates to support personalized CP rehabilitation, with low latency and calibrated uncertainty.

Method: OpenSim-derived static parameters, temporal joint kinematics, true muscle capacities, and training-time perturbations to train a subject-conditioned causal neural surrogate. Validation uses leave-one-subject-out on six development subjects and a frozen test on three locked subjects. Assess musculotendon lengths and muscle forces; compare to direct force estimation; conduct Monte Carlo credibility with +/-5% anthropometry variation.

Result: Musculotendon length prediction achieves R^2 ≈ 0.92 in development and ~0.95 on locked subjects, nRMSE < 8%. Inference latency is sub-millisecond to a few milliseconds. Direct muscle-force estimation is unstable at this scale. Monte Carlo credibility shows severely overconfident nominal 90% intervals (≈4% force coverage and <1% MT-length coverage) under small perturbations.

Conclusion: Establishes a leakage-free evaluation and credibility framework for pediatric MSK surrogates; identifies force modeling and epistemic uncertainty as central next challenges for clinically credible digital twins.

Abstract: Real-time musculoskeletal (MSK) surrogates could support personalized rehabilitation for children with cerebral palsy (CP), but their credibility depends on subject-wise evaluation, low inference latency, and calibrated uncertainty. We develop a subject-conditioned causal neural surrogate using OpenSim-derived static parameters, temporal joint kinematics, true muscle capacities, and training-only perturbations. On a real pediatric CP gait dataset comprising nine children, we use leave-one-subject-out validation on six development subjects and evaluate a frozen configuration once on three locked test subjects. The surrogate accurately reproduces musculotendon lengths (R-square = 0.92 in development validation and approximately 0.95 on locked subjects; nRMSE < 8%) while requiring only sub-millisecond to few-millisecond neural inference, well below a 100 ms interactive-rehabilitation target. In contrast, direct muscle-force estimation remains unstable at this small, heterogeneous scale: pooled metrics can overstate within-subject, per-muscle accuracy. A Monte Carlo credibility pilot further shows that propagating only +/-5% anthropometry and muscle-capacity variation produces severely overconfident nominal 90% intervals (approximately 4% force coverage and below 1% MT-length coverage). These results establish a leakage-free evaluation and credibility framework for pediatric MSK surrogates, while identifying force modeling and epistemic uncertainty as the central next challenges for clinically credible digital twins.

</details>


### [62] [Semantic Head Specialization Guides Hybrid ViT Attention for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28383)
*Chenhong He,Lei Li,Shicheng Li,Hanglong Lv,Lingpeng Kong,Qi Liu,Tong Yang,Shuhuai Ren*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Semantic Head Specialization (SHS) reveals object- vs background-specialist ViT heads; SHS-Index quantifies specialization and tracks performance, guiding three design principles to create Ariadne Attention, achieving full-attention performance with 6.5x less compute across 22 tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand why hybrid attention patterns in ViTs outperform or fail in multimodal LLMs; fill gap left by obscure attention behaviors; introduce measurable SHS to diagnose and guide hybrids.

Method: Empirically analyze ViT attention heads, define SHS, compute SHS-Index, compare full-attention vs chunk-window ViTs, identify three shaping factors (window interaction, token serialization, local softmax allocation), design Ariadne hybrid attention.

Result: SHS-Index differentiates full vs chunk-window ViTs and tracks downstream performance; three factors shape SHS; Ariadne matches full attention on 22 image/video tasks with 6.5x less attention compute.

Conclusion: Head specialization is a measurable property for diagnosing and designing principled hybrid ViT attention at multimodal-LLM scale.

Abstract: Hybrid attention dominates frontier LLMs, yet Vision Transformers (ViTs) in multimodal LLMs lack a satisfactory hybrid design, with no consensus on why certain attention patterns work better. To fill this gap, we study ViT attention heads and find they differentiate into object- and background-specialist roles, a pattern most pronounced under full attention; we call this Semantic Head Specialization (SHS). We propose SHS-Index to quantify this specialization, show that it distinguishes full-attention from chunk-window ViTs, and find that it strongly tracks downstream benchmark performance. We then identify three structural factors that shape SHS---window interaction, token serialization, and local softmax allocation---and use them as design principles for hybrid attention. Guided by these factors, we design Ariadne Attention, a hybrid that matches full attention on 22 image and video tasks at 6.5x less attention compute. Our findings establish head specialization as a measurable property for diagnosing and designing principled hybrid ViT attention at the multimodal-LLM scale.

</details>


### [63] [GraspHOI: Full-Body 3D Human-Object Reconstruction with Finger-Level Grasps from a Single In-the-Wild Image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28386)
*Semin Kim,Haechan Shin,Jongyoo Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GraspHOI: monocular full-body 3D HOI with explicit finger-level grasp optimization and category-agnostic object reconstruction, yielding improved placement, hand accuracy, and contact plausibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing monocular HOI methods struggle to enforce physically plausible finger-object contacts and rely on predefined object categories or meshes; a need exists for unified, category-agnostic, finger-aware 3D reconstruction from a single image.

Method: Reconstructs body, hands, and object separately and aligns them in metric camera space via depth-based registration and image-space alignment. Uses occlusion-aware palmar correspondences to seat the object against the grasping hand and employs contact-aware optimization to refine arm and finger articulation for surface contact with minimal penetration, without predefined meshes or category vocabulary.

Result: Evaluated across four benchmarks and six baselines, GraspHOI shows improvements in relative human-object placement, hand accuracy, and contact plausibility.

Conclusion: First framework to reconstruct a full-body 3D HOI from a single image with explicit finger articulation and category-agnostic object reconstruction; an end-to-end approach with public code release planned.

Abstract: Existing monocular full-body 3D human-object interaction (HOI) methods do not combine explicit finger-level grasp optimization with category-agnostic object reconstruction. Despite plausible body-object configurations, their fingers may float from or penetrate objects instead of forming a grasp. We present GraspHOI, the first framework that reconstructs a full-body 3D HOI from a single image while explicitly optimizing finger articulation against the reconstructed object. GraspHOI recovers object geometry directly, without predefined meshes or a fixed category vocabulary. It reconstructs the body, hands, and object separately, aligning them in metric camera space via depth-based registration and image-space alignment. Occlusion-aware palmar correspondences seat the object against the grasping hand, and contact-aware optimization refines arm and finger articulation to form surface contact without excessive penetration. Across four benchmarks and six baselines, GraspHOI improves relative human-object placement, hand accuracy, and contact plausibility. Full pipeline code will be released.

</details>


### [64] [How Far Can 5,500 Hours of Driving Take You? A Scaling Law Analysis of Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28404)
*Victor Besnier,Anh-Quan Cao,Elias Ramzi,Spyros Gidaris,Tuan-Hung Vu,Andrei Bursuc,Eloi Zablocki,Matthieu Cord*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Scaling laws for video diffusion models in autonomous driving show longer training beats larger models under fixed compute, but bigger models win with more compute/data; a 9B-parameter model sets new open-source SOTA on nuScenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Driving video data is costly, privacy-bound, and not web-scale. The paper seeks practical scaling laws to allocate limited compute/data effectively and maximize performance from a fixed corpus.

Method: Systematic scaling study of video diffusion models trained from scratch on driving data, spanning model sizes from 1M to 9B parameters and training exposures up to 5,500 hours; evaluation on nuScenes; open-source release of code/pretrained models; NATIX data release planned.

Result: Validation loss follows power laws in both model size and training exposure. Training longer yields greater loss reductions than increasing model size under fixed compute; larger models reach lower asymptotic loss. A 9B parameter model achieves open-source SOTA on driving video generation (nuScenes).

Conclusion: For fixed compute budgets, prioritize longer training; when compute/data permit, scale model size to push asymptotic performance. The study provides practical guidance for budgeting and demonstrates the feasibility of large driving-video diffusion models.

Abstract: Video generation for autonomous driving cannot follow the web-scale route: driving data is expensive to collect, bound by privacy requirements, and cannot be scraped at will, so models must make the most of a fixed corpus. We present a systematic scaling-law study of video diffusion models trained from scratch on driving data: a family of models from 1M to 9B parameters, trained at different exposures on up to 5,500 hours of driving. Validation loss follows consistent power laws in both model size and training exposure, answering the questions that shape a training budget: whether compute is better spent on longer training or on a larger model, and whether more data is needed. Loss improves much faster with training exposure than with model size, making longer training the most effective way to improve a fixed model under limited compute. However, larger models continue to achieve lower asymptotic loss, so compute-optimal scaling still favors increasing model size when sufficient compute and data are available. Guided by these laws, we train a 9B-parameter model, to our knowledge the largest video diffusion model trained from scratch on driving data: it sets a new open-source state of the art for driving video generation, as measured on nuScenes. Our code and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/valeoai/VATIX. NATIX is separately releasing the underlying driving data in stages.

</details>


### [65] [Post-Training VLMs for Video Mistake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28406)
*Federico Spurio,Olga Zatsarynna,Lars Doorenbos,Emad Bahrami,Gianpiero Francesca,Juergen Gall*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes the MD-VQA benchmark and a video-language-model post-training approach with a tailored reward to detect mistakes in instructional videos, aiming for generalizable mistake detection beyond closed-set tasks; shows improved results and releases code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mistake detection in instructional videos is hampered by closed-set assumptions; a generalizable notion of mistakes across procedures is needed for broader applicability.

Method: Introduce the Mistake Detection Video Question Answering (MD-VQA) protocol and benchmark; develop a video-language-model post-training method using a reward function that emphasizes detecting discrepancies between an instruction and its video, and evaluate against zero-shot, fine-tuned, and other post-training baselines.

Result: The proposed method outperforms baselines, with strong generalization to unseen procedures (e.g., up to 11.6% improvement on EP-VQA); code and benchmark released at the provided repository.

Conclusion: Presents a generalizable framework for mistake detection in videos via the MD-VQA protocol and a VLM post-training approach, demonstrating notable generalization to unseen tasks and setting a new direction for future research.

Abstract: Human mistakes are inevitable when following instructions, yet they can lead to severe consequences. As such, there has been an increased interest in developing methods for detecting mistakes in videos, with current methods mostly focusing on closed-set protocols. While successful in controlled settings, the closed-set assumption limits their wider applicability, as any changes to the task require collecting new data and re-training models. Instead, we argue that mistake detection methods should learn the general concept of a mistake, rather than overfitting to step-specific details. To reflect this, we introduce the Mistake Detection Video Question Answering (MD-VQA) protocol and accompanying benchmark. MD-VQA tests whether methods can discern if a step was executed correctly with respect to its description, for both seen and unseen actions. To address this important challenge, we propose the first video-language-model post-training technique for mistake detection. Our method uses a tailored reward function to encourage the model to identify discrepancies between an instruction and the corresponding video. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that this approach outperforms zero-shot, supervised fine-tuning, and post-training baselines. Notably, our method generalizes especially well to unseen procedures, for instance, with an improvement of up to 11.6% over the best-performing baseline on EP-VQA, paving the way toward general mistake detection. We release our code and benchmark at https://github.com/FedeSpu/mstk.

</details>


### [66] [Lossy Event Compression: From Event Stream Distortion to Task Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28429)
*Zahra Rezaee,Catarina Brites,João Ascenso*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Two complementary event-data compression pipelines (aggregation-based and frame-free point cloud) are proposed and evaluated under a unified task-driven framework; five classification-based distortion metrics predict downstream task performance, enabling efficient codec optimization without repetitive task-specific testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event cameras generate massive, asynchronous data; existing metrics poorly predict how compression degrades task performance; need a unified framework to relate distortion to application-level outcomes.

Method: 1) aggregation-based pipeline converting events to polarity histograms for JPEG 2000 compression; 2) frame-free 3D point-cloud pipeline using G-PCC octree codec; evaluation across four tasks (video reconstruction, object detection, optical flow, asynchronous feature tracking) within a unified framework; application of five distortion metrics.

Result: The proposed metrics reliably predict task degradation across coding frameworks; demonstration that distortion assessment can replace repeated task-specific evaluations.

Conclusion: Task-driven distortion metrics are effective surrogates for codec optimization in event data, guiding future coding solutions.

Abstract: Event cameras generate asynchronous, sparse data streams with microsecond temporal resolution, but in moderate-to-high motion scenes they can produce as many as hundreds of millions of events per second, creating significant bandwidth and storage challenges. Lossy compression is therefore essential for practical deployment, yet existing event stream distortion metrics fail to reliably predict compression-induced degradation at the task level, forcing codec optimization to rely on expensive task-specific evaluations. To address this gap, this paper introduces two fundamentally different event compression pipelines: i) an aggregation-based pipeline that converts the event stream into polarity-based histogram frames for compression with the conventional image codec JPEG 2000, and ii) a frame-free point cloud-based pipeline that codes events natively as 3D points using the octree-based codec G-PCC. Both pipelines are then assessed within a unified task-driven evaluation framework that relates event stream distortion to downstream application performance across four representative tasks: i) video reconstruction, ii) object detection, iii) optical flow estimation, and a delay-sensitive task iv) asynchronous feature tracking under a reference-relative protocol. Building on this framework, five classification-based distortion metrics are applied to event compression for the first time, to the best of the authors' knowledge, and benchmarked against existing event stream metrics. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed metrics reliably predict compression-induced task degradation across different coding frameworks. This demonstrates that event stream distortion assessment can be an efficient alternative to repeated task-specific evaluation, providing direct guidance for the development and optimization of future event data coding solutions.

</details>


### [67] [Prompt-Guided Interactive Segmentation of Interstitial Lung Disease in Thoracic CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28453)
*Vasilis Dedousis,Lubnaa Abdur Rahman,Lorenzo Brigatο,Ethan Dack,Andreas Christe,Christoph Frank,Manuela Funke-Chambour,Justus Roos,Adrian Huber,Lukas Ebner,Stavroula Mougiakakou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Fine-tune MedSAM2 for interactive 3D ILD segmentation on thoracic CT using multiple prompts; full-model fine-tuning beats MedSAM2; BBox prompts strongest; end-to-end workflow with automatic prior and radiologist refinements; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ILD segmentation requires accurate quantitative assessment but dense annotations and static predictions hinder clinical adoption; interactive promptable models promise refinement with limited annotations; ILD adaptation of prompt-based foundation models is underexplored.

Method: Investigate three fine-tuning strategies (unspecified) and evaluate four prompt types: bounding-boxes, point, lasso, and scribble on a 3D ILD segmentation task across seven ILD patterns; use thoracic CT; measure Dice; compare with MedSAM2; propose an end-to-end workflow initialized from automatic segmentation and refined by radiologist prompts; release weights and plug-ins.

Result: Full-model fine-tuning yielded the best Dice improvement (+4.7pp) over MedSAM2; BBox prompts yielded strongest performance; non-native prompts (lasso, scribble) also effective; designed a proof-of-concept end-to-end workflow; code and plug-ins available at the given repo.

Conclusion: Prompt-guided foundation models can be effectively adapted for ILD segmentation; full fine-tuning with prompt-aware interaction enhances accuracy; interactive workflows with radiologist prompts are viable, facilitating refinement from an automatic prior; contribution includes model weights, plugins, and evaluation on diverse ILD patterns.

Abstract: Accurate segmentation of interstitial lung disease (ILD) patterns is essential for quantitative disease assessment and longitudinal monitoring. However, existing approaches remain limited by relying on dense annotations and producing static predictions that cannot be refined, motivating interactive approaches. While promptable models show promise in interactive segmentation, their adaptation to ILDs remains largely unexplored. To address this gap, we investigate prompt-guided foundation models for ILD refinement and present, to the best of our knowledge, the first adaptation of MedSAM2 for interactive 3D ILD segmentation on thoracic CT. We investigate three fine-tuning strategies and multiple clinically motivated prompts: bounding-boxes (BBox), point, lasso, and scribble. On a dataset spanning seven ILD patterns and healthy lung tissue, full model fine-tuning performed best, improving the average Dice score by 4.7 percentage points over MedSAM2.While BBox prompts achieve the strongest performance, non-native MedSAM2 interactions such as lasso and scribble prompts also prove effective. Finally, we present and evaluate a proof-of-concept end-to-end workflow in which MedSAM2 is initialized from an automatic segmentation prior and subsequently refined using radiologist prompts. Model weights and plug-ins made available at: https://github.com/AIHNlab/ILD-SemiSegTool.

</details>


### [68] [ARC-CT: Anatomy-Routed Contrastive Vision-Language Learning for 3D Chest CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28455)
*Huseyin Umut Isik,Mehmet Alp Ozaydin,Sila Kurugol,Şeyda Ertekin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Region-aware contrastive learning for 3D chest CT (ARC-CT) that localizes evidence with AnatomyQFormer, uses a label-Jaccard soft InfoNCE to share findings across studies, and aligns organ-level features with LLM-extracted organ texts; achieves 0.86 macro AUC on 18 abnormalities with a 3D ResNet-18 backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global contrastive learning on chest CT dilutes small/localized abnormalities and treats other scans as negatives, which misrepresents shared findings across studies.

Method: ARC-CT comprises: (1) AnatomyQFormer for localizing evidence via organ-masked queries; (2) a label-Jaccard soft InfoNCE objective that blends one-hot targets with label-set overlap to reduce false negatives; (3) an organ-level alignment loss that connects mask-pooled visual features to organ-specific report text extracted offline with an LLM; operates without manual annotations or bounding boxes.

Result: Achieves a 0.86 mask-free macro AUC across 18 abnormalities using a compact 3D ResNet-18 backbone, outperforming comparable efficient baselines and several larger transformer models.

Conclusion: ARC-CT shows that combining region-aware localization, soft-label contrastive objectives, and organ-level multimodal alignment yields strong performance in 3D chest CT abnormality detection without manual labels; code and weights available.

Abstract: Contrastive vision-language learning uses paired chest CT volumes and radiology reports to learn abnormality classifiers without manually annotated labels. However, two characteristics of chest CT challenge conventional global contrastive learning. First, many critical abnormalities are small or anatomically localized, and pooling an en- tire volume into a single embedding may dilute their visual evidence. Second, the standard contrastive objective treats every other scan in a batch as a negative. Because many chest CTs share abnormalities, this objective incorrectly pushes co-positive pairs apart. We propose Anatomy-Routed Contrastive Learning for 3D Chest CT (ARC-CT), a region-aware framework that addresses these limitations using only la- bels extracted from reports by an LLM, with no manual annotations or bounding boxes. ARC-CT combines three components: (1) an Anato- myQFormer localizing evidence via queries constrained by automatically generated organ masks; (2) a label-Jaccard soft InfoNCE objective in- tegrating the standard one-hot target with the label-set overlap of each pair, which reduces false-negative penalties between studies that share clinical findings; and (3) an organ-level alignment loss connecting mask- pooled visual features to organ-specific report text extracted offline with a large language model. ARC-CT achieves a 0.86 mask-free macro AUC across 18 abnormalities using a compact 3D ResNet-18 backbone. Over- all, ARC-CT outperforms both comparable efficient baselines and sev- eral larger transformer models. Our code and weights are available at https://github.com/arc-ct/arc-ct.

</details>


### [69] [LayerRecall: A State-Conditioned Memory Router for Long-Horizon Consistency in Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28460)
*Yixuan Ding,Jiahao Kong,Wei Huang,Ruijie Quan,Yi Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LayerRecall with CHPM enables selective, layer-wise memory retrieval to improve long-range coherence in autoregressive video diffusion, achieving state-of-the-art long-video performance with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long videos require reusing distant cues; existing recency-based caches and memory mechanisms fail to leverage long-range history effectively; there is a need to decide not just what to retrieve but also where to apply it.

Method: LayerRecall: a current-conditioned, layer-selective memory router that retrieves relevant historical K/V states and injects them only into memory-sensitive backbone layers while preserving local attention elsewhere. Cross-Horizon Prediction Matching (CHPM): supervises the bounded-memory router using a privileged long-context reference in prediction space to reduce data requirements for memory allocation labels.

Result: LayerRecall achieves the best overall results on MemoBench and MovieBench and matches the backbone on VBench-Long, indicating stronger long-range recovery without sacrificing local continuity. Qualitative analyses show memory-guided self-correction, cross-backbone portability, and negligible inference overhead.

Conclusion: Selective, layer-aware memory integration combined with horizon-aware supervision effectively enhances long-range memory in video diffusion, enabling better long-video fidelity with preserved local dynamics and efficient inference.

Abstract: Autoregressive video diffusion enables scalable long-video generation by producing chunks from a bounded recent context. While recency-based caching preserves local continuity, it evicts historical cues needed when subjects, objects, scenes, or attributes reappear. Existing memory mechanisms expose models to nonlocal history, but access alone does not ensure effective use. Our analysis reveals that video DiT layers exhibit distinct preferences for current, recent, and distant context, suggesting that long-range memory requires deciding both what to retrieve and where to use it. We introduce LayerRecall, a current-conditioned, layer-selective memory router that retrieves relevant historical K/V states and injects them only into backbone-specific memory-sensitive layers while preserving local attention elsewhere. To reduce reliance on scarce high-quality long-horizon videos and explicit memory-allocation labels, we further propose Cross-Horizon Prediction Matching (CHPM), which uses a privileged long-context reference to supervise the bounded-memory router in prediction space. Across 100 multi-shot evaluation prompts, LayerRecall achieves the best overall results on MemoBench and MovieBench while matching its backbone on VBench-Long, demonstrating stronger long-range recovery without sacrificing local continuity. Qualitative analyses further reveal memory-guided self-correction, whereby initially mismatched local attributes return to their historical appearance without resetting ongoing motion or scene structure. Additional analyses show cross-backbone portability and negligible inference overhead.

</details>


### [70] [Anatomy-Aware Promptable Segmentation with Online Interactive Training for AUTOPET V](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28461)
*Pablo Lozano-Jimenez,Sergio Romero-Tapiador,Ruben Tolosana*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Anatomy-aware, interactive nnU-Net for whole-body PET/CT lesion segmentation with tracer routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incorporate anatomical context and multi-tracer handling to reduce false positives and improve segmentation when FDG/PSMA uptake patterns vary, without requiring tracer info at inference.

Method: Two-stage training: (i) pre-training for strong initialization; (ii) online interactive stage using scribble prompts to refine predictions. Organ supervision with a shared head predicts lesions and organs from the same features. Tracer routing via a classifier (image processing + random forest on coronal MIP features) to select a combined FDG+PSMA or PSMA-only model. Evaluation via four-fold cross-validation.

Result: Organ-supervised model yielded the best and most stable performance; interactive prompts monotonically improved Dice with each prompt; PSMA-specific training gave the strongest tracer-wise results.

Conclusion: Anatomical context with organ supervision and interactive prompting enhances whole-body lesion segmentation in multi-tracer PET/CT, with tracer-aware routing improving specialization.

Abstract: We present an anatomy-aware, promptable model for whole-body lesion segmentation in FDG and PSMA PET/CT, developed for the AUTOPET V challenge. The proposed method is built as family of nnU-Net-based models and trained in two stages: i) a pre-training stage that produces a strong initial segmentation, and ii) an online interactive stage that learns to exploit scribble prompts, refining the prediction over successive interactions. Anatomical context is incorporated through organ supervision using a single shared head that predicts lesions and organs from the same features, which reduces false positives arising from physiological uptake. Also as the tracer (i.e., FDG/PSMA) is not provided at inference, we add a tracer classifier based on image processing and a random forest over coronal MIP features, routing each study to a combined FDG+PSMA model or to a PSMA-specific model. Across four-fold cross-validation the organ-supervised model achieves the best and most stable performance, the interactive stage improves the Dice score monotonically with each prompt, and PSMA-specific training yields the strongest tracer-wise results.

</details>


### [71] [Learning the Target Priors Before Image Translation: A Decoupled Training Paradigm for Cross-Modal Image Translation in Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28517)
*Keyan Hu,Mingtao Wang,Ziyu Zhou,Tiandong Shi,Haifeng Li,Ji Qi,Chao Tao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A prior-first framework (LTP-BIT) decouples target-domain prior learning from cross-modal translation, achieving state-of-the-art SAR/NIR-to-RGB translation with minimal task-specific parameters and reduced paired data requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is an intrinsic asymmetry: the target-domain prior can be learned from abundant unpaired imagery, while cross-modal correspondence for translation requires scarce paired data. Formal analysis via conditional-score and denoising-risk motivates decoupling these tasks.

Method: Learn a large-scale unpaired target-domain prior first; preserve backbone weights; apply P-DART, a parameter-efficient dual-stream architecture, to learn source-conditioned control. The framework distinguishes the impact of prior matching (realism) versus conditional adaptation (fidelity).

Result: State-of-the-art performance on SAR-to-RGB and NIR-to-RGB benchmarks with only 9.81% task-specific parameters. On QXS-SAROPT, achieves near-full fidelity using just 25% of paired samples.

Conclusion: The prior-first paradigm effectively decouples prior estimation from cross-modal translation, improving realism via prior matching and fidelity via conditional adaptation, with strong data and parameter efficiency.

Abstract: Cross-modal image translation in remote sensing must preserve source-observed content while matching the target-domain distribution. Existing methods jointly learn the target prior and cross-modal dependence from scarce paired data, overlooking a key asymmetry: only the latter intrinsically requires cross-modal correspondence. We formalize this distinction through conditional-score and denoising-risk analyses and propose Learning the Target Priors Before Image Translation (LTP-BIT), a prior-first paradigm that decouples the two learning tasks. LTP-BIT first learns a target-domain generative prior from large-scale unpaired imagery, then retains the pretrained backbone weights and learns source-conditioned control through P-DART, a parameter-efficient dual-stream architecture. Controlled experiments show that prior matching and scaling primarily improve target-domain realism, whereas instance fidelity relies more strongly on conditional adaptation. LTP-BIT achieves state-of-the-art performance across SAR-to-RGB and NIR-to-RGB benchmarks using only 9.81% task-specific parameters. On QXS-SAROPT, it retains near-full-data instance fidelity with only 25% of the paired samples.

</details>


### [72] [Texture Image Classification Using DWT AlexNet Feature Fusion and Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28524)
*Arun D. Kulkarni*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A hybrid texture classifier that fuses Discrete Wavelet Transform features with AlexNet deep features to improve texture classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Texture images contain rich multiscale spatial-frequency information. Handcrafted features capture local texture patterns but may miss complex structures; deep features learn discriminative representations but may not fully exploit multiscale frequency content. A fusion of both can yield more robust texture representations.

Method: Compute DWT-based features to capture multiscale frequency information, extract deep features from AlexNet for the same images, fuse the feature sets at the feature level (e.g., concatenation or integration), and train a DNN classifier on the fused representation (DWT_AlexNet_DNN).

Result: The proposed fusion framework shows improved texture classification performance over using DWT or AlexNet features alone, indicating complementary benefits of combining multiscale frequency information with deep representations.

Conclusion: Hybrid fusion of DWT and AlexNet features is effective for texture image classification and demonstrates the value of integrating frequency-domain and deep-learned representations.

Abstract: Texture image classification plays a significant role in computer vision applications, including industrial inspection, medical image analysis, remote sensing, and object recognition. Handcrafted features can capture local texture characteristics but may have limited capability to represent complex visual patterns. In contrast, deep learning models automatically learn discriminative representations but may not fully exploit the multiscale spatial-frequency information inherent in texture images. This paper proposes a hybrid feature fusion framework, termed DWT_AlexNet_DNN, which combines Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT) features with deep features extracted using AlexNet for texture image classification.

</details>


### [73] [Video Generative Models as Geometry Learner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28549)
*Haosen Yang,Jifei Song,Zhensong Zhang,Xiatian Zhu,Jiankang Deng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoNeXt repurposes pretrained video diffusion models for joint image-geometry estimation by framing it as next-frame prediction, achieving zero-shot monocular depth and surface normals with high data efficiency and strong SOTA-like performance across datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diffusion-based geometry estimation frameworks either train task-specific geometry heads independently (missing cross-target correlations) or fine-tune diffusion backbones heavily (demanding large labeled data). A principled, data-efficient, unified approach leveraging video priors could exploit correlations among geometry targets and reduce data needs.

Method: Utilize pretrained video diffusion models as a unified framework for geometry estimation, formulating learning as next-frame prediction. Adapt the model to jointly handle images and geometry targets (image <-> geometry), yielding GeoNeXt which inherits video priors and enables data-efficient joint modeling of vision and geometry.

Result: Extensive experiments show zero-shot monocular depth and surface normal estimation across diverse datasets, outperforming both task-specific and unified generative baselines with substantially less training data. The method rivals discriminative SOTA that is trained on orders of magnitude more data and achieves strong results on multiple benchmarks.

Conclusion: GeNeXt demonstrates that video diffusion priors can be repurposed to provide a principled, data-efficient solution for joint image-geometry estimation, offering competitive performance without large-scale supervision and highlighting the value of cross-target correlations in geometry tasks.

Abstract: Recent generative approaches to geometry estimation adapt pretrained image diffusion models and treat the task as image-conditioned generation. Leveraging off-the-shelf image diffusion models, they either (i) train task-specific geometry models (for depth and surface normal estimation) independently, losing the opportunity of exploring the intrinsic correlation of these geometric targets, or (ii) jointly fine-tune modified image diffusion backbones (e.g., altered self-attention), which typically demands substantial labeled data. To overcome these limitations in a principled fashion, we repurpose pretrained video generative models as a unified and data-efficient framework for geometry estimation, formulated innovatively as a next-frames prediction task. Our method, GeoNeXt, inherits naturally structured knowledge and richer priors from the video model, while further adapting them for joint modeling of images and geometry targets (image <-> geometry), enabling more data efficient and effective learning of geometry. Extensive experiments validate our method for zero-shot monocular depth and surface normal estimation across diverse datasets, outperforming both previous task-specific and unified generative competitors while using substantially less training data. Notably, our method rivals discriminative state-of-the-art approaches trained on over 100x more data and even standouts on several benchmarks.

</details>


### [74] [GeBDA: Building Damage Assessment as Text-Based Sequence Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28567)
*Olivier Dietrich,Krishna Sapkota,Konrad Schindler,Genady Beryozkin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: General-purpose Vision-Language Model (Gemma) used to localize buildings and grade damage via autoregressive generation of bounding boxes from bi-temporal satellite imagery, without dedicated BDA architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explore whether VLMs can perform Building Damage Assessment without specialized architectures or fine-tuning, leveraging prompts and sequence generation.

Method: Cast BDA as variable-length set prediction; each item is a bounding box with coordinates and a damage label; autoregressive sequence generation; implementation based on open Gemma model; uses bi-temporal satellite images and a suitable text prompt.

Result: Preliminary implementation achieves promising damage mapping results.

Conclusion: Suggests VLMs can handle BDA tasks with prompt-driven, autoregressive generation; further work needed to robustly evaluate and compare to specialized methods and address practical deployment concerns.

Abstract: Conventionally, Building Damage Assessment (BDA) is tackled either with dedicated network architectures or by fine-tuning geospatial image foundation models. In this work, we ask whether a general-purpose Vision-Language Model (VLM) can localize buildings and grade their damage through autoregressive sequence generation alone. We cast BDA as predicting a variable-length set of bounding boxes, each specified by its coordinates and a damage label. Our preliminary implementation, based on the open Gemma model, achieves promising damage mapping results from only bi-temporal satellite images and a suitable text prompt.

</details>


### [75] [SignRR: Retrieve and Refine Real Motion for Sign Language Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28568)
*Fidel Omar Tito Cruz,Angie Sanchez Marquina,Summy Farfan,Gissella Bejarano*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A retrieve-and-refine framework (SignRR) improves sign language generation by starting from real retrieved motion and refining it with a part-aware Residual VQ-VAE to ensure global coherence and fine articulation, achieving state-of-the-art back-translation on PHOENIX14T and CSL-Daily while preserving pose quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative models struggle to preserve rare hand configurations and signer-specific articulation, as they synthesize motion from priors or noise without reference. Retrieval methods risk rhythm/style inconsistencies when concatenating segments from different signers and contexts, leading to poor global coherence.

Method: Propose a retrieve-and-refine paradigm (SignRR): initialize motion from a dictionary of real sign segments and refine the full sequence with a part-aware Residual VQ-VAE. Residual quantization preserves fine hand articulation; temporal length differences are managed in the latent space.

Result: Empirical evaluation on PHOENIX14T and CSL-Daily shows SignRR achieves state-of-the-art back-translation performance while maintaining competitive pose quality.

Conclusion: Retrieving real motion provides realistic articulation, while the refinement stage enforces global coherence. The approach combines the strengths of retrieval and learning-based refinement to produce high-quality, coherent signing sequences.

Abstract: Sign language production (SLP) aims to generate continuous signing motion from spoken language, often through gloss-to-pose generation. Prior work mainly follows two paradigms. Generative models synthesize motion from a learned prior or from noise, without reference to an observed signing instance, making rare hand configurations and signer-specific articulation difficult to preserve. Retrieval-based methods reuse real, well-articulated motion segments, but concatenating segments from different signers and co-articulation contexts can introduce rhythm and style inconsistencies across the full sequence, not only at segment boundaries. These limitations suggest a complementary solution: use retrieval to provide realistic articulation, and use learned refinement to impose the global coherence that retrieval alone lacks. We therefore propose retrieve-and-refine, a paradigm that starts from real retrieved motion and refines it into a globally coherent signing sequence rather than generating motion from scratch. Our framework, SignRR, initializes motion from a dictionary of real sign segments and refines the full sequence with a part-aware Residual VQ-VAE, where residual quantization preserves fine hand articulation and temporal length differences are handled in the latent space. Experiments on PHOENIX14T and CSL-Daily show that SignRR achieves state-of-the-art back-translation performance while maintaining competitive pose quality.

</details>


<div id='cs.CG'></div>

# cs.CG [[Back]](#toc)

### [76] [Optimal exponential memory for sequential Euclidean connection: edge-power costs and phase transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27777)
*Pedro M. M. de Castro*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Online geometric rule with a single-persistence state parameter γ optimizes the sum of α-powers of edge lengths in a sequential setting. The analysis reveals phase transitions and precise asymptotics for both uniform and worst-case inputs, including exact results for even α and high-dimensional expansions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how to optimally trade persistence of a single-state memory against geometric edge costs in streaming/online settings, and to characterize when optimizers are at boundary vs interior, along with finite-sample and asymptotic behaviours.

Method: Asymptotic and probabilistic analysis of the stationary problem for uniform inputs, finite-N scaling near a critical α_N = 1+ε_N with ε_N log N → λ, and threshold analyses. Uses scaling arguments, separation blocks for high α, periodic structure, and exact recursions for even powers, plus worst-case analysis for arbitrary inputs.

Result: Identifies a phase transition at α=1 between boundary and interior minimizers in the stationary uniform-input problem. Determines the finite optimizer in the joint window α_N=1+ε_N with described scaling. Establishes threshold scales for optimizer location (N^{-1/2}, then √(log N/(N log log N)), then stationary-root with corrections). Finds a second threshold governing the dominant correction and proves eventual uniqueness. At α=3d+8 the linear term at the stationary endpoint changes sign, creating a branch of strict local maxima. For arbitrary inputs, explicit optimal γ and worst-case edge-cost for 0<α≤3. High-α results: costs asymptotically 2 log 2 / log α, with exact α=2, α=4 results, recursions for even α, and a high-dimensional expansion that describes the optimizer.

Conclusion: The study uncovers clear phase-transition phenomena controlling the optimal persistence parameter and edge-costs in online geometric filtering. It provides a comprehensive set of asymptotic and exact results across regimes (uniform vs worst-case inputs, small vs large α, even α), including finite-sample scaling, threshold behavior, and high-dimensional extensions.

Abstract: We consider an online geometric connection rule that stores one point of state. After processing $p_i$, the state is updated by $x_i=γx_{i-1}+(1-γ)p_i$, and the two segments from $x_{i-1}$ to $x_i$ and from $x_i$ to $p_i$ are retained. The design parameter $γ$ controls the persistence of the state. We minimize the sum of the $α$-powers of the resulting edge lengths under independent uniform input and under arbitrary input sequences.
  For uniform points in the unit ball, the stationary problem has a transition at $α=1$. Its continuous extension is minimized at the boundary for $0<α\leq1$, and every global minimizer is interior for $α>1$. The main result determines the finite optimizer in the joint window $α_N=1+\varepsilon_N$, $\varepsilon_N\log N\toλ$. Below an explicit threshold it lies on the $N^{-1/2}$ scale. At the threshold its scale is $\sqrt{\log N/(N\log\log N)}$, and above the threshold it approaches an explicit stationary root with two computable corrections. A second threshold identifies which correction governs the location, and differentiated estimates prove eventual uniqueness. At $α=3d+8$, the linear coefficient at the stationary endpoint changes sign and a branch of strict local maxima enters the parameter interval. Under arbitrary input sequences, the optimal parameter and asymptotic worst-case edge-power cost per processed point are explicit for $0<α\leq3$. At high powers, periodic blocks and a separation argument show that this optimized cost is asymptotic to $2\log2/\logα$. Exact results for powers two and four, a rational recursion for all even powers, and a high-dimensional expansion provide additional descriptions of the optimizer.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [77] [Time Capsule of Testable Human Knowledge: 41 Years of Jeopardy! in a Single Free Local Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27459)
*David Noever,Forrest McKee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A compact open-weight model (Qwen2.5-14B, 4-bit, ~9 GB) can answer a broad, historical knowledge dataset (41 years of Jeopardy clues) with 67.0% overall accuracy and 85% in factoid categories, and remains effective on post-cutoff clues, demonstrating portability and shared training-data exposure across systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether portable open-weight language models can store and leverage a culture's general knowledge beyond their training data, and compare their capabilities to sealed, curated systems like Watson and contemporary models.
