Method: Evaluate a single 9 GB open-weight model (Qwen2.5-14B, 4-bit) on the complete open Jeopardy clue dataset (529,939 clues across 41 seasons, 1984–2025), using a strict forced-response protocol with exact and fuzzy matching; compare performance to IBM Watson and Claude Opus 4.8; examine clues aired after the model's training cutoff.

Result: The model answers 67.0% of clues overall and exceeds 85% on factoid categories; for clues aired after training cutoff, the local model achieves ~65% while Claude Opus 4.8 achieves 95% and Watson scores 0 (by construction); demonstrates portability of knowledge from server-centric artifacts to portable models.

Conclusion: A snapshot of a culture's knowledge can be portable and usable outside its original training context, challenging the notion that such knowledge is locked behind curated, server-bound corpora; training-data exposure is a shared trait among systems rather than a flaw of language models.

Abstract: In 2011, IBM's Watson was something like a sealed capsule of its era's queryable knowledge. Its DeepQA system defeated the strongest human Jeopardy! champions, but the knowledge that let it do so lived in a curated billion-document corpus running on a cluster of POWER7 servers, frozen at build time and impossible to move or copy. We show that the same kind of artifact, a snapshot of what a culture can answer, is now portable and essentially free. We evaluate a single 9 GB open-weight model (Qwen2.5-14B, 4-bit) against the complete open Jeopardy! clue dataset, 529,939 clues across all 41 broadcast seasons from 1984 to 2025. To our knowledge this is the first time a model has been run over the full corpus. The 41 years mark only how long the questions were collected. What they test is far older and broader: the accumulated body of human general knowledge a culture considers worth knowing, from ancient history and dead languages to science, literature, and geography, with a verified answer for every item. The model answers 67.0% of all clues under a strict forced-response protocol with exact and fuzzy matching, and exceeds 85% on factoid categories. We treat training-data exposure as something both systems share rather than a flaw unique to language models. Watson's case is in fact the more extreme one. Its corpus was assembled to contain Jeopardy answers and it was tuned on past clues, and it could not answer anything outside that curated distribution. The decisive test is whether a model can answer clues that did not exist when it was built. On clues aired after its training cutoff, the local model holds 65% and Claude Opus 4.8 holds 95%, while Watson by construction scores zero. The capability survives the move from a server room to a file you could seal in a time capsule, and unlike Watson it is not frozen to its own moment.

</details>


### [78] [Rating the Raters: Rasch Measurement Theory for LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27463)
*Pratik S. Sachdeva,Nathan Boudol*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Rasch measurement theory (RMT) is applied to the LLM-evaluation landscape to disentangle multiple measurement facets and diagnose biases, revealing systematic LLM-human rater differences that standard metrics miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the fragmentation in current evaluation practices (LLMs as examinees, judges, and raters) and to provide a principled framework that yields diagnostics for miscalibration, bias, and scale effects in LLM evaluations.

Method: Applied many-facet Rasch models to annotations from nine LLMs across diverse families and capability levels on the Measuring Hate Speech corpus, a construct built under RMT; compared LLMs to human raters across facets such as severity, item calibration, order effects, target-identity sensitivity, and rating-scale use.

Result: LLMs systematically differ from human raters on multiple dimensions (severity, item calibration, order robustness, target-identity sensitivity, and rating-scale functioning); these differences would be obscured by conventional evaluation approaches.

Conclusion: RMT should be included in the toolkit for evaluating LLMs-as-examinee, -judge, and -rater to gain diagnostic insights and improve measurement validity.

Abstract: LLMs now sit on every side of evaluation: as examinees scored on benchmarks, judges of other models' outputs, and raters of human-generated content. Each paradigm can be viewed as a measurement problem, where a latent property of an object is probed with items from an instrument (e.g., benchmark) by raters. Standard evaluation practices often neglect the contributions of each core component to the end result, limiting our understanding of what is being measured. Rasch measurement theory (RMT) is well-suited to this kind of problem. RMT decomposes ordinal ratings into separable facets on a common scale. It further provides a battery of diagnostics that can identify miscalibrated measurements and rater biases. We present a case study of RMT applied to the LLM-as-rater paradigm using the Measuring Hate Speech corpus, whose construct was itself built under RMT. We fit a series of many-facet Rasch models to annotations from nine LLMs spanning families and capability levels. Our analyses show that LLMs systematically differ from human raters in severity, item-level calibration, question-order robustness, target-identity sensitivity, and rating scale use, which all would be obscured by standard evaluation practice. Overall, we argue that RMT belongs in the toolkit for evaluating LLM-as-examinee, -judge, and -rater paradigms.

</details>


### [79] [Not All Explanations Are Sought: Information-Seeking Psychology for Human-Centered XAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27464)
*Andrea Beretta,Salvatore Rinzivillo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A theoretical framework linking human-centered XAI with information-seeking psychology to improve explanations by aligning them with users' instrumental, hedonic, and cognitive motives, and accounting for biases that drive over- or under-information-seeking, especially in agentic AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address HCXAI limitations by incorporating psychology of information seeking, ensuring explanations are actually sought and used, not merely available.

Method: Theoretical synthesis of Sharot and Sunstein’s information-seeking motives with HCXAI concepts; map three utilities to biases; identify failure modes; propose design guidance to shift from availability to elicitation of explanations.

Result: A conceptual framework and design guidance showing how information-seeking psychology can inform HCXAI to create explanations that users want to engage with, including considerations for agentic AI where cascading actions and risk assessment matter.

Conclusion: Integrating information-seeking psychology into HCXAI can make explanations more usable by aligning them with when and why users want to know, addressing both over- and under-information-seeking and improving anticipation of actions and risks in agentic systems.

Abstract: This position paper argues that human-centered explainable AI (HCXAI) should incorporate insights from the psychology of information seeking. Drawing on Sharot and Sunstein's framework of information-seeking motives, we propose that people evaluate whether to engage with explanations based on three types of expected utility: instrumental (will it help me act better?), hedonic (will it make me feel better?), and cognitive (will it improve my understanding?). Each utility is estimated through a lens shaped by well-documented cognitive biases, including illusion of control, automation bias, unrealistic optimism, impact bias, overconfidence, and confirmation bias. These biases can lead to two failure modes: excessive information-seeking that fragments attention without improving decisions, and insufficient information-seeking that leaves critical risks and misunderstandings unexamined. This challenge is particularly acute for agentic AI systems, where explanations must support not just understanding a single output but anticipating cascading actions, assessing risks, and deciding when to intervene. By integrating information-seeking psychology into HCXAI, we advocate for a shift from making explanations available to making them sought: designing systems that account for when and why users actually want to know.

</details>


### [80] [Retrieving Relations, Detecting Fallacies: A RAG Approach to Political Debate Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27471)
*Deborah Dore,Greta Damo,Elena Cabrio,Serena Villata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Guided retrieval-augmented fallacy detection using argumentative relations to steer knowledge retrieval; achieves strong macro-F1 gains on ElecDeb60to20.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fallacy detection requires world knowledge and discourse structure beyond surface text; static features limit performance. External knowledge retrieved in a dialogue-aware way may improve detection and classification.

Method: Introduce a guided retrieval-augmented framework that uses argumentative relations of support and attack to dynamically steer retrieval from a 15GB knowledge-base of political documents; evaluate across 42 retrieval configurations and 14 models on the ElecDeb60to20 benchmark.

Result: Macro-F1 for fallacy detection reaches up to 0.864; macro-F1 for classification reaches up to 0.725, outperforming non-retrieval baselines.

Conclusion: External knowledge augmented with argument-guided retrieval substantially improves fallacy detection and classification, demonstrating the value of discourse-aware retrieval in political-context analyses.

Abstract: Fallacies are arguments that employ invalid reasoning, making their automatic detection critical in sensitive contexts such as high-stakes political debates, where public opinion is shaped. Spotting a fallacious argument requires contextual knowledge beyond its pure surface text. This entails world knowledge pertaining to the subject matter under discussion, as well as knowledge of the relationships that exist between arguments within the argumentative discourse. Prior work on fallacy analysis has shown that argumentative discourse structure can beneficially improve classification performance. However, such structure is typically encoded only as static classifier features, limiting its flexibility. Building on this intuition while addressing this limitation, we introduce a guided retrieval-augmented methodology for fallacy detection and classification that leverages argumentative relations of support and attack to dynamically steer the extraction of relevant documents. We evaluate our approach on the ElecDeb60to20 benchmark across 42 retrieval configurations and 14 models, performing retrieval over a 15GB knowledge base of collected political-related documents. Our approach improves macro-F1 up to 0.864 for fallacy detection and up to 0.725 for classification over non-retrieval baselines. These results show that incorporating external knowledge significantly enhances fallacy detection and classification when retrieval is argumentatively guided.

</details>


### [81] [LLM-Augmented Causal Discovery: Probabilistic Fusion of Edge Existence and Orientation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27472)
*Neville K. Kitson,Anthony Constantinou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Probabilistic Dependency Graph (PDG) framework fuses BNSL and LLM-derived causal knowledge by representing edge states probabilistically, yielding improved causal graph accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address orientation identifiability in Bayesian Network Structure Learning and the unreliability of standalone LLM causal knowledge by combining complementary sources in a probabilistic fusion.

Method: Build PDGs where each edge carries a distribution over three states (directed, undirected, absent). Fuse ensembles of three BNSL algorithms (FGES, Tabu, PC) with three LLMs (Gemini, Claude, GPT) across multiple prompts/seeds via 50/50 weighted averaging. Evaluate on 26 benchmark networks using F1, recall, and orientation accuracy.

Result: 50/50 fusion improves F1 over the best single source in 22/26 networks; mean F1 improvement 0.056 (p<0.001). BNSL yields higher edge skeleton recall (≈80%) than LLMs (≈60%), while LLMs provide more accurate edge orientation (≈96%) than BNSL (≈77%). The two sources complement each other.

Conclusion: Representing both sources as probabilistic uncertainty over edge existence and orientation is a practical, effective approach to improve causal graph accuracy.

Abstract: Bayesian network structure learning (BNSL) from observational data struggles with orientation identifiability, while large language models (LLMs) offer broad but often unreliable causal knowledge. We propose combining these complementary sources through a novel representation, termed Probabilistic Dependency Graphs (PDGs). In a PDG, each edge is associated with a distribution over directed, undirected, and absent states, enabling fusion via weighted averaging. We evaluate this approach on 26 benchmark networks, combining ensembles of three BNSL algorithms (FGES, Tabu, PC) with three LLMs (Gemini, Claude, GPT) across multiple prompts and random seeds. A simple 50/50 fusion improves F1 over the better of either source alone in 22 of 26 networks, with a statistically significant mean improvement of $0.056$ $(p<0.001)$. Analysis reveals that the two sources play complementary roles: BNSL contributes a high-recall edge skeleton (80\% vs 60\% for LLM), while LLM contributes accurate edge orientation (96\% vs 77\% for BNSL). Our results show that representing both sources as probabilistic uncertainty over edge existence and orientation is a practical and effective way to improve causal graph accuracy.

</details>


### [82] [Hypothesize, Evaluate, Refine: A Scientific Agent for PDE Discovery with Unknown Spatial Coefficient Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27475)
*YuJie Huang,WenWu He,ZhuoEr Lin,Congcong Liu,Dong Liang,Zhuo-Xu Cui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HER-PDE is a scientific-agent framework that jointly discovers PDE operators and nonparametric, time-invariant coefficient fields in heterogeneous media by coordinating hypothesis generation, evaluation, and refinement. It uses two differently excited, noisy trajectories, evaluates hypotheses through a tailored interface that estimates only declared fields, and audits the selected law on a sealed interval. It recovers the generating operator in five 2D systems with 5% noise and robustly recovers coefficient fields (median Pearson ~0.85, median rel L2 error ~0.28).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying PDEs in heterogeneous media is hard because the governing operator and spatial coefficient fields are coupled; changing field placement can alter the effective differential law, and flexible fields can mask structural errors on single trajectories. A joint, agent-guided approach is needed to discover both operator structure and nonparametric coefficient fields.

Method: Hypothesize, Evaluate, Refine for PDE Discovery (HER-PDE) uses an Agent that analyzes two noisy trajectories from different excitations, proposes complete expression-tree hypotheses, and performs structure exploration plus local refinement. The Hypothesis Evaluation Interface (HEI) estimates only the fields explicitly declared in each hypothesis (never adds missing terms) and scores structures via bidirectional cross-excitation transfer. The selected law is audited on a sealed temporal interval.

Result: On five controlled 2D systems with 5% relative Gaussian state noise, the Agent recovers the generating operator in all five cases, including equivalent signed-field and product-rule parameterizations. Across nine unknown coefficient fields, recovered fields achieve a median Pearson correlation of ~0.85 and a median relative L2 error of ~0.28.

Conclusion: Agent-guided hypothesis refinement can recover heterogeneous governing laws without prescribing a parametric form for their spatial coefficients.

Abstract: Discovering PDEs in heterogeneous media requires jointly identifying the governing operator and the unknown spatial fields that parameterize it. These tasks are coupled: changing field placement changes the differential law, while a sufficiently flexible field can conceal structural error on a single trajectory. We present Hypothesize, Evaluate, Refine for PDE Discovery (HER-PDE), a scientific-agent framework that discovers compositional PDE structure together with nonparametric, time-invariant coefficient fields. The Agent analyzes two noisy trajectories generated by different excitations, proposes complete expression-tree hypotheses, and combines creative structural exploration with local candidate refinement. Its Hypothesis Evaluation Interface (HEI) estimates only the fields explicitly declared in each hypothesis, never adds missing terms, and scores structures by bidirectional cross-excitation transfer. The selected law is subsequently audited on a sealed temporal interval. Across five controlled two-dimensional systems observed with 5 percent relative Gaussian state noise, the Agent recovers the generating operator in all five cases, including equivalent signed-field and product-rule parameterizations. Across nine unknown coefficient fields, the recovered fields attain a median Pearson correlation of approximately 0.85 and a median relative L2 error of approximately 0.28. These results show that agent-guided hypothesis refinement can recover heterogeneous governing laws without prescribing a parametric form for their spatial coefficients.

</details>


### [83] [Class-Based Heuristic Selection for Solving the Flying Block Puzzle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27476)
*Sanyar Ahmadi,Pedram Asadzadeh,Amanj Khorramian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Class-Based Heuristic A* (CBHA*) for the Flying Block Puzzle uses a seven-class kinematic taxonomy and a General Move Constraint to provide admissible, adaptive heuristics and tie-breaking; it dramatically improves success rates and prunes search compared to standard A*, BFS, and depth-prioritized A*.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generic heuristics underperform in constrained spatial domains. The Flying Block Puzzle provides a rigorous NP-complete benchmark mirroring real-world clearance-to-size constraints; there is a need for heuristics that exploit structural constraints to avoid catastrophic search degradation.

Method: CBHA* integrates a General Move Constraint to capture minimum displacement with scarce vacant units; defines a seven-class kinematic taxonomy yielding admissible heuristics based on vacancy ratio and goal-piece geometry; incorporates a class-conditional tie-breaking mechanism that switches between depth-priority and vertical-distance ordering to overcome f-value plateaus; evaluated on 146 benchmark instances.

Result: CBHA* achieves 93.4% success vs 64% (Depth-Prioritized A*), 39% (Standard A*), 17% (BFS); reduces node expansions by 87.98% relative to Standard A*; maintains an average effective branching factor around 3.

Conclusion: Class-triggered adaptive heuristics provide a principled mechanism for efficient spatial planning in physically constrained systems and generalize beyond the Flying Block Puzzle to similar constraint-laden domains.

Abstract: Heuristic search underlies planning in autonomous systems ranging from warehouse logistics to robotic navigation, yet generic heuristics fail to exploit the structural constraints that govern constrained spatial domains, causing search performance to degrade catastrophically on harder instances. We study this problem through the two-column Flying Block Puzzle, a rigorously NP-complete spatial planning microworld whose bottleneck geometry mirrors clearance-to-size constraints encountered in multi-agent path finding, autonomous vehicle navigation, and block relocation systems. We introduce the Class-Based Heuristic A* (CBHA*) algorithm, which integrates a General Move Constraint to capture minimum displacement costs when vacant units are scarce, a formal kinematic taxonomy partitioning the state space into seven mutually exclusive classes with provably admissible heuristics based on vacancy ratio and goal-piece geometry, and a class-conditional tie-breaking mechanism that dynamically switches between depth-priority and vertical-distance ordering to overcome f-value plateaus. Over 146 benchmark instances, CBHA* achieves a 93.4% success rate against 64% for Depth-Prioritized A*, 39% for Standard A*, and 17% for BFS, while reducing node expansions by 87.98% relative to Standard A* and sustaining an average effective branching factor of approximately 3, demonstrating that class-triggered adaptive heuristics constitute a principled mechanism for efficient spatial planning that generalizes structurally to physical constraint systems.

</details>


### [84] [Benchmarking General Mobile Assistants in Challenging Real-World Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27477)
*Yiqi Zhu,Feiyu Gao,Jiaxing Fan,Jiahui Zeng,Minggang Wu,Chenliang Li,Haiyang Xu,Peng Li,Ming Yan,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GMA is a new benchmark for evaluating general mobile assistants with 7 apps, 300 tasks across four difficulty tiers, revealing that performance drops with task complexity; harness design matters and varies by model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mobile agent benchmarks (AndroidWorld/MobileWorld) lack sufficient coverage of real-world mobile usage; a more diverse, challenging testbed is needed to study agent capabilities and harness design.

Method: Introduce GMA with seven open-source applications spanning lifestyle sharing and travel planning, totaling 300 tasks across four difficulty tiers. Evaluate eight frontier models and perform controlled ablations of agent harnesses—such as context retention and explicit state tracking—under a unified environment and task taxonomy.

Result: Agent performance declines as task complexity increases; current agents struggle to meet realistic user requirements. Harness design can meaningfully improve performance on demanding workflows, but effectiveness varies across foundation models.

Conclusion: GMA complements existing benchmarks by expanding application coverage and task complexity, serving as a challenging testbed for evaluating mobile agents and understanding how harness design supports reliable execution in complex mobile workflows.

Abstract: Graphical user interfaces have emerged as an important environment for evaluating autonomous AI agents on multimodal interactive tasks. Existing benchmarks such as AndroidWorld and MobileWorld provide strong foundations for mobile agent evaluation, but their application coverage and task design do not yet fully capture the diversity and complexity of realistic mobile use. We present GMA, a benchmark for evaluating general mobile assistants in challenging real-world scenarios. GMA introduces seven applications based on open-source projects, spanning domains such as lifestyle sharing and travel planning, and 300 tasks across four difficulty tiers, from atomic actions to complex multi-step workflows. We evaluate eight frontier models and find that performance declines substantially as task complexity increases, with current agents remaining far from reliably handling realistic user requirements. We further conduct controlled ablation studies of agent harness choices, including context retention and explicit state tracking, under a shared environment, model setting, and task taxonomy. Results show that appropriate harness design can meaningfully improve performance, particularly on demanding workflows, while the effectiveness of specific designs can vary across foundation models. Overall, GMA complements existing benchmarks by expanding application coverage and task complexity, providing a challenging testbed for evaluating mobile agents and studying how harness design supports reliable execution in complex mobile workflows.

</details>


### [85] [Effectiveness of IoT and Deep Learning for Detection and Severity Assessment of Postelectrotermes militaris in Tea Plantations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27480)
*D. K. C. Senevirathna,A. A. E. Nanayakkara,H. M. C. K. Kulathunga,J. K. D. P. Nadula,R. M. Mapatuna,Malithi Nawarathne,Jaliya L. Wijayaraja,P. D. Senanayake,Samitha Vidhanaarachchi,Kalpani Manathunga*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IoT-enabled acoustic monitoring using CNN-based spectral features for early ULWT infestation detection in tea plantations; includes a severity index and geospatial mapping; achieves ~81% accuracy on held-out data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early detection of Upcountry Live Wood Termite to reduce crop losses; existing monitoring is manual and slow; integrating IoT sensing, deep learning, and geospatial analysis enables timely, data-driven interventions.

Method: Collect non-invasive audio from tea trunks via Raspberry Pi-based IoT device; 2,000 samples (1,000 healthy, 1,000 infested) split into 1,600 train, 200 val, 200 test; compute Fourier-derived spectrograms; train CNN for infestation classification and probability estimation; build a weighted severity model using CNN probability, mean acoustic amplitude, and proximity of nearby infested plants within 5 m; visualize using geospatial mapping; dataset derived from Kaggle (Senevirathna et al. 2026); field trial in Pundaluoya with realistic environmental noise.

Result: On held-out test: accuracy 81.5%, precision 80.6%, recall 83.0%, F1 81.8%, ROC-AUC 0.819; 2,000 samples with 1,600/200/200 split; severity outputs enable prioritization of inspections and targeted control; geospatial distribution maps identify high-risk zones.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of an IoT+DL+geospatial framework for early detection and severity assessment of ULWT infestations, supporting proactive management in tea plantations; further work needed to validate across sites and optimize deployment.

Abstract: Tea plantations are vulnerable to Postelectrotermes militaris, commonly known as the Upcountry Live Wood Termite (ULWT), which can cause substantial damage when infestations remain undetected. This study proposes an IoT-enabled acoustic monitoring framework integrated with deep learning for early detection and severity assessment of ULWT infestations in tea plantations.
  Research Method: Audio signals were captured non-invasively from tea trunks using a high-sensitivity microphone connected to a Raspberry Pi-based IoT device, with geographic coordinates recorded for spatial tracking. After trimming, resampling, and segmentation, 2,000 ten-second samples were obtained, comprising 1,000 healthy and 1,000 infested samples, and divided into 1,600 training, 200 validation, and 200 test samples. The dataset used in this study is publicly available on Kaggle (Senevirathna et al. 2026). Fourier-derived spectrograms trained a CNN for infestation classification and probability estimation. A weighted severity model combined CNN probability, mean acoustic amplitude, and nearby infested plants within 5 m, with geospatial mapping used to visualize infestation distribution.
  Findings and Values: Field trials in a ULWT-affected tea plantation in Pundaluoya demonstrated feasibility under realistic environmental noise. On the held-out test set, the CNN achieved 81.5% accuracy, 80.6% precision, 83.0% recall, 81.8% F1-score, and 0.819 ROC-AUC. Beyond binary infestation detection, the framework introduced quantitative severity assessment using infestation probability, acoustic amplitude, and nearby infested plants. The resulting severity and geospatial outputs can support plantation managers in identifying high-risk areas, prioritizing field inspections, and implementing more timely and targeted control measures.

</details>


### [86] [Context Localization for Generalized Level-Based Evaluation in Knowledge-Based Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27482)
*Ondrej Hutník,Natália Puškárová*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Characterization of when context filtering matches intersection-based localization in generalized level-based evaluation, via two structural conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To ensure consistent, context-dependent aggregation of non-additive evidence in knowledge-based systems and to understand when filtering by a context preserves admissible-context structure.

Method: Theoretical analysis of generalized level measures over monotone set functions; derivation of necessary and sufficient conditions; examination of pointwise and block-generated reduction mechanisms; extension to parameterized systems.

Result: A characterization: filtering by a context B is equivalent to intersecting admissible contexts with B for all monotone set functions iff (i) monotonicity with respect to contexts and (ii) a reduction property excluding positive localized support outside B. The result is extended to parameterized systems and interpreted as a stability criterion for context-dependent evidence selection and non-additive support evaluation.

Conclusion: Provides a stability criterion for context-dependent evidence aggregation and informs design of level-based knowledge systems with non-additive measures; clarifies when localizing contexts preserves aggregated level evaluations.

Abstract: We study context localization for generalized level-based evaluation in knowledge-based systems. The framework models situations where a structured nonnegative score, defined on facts, rules, cases, criteria or evidence units, is evaluated through conditional aggregation tests on admissible knowledge contexts. The generalized level measure maximizes a monotone set function over all contexts whose aggregated support reaches a prescribed level. We characterize when filtering the score by a context $B$ is equivalent to localizing the admissible contexts by intersection with $B$. The main theorem shows that this consistency holds for all monotone set functions if and only if two structural conditions are satisfied: monotonicity with respect to contexts and a reduction property excluding positive localized support outside $B$. We analyze pointwise and block-generated mechanisms producing the reduction property, extend the result to parameterized systems, and interpret it as a stability criterion for context-dependent evidence selection, non-additive support evaluation and level-based knowledge aggregation.

</details>


### [87] [CareGraph: An Auditable Hybrid AI Framework for Evidence-Grounded Personalized Longitudinal Health Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27484)
*Pratik Ghawate,Tanvi Patil*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CareGraph is an auditable, hybrid AI framework that converts heterogeneous health records into interpretable outputs (trends, context indicators, next steps, discussion prompts, provenance), without making autonomous clinical decisions. It emphasizes safety, provenance, and explainability, with a pipeline spanning deterministic analysis to constrained language model synthesis and provenance-linked explanations. On synthetic holdout data and comparative baselines, it achieves competitive accuracy/F1, strong missing-context detection, high safety precision, and faster generation than a monolithic GPT baseline, while maintaining graph auditing and retrieval guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fragmented, heterogeneous clinical, self-reported, and wearable health evidence challenges interpretation and traceability. There is a need for auditable, constrained AI systems that can summarize evidence, surface gaps, and preserve provenance without making autonomous clinical decisions.

Method: Hybrid AI pipeline: deterministic trend analysis (e.g., frozen ordinary least squares trend rule with sufficiency gate), context detection, graph construction, constrained language model synthesis, evidence validation, safety controls, and release gating. Includes missing-context detectors, safety ruleset versioning, and graph auditing with provenance verification. Evaluation on synthetic cohorts (n=400 per split) and comparative baselines (GPT-5.6) with an authored holdout benchmark.

Result: Holdout: accuracy 0.827, macro F1 0.837, insufficient-data F1 0.974; missing-context detector 0.815 strict micro F1 vs 0.318 legacy; safety ruleset 1.000 precision, 0.950 recall, 0.974 F1; audit across 80 patients yielded 79 syntheses and 78 presentations with no fallback; one output blocked, one failed due to invalid evidence key. Against GPT-5.6 baseline (56 matched patients): CareGraph faster (40.15s vs 49.62s), shorter (661 vs 1,163 words), better lexical alignment with targets; baseline used fewer tokens but cited more raw evidence. Graph auditing confirmed provenance and deterministic retrieval; incremental effects on generation require paired evaluation.

Conclusion: CareGraph provides a safety-bounded foundation for intelligent personalized health systems, delivering auditable provenance and structured evidence outputs while avoiding autonomous clinical decisions. It shows competitive performance and robust auditing, though evaluation of incremental graph effects and scaling to real-world data remains to be established.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is transforming personalized healthcare, yet fragmented clinical, self reported, and wearable evidence remains difficult to interpret and trace. We present CareGraph, an auditable hybrid AI framework that converts heterogeneous records into prioritized trends, missing context indicators, bounded next steps, discussion questions, and provenance linked explanations. CareGraph organizes evidence without diagnosing, predicting outcomes, selecting treatment, or making autonomous clinical decisions. Its pipeline covers deterministic analysis, context detection, graph construction, constrained language model synthesis, evidence validation, safety controls, and release gating. Tests used synthetic cohorts of 400 patients each for development, validation, and holdout. On holdout data, a frozen ordinary least squares trend rule with a sufficiency gate achieved 0.827 accuracy, 0.837 macro F1 with a 95 percent confidence interval of 0.819 to 0.854, and 0.974 insufficient data F1. Missing context detection achieved 0.815 strict micro F1 versus 0.318 for the legacy detector. On an authored holdout benchmark, safety ruleset version 1.2 achieved 1.000 precision, 0.950 recall, and 0.974 F1. An audit requiring graph retrieval across 80 patients yielded 79 syntheses and 78 presentations without fallback; one output was blocked and one failed closed because of an invalid evidence key. Against monolithic GPT 5.6 on 56 matched patients, CareGraph was faster at 40.15 versus 49.62 seconds, shorter at 661 versus 1,163 words, and showed better exploratory lexical alignment with longitudinal targets; the baseline used fewer tokens and cited more raw evidence. Graph auditing verified provenance and deterministic retrieval; incremental graph effects on generation require paired evaluation. CareGraph offers a safety bounded foundation for intelligent personalized health systems.

</details>


### [88] [Thinking Costs Tokens: When More Structure is Worth the Price](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27506)
*Thomas Nolasque,John Grey,Calista Pham,Ankit Vani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: There exists a token-budget threshold in which adding inference structure to an LLM switches from being detrimental to beneficial for multi-hop financial reasoning tasks, with a crossover around 1,000–1,500 tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether planning and verification overhead helps or harms performance within different token budgets for financial reasoning tasks, and to quantify the trade-off between a monolithic LLM prompt and a verified search architecture.

Method: Compare two architectures (monolith vs. verified search with planning, label-blind checking, and repair) on FinQA and TAT-QA across 14 budget tiers (250–42,000 tokens) using GPT-5.4 mini. Evaluate 1,000 cases (28,000 cells) per architecture; measure accuracy at each budget tier.

Result: Performance of the monolith and verified search converge at low budgets (0% accuracy at 250–500 tokens). With 1,000 tokens, monolith ~18% accuracy; verified search ~0% due to overhead. From 1,500 tokens onward, verified search surpasses monolith, achieving ~44% at high budgets, while monolith reaches ~40%. Crossover is between 1,000 and 1,500 tokens; statistical test confirms intersection (p ≤ 0.001 at endpoints).

Conclusion: There is a distinct token-budget threshold where the added planning and verification overhead becomes advantageous. Above roughly 1,000–1,500 tokens, the verified search architecture yields a measurable performance advantage on financial reasoning tasks, while below this threshold the overhead dominates.

Abstract: Adding inference structure to a language model lets it search, verify, and revise, but these actions consume the very budget they are supposed to use well. In this paper, we investigate whether there exists a token-budget threshold, below which the overhead of planning and verification hurts performance and above which it helps. We evaluate two systems on FinQA and TAT-QA financial reasoning tasks, using GPT-5.4 mini across 14 budget tiers ranging from 250 to 42,000 output-equivalent tokens. The first system is a monolith, which is a single LLM call. The second is a verified search architecture that adds planning, label-blind checking, and repair capabilities. We run 1,000 cases for a total of 28,000 completed cells. Both systems score 0% at the two lowest tiers, where neither can fit a complete prompt. At 1,000 tokens, the monolith reaches 18% accuracy while verified search scores near 0%, since the planning overhead leaves no room for an answer. From 1,500 tokens onward, verified search surpasses the monolith and maintains a consistent advantage, reaching approximately 44% at the highest tiers while the monolith reaches approximately 40%. The crossover occurs between 1,000 and 1,500 output-equivalent tokens, confirmed by a strict intersection-union test ($p \le 0.001$ at both endpoints).

</details>


### [89] [WM-R1: Training GUI Agents to Reason and leverage World Models with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27508)
*Yu Han,Tianwen Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WM-R1 trains mobile GUI agents using world models for all rollouts, removing real-environment interaction and enabling planning, achieving stronger performance than GRPO baselines and inference-time simulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for GUI on mobile requires extensive real-environment data, which is costly and unstable; a data-efficient, stable approach is needed.

Method: Introduce WM-R1, a reinforcement learning framework that uses world models as the source of state transitions for all training rollouts. World models are also incorporated into the agent's thinking process to simulate consequences before action. The framework supports massively parallel, step-level trajectory generation grounded in world models, plus a multi-dimensional rule-based reward that balances task success, trajectory efficiency, and world-model utilization. A curated dataset of 2000 challenging tasks is used for efficient training.

Result: Experiments on Android mobile benchmarks show WM-R1-trained agents significantly outperform GRPO-only baselines and inference-time simulation methods.

Conclusion: World-model-based RL for mobile GUI is feasible and data-efficient, enabling planning-like reasoning and stable, scalable training without real-environment interaction.

Abstract: GUI agents trained with reinforcement learning (RL) have showcased strong environment learning capabilities on mobile platforms. However, RL typically demands extensive real-environment interactions, leading to high resource costs and instability, especially in GUI scenarios. To address these, we propose WM-R1, the first reinforcement learning framework that trains mobile GUI agents with world models instead of real environments. Specifically, world models serve as the source of state transitions during all rollouts, replacing the real Android environment within the training loop. WM-R1 also embeds world models directly into the thinking process, enabling agents to reason about the consequences of candidate actions before committing to the final action. Crucially, WM-R1 eliminates the need for real-environment interaction, supports massively parallelized and step-level granularized trajectory generation grounded in world models, and introduces a multi-dimensional rule-based reward that jointly optimizes task success, trajectory efficiency, and world model utilization. For efficient training, we curate a high-quality dataset of 2000 challenging tasks. Experiments on Android mobile benchmarks demonstrate that WM-R1-trained agents significantly outperform GRPO-only baselines and inference-time simulation methods. Code is available at https://github.com/genalyu/WM-R1 .

</details>


### [90] [SETU: An Agentic Ecosystem for Multilingual, Persona-Aware Communication Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27524)
*Jonnalagadda Maruthi Tejas,Uponika Barman Roy,Tilottama Goswami,Samir Goswami,Mousita Dhar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SETU is an agentic, multi-modal coaching ecosystem that integrates video, audio-speech, and text relevance analysis with trust-aware orchestration to generate modality-attributed coaching reports for corporate communication coaching, focusing on sales pitches with audience adaptation (scenario ii).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for scalable, explainable tools to improve multilingual workforce communication. Existing systems often operate in isolation or produce opaque outputs, hindering auditability and coaching effectiveness in real-world corporate settings.

Method: A decomposed pipeline of specialized agents (video analysis, audio-speech analysis, text relevance, scoring, notification, reporting) coordinated via trust-aware orchestration, producing modality-attributed coaching reports with human reviewers retaining final judgment.

Result: Evaluation reported for scenario (ii) sales pitches with audience adaptation; due to limited resources, scenario (i) recruiter-candidate calls was not evaluated. The system outputs coaching reports that are intended for formative training and coaching with human-in-the-loop review.

Conclusion: SETU aims to bridge communication gaps across languages and audience expectations through an agentic, auditable coaching ecosystem, balancing automation with human oversight to support scalable, explainable coaching.

Abstract: Corporate training teams need scalable and explainable tools to improve workforce communication in multilingual settings. Existing systems often score text, audio, or video in isolation, or produce black-box outputs that are difficult to audit for coaching use. This paper presents SETU, an agentic ecosystem for corporate communication coaching aimed at recruiters, frontline sales professionals and training units who prepare for audience specific conversations. SETU is designed for two scoped scenarios: (i) recruiter-candidate eligibility-and-interest calls with persona context and (ii) sales pitches with target-audience adaptation; owing to limited evaluation resources, this paper reports results on scenario (ii) only. The ecosystem decomposes analysis into specialized video, audio-speech, text-relevance, scoring, notification and reporting agents coordinated through trust-aware orchestration. It generates modality-attributed coaching reports for formative training, with human reviewers retaining final judgment. The name SETU (bridge in several Indic languages) reflects the goal of bridging communication gaps across regional languages and audience expectations.

</details>


### [91] [Nemotron 3.5 Content Safety Moderator: A Compact Multimodal, Multilingual, and Reasoning Enabled Content Safety Moderator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27548)
*Varun Singh,Anuj Doshi,Makesh Narsimhan Sreedhar,Shaona Ghosh,Katherine Luna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nemotron 3.5 CS is a compact 4B vision-language safety moderator that jointly classifies prompts, images, and responses across 12 languages, with optional reasoning traces and a new multimodal/multilingual safety dataset. It aims to provide broad coverage, policy customization, and low compute cost, serving as a deployable front-line safety component.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety moderation must handle multimodal inputs and domain-specific policies while remaining efficient; existing guardrails often cover only parts of the problem, and a compact model with multilingual support can enable scalable, policy-driven moderation.

Method: Deploy/train a compact 4B parameter vision-language model for safety classification across prompts, images, and assistant responses; multilingual (12 languages); supports generating concise reasoning traces when requested; release a multimodal safety dataset with real-image moderation, benign vision-language/document tasks, synthetic rare-risk/jailbreak cases, and custom-policy examples. Evaluate on multimodal safety, text moderation, multilingual robustness, custom-policy following, benign false positives, and latency.

Result: Demonstrates a practical coverage tradeoff: image-conditioned and policy-conditioned moderation added while staying competitive with specialized guard models; feasible as front-line safety; reasoning traces useful for audit; dataset supports guard training.

Conclusion: Compact vision-language moderators are viable deployable front-line safety components; reasoning traces can aid policy review; dataset provides resources for guard training; supports broad, low-cost moderation across modalities and languages.

Abstract: Safety moderation for deployed AI applications is moving beyond text-only prompts: systems increasingly need to judge images, documents, screenshots, and generated responses under policies that vary across domains. Existing guardrails usually cover only part of this setting, making it difficult to combine broad coverage, custom policy control, and low compute cost. We present Nemotron 3.5 Content Safety Moderator, also referred to as Nemotron 3.5 CS in this paper for brevity, a compact 4B vision-language safety moderator that jointly classifies user prompts, images, and assistant responses across 12 languages. Nemotron 3.5 CS returns safety labels for latency-sensitive moderation and can additionally produce concise reasoning traces that apply supplied custom policies and identify violated categories when reasoning is requested. We also release a multimodal and multilingual safety dataset for guard training, spanning human-labeled real-image moderation, benign vision-language and document tasks, synthetic rare-risk and jailbreak cases, and custom-policy examples. Across evaluations spanning multimodal safety, text moderation, multilingual robustness, custom-policy following, benign false positives, and latency, Nemotron 3.5 CS demonstrates a practical coverage tradeoff: it adds image-conditioned and policy-conditioned moderation while remaining broadly competitive with specialized guard models. These results suggest that compact vision-language moderators can serve as deployable front-line safety components, with reasoning used selectively for audit and policy review.

</details>


### [92] [LongGuard: Mechanistic Analysis and Training-Free Mitigation of Long-Context Failure in Safety Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27580)
*Ziyang Chen,Xing Wu,Songlin Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LongGuard introduces a long-context safety evaluation and mitigation framework for LLM guardrails, revealing that unsafe recall collapses with context length due to attention dilution and logit dynamics; it offers training-free mitigations (Chunked Detection and Attention-Head Sharpening) and a Context-Aware Hyperparameter Routing deployment protocol, achieving notable improvements across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Guardrails are typically developed and tested on short text, leaving long-context vulnerability unexamined. There is a need to understand and mitigate long-context guardrail failures to prevent unsafe outputs in extended LLM interactions.

Method: Formulate the task as Safety Needle-in-a-Haystack (SafetyNIAH) over a long context length grid. Perform a three-layer attention-logit-behavior analysis to identify the mechanism of failure, isolate guard-specialized retrieval heads, and propose training-free mitigations (Chunked Detection, Attention-Head Sharpening) plus a deployment protocol (Context-Aware Hyperparameter Routing) that selects configurations by context length. Evaluate on five benchmarks with six guardrails.

Result: Across 15 guardrails, unsafe recall decreases by >50% with increasing length; the mechanism is an attention mass dilution on the unsafe needle leading to compressed unsafe-over-safe logit margins and collapsed detections, consistent even after partialling out length. A sparse set of guard-specialized retrieval heads shows partial specificity. CAHR-CD and CAHR-AHS improve the six-guardrail average by 22% and 13% respectively across five benchmarks.

Conclusion: Long-context guardrail failures are mechanistically tractable and can be mitigated with training-free methods and context-aware deployment. The work provides a repeatable framework and demonstrates practical gains, with code and data available.

Abstract: Safety guardrails serve as the last line of defense against harmful inputs and outputs of large language models (LLMs), yet they are trained and evaluated almost exclusively on short text. We present LongGuard, a framework that evaluates, mechanistically analyzes, and mitigates long-context guardrail failure. We formulate the task as Safety Needle-in-a-Haystack (SafetyNIAH) over a 0.25k-32k length grid; across 15 mainstream guardrails, unsafe recall drops monotonically by more than 50% on average, and a paired Benign-Fill vs. Needle-Repeat design attributes the failure to proportional dilution of the unsafe needle rather than to absolute length. A three-layer attention-logit-behavior analysis on six guardrails locates the mechanism: attention mass on the unsafe needle is diluted, the unsafe-over-safe logit margin is compressed in lockstep, and the detection decision collapses accordingly, with this attention->logit->behavior chain remaining consistent after partialling out length. We further isolate a sparse set of guard-specialized retrieval heads that exhibit partial specificity relative to their base models. Building on the analysis, we propose two training-free mitigations - Chunked Detection (CD) and Attention-Head Sharpening (AHS) - and a deployment protocol, Context-Aware Hyperparameter Routing (CAHR), that selects configurations by context length and audit side. Across five benchmarks spanning synthetic data, long-context attacks, and reasoning-model outputs, CAHR-CD and CAHR-AHS improve the six-guardrail average by 22% and 13%, respectively. Code and data are available online.

</details>


### [93] [Generative AI Expands the Intellectual Reach of Course Based Undergraduate Research Experiences (CUREs)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27638)
*Aditi Babar,Kristin J. Davin,Alex Dornburg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GenAI integration in a CURE expands novice researchers' inquiry space, supports distributed collaboration, and preserves human disciplinary judgment; three main effects: (1) personalized scaffolding enables self-directed, topic-specified projects; (2) GenAI as part of distributed cognition aids cross-expertise coordination without eroding specialization; (3) AI outputs are scrutinized and revised by students, fostering autonomous scientific reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine how embedding Generative AI within a course-based undergraduate research experience (CURE) affects student inquiry, collaboration, and scientific reasoning as research problems grow in complexity.

Method: Longitudinal qualitative study across three semesters in a bioinformatics/genomics CURE, analyzing student conversations, artifacts, and reflective data to track how GenAI influences inquiry, collaboration, and judgment.

Result: GenAI expanded the research reach in three ways: (1) personalized, on-demand scaffolding enabled self-directed projects beyond instructor-provided topics; (2) GenAI became part of the distributed cognitive system, improving communication and coordination across differentiated expertise without erasing specialization; (3) AI-generated contributions were increasingly validated, revised, or rejected by students, indicating retained intellectual responsibility and growing research independence.

Conclusion: GenAI can extend the reach of CUREs by broadening what novice researchers can investigate, how they collaborate, and the level of responsibility they can assume, while preserving human judgment central to authentic scientific inquiry.

Abstract: Course-based undergraduate research experiences (CUREs) broaden access to authentic scientific inquiry through responsive instructor support as research problems become increasingly complex. Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) may extend this support by providing individualized assistance that can adapt as student needs change. However, how embedding GenAI within a CURE to provide support across the research process impacts student inquiry, collaboration, and scientific reasoning remains unresolved. Here we use longitudinal qualitative data collected across three semesters of a bioinformatics and genomics CURE to show that GenAI expanded the intellectual reach of the research experience in three distinct ways. First, personalized, on-demand scaffolding allowed students to move beyond the boundaries of instructor expertise and transform their own interests into researchable inquiry, with all teams developing distinct self-directed projects rather than selecting instructor-provided topics. Second, GenAI became part of the distributed cognitive system of research teams, helping novice researchers communicate and coordinate across differentiated expertise without eliminating specialization. Third, expanded capability did not replace the need for disciplinary judgment. Students increasingly validated, revised, or rejected AI-generated contributions, such that research independence emerged through retained intellectual responsibility. Together, these findings suggest that GenAI can extend the reach of CUREs by expanding what novice researchers can investigate, how they can collaborate, and the level of responsibility they can assume while preserving human judgment central to authentic scientific inquiry.

</details>


### [94] [If Agents Were Angels, No Governance Would Be Necessary: Out-of-Band Policy Enforcement at a Trusted Tool Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27646)
*Marc Millstone,Tyler Akidau,Johannes Brüderl,Marat Pekker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Out-of-band policy enforcement (OBPE) provides a trusted boundary to constrain agent actions beyond prompts, via a typed Cedar policy core in an HTTP proxy; results show substantial reduction in data leakage and improved safe-useful completion in benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompts are brittle and agents with credentials can access and exfiltrate data; a boundary outside agent reasoning is needed to enforce policies and limit leakage while preserving useful functionality.

Method: Introduce OBPE proxy that authorizes typed operation and resource, narrows the query before backend calls, then filters/masks records or fields in responses. Semantic gating can deny or hold calls based on argument values or external state. A data policy owner sets the maximum grant; agent policy can only narrow it. Implemented a production-like system with a Cedar policy core; evaluated via HTTP proxy prototype against Jira/ServiceNow mocks across four models and 20 red-team tasks, comparing prompted agents with and without OBPE.

Result: Trace failures dropped from 57.6% to 0.2% over 3,621 trials; fulfillment fell from 79.1% to 60.9%; safe-useful completion rose by 21.8 points; cluster-weighted reduction of 41.2 points (95% CI: 27.7, 54.9). Some reconstructions occurred without context, or using filtered counts as oracle; field removal is limited to a single execution; masking/history rules are weaker and not fully evaluated.

Conclusion: OBPE provides a rigorous boundary for data governance by enforcing typed policies outside agent reasoning, reducing leakage while preserving useful outputs under defined conditions. The work notes remaining gaps (e.g., one-execution field removal, non-covered controls like durable approvals, temporal/aggregate policies) and cautions that proxy-based enforcement is not a universal solution but a practical augmentation for safer agent-enabled workflows.

Abstract: Give an agent a human's credential and it inherits the person's reach without the judgment that limits its use. It can sweep every reachable record into model context, where hidden instructions steer its next call, and every request stays credential-valid while the agent exceeds its job or absorbs a secret. Prompts are a brittle guardrail: one fallible reasoner interprets the task and enforces its limits.
  We present Out-of-Band Policy Enforcement (OBPE), a trusted boundary outside agent reasoning. It authorizes the typed operation and resource, narrows the query before the backend call, then filters records and fields or masks values in the response. Semantic gating can deny or hold an authorized call on argument values or external state. A data policy owner sets the maximum grant; agent policy can only narrow it. We prove, under stated conditions, that the policy plan is order-independent and agent policy cannot widen the ceiling. Field removal covers one execution; masking and history rules claim less.
  We release an HTTP proxy prototype simplified from our production system, with conformance tests tying its typed Cedar policy core to the model. Against Jira and ServiceNow mocks, our benchmark compares prompted agents with and without OBPE on four models, including 20 adaptive red-team tasks. A trace failure means protected data entered agent context, an exact value appeared in the answer, or a forbidden effect completed. In 3,621 trials it fell from 57.6% to 0.2%, a cluster-weighted reduction of 41.2 points [95% CI: 27.7, 54.9]; fulfillment fell from 79.1% to 60.9%, while paired safe-useful completion rose 21.8 points [9.5, 35.2]. Some answers reconstructed a value that never entered context or used filtered row counts as an oracle: shaping one execution is not noninterference. Write controls, durable approval, and temporal and aggregate policies lie outside this evaluation.

</details>


### [95] [A Framework for Object-Centric Predictive Monitoring of Collaborative Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27671)
*Daniel Calegari,Andrea Delgado,Leonel Peña,Martín Rubio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework unifies predictive process monitoring for collaborative, inter-organizational processes with object-centric process mining by mapping extended collaborative logs to OCEL 2.0, reframing tasks as object-centric, and providing a reproducible converter/pipeline; evaluated on multiple logs with fourteen tasks and five predictive strategies, showing explicit collaboration structure and outlining trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collaborative, cross-organizational processes involve multiple interdependent entities (participants, messages, local executions, global cases). Traditional single-case event logs miss relational structure; object-centric process mining (OCPM) introduces first-class objects and relations, enabling richer representations and targets.

Method: (i) Semantic mapping from extended collaborative event logs to an OCED-conformant object-centric representation serialized in OCEL 2.0; (ii) Reformulation of collaborative prediction tasks as object-centric prediction tasks; (iii) A reproducible converter and prediction pipeline implementing the mapping. Evaluation on four public collaborative event logs and a fifth derived from BPI Challenge 2013, executing fourteen reformulated tasks with five predictive strategies across tabular, sequential, and graph-native encodings.

Result: The framework supports evaluation across multiple encodings and datasets; the object-centric representation makes collaboration structure explicit and enables prediction targets based on object relations beyond the case-centric taxonomy. However, it increases relational complexity and relies on object-centric tooling. The authors discuss benefits, limitations, and threats to validity.

Conclusion: The approach advances predictive process monitoring for collaborative processes by leveraging object-centric representations and explicit inter-object relations to enable richer, non-case-centric prediction targets, while acknowledging increased complexity and tooling dependencies.

Abstract: Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM) of collaborative, inter-organizational processes requires reasoning over multiple interdependent entities, including participants, messages, local executions, and the global collaboration case. Existing approaches extend traditional event logs with collaboration attributes but retain a single-case perspective, leaving much of this structure implicit. Object-centric process mining (OCPM) provides an alternative by representing these entities as first-class objects with explicit relations and multiple notions of case. This study connects collaborative PPM and OCPM through three contributions: (i) a formal semantic mapping from extended collaborative event logs to an OCED-conformant object-centric representation, serialized in OCEL 2.0; (ii) a reformulation of collaborative prediction tasks as object-centric prediction tasks; and (iii) a reproducible converter and prediction pipeline implementing the proposed mapping. We evaluate the framework on four public collaborative event logs and a fifth derived from the BPI Challenge 2013 incident-management log by executing the fourteen reformulated tasks using five predictive strategies across tabular, sequential, and graph-native encodings. We further discuss the benefits, limitations, and threats to the approach's validity. The representation makes collaboration structure explicit and makes it natural to state prediction targets based on object relations that fall outside the case-centric taxonomy, at the cost of increased relational complexity and dependence on object-centric tooling.

</details>


### [96] [Agents for Everyone: A Workshop Framework for Building Agentic AI Capabilities in a Distributed Curation Community](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27675)
*Seth Carbon,Sierra Moxon,Kimberly Van Auken,Pascale Gaudet,Christopher J. Mungall*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cloud-based agentic environment for GO curation; four-module training; workshop shows access and training are central to adopting agentic AI in biology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address barriers to adoption of agentic AI in biological data curation, focusing on access, training, and workflow integration.

Method: Deploy JupyterHub-based environment with Claude Code harness; browser-terminal access; centralized API gateway; four training modules culminating in GO-CAM curation; four-hour workshop with 37 participants.

Result: Demonstrated feasibility and benefits of centralized access; gradual capability introduction; training design and grounding in familiar tasks enable agentic AI use; 37 participants successfully engaged in curation workflows.

Conclusion: Overcoming access barriers and thoughtful workflow/training design are key to building community capability for agentic AI in distributed scientific communities.

Abstract: Agentic AI has the potential to accelerate curation of biological databases and knowledge bases. However, uptake has been hindered by a number of challenges and obstacles, including access to agents and appropriate training. Here we describe how we have attempted to address and mitigate these challenges and obstacles through the deployment of a cloud-based agentic environment, and the development of an interactive training workshop for the Gene Ontology Consortium. Our cloud environment for agentic-assisted curation was based on the JupyterHub platform, and utilized Claude Code as a universal harness. This allows curators to interact with an agent session through a terminal running in the browser, and has additional benefits such as centralization of access through a single API gateway, removing the need for participants to manage subscriptions or install software locally. We created four training modules, walking participants through basic agentic tool use first and then working up to agentic biological pathway curation using the existing GO-CAM (GO Causal Activity Model) curation tool. Thirty-seven participants took part in the four-hour workshop. Our key takeaway from this workshop is that building community capability with agentic AI is primarily a problem of access, workflow design, and training. Removing technical barriers, introducing capabilities gradually, grounding exercises in familiar curation tasks, and giving curators direct experience evaluating agent output can provide a practical route toward building shared agentic AI capability in distributed scientific communities.

</details>


### [97] [PCFBench: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Product Carbon Footprint Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27716)
*Krishna Rao,Andrew Dumit,Shaena Ulissi,Jacob Feintzeig,P. James Joyce,Daniel Frank,Steven Watson,Jonathan Glidden,Gizem Ilayda Dinc,Travis M. Kwee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PCFBench is a novel benchmark for product carbon footprint modeling that decomposes PCF into six independently evaluable tasks (decomposition, retrieval, ontology matching, numerical extraction) across 614 expert-labelled items. It reveals that no model dominates among eight frontier LLMs; while total-emission accuracy can be reasonable for some models, step-by-step reasoning shows substantial errors and mass-conservation violations, underscoring transparency and debiasing needs. The authors release the dataset and evaluation harness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations either optimize for total emissions (hiding internal errors) or isolate sub-tasks (missing compositional interactions). There is a need for a benchmark that enforces decomposition, retrieval, ontology alignment, and precise numerical extraction to assess AI reliability in high-stakes PCF calculations.

Method: Construct PCFBench with six tasks and 614 expert-labelled items designed to probe decomposition, retrieval, ontology matching, and numerical extraction under under-specification, conflicting context, and numerical constraints. Evaluate eight frontier LLMs from four providers using the benchmark, and analyze stepwise generation versus end-to-end emission totals.

Result: Across eight frontier LLMs, no single model dominates. The strongest models estimate total emissions within 2x of declared totals for 77% of products, but this drops to 37–58% when generating PCFs step by step, with only 45–75% obeying mass conservation.

Conclusion: PCFBench provides a targeted, interpretable evaluation framework that exposes compositional and numerical reasoning weaknesses in current LLMs for PCF modeling. The released dataset and harness enable focused progress toward transparent, auditable PCF generation for decarbonization efforts.

Abstract: AI systems are being deployed on high-stakes, domain-specific workflows that demand correctness not just in the final output, but at every intermediate step. One such workflow is estimating a product carbon footprint (PCF), the greenhouse-gas emissions attributable to a physical product. AI agents are increasingly being used to generate PCFs, but existing evaluations score either total emissions (hiding error sources and cancelling mistakes) or sub-tasks in isolation (missing compositional interactions). We introduce PCFBench, the first benchmark to carve PCF modeling into independently-evaluable tasks that require decomposition, retrieval, ontology matching, and numerical extraction. It comprises 614 expert-labelled items across six tasks. Together they probe reasoning under under-specification, conflicting context, and numerical constraints. Across eight frontier LLMs from four providers, no single model dominates. Although the strongest models estimate total product emissions within 2 times of declared totals on 77% of products, this rate drops to 37-58% when the PCF is generated step by step, with only 45-75% obeying mass conservation. These failures undermine the transparency practitioners need to compare products and drive decarbonization. We release the dataset and evaluation harness to support targeted progress.

</details>


### [98] [Probing Perceptual Priors of MLLMs via Gibbs Sampling with Interpretable Generative Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27727)
*Manuel Cherep,Pattie Maes,Nikhil Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A method to elicit and analyze a model's perceptual priors by steering a generative model to create stimuli and using Gibbs sampling with the model as judge, revealing canonical and novel priors beyond prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address interpretability gaps: priors shape behavior but are hard to observe; traditional analyses fix inputs and view responses or probe internal representations, which miss the prior distribution, especially in high-dimensional real-world input spaces.

Method: Generate stimuli along controllable axes using a generative model; perform Gibbs sampling over this stimulus space, treating the target model as the evaluator to infer its perceptual priors across categories (e.g., trustworthiness in faces, cheapness in art).

Result: Reportedly recovers canonical biases and uncovers surprising priors invisible to direct prompting, demonstrating the method can uncover priors that influence model behavior and suggesting diverse downstream effects.

Conclusion: Provides a direct approach to studying model priors, enabling discovery of hidden biases; encourages further work on their downstream implications and how priors interact with inputs to shape decisions.

Abstract: A model's behavior on a task is jointly determined by the input it receives and the prior it brings in, i.e. the distribution over stimuli it implicitly expects. Interpretability research has traditionally studied models by holding inputs fixed and examining model responses either mechanistically, probing how internal structure represents inputs, or behaviorally, measuring how variation in inputs leads to variation in outputs. Neither reconstructs the prior distribution itself, since internal structure shows what a model can represent, not what it expects, and any fixed stimulus set leaves most of the possible input space unseen. In particular, such an input space in real-world settings, such as images seen by VLMs, is extremely high-dimensional and diverse. These priors thus remain a poorly understood component of models that nonetheless influence real-world behavior. We propose a method to sample from models' perceptual prior distributions directly, by steering a generative model to produce stimuli along controllable axes and running Gibbs sampling over that space with the model under study as the judge. We apply this to a variety of categories and target variables (such as trustworthiness in faces and cheapness in art images) and recover both canonical biases and surprising novel priors invisible to direct prompting, warranting further investigation of their downstream effects.

</details>


### [99] [Why Didn't It Check? Unsupported Final Claims and Their Repair in Two Tool-Equipped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27768)
*Justin Bronder*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LM with tool access can commit to unsupported final claims; defines occurrence and conditional repair; experiments on Qwen3-32B and Gemma 4 show evidence-driven repair; results limited to synthetic setups; generalization unclear.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify failure modes of tool-enabled LMs and how evidence affects final claims; separate occurrence from repair; understand limitations in real deployments.

Method: Two fixed-model setups (Qwen3-32B, Gemma 4) on synthetic task families; measure occurrence of unsupported claims; replay with exact state; alter only tool response codes; automatic evidence-checking rule; compute repair rates; additional experiment on 64 cases with evidence needed; track unrevised vs corrected claims.

Result: Qwen3-32B: 33/512 first responses ended with unsupported claim; when evidence provided, all 33 repaired; a non-informative tool response repaired 0 of 33. If evidence supported original answer, preserved 33/33. 64 cases with evidence rule added: corrected all 10 wrong unsupported claims; preserved 11 correct; did not corrupt correct to wrong. Gemma 4: tool used in all 512 first responses; no unsupported final claim; conditional repair not measurable. These are localized, synthetic-task results; not indicative of real-world prevalence or general mechanism.

Conclusion: Findings illustrate two local, synthetic-task limitations and caution against extrapolating; further work needed to establish prevalence and general mechanisms across models.

Abstract: A language model with access to tools can commit to a final claim unsupported by the evidence it has seen, even when a single available tool call would resolve the uncertainty and its instructions explicitly forbid assumptions and guesses. We separate this failure into two precisely defined quantities: occurrence, how often the model makes an unsupported claim on its own, measured from the visible evidence and final claim without using the hidden correct answer; and conditional repair, how often those same naturally occurring unsupported claims are repaired when the missing evidence is supplied. On one fixed Qwen3-32B setup, 33 of 512 first responses to 256 new prompt templates ended with an unsupported established claim. We replayed each case from an exact copy of the state in which the claim occurred; within each matched replay, the alternative tool responses had the same structure and length and differed only in a one-character response code. Resolving evidence repaired 33 of 33 claims; a matched response carrying no useful information repaired 0 of 33. When the evidence supported the original answer, the model preserved 33 of 33, with no observed harm. In a separate experiment, on 64 cases where evidence was needed, an automatic checking rule added 21 evidence calls, corrected all 10 wrong unsupported claims, preserved the 11 that were correct by accident, and never changed a correct answer into a wrong one. On a fixed Gemma 4 setup using the same sampling settings, the model called the tool in all 512 first responses and never made an unsupported final claim, so conditional repair could not be measured for that setup. These results describe two local fixed model setups on two synthetic task families. They do not show how common this failure is in real-world deployments, nor that it reflects a general mechanism shared across models.

</details>


### [100] [Credo: Reusable Declarative Primitives for Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27790)
*Duo Lu,Andrew Crotty,Uğur Çetintemel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Credo converts opaque searched harnesses into structured, metadata-tagged declarative catalogs to enable reusable, provable harness generation for new LLM tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the inability to reuse and inspect knowledge embedded in imperative harness code produced by harness search for LLM agents.

Method: Extract a structured declarative description from searched harness, tag primitives with metadata, catalog with provenance, and use a compiler to bind stored primitives to generate harnesses for new tasks; includes preliminary results.

Result: Preliminary results show the approach is feasible and that declarative catalogs can support harness reuse; paper also outlines a broader research agenda.

Conclusion: A research agenda for the database community on cost-based compilation over declarative catalogs and maintenance under model/workload drift; harness reuse is viable via declarative catalogs.

Abstract: An LLM application depends on both a model and a harness: the program that determines what each call sees, how many calls to make, and which answers to trust. Coding agents can now discover strong harnesses by searching over candidate programs, but the resulting artifact is an opaque block of imperative code whose logical steps, runtime signals, physical execution decisions, and prompt strategies remain implicit and task-specific, forcing subsequent tasks to start the harness search process from scratch. The potential for reuse, however, is substantial. A searched harness encodes significant knowledge, such as the logical steps that work, the signals that matter, the physical operator decisions that adapt execution, and the prompt strategies that are effective, yet this knowledge is buried in imperative code with no inspectable or reusable structure, nor does it carry any provenance or metadata. Credo addresses this problem by recovering a structured declarative description of a searched harness, tagging each extracted primitive with relevant metadata, and cataloguing all of it with provenance. A compiler can then bind stored primitives to generate harnesses for new tasks without having to start the search over from scratch. This paper provides preliminary results demonstrating the potential of our approach and lays out a related research agenda that the database community is well-positioned to tackle, including cost-based compilation over declarative catalogs and catalog maintenance under model and workload drift.

</details>


### [101] [ReToolSQL: Agentic Reinforcement Learning for Robust Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27796)
*Pratik Kakkar,Chandra Dhir,Ravi Shankar,Pareekshit Reddy Gaddam,Anup Shirgaonkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-stage training (SFT on rejected reasoning traces + agentic RFT on multi-turn tool-use) for text-to-SQL improves single-pass and multi-turn performance, achieving top results on BIRD with a 31B model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the one-turn limitation in text-to-SQL and enable smaller models to reach larger-system performance by leveraging execution feedback and iterative refinement.

Method: Stage 1: supervised fine-tuning on rejection-sampled reasoning traces from a privileged teacher to expand solvable cases. Stage 2: agentic reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) over multi-turn tool-use trajectories with composite rewards based on execution accuracy. Initialize RFT from SFT. Evaluate on Gemma 4 (31B) with and without self-consistency.
