Result: RFT alone achieves 73.66% EX on BIRD-SQL dev (74.12% with self-consistency). SFT→RFT yields 74.32% EX single-pass and 74.77% EX with self-consistency, ranking first on the BIRD single-model dev set.

Conclusion: An SFT→RFT pipeline over tool-use trajectories is a practical, annotation-light path to robust enterprise-grade text-to-SQL, improving both coverage (pass@k) and single-pass accuracy through iterative verification, retrieval, and repair guided by execution feedback.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that reinforcement learning from execution feedback can substantially improve text-to-SQL performance, often enabling smaller models to match or exceed much larger systems. However, most existing approaches treat SQL generation as a single-turn task, limiting the model's ability to recover from errors through iterative refinement. We present ReToolSQL, a two-stage training framework for text-to-SQL that combines (i) a supervised warm-start on rejection-sampled reasoning traces with (ii) agentic reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) over multi-turn tool-use trajectories. The key insight is that the two stages act on complementary axes, the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on verified privileged-teacher traces expands the set of solvable questions (raising pass@k coverage on the hardest cases), while RFT converts that expanded capability into higher single-pass accuracy by teaching the model when to verify, what evidence to retrieve, and how to repair faulty SQL from execution feedback. Applied to Gemma 4 instruction-tuned (31B), RFT alone achieves 73.66% execution accuracy (EX) on the BIRD-SQL development benchmark (74.12% EX with self-consistency). Initializing RFT from the SFT checkpoint (SFT$\to$RFT) yields our strongest model at 74.32% EX single-pass and 74.77% EX with self-consistency. At the time of writing, this ranked first on the BIRD single-model development-set leaderboard. The approach uses composite rewards anchored on execution correctness, requires no human annotation beyond the benchmark itself, and operates within a single dense 31B model, showing that a properly designed SFT$\to$RFT pipeline over tool-use trajectories is a practical path toward robust enterprise-grade text-to-SQL.

</details>


### [102] [CEDAR: Automata as Verifiable Interfaces for Language-Guided Embodied Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27797)
*Lekai Chen,Alvaro Velasquez,Ashutosh Trivedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CEDAR grounds natural-language constraints into deterministic finite automata via counterexample-guided refinement, enabling verifiable, composable control for embodied agents and reducing reliance on repeated LLM prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural-language tasking often imposes persistent or safety constraints; free-form program outputs by LLMs are brittle, unverified, and hard to compose or repair. A stable, verifiable representation is needed to enforce constraints as agents operate in changing environments.

Method: A counterexample-guided framework grounds instructions as regular languages over environment event traces. Uses a language model for semantic judgments and observed traces for corrections; represents skills and specifications as DFAs; intersects skill DFAs with constraint DFAs to yield executable controllers. Evaluated in Minecraft with matching simulator/API observations to a program-generating baseline, comparing constraint preservation and LLM-query load.

Result: CEDAR preserves temporal/spatial constraints that baseline fails to maintain, reuses learned skills across tasks, and reduces cumulative LLM queries by amortizing constraint enforcement through automata operations.

Conclusion: Regular languages provide a practical verification layer between natural-language instructions and embodied policies. DFA-based controllers enable reliable constraint enforcement and modular composition, with potential broad applicability beyond the tested domain.

Abstract: Natural-language tasking of embodied agents is rarely just goal specification: users also impose constraints that must persist while the world changes. Code-generating LLM agents can produce plausible behaviors for such instructions, but their free-form programs provide no stable object to verify, compose with new constraints, or repair from a failing trace. We present CEDAR, a counterexample-guided framework that grounds instructions as regular languages over environment event traces. CEDAR uses a language model for semantic judgments and execution traces for correction, then represents both skills and specifications as deterministic finite automata. This turns constraints into executable finite-state objects: a learned skill can be intersected with a learned sleep at night or stay in this biome specification, yielding a controller that enforces the learned constraint by construction rather than by repeated prompting. In Minecraft, with the same simulator/API observations available to a program-generating baseline, CEDAR maintains temporal and spatial constraints that the baseline fails to preserve and amortizes reuse of learned skills, reducing cumulative LLM queries. These results suggest that regular languages offer a practical verification layer between natural-language instructions and embodied-agent policies.

</details>


### [103] [CURA: Certified Runtime Alarms for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27808)
*Divake Kumar,Sina Tayebati,Devashri Naik,Amanda Sofie Rios,Nilesh Ahuja,Omesh Tickoo,Ranganath Krishnan,Amit Ranjan Trivedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: External, telemetry-based runtime monitoring (CURA) with certified false-alarm control improves early failure detection in computer-use agents, enabling alarm-gated oversight and cascades, but its advantage over a token-based baseline is not statistically significant; performance is credible but not universally superior, with notable gaps where monitoring is uninformative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-reporting is cheap but often unreliable for oversight of capable computer-use agents; need an external, model-agnostic monitoring approach that bounds false alarms and provides verifiable oversight.

Method: Evaluate a three-component pipeline (read-only feasibility gate, planner, GUI executor) on 361 OSWorld tasks. Introduce CURA—an external monitor that reads only harness-visible telemetry, no model internals, no extra LLM calls, no prompt changes—and transform the trajectory into a sequential test with certified false-alarm control. Use CUSUM-based alarms at alpha=0.10 and 0.20. Report online and retrospective AUROCs, recall under certified budgets, and cascade outcomes (alarm-gated oversight). Compare to a total-token baseline; analyze when behavioral monitoring is informative vs not.

Result: Mean task score: 82.9 (vs 72.4 human reference). 64/71 failures end with a success claim; 61 acknowledge no blocker; explicit failure affordance unused in ~9,100 calls. CURA detects 42.3% of failures a median of 31 steps before termination at realized false-alarm rate 0.066. Gate probe AUROC 0.69. Retrospective composite AUROC 0.828 (floor 0.802). Delta vs total-token baseline 0.026 (p=0.101). Online recall: 0.41 vs 0.34 at alpha=0.10; 0.56 vs 0.38 at alpha=0.20. Alarm-gated mid-execution oversight recovers 23/70 failures; frontier overseer engaged on 38; deployable cascade yields mean score 86.8 and 84.5% full-solve (305/361). Certificate bounds false alarms; identifies regimes where behavioral monitoring is uninformative.

Conclusion: CURA offers certified, external oversight with improved early detection and actionable cascades, but the online advantage over a token baseline is not statistically significant. The approach is promising for deployable overseers, yet some failures remain unflagged and monitoring is not universally informative. Further work should address statistical significance, generalizability, and the contexts where monitoring yields no added value.

Abstract: Self-report is the cheapest oversight channel a deployer has, and on capable computer-use agents (CUAs) it fails precisely where oversight matters. On 361 OSWorld tasks our pipeline, a read-only feasibility gate, a planner, and a GUI executor, reaches a mean task score of 82.9, above the 72.4 human reference, yet 64 of its 71 failures (90%) end with a success claim, 61 acknowledging no blocker, and the explicit failure affordance is never used in roughly 9,100 calls. We introduce CURA (Certified Runtime Alarms for Computer-Use Agents), an external monitor that reads only harness-visible telemetry, with no model internals, extra LLM calls, or prompt changes, and turns the running trajectory into a sequential test with certified false-alarm control. At alpha = 0.10 its CUSUM alarm detects 42.3% of failures a median of 31 steps before termination at a realized false-alarm rate of 0.066, and risk is partly resolvable before the first action (gate probe, 0.69 AUROC). Retrospectively the composite reaches 0.828 AUROC (fold-internal floor 0.802), but its margin over a total-token baseline is not significant (Delta = +0.026, p = 0.101); the separation is online, where CURA recalls more at matched certified budgets: 0.41 versus 0.34 at alpha = 0.10, 0.56 versus 0.38 at alpha = 0.20. Alarm-gated mid-execution oversight recovers 23 of 70 failures while spending a frontier overseer on 38, giving a deployable cascade at mean score 86.8 and 84.5% full-solve (305 of 361). The certificate bounds false alarms only. We also report where behavioral monitoring is uninformative.

</details>


### [104] [AcCoRD: Evaluating User-Agent Collaboration Under Realistic User Preference Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27818)
*Tejas Srinivasan,Shikib Mehri,Nandita Shankar Naik,Anirban Das,William M. Campbell,Jesse Thomason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces AcCoRD, a benchmark to test how agents handle dynamic user preferences during interaction across online shopping and travel planning. Frontier LLMs cope with underspecification but struggle with preferences that emerge or evolve mid-interaction; uncertainty-guided prompting helps but is not sufficient. Provides a resource to drive development of agents capable of navigating full real-world complexity of user preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world user preferences are dynamic, partially specified, and revealed or relaxed during interaction. Existing benchmarks focus on underspecified preferences, neglecting evolving preferences and ambiguity resolution that occur in practice.

Method: Evaluate five frontier LLMs using two prompting strategies (vanilla ReAct and an uncertainty-guided variant prompting models to identify and resolve ambiguity about user preferences) on two domains (online shopping and travel planning) using the AcCoRD benchmark.

Result: Frontier models manage underspecification but struggle to adapt to preferences that emerge or change during the interaction. Uncertainty-guided prompting provides limited improvements; prompting alone is insufficient to elicit robust uncertainty recognition. The benchmark is released as a resource to foster development of agents capable of handling full complexity of real-world user preferences.

Conclusion: AcCoRD fills a gap by evaluating dynamic preference handling and encourages development of more sophisticated uncertainty modeling and interaction strategies beyond static or underspecified preference handling.

Abstract: User preferences in user-agent collaboration are rarely static and fully-specified upfront: preferences are formed, revealed, adjusted, and relaxed during interaction. Existing benchmarks for evaluating user-agent collaboration focus almost exclusively on resolving underspecified preferences, thereby failing to capture the richer dynamics of real-world interaction. We introduce AcCoRD, a user-agent collaboration benchmark requiring agents to handle diverse user preference dynamics in two domains: online shopping and travel planning. We evaluate five frontier LLMs under two prompting strategies: vanilla ReAct, and an uncertainty-guided variant that prompts models to identify and resolve ambiguity about user preferences. Our results reveal that frontier models can handle underspecification but struggle to satisfy preferences that emerge or evolve mid-interaction and require more sophisticated uncertainty modeling. Further, prompting alone fails to elicit the required uncertainty recognition. We release AcCoRD as a resource for developing agents that can navigate the full complexity of real-world user preferences.

</details>


### [105] [Evidential-Based Higher-Order Set Argumentation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27824)
*Shuai Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EHSAF unifies evidential, higher-order, and set-based argumentation with two complete semantics (adjacent three-valued labellings and extension-based well-founded support), divergence on support cycles but equivalence under acyclicity, and propositional encodings in three-valued Łukasiewicz plus continuous fuzzy logics, enabling both qualitative and quantitative evidential reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the fragmentation of evidential support, higher-order relation handling, and collective interactions in existing frameworks by providing a unified, expressive framework (EHSAF) that supports both multiple-valued semantics and fuzzy encodings.

Method: Define EHSAF with higher-order, set-based interactions. Develop adjacent complete labelling semantics (3-valued) and extension-based complete semantics. Prove divergence with support cycles and equivalence under support-acyclicity. Provide a normal propositional encoding; prove 3-valued Łukasiewicz models correspond to adjacent labellings. Extend encoding to Gödel, Product, and Łukasiewicz fuzzy logics and establish properties (continuity, monotonicity, boundary, existence) and that ternarisation recovers adjacent labellings under natural t-norms.

Result: Two complete semantics for EHSAF; divergence on cycles, equivalence under acyclicity. A normal propositional encoding whose 3-valued Łukasiewicz models match adjacent labellings. Extension to continuous fuzzy logics with preserved properties and correspondences via ternarisation.

Conclusion: EHSAF provides a unified framework that combines expressive evidential argumentation with principled three-valued and fuzzy semantics, bridging qualitative and quantitative reasoning about evidence.

Abstract: Evidential argumentation extends Dung's abstract argumentation by requiring arguments and interactions to be backed by chains of evidence rooted in prima-facie elements. However, existing formalisms lack a unified treatment of evidential support, higher-order relations (attacks and supports targeting arbitrary elements), and collective interactions (sources as sets). In this paper, we introduce the Evidential-Based Higher-Order Set Argumentation Framework (EHSAF), which conservatively generalises several existing frameworks within a single expressive setting. We develop two complete semantics for EHSAFs: an \emph{adjacent complete labelling semantics} that admits multiple truth values (true, false, undecided) for arguments in support cycles, reflecting an open epistemic attitude toward future evidence; and an \emph{extension-based complete semantics} that follows a strict evidentialist stance, accepting only arguments with well-founded support chains. We show that these two semantics diverge in the presence of support cycles, and prove their equivalence under support-acyclicity. To enable computational reasoning, we provide a normal propositional encoding of EHSAFs and prove that, in three-valued Łukasiewicz logic, its models correspond precisely to the adjacent complete labellings. We further extend this encoding to continuous fuzzy logics (G{ö}del, Product, and Łukasiewicz), defining a continuous fuzzy normal encoded semantics. We establish that this fuzzy semantics satisfies key properties---continuity, monotonicity, boundary conditions, and solution existence---and that its ternarisation recovers the adjacent complete labellings under natural t-norm conditions. Our framework thus unifies expressive argumentation with principled three-valued and fuzzy semantics, bridging the gap between qualitative and quantitative reasoning about evidence.

</details>


### [106] [RealSWE: A Compositional Evaluation of Coding Agents under Realistic User Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27831)
*Gyuhyeong Kim,Hyojung Gwon,Jeonghyeon Kim,Kyuhong Shim,Sunjae Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: There is a notable gap between SWE-bench benchmark prompts and real user prompts; the authors propose a sys framework with 381 multi-variant task families, showing that including Desired Behavior and Motivation improves LLM performance on software engineering tasks, while other information and linguistic style have limited gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterize the mismatch between real prompts and benchmark datasets to guide better prompt design and evaluation for SE-oriented LLMs.

Method: Define a six-category information taxonomy and four linguistic style dimensions; apply to real SWE-chat prompts and SWE-bench problems; create sys with 381 multi-variant task families; evaluate seven contemporary LLMs on sys; analyze the effects of input realism, content components, and linguistic style on performance.

Result: Real prompts are shorter and less formal (88% are solely problem statements; 87% casual); introducing Desired Behavior and Motivation boosts performance; Environment Information and Reproduction Steps add tokens without benefit; realism lowers resolution rates by 6.4 percentage points and can alter model rankings; linguistic style has small effects; seven LLMs show improved SE performance when prompts specify goals.

Conclusion: To improve LLM-based software engineering performance, users should explicitly state desired behavior and motivation; system-informed prompts are a practical route to bridging the prompt realism gap.

Abstract: Coding agents are now commonly evaluated on the SWE-bench family of benchmarks, whose tasks are built from curated GitHub issues--long, structured, and information-rich. Real user requests, however, are typically far shorter and less structured. To characterize this gap, we define a six-category information taxonomy and four dimensions of linguistic style, and apply them to real user prompts from SWE-chat and problem statements from SWE-bench Verified and Pro. We find that requests carrying only a problem statement, alone or with limited additional context, account for 88% of real prompts but just 7% of benchmark problems. Furthermore, 87% of real prompts are casually written whereas 94% of benchmark problems are formal. Guided by these observations, we introduce sys, 381 multi-variant task families derived from SWE-bench Verified and Pro. Variants within each family share the same underlying task and gold patch while differing only in information composition and linguistic style. Evaluating seven contemporary LLMs with sys, we find that i) realistic inputs reduce resolution rates by 6.4 pp on average and can change model rankings. Controlled analysis further shows that ii) including Desired Behavior and Motivation significantly affects performance, whereas Environment Information and Reproduction Steps merely add tokens without measurable benefit; iii) linguistic style has only small, model-dependent effects. These findings provide actionable guidance for users and agents: explicitly stating the desired behavior and motivation--which most real prompts omit--substantially improves the LLM's software engineering performance.

</details>


### [107] [AcrossVAM1.0: Particle World Modeling for Text-Assisted Robot Video Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28491)
*Yafei Zhang,Nan Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Text-guided, factorized robot video predictor leveraging semantic particles and a lightweight spatio-temporal model to separate motion and appearance; yields improved trajectory accuracy and PSNR/SSIM, but LPIPS and language grounding remain challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate entanglement of motion reasoning and high-frequency appearance in monolithic pixel models by factorizing prediction into explicit, low-dimensional dynamics (semantic particles) and appearance delivery guided by language.

Method: A frozen SAM-DLP codec extracts semantic particles for robot components (robot, arm, gripper) and background from four context frames. A 0.28M temporal Transformer aligns particle identities and advances their states, modulated by a frozen OpenCLIP instruction embedding via FiLM. A causal dual-stream decoder renders motion from particles and appearance solely from the last observed frame; a residual refiner and learned delivery mask produce five future frames without future appearance access.

Result: On a VRS benchmark: particle dynamics reduce trajectory error by 21.0% versus persistence. PSNR/SSIM for future frames improve from 19.97/0.796 to 20.573/0.8004; motion-region PSNR improves from 11.89 to 13.23. LPIPS remains worse than persistence, and correct-versus-shuffled language changes trajectory error by 2.8–3.1%. Analyses include oracle, negative-control, multi-seed, and per-robot settings.

Conclusion: Explicit particle dynamics constitute a promising low-dimensional interface for robot video prediction, but robust language grounding and appearance delivery are current primary challenges and avenues for future work.

Abstract: Predicting robot videos requires both precise motion reasoning and preservation of high-frequency appearance, yet monolithic pixel models entangle these objectives and often conceal their progress behind a strong last-frame baseline. We present AcrossVAM1.0, a lightweight, text-assisted video action model that factorizes future prediction into object-centric motion and dense appearance. A frozen SAM3-DLP codec decomposes four context frames into semantic particles for the robot, arm, and gripper, together with a background latent. A 0.28M-parameter spatio-temporal Transformer aligns particle identities, rolls their states forward, and is modulated by a frozen OpenCLIP instruction embedding through FiLM. A causal dual-stream decoder combines particle-rendered motion with appearance encoded exclusively from the last observed frame; a residual refiner and learned delivery mask produce five future frames without access to future appearance. On our VRS benchmark constructed from diverse real-robot trajectories, particle dynamics reduce trajectory error by 21.0\% over persistence. Across three delivery-mask seeds, AcrossVAM1.0 improves future-frame PSNR/SSIM from 19.97/0.796 to 20.573/0.8004, while raw particle generation improves motion-region PSNR from 11.89 to 13.23. The delivered model does not yet beat persistence in LPIPS, and correct-versus- shuffled language changes trajectory error by only 2.8--3.1%. We report these limitations alongside oracle, negative-control, multi-seed, and per-robot analyses. The results show that explicit particle dynamics are a promising low-dimensional interface for robot video prediction, while robust language grounding and appearance delivery remain the principal open challenges.

</details>


### [108] [KLOD: Locality-Preserving Knowledge Editing via Non-Target Distribution Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27839)
*Hojun Jeong,Gyunyeop Kim,Sangwoo Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KLOD is a bounded, distribution-preserving fine-tuning objective for knowledge editing that stops target amplification at a probability threshold and preserves non-target distributions, reducing locality degradation while maintaining edit reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard cross-entropy-based editing increases the edited target probability without constraining changes to the non-target output distribution, causing accumulation of distributional drift (locality degradation) in sequential editing.

Method: Introduce KLOD, a bounded objective that (1) halts target probability amplification once a threshold is reached; (2) preserves the target-excluded non-target distribution at target positions; (3) preserves the full next-token distribution at prefix positions, enabling distributional stability during fine-tuning-based edits.

Result: On CounterFact and ZsRE with Llama3-8B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, KLOD substantially mitigates locality degradation while maintaining high edit reliability. The target probability threshold enables a controllable Generalization–Locality trade-off. Ablation, multi-seed, and distributional KL analyses indicate locality gains arise from preserving output distributions rather than merely weakening the edit.

Conclusion: KLOD provides a controllable balance between generalization and locality in knowledge editing, with code available on GitHub.

Abstract: Fine-tuning-based knowledge editing is simple and architecture-agnostic, but standard cross-entropy increases the edited target probability without explicitly constraining changes in the non-target output distribution. In sequential editing, such unconstrained redistribution can accumulate as distributional drift and contribute to locality degradation. We propose KLOD, a bounded and distribution-preserving objective for fine-tuning-based knowledge editing that separates the intended target update from distributions that should remain stable. KLOD stops target amplification once a probability threshold is reached, while preserving the target-excluded non-target distribution at target positions and the full next-token distribution at prefix positions. Experiments on CounterFact and ZsRE with Llama3-8B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct show that KLOD substantially mitigates locality degradation while maintaining high edit reliability. The target probability threshold further provides a controllable Generalization--Locality trade-off. Ablation, multi-seed, and distributional KL analyses support the interpretation that KLOD's locality gains are associated with preserving output distributions rather than simply weakening the edit. Code is available on GitHub https://github.com/Hostoday/KLOD .

</details>


### [109] [When Robots Mishear Us: Mapping the Safety Risks of Voice-Controlled Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28518)
*Sihan Jia,Oliver Lemon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASR errors can undermine embodied AI safety by allowing harmful instructions to be accepted or executed; some errors preserve meaning but add ambiguity; others reduce model refusal. Automatic correction helps in some cases but is not universally effective; overall, ASR inaccuracies create substantial safety risks for embodied AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how automatic speech recognition (ASR) errors in user input affect the safety of Embodied AI (EAI) systems, and to quantify how different error types interact with existing safety mechanisms.

Method: Simulate ASR errors on user commands and evaluate their impact on safety using benchmarks SafeAgentBench and POEX. Analyze how various error types alter semantic structure, ambiguity, model refusal behavior, and the generation/execution of unsafe plans. Assess whether automatic correction of ASR errors mitigates risk.

Result: Certain ASR errors preserve semantic structure while increasing harmful ambiguity; others degrade the model’s refusal behavior, enabling unsafe plans to be generated and executed. Automatic error correction reduces risk in some scenarios but is not universally effective. Overall, ASR errors significantly elevate safety risks in embodied AI.

Conclusion: ASR inaccuracies constitute a meaningful safety risk for embodied AI, necessitating robust safety controls that account for input noise, error correction limitations, and ambiguity in user commands.

Abstract: We investigate whether automatic speech recognition (ASR) errors in user input can lead to unsafe outputs from Embodied AI (EAI) models. We find that ASR errors can lead to harmful instructions being accepted and executed by EAI models, thereby reducing safety. We simulate ASR errors and combine them with existing safety benchmarks (SafeAgentBench and POEX) to evaluate how different errors affect embodied AI safety. We find that some of them preserve semantic structure but increase harmful ambiguity, while others weaken the model refusal behaviour and allow unsafe plans to be generated and executed. We show that in some cases automatic correction of ASR errors can reduce the risk, but this is not always effective. Overall, we show that ASR errors lead to significant safety risks for embodied AI.

</details>


### [110] [An Empirical Evaluation of Cross-City POI Recommendation on a Large-Scale Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27840)
*Peibo Li,Yang Song,Hao Xue,Maarten de Rijke,Flora D. Salim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Trip World-based re-evaluation reveals three bottlenecks in cross-city POI recommendation: overreliance on destination-region priors, deteriorating accuracy-efficiency at scale, and limited benefit from semantic metadata; underscoring the need for task-specific designs for cross-city transfer, semantic grounding, and scalable reasoning over unseen inventories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether conclusions from small benchmarks generalize to worldwide coverage, low home-destination overlap, and large, semantically rich POI inventories, and to probe the efficacy of hometown-aware, agentic, and metadata-driven approaches.

Method: Empirical evaluation on the Trip World benchmark: systematic assessment of hometown-aware models, analysis of accuracy vs. efficiency at scale, evaluation of semantic metadata integration, and a diagnostic pilot applying agentic methods from next-POI recommendation.

Result: Three bottlenecks identified: (1) hometown-aware models rely more on destination-region priors than on user-specific transfer; (2) the accuracy–efficiency trade-off worsens at scale, with the simplest model being among the strongest; (3) existing mechanisms for integrating semantic metadata yield little benefit. A diagnostic pilot shows naive agentic adaptation underperforms a simple popularity baseline despite the presence of semantic signals.

Conclusion: There is a need for task-specific designs that support cross-city preference transfer, robust semantic grounding, and scalable reasoning over unseen destination inventories.

Abstract: Cross-city point-of-interest (POI) recommendation is crucial for navigating unfamiliar urban environments, yet its progress has historically been constrained by data limitations. Using the recently proposed large-scale benchmark Trip World, we empirically re-examine whether conclusions drawn on small prior benchmarks still hold under worldwide coverage, low home-destination region overlap, and large, semantically rich POI inventories. Our evaluation surfaces three bottlenecks of representative state-of-the-art methods: (1) hometown-aware models appear to rely more on destination-region priors than on user-specific preference transfer; (2) their accuracy-efficiency trade-off degrades at this scale, where the simplest model is among the strongest; and (3) existing mechanisms for integrating semantic metadata yield little benefit. We further include a diagnostic pilot on agentic methods adapted from next-POI recommendation, finding that naive adaptation trails a simple popularity prior even though the relevant semantic signal is present in the data. These results highlight the need for task-specific designs that support cross-city preference transfer, semantic grounding, and scalable reasoning over unseen destination inventories.

</details>


### [111] [From Uncertainty to Clinical Risk: Severity-Aware Conformal Planning for Interactive Medical Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27847)
*Yue Zhou,Haiyang Zhou,Jin Zhang,Kong Wang,Yongxin Ni,Youhua Li,Hanwen Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Severity-Aware Conformal Clinical Planning (SAC-CP): a risk-sensitive framework for interactive medical diagnosis that combines conformal risk estimates with MCTS to optimize long-horizon questions vs. diagnosis, yielding higher accuracy with fewer questions and fewer high-risk errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address asymmetric clinical risk not captured by predictive uncertainty and the lack of long-horizon planning in existing uncertainty-driven interactive diagnosis methods.

Method: Maintain complementary beliefs: diagnostic, safety, and masked-evidence. Calibrate turn-specific predictive sets and severity-weighted differential-diagnosis risk on held-out diagnostic trajectories. Integrate calibrated clinical risk into Monte Carlo Tree Search to evaluate long-horizon Ask and Commit trajectories.

Result: Experiments on DDXPlus and MediQ show improved diagnostic accuracy with fewer questions across multiple LLMs; enhanced differential-diagnosis quality and reduced high-risk errors in severe cases.

Conclusion: Clinical risk should guide information-acquisition and diagnostic decision-making; supports risk-aware planning and motivates future work on clinical-risk-oriented interactive diagnosis and information-acquisition methods.

Abstract: Interactive medical diagnosis dynamically acquires patient information through multiple rounds of questioning, supporting accurate, efficient, and safe clinical decisions under incomplete evidence. Existing methods commonly guide information acquisition with predictive uncertainty or label ambiguity, but overlook the asymmetric clinical risk of missing severe diseases and lack unified long-horizon planning over whether to continue asking questions or commit to a diagnosis. To address these limitations, we propose Severity-Aware Conformal Clinical Planning, which formulates interactive diagnosis as a risk-sensitive sequential decision problem. The framework maintains complementary diagnostic, safety, and masked-evidence beliefs; calibrates turn-specific diagnostic prediction sets and severity-weighted differential-diagnosis risk on held-out diagnostic trajectories; and introduces the calibrated clinical risk into Monte Carlo Tree Search to jointly evaluate long-horizon Ask and Commit trajectories. Experiments on DDXPlus and MediQ show that our method achieves more accurate diagnoses with fewer questions across multiple large language models, while improving differential-diagnosis quality and reducing high-risk errors in severe cases. These findings validate the value of using clinical risk, rather than predictive uncertainty alone, as a planning signal and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework for information acquisition and risk-aware diagnostic decision making. They also motivate future work on clinical-risk-oriented interactive diagnosis and information-acquisition methods.

</details>


### [112] [SpikeOPD: Stable On-Policy Distillation for Autoregressive Spiking Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27857)
*Enqiao Lu,Xingrui Yu,Yiwei Fu,Zhenglin Wan,Pengfei Zhou,Wangbo Zhao,Muqing Jian,Xueyi Zhang,Yang You,Ivor Tsang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SpikeOPD is a stable on-policy distillation framework for autoregressive SNNs that combines full-KL teacher corrections with matched-prefix policy anchoring and layerwise spike regularization to enable learning from self-generated prefixes while maintaining rollout stability, achieving higher accuracy across model scales without sacrificing sparsity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive ANN-to-SNN distillation suffers from prefix-source mismatch and rollout instability when using self-generated prefixes; on-policy distillation alone can suffer delayed feedback collapse, necessitating a stable framework.

Method: SpikeOPD integrates (i) full-KL teacher correction to reduce output-policy mismatch, (ii) matched-prefix policy anchoring to constrain policy drift on the same prefixes, and (iii) layerwise spike regularization to limit firing-rate deviations during on-policy adaptation; evaluated across three model scales with self-generated prefixes.

Result: Across 0.125B, 0.35B, and 1.3B models, SpikeOPD yields average accuracy gains of 0.8, 1.7, and 2.9 points over corresponding KD SNNs while preserving sparse-compute profiles.

Conclusion: SpikeOPD provides a stable, effective on-policy distillation framework for autoregressive SNNs, mitigating instability of previous OPD approaches and achieving improved accuracy with maintained sparsity; on-policy coverage alone is insufficient for stability.

Abstract: Spiking neural networks (SNNs) offer a path to energy-efficient language modeling through sparse encoding and event-driven computation, but training capable spiking language models from scratch remains difficult. A practical alternative is ANN-to-SNN migration through knowledge distillation (KD), where a pretrained artificial neural network (ANN) teacher supervises an SNN student. Existing migration approaches distill on fixed corpus prefixes, whereas autoregressive inference conditions on self-generated prefixes, creating prefix-source mismatch. It manifests as output-policy mismatch with the ANN teacher and internal spiking-dynamics drift between self-generated and matched corpus prefixes. On-policy distillation (OPD) offers a natural way to mitigate both manifestations by continuing teacher supervision on self-generated prefixes. We evaluate a teacher-only full-KL variant, Vanilla OPD, via a controlled stress test and observe it may suffer from delayed rollout-feedback collapse. This result shows that on-policy coverage alone does not ensure stable adaptation. Motivated by these findings, we propose SpikeOPD, a stable on-policy distillation framework for autoregressive SNNs that learns from self-generated prefixes while maintaining rollout stability. It applies full-KL teacher correction to reduce output-policy mismatch, while matched-prefix policy anchoring constrains policy departure from the frozen reference SNN on the same prefixes. Layerwise spike regularization further limits firing-rate deviations during on-policy adaptation. Across three model scales, SpikeOPD improves average accuracy over the corresponding KD SNNs by 0.8, 1.7, and 2.9 points at 0.125B, 0.35B, and 1.3B, respectively, while preserving their sparse-compute profiles.

</details>


### [113] [CoRe-MoE: Compact Reusable MoE for Continual Multimodal Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27867)
*Runze Liu,Naibin Gu,Mingxu Ai,Yuqing Li,Peng Fu,Zheng Lin,Weiping Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoRe-MoE is a compact reusable MoE for continual multimodal instruction tuning that reuses direction subspaces and trains compact coordinates with task-specific low-rank routers, achieving higher final average performance with ~1% trainable parameters vs sequential LoRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full expert expansion in LoRA-MoE is parameter-heavy; analyses reveal substantial overlap in input- and output-side update subspaces across tasks, suggesting that reusable subspace bases can capture common adaptation directions for continual learning.

Method: Construct an initial expert bank and extract reusable input- and output-side direction bases (via subspace decomposition, e.g., SVD) from it; for subsequent tasks, train only compact coordinate experts and task-specific low-rank routers while keeping base experts fixed.

Result: On two representative multimodal LLMs, CoRe-MoE improves final average performance over the strongest competing baseline by up to 5.90 points and uses less than 1% of the trainable parameters required by sequential LoRA for later tasks; code available at provided URL.

Conclusion: CoRe-MoE effectively reduces parameter overhead in continual multimodal instruction tuning by reusing shared subspaces, enabling strong task adaptation with substantially fewer trainable parameters.

Abstract: Continual multimodal instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to acquire new task abilities sequentially while preserving previously learned knowledge. LoRA-MoE provides a promising solution by introducing expert-based capacity, but repeatedly learning and maintaining full LoRA experts leads to substantial parameter overhead. This raises a natural question: is full expert expansion necessary for every new task? To answer it, we analyze the SVD of task-specific LoRA updates and observe substantial overlap in their input- and output-side LoRA direction subspaces, with task-specific adaptation largely captured by lightweight coordinates over these subspaces. Motivated by this observation, we propose CoRe-MoE, a Compact Reusable MoE framework for parameter-efficient continual multimodal instruction tuning. CoRe-MoE extracts reusable input- and output-side direction bases from an initial expert bank, and for subsequent tasks trains only compact coordinate experts together with task-specific low-rank routers. Experiments on two representative MLLMs show that CoRe-MoE improves final average performance over the strongest competing baseline by up to 5.90 points, while using less than 1% of the trainable parameters required by sequential LoRA for later tasks. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/runzezz/CoRe-MoE.

</details>


### [114] [See, Hypothesize, Validate: Multimodal Agentic Framework for Discovering Governing PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27869)
*Sarang Manoj Pekhale,Amartya Roy,Rajat Sarkar,Souvik Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A library-free, agent-based framework (MAGE) for discovering governing PDEs via a confidence-driven hypothesis validation loop, achieving strong structural recovery and reduced coefficient error compared to baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome limitations of sparse/symbolic/LLM-based PDE discovery that rely on predefined libraries, are sensitive to noise or hallucinate, and have limited iterative refinement; need structured iterative validation.

Method: Four specialized agents: Differential Observer (derivatives/diagnostics), Phenomenology Extractor (multimodal cues), Governing Law Synthesizer (LLM-based candidate generation without library), Equation Arbiter (coefficient fitting and confidence scoring); iterative loop with accept/reject until threshold.

Result: On canonical PDE suite: 8/8 exact structure recovered; lowest coefficient error among methods on 7/8 systems; up to 4 orders of magnitude improvement; geometric-mean ~3 orders; also recovers operators in two complex geometries; lab sensor record selects a cubic restoring-force model with held-out R^2=0.98538.

Conclusion: Supports structured agentic reasoning for library-free PDE discovery; generalization beyond tested cases remains to be evaluated.

Abstract: Discovering governing partial differential equations (PDEs) from observational data remains a core challenge across the sciences. Existing sparse-regression, symbolic-regression, and LLM-based approaches can be constrained by predefined libraries, noise sensitivity, hallucination, or limited iterative refinement. We introduce \textbf{MAGE} (\textbf{M}ultimodal \textbf{A}gentic \textbf{G}overning \textbf{E}quation Discovery), an agentic framework that organizes PDE discovery as a \textit{confidence governed hypothesis validation loop} inspired by the scientific cycle of observation, hypothesis, and falsification. Four role-specialized agents collaborate: a \textit{Differential Observer} computing derivatives and diagnostic visualizations; a VLM-powered \textit{Phenomenology Extractor} distilling qualitative cues from multimodal diagnostics; an LLM-driven \textit{Governing Law Synthesizer} proposing candidates without a predefined library; and an \textit{Equation Arbiter} fitting coefficients and assigning confidence scores. Discovery iterates until the top candidate clears a user-specified threshold, providing a structured process with an explicit accept-reject protocol. On the evaluated canonical PDE suite, MAGE obtains \textbf{8/8} exact structural recovery and the lowest coefficient error among the compared methods on \textbf{7/8} systems, with improvements of up to \textbf{4 orders of magnitude} and a geometric-mean improvement of approximately \textbf{3 orders of magnitude}. The pipeline also recovers the expected operators in two complex geometries and, on one laboratory sensor record, selects a cubic restoring-force model with held-out $R^2=0.98538$. These results support further study of structured agentic reasoning for library-free governing-law discovery, while broader generalization remains to be evaluated.

</details>


### [115] [HyQuant: Hybrid-Precision Quantization for LLM Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27875)
*Jiatong Ding,Bingxin Xing,Yu Zhang,Dian Ding,Xiaodong Yi,Xianbin Ouyang,Feihu Zhou,Kun Zhang,Zhenyu Guo,Hao Pan,Guangtao Xue,Yiming Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid quantization for LLM attention (HyQuant): keep a small set of vertical-line tokens and local-window states in high precision while quantizing the rest to low-bit formats, achieving near-lossless accuracy with low overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-bit quantization of attention induces large errors at very low bit-widths; smoothing alone is insufficient to handle outliers—there is a need to protect accuracy-critical regions.

Method: HyQuant introduces a hybrid-precision quantization framework. It quantizes most attention states to low-bit formats but retains vertical-line tokens and local-window states in high precision, selected via lightweight vertical-line-aware attention signals. In the Prefill stage, a hybrid-precision attention operator preserves vertical-line tokens and the local sliding window in full precision while quantizing the remaining context. In the Decode stage, the same principle applies to KV-cache compression and fuses KV dequantization with attention computation to improve memory and hardware efficiency.

Result: Across diverse tasks, models, and datasets, HyQuant achieves nearly lossless accuracy with a simple design, demonstrating both efficiency and practical feasibility; code is available at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: Hybrid-precision quantization for LLM attention balances accuracy and efficiency, mitigating quantization error in attention with low overhead and enabling practical deployment for training/inference.

Abstract: Quantization has been widely adopted in LLM training and inference to reduce cost and improve efficiency. However, low-bit quantization of the \emph{attention} module often introduces large errors at very low bit-widths, causing performance degradation. Existing methods mainly rely on smoothing techniques to handle outliers, while we propose a hybrid quantization design to better balance accuracy and efficiency. Specifically, we propose \textbf{HyQuant}, an efficient hybrid quantization framework for LLM attention. HyQuant quantizes most attention states into low-bit formats while retaining a small set of vertical-line tokens and local-window states in high precision. These accuracy-critical regions are selected using lightweight vertical-line-aware attention-pattern signals, reducing quantization error with limited overhead. In the Prefill stage, HyQuant uses a hybrid-precision quantized attention operator that preserves vertical-line tokens and a local sliding window in full precision while quantizing the remaining context. In the Decode stage, HyQuant applies the same principle to KV-cache compression and fuses KV dequantization with attention computation to improve memory and hardware efficiency. Across diverse tasks, models, and datasets, HyQuant maintains nearly lossless accuracy with an extremely simple design, demonstrating the efficiency and practical feasibility of hybrid quantization for LLM attention. Code is available at: https://github.com/jerrysfls/HyQuant .

</details>


### [116] [Resource Constraints and Performance in Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27886)
*Amaz Salman,Malka Halgamuge,Teo Susnjak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenClaw vs NanoBot as agentic systems show no clear winner on full task completion; NanoBot shows higher partial completion and efficiency in some cases, while OpenClaw uses more time and memory. The two evidence layers yield different outcomes, underscoring the need to link task success with resource usage and execution provenance for evaluating autonomous AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems become increasingly autonomous, it is critical to evaluate how agentic architectures perform on real tasks while accounting for resource costs and the traceability of results to specific executions.

Method: Comparative evaluation using a paired primary benchmark and an instrumented, detailed subset of prompts. Measurements include full-task completion rates, partial completion, wall time, peak memory, and dominance relationships across prompts, with results linked to the corresponding executions.

Result: Primary benchmark: OpenClaw 31% full completion vs NanoBot 25% (difference 6 percentage points; 95% bootstrap CI -3 to 15; not statistically decisive). Instrumented layer: both systems 26% full completion; NanoBot partial completion 43% vs OpenClaw 26%; OpenClaw slower on 83% of prompts; higher peak memory on every prompt (GM wall time 2.98; GM peak memory 19.44). In the ten detailed prompts where at least one achieved partial/full completion, NanoBot dominated on eight; across 23 prompts, ten of eighteen dominance cases were cheaper joint failures. Outcome labels differ across layers, illustrating that capability and resource measurements must connect to attempt-level execution and scoring provenance.

Conclusion: Evaluation of autonomous AI should integrate verified task completion with observed resource use and a transparent record of the execution that produced each result. The study highlights tradeoffs between capability and efficiency and the importance of provenance in judging agentic systems.

Abstract: Progress toward more autonomous AI increasingly depends on agentic systems that combine a language model with tools, memory, state management, and multi-step execution. These mechanisms shape both task capability and operational burden. We compare OpenClaw and NanoBot as complete agentic systems using a paired primary benchmark and a more detailed instrumented subset of paired prompts. In the primary benchmark, the rate of full task completion was 31% for OpenClaw and 25% for NanoBot, a six-percentage-point difference with a 95% task-bootstrap interval from -3 to 15 percentage points, providing no statistically established full-completion advantage for either system. In the instrumented layer, both systems achieved 26% full completion, while NanoBot reached at least partial completion on 43% of prompts compared with 26% for OpenClaw. OpenClaw took longer on 83% of prompts and had a higher recorded peak-memory value on every prompt, with geometric mean ratios of 2.98 for wall time and 19.44 for peak memory. Among the ten detailed-layer prompts on which at least one system achieved partial or full completion, NanoBot weakly dominated on eight; across all 23 prompts, however, ten of its eighteen dominance cases were cheaper joint failures. Outcome labels differ across the two evidence layers, showing why agent-system evaluation should connect capability and resource measurements to attempt-level execution and scoring provenance. These findings show that progress toward more autonomous AI should be evaluated through verified task completion, observed resource use and records linking each result to the execution that produced it.

</details>


### [117] [Rubric-to-Code Credit Assignment for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27906)
*Rui Jin,Jikai Chen,Yihan Chen,Hao Zhou,Demin Zhu,Kaichen Yang,Dong Wang,Chenyi Zhuang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RCCA introduces rubric-level credit assignment for interactive web app generation, improving code-level optimization signals over existing sequence-level rewards. It enables Ling-RCCA-Flash to outperform prior models on MiniAppBench and ArtifactsBench, with transferable gains and top leaderboard performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard sequence-level RL rewards inadequately assign credit to localized, functional code regions (event handlers, state updates, DOM/CSS selectors). There is a need to propagate rubric-based functional feedback to granular code spans to improve implementation quality.

Method: RCCA transforms rubric-level feedback into localized optimization signals across generated code. It constructs training tasks around explicit functional rubrics, employs a hierarchical reward (format, source-code, runtime, functional failures), and aligns evaluator-attributions with the corresponding code spans and tokens to improve credit assignment.

Result: Ling-RCCA-Flash achieves 41.25 on MiniAppBench (41.25 vs Ling-3.0-Flash +32.20) and 76.19 on ArtifactsBench (top score, surpassing GPT-5; +4.48 over SFT baseline). It also outperforms Claude Opus 4.5 on MiniAppBench.

Conclusion: The rubric-based credit assignment framework yields transferable, implementation-level gains in interactive web app generation, demonstrating improved alignment between rubric feedback and code-level outcomes and establishing a new high bar on relevant benchmarks.

Abstract: Interactive web application generation requires models to produce usable HTML, CSS, and JavaScript applications from natural language requests. Unlike conventional code generation, application quality depends on multiple user-facing functional requirements, each often tied to localized code regions such as event handlers, state updates, DOM fragments, or CSS selectors. Standard GRPO collapses these structured outcomes into a single sequence-level reward and applies the resulting advantage uniformly to all tokens, weakening credit assignment. We propose \textbf{Rubric-to-Code Credit Assignment} (RCCA), a reinforcement learning framework that converts rubric-level functional feedback into localized optimization signals over generated code. RCCA builds training tasks around explicit functional rubrics, uses a hierarchical reward to separate format, source-code, runtime, and functional failures, and aligns evaluator-generated textual attributions with responsible code spans and generated tokens. The resulting model, \textbf{Ling-RCCA-Flash}, scores 41.25 on MiniAppBench, improving Ling-3.0-Flash by 32.20 points and slightly surpassing Claude Opus 4.5. It also reaches 76.19 on ArtifactsBench, improving the SFT model by 4.48 points and establishing a new top score under the official ArtifactsBench leaderboard setting by surpassing the GPT-5 score by 3.64 points, suggesting transferable implementation-level gains.

</details>


### [118] [AI Alignment through a Game-theoretic Lens: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27910)
*Yanan Cai,Zhongrui Zhao,Zhigang Lu,Ickjai Lee,Wei Emma Zhang,Minhui Xue,Yihong Zhang,Shuchao Pang,Wei Xiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey reframes AI alignment as a game-theoretic problem, organizing recent work around preference diversity, alignment priority, and temporal dynamics to identify where game theory helps, where it remains loose, and what open challenges remain for robust, adaptive, and verifiable AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs and capable agents are deployed in high-risk contexts, aligning complex, context-dependent human values remains challenging; existing methods improve helpfulness, harmlessness, and controllability but struggle with dynamic, non-transitive, multi-party preferences; a game-theoretic lens offers a unifying framework.

Method: Literature survey organizing progress around core game-theoretic elements and synthesizing along three challenges: preference diversity, alignment priority, and temporal dynamics.

Result: Clarifies when game-theoretic analysis is beneficial to alignment, where the framework is looser, and what remaining challenges exist for building robust, adaptive, and verifiable AI systems.

Conclusion: A game-theoretic perspective clarifies contributions and gaps in AI alignment and guides future work toward robust, adaptive, and verifiable AI systems.

Abstract: As large language models and increasingly capable AI agents are deployed in high-risk settings, aligning them with complex human values has become a central challenge. Existing alignment methods, while effective in improving helpfulness, harmlessness, and controllability, often struggle to capture real-world preferences that are context-dependent, non-transitive, and shaped by dynamic multi-party interactions. This survey reviews AI alignment through a game-theoretic lens. Specifically, it organizes recent progress around key game-theoretic elements and synthesizes the literature along three challenges: preference diversity, alignment priority, and temporal dynamics. This perspective clarifies where current alignment methods genuinely benefit from game-theoretic analysis, where the framework is looser, and what challenges remain in building robust, adaptive, and verifiable AI systems.

</details>


### [119] [From Documents to Reasoning: A Validated Synthetic Data Pipeline and Semantic-Aware Fine-Tuning for Financial Numerical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27919)
*Lokendra Birla,Milind Savagaonkar,Visnu Srinivasan,Sowmya Rasipuram,Shubhashis Sengupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes synthetic-data-driven fine-tuning of smaller LMs for financial QA using QLoRA, with aggressive data validation, a new arithmetic-expression-based evaluation metric, and a loss combining semantic similarity with cross-entropy, achieving gains on ConvFinQA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address evaluation reliability and consistency issues in financial QA across complex data formats (tables, charts, text) and reduce training costs by leveraging small language models with quantized adapters and synthetic data.

Method: Generate high-quality synthetic QA pairs with aggressive validation ensuring relevance and correctness; fine-tune small language models using Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA); introduce an evaluation metric that computes answer equivalence via arithmetic expressions rather than ground-truth; propose a loss that combines semantic similarity of predicted vs. reference expressions with standard cross-entropy.

Result: Significant improvements in QA accuracy on the ConvFinQA benchmark after synthetic data generation and fine-tuning with the proposed loss and evaluation metric.

Conclusion: Synthetic data generation, a specialized arithmetic-expression-based evaluation metric, and a hybrid loss with QLoRA enable improved financial QA performance and evaluation reliability, with potential applicability to other domain-specific multi-modal QA tasks.

Abstract: Financial question answering (QA) has emerged as a key benchmark for evaluating the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on domain-specific tasks involving complex data formats such as tables, charts, and rich textual narratives. While recent advancements have enabled models to reason across modalities and perform multi-step arithmetic operations, limitations remain in performance consistency, and evaluation reliability. In particular, standard evaluation metrics like Exact Match (EM) often fail to account for minor variations such as differences in units or formats, misleading performance assessments.
  In this work, we propose a comprehensive pipeline for improving financial QA systems through high-quality synthetic data generation and fine-tuning of smaller language models (SLMs) using Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA). Our pipeline includes aggressive data validation for synthetic question answer generation to ensure the relevance and correctness of synthetic question-answer pairs. We introduce a novel evaluation metric that matches answers computed from arithmetic expressions rather than ground-truth answers; providing a more accurate reflection of model reasoning capability. Furthermore, we propose a modified loss function that aligns predicted and reference expressions using semantic similarity, our novel evaluation metric and standard cross-entropy, resulting in improved performance. Experimental results on benchmark datasets, ConvFinQA demonstrate significant gains in QA accuracy after fine-tuning using synthetic dataset and proposed loss function.

</details>


### [120] [A Deep Learning-Based Stacking Ensemble Framework for Turbofan Engine Remaining Useful Life Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27940)
*Limon Bin Hossain,Md. Salehin Seyam,Md Rashedul Islam,Abdur Rahman,Md Sharifuzzaman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Stacking ensemble of four DL models (LSTM, CNN, CNN-LSTM, CNN-GRU) with an XGBoost meta-learner for RUL prediction on NASA C-MAPSS FD001/FD003.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address degradation-pattern complexity and model biases in RUL prognostics by leveraging diverse DL architectures and a strong meta-learner.

Method: Two-level stacking: four heterogeneous DL base learners generate out-of-fold predictions; XGBoost meta-learner combines them to predict RUL; evaluated on FD001 and FD003; compared with TCAT baseline; robustness checks via feature correlation, residuals, and convergence curves.

Result: RMSE 9.989 (FD001) and 8.613 (FD003); MAE 7.081 and 5.195; R^2 0.899 and 0.906. RMSE reductions of 10.2% and 21.8% versus TCAT baselines. Robustness supported by correlation/residual/convergence analyses.

Conclusion: Stacking DL-based ensemble with a powerful meta-learner yields superior, robust RUL prognostics on C-MAPSS, with clear gains over the baseline.

Abstract: This study proposes a two-level stacking ensemble framework for Remaining Useful Life (RUL) prediction of turbofan engines, evaluated on the NASA C-MAPSS benchmark using the FD001 and FD003 subsets. The framework integrates four heterogeneous deep learning base learners: Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM), Convolutional Neural Network (CNN), CNN-LSTM, and CNN-GRU, whose out-of-fold predictions are combined by an XGBoost meta-learner to capture complex degradation patterns while mitigating individual model biases. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that the stacking ensemble achieves superior predictive performance, with Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) of 9.989 and 8.613, Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of 7.081 and 5.195, and R-squared values of 0.899 and 0.906 for FD001 and FD003, respectively. Compared to the best-reported baseline (TCAT: RMSE 11.12 and 11.02), the proposed method achieves RMSE reductions of 10.2 percent and 21.8 percent for FD001 and FD003, respectively. Feature correlation analysis, residual diagnostics, and training convergence curves validate the model's robustness. These findings underscore the efficacy of stacking ensemble methods for prognostics and health management in safety-critical aerospace applications.

</details>


### [121] [CASTANET: Causality-Aware Spatio-Temporal Adversarial Network Using Traffic Incident Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27942)
*Toshiya Kitahara,Ryu Shirakami,Koh Takeuchi,Hisashi Kashima*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CASTANET predicts non-periodic traffic congestion caused by incidents by integrating spatio-temporal GNNs with causal treatment effect estimation, yielding RMSE improvements on Tokyo data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-periodic congestion due to incidents is sparse, localized, and context-dependent; standard DL for periodic forecasting underutilizes incident records and suffers selection bias.

Method: A framework named CASTANET that fuses spatio-temporal graph neural networks with causal treatment effect estimation to leverage incident records while mitigating bias.

Result: On real-world Tokyo traffic and incident data, CASTANET reduces RMSE by 4.0% overall vs the best baseline, 10.1% on incident-conditioned evaluation, and up to 14.55% gains under severe congestion.

Conclusion: Integrating incident data with causal treatment effects improves non-periodic congestion forecasting, addressing sparsity and bias issues in incident-driven traffic.

Abstract: Predicting non-periodic traffic congestion caused by sudden incidents (e.g., accidents and road damage) is crucial for advanced intelligent transportation systems. However, incident-driven congestion is difficult to forecast because incidents are extremely sparse, occur at specific times and locations, and have heterogeneous impacts depending on the traffic context. While recent deep learning approaches have significantly improved periodic traffic forecasting, their performance on non-periodic congestion remains limited, partly because incident records are not explicitly incorporated and their occurrence is strongly biased in space and time. To address these challenges, we propose CASTANET, which integrates spatio-temporal graph neural networks and causal treatment effect estimation to utilize incident records while mitigating selection bias. Experiments on real-world traffic data and accident records from Tokyo, which we treat as incidents, show that CASTANET reduces RMSE by 4.0% overall compared to the best baseline and by 10.1% on incident-conditioned evaluation, with gains reaching 14.55% under severe congestion.

</details>


### [122] [Cross-Session Decomposition Attacks: Scaling Risk and Intent-Aligned Retrieval Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27945)
*Disen Liao,Yihan Wang,Freda Shi,Yaoliang Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This work analyzes safety risks from cross-session decomposition attacks in LLMs, formalizes a compositional safety risk, and derives a conditional risk-transfer bound. It shows scaling can increase harmful uplift under a fixed decomposition–composition pipeline, and proposes IntentAlign-MiniLM as a defense with strong intent-retrieval performance. Code is available on GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling laws that improve language-modeling loss may inadvertently amplify safety risks when adversaries compose benign interactions across sessions. The paper formalizes this risk and seeks both theoretical guarantees and empirical evidence, plus a mitigation strategy.

Method: Theoretical derivation of a conditional risk-transfer bound: the gap between deployed composed risk and reference composed risk is bounded by the model’s excess loss on allowed subqueries, given dispersed evidence in the reference environment. Empirical work includes synthetic withholding experiments with wider transformers, and a 600-intent pretrained-LLM evaluation comparing Qwen3 and Gemma3 family models under a fixed decomposition–composition pipeline. A defense via IntentAlign-MiniLM (22M parameters) is proposed and evaluated on held-out intent retrieval and harmful-recall under guardrails. Code is released on GitHub.

Result: The bound shows the risk gap is controlled by the model’s excess loss on allowed subqueries. Wider transformers exhibit lower loss on held-out, unrevealed instructions that are recoverable from injected supporting facts. Larger Qwen3 and Gemma3 family models can yield greater harmful-capability uplift under the fixed decomposition–composition pipeline. IntentAlign-MiniLM outperforms larger embeddings for held-out intent retrieval and achieves the best learned-retriever harmful-recall across tested guardrails.

Conclusion: Compositional safety risk is a tangible consequence of scaling that can be mitigated by intent-aligned retrieval. The paper provides a formal bound, empirical evidence, and a practical defense; the codebase enables replication and further study.

Abstract: Scaling laws are usually read as a capability story: lower language-modeling loss yields more useful models. We study a safety consequence of this mechanism in \emph{cross-session decomposition attacks}, where benign-looking subqueries are asked across independent interactions and later recomposed toward a forbidden objective. We formalize this setting as \emph{compositional safety risk} and prove a conditional risk-transfer bound: when the reference environment already contains dispersed evidence for a risky reconstruction, the gap between deployed composed risk and reference composed risk is controlled by the model's excess loss on allowed subqueries. Synthetic withholding experiments show that wider transformers assign lower loss to held-out instructions that never appear verbatim in training but are recoverable from injected supporting facts. A 600-intent pretrained-LLM evaluation shows that larger Qwen3 and Gemma3 family members can yield greater harmful-capability uplift under a fixed decomposition-composition pipeline. As a defense, IntentAlign-MiniLM, our 22M-parameter intent-aligned retriever, outperforms much larger embedding models on held-out intent retrieval and yields the best learned-retriever harmful recall across tested guardrails. Code is available in \href{https://github.com/liaodisen/Cross-Session-Decomposition-Attacks}{our GitHub repository}.

</details>


### [123] [The Illusion of $\textit{What If}$: Evaluating the Breakdown of Counterfactual Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27953)
*Yucheng Wang,Yuetian Du,Zhengyi Liu,Rongyu Zhang,Bing Zhao,Boyu Yang,Ming Kong,Lin Qu,Hu Wei,Jie Liu,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WhatIfBench is a diagnostic benchmark for open-domain, open-form, long-horizon counterfactual causal reasoning, paired with PRISM, a graph-based evaluation framework for explanations; together they reveal current LLMs' causal reasoning remains underdeveloped, with room for improvement and non-saturated performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks focus on bounded settings with fixed variables or single outcomes, failing to test open-domain causal-process evaluation and long-horizon reasoning; there is a need to assess whether explanations map to valid causal structures and processes.

Method: Introduce WhatIfBench with 220 questions across STEM, HSS, and Hybrid domains; develop PRISM that converts natural-language explanations into a Response-Derived Semantic Causal Graph (events, states, mechanisms) and applies two metrics: a Process Metric for graph-level causal validity and a Rubric Metric for answer-level explanatory adequacy; evaluate six frontier LLMs.

Result: Models are far from saturating WhatIfBench; the strongest model attains 64.62% final score; analyses show persistent causal gaps, premise drift, and topology fragmentation despite fluent counterfactual narratives.

Conclusion: WhatIfBench diagnoses gaps in open-domain causal reasoning; the framework can drive future improvements in both benchmark design and LLM capabilities; the benchmark, code, and evaluation scripts are available publicly.

Abstract: Counterfactual reasoning requires models to reason beyond the observed world and explain how altered conditions propagate through downstream consequences. Existing benchmarks largely target bounded settings with fixed variables or single gold outcomes, overlooking open-domain scenarios requiring causal-process evaluation. To this end, we present $\textbf{WhatIfBench}$, a diagnostic benchmark for open-domain, open-form, long-horizon counterfactual causal reasoning, containing 220 what-if questions across STEM, HSS, and Hybrid scenarios. To evaluate free-form responses, we further propose $\textbf{PRISM}$, which first converts each natural-language explanation into a Response-Derived Semantic Causal Graph of events, states, and mechanisms. On top of this graph, PRISM then jointly applies a Process Metric assessing graph-level causal validity and a Rubric Metric assessing answer-level explanatory adequacy. Evaluating six frontier LLMs with this framework, we find that WhatIfBench remains far from saturated: even the strongest model reaches only a 64.62% final score. Further analysis reveals persistent causal gaps, premise drift, and topology fragmentation, suggesting that fluent counterfactual narratives often mask fragile causal processes. The benchmark, code, and evaluation scripts are available at $\href{https://github.com/zju-gt/WhatIfBench}{WhatIfBench}$.

</details>


### [124] [When Teacher Guidance Misleads: Reward-Aligned On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27960)
*Siyuan Gan,Yuhan Li,Xiran Wang,Linjian Meng,Boyan Wang,Zhen Zhao,Jing Huo,Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RA-OPD introduces trajectory-level reward alignment to standard on-policy distillation by discarding misaligned guidance; yields improved performance on math and code benchmarks when using Qwen3 and DeepSeek-R1 models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mitigate misaligned teacher guidance in on-policy distillation (OPD), where teacher-induced updates may steer the student away from high-reward trajectories.

Method: For each sampled trajectory, RA-OPD checks whether its trajectory-level distillation return is consistent with its outcome reward and filters out misaligned trajectories. The training uses only the retained trajectories, achieving improved performance without additional computational cost.

Result: RA-OPD shows significant performance gains over standard OPD and other OPD variants across seven math benchmarks and three code benchmarks using Qwen3 and DeepSeek-R1 model families.

Conclusion: Reward-aligned on-policy distillation provides a simple, effective, and data-efficient mechanism to align teacher guidance with outcome rewards, improving student performance on math and coding tasks.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has recently emerged as a popular post-training paradigm for large language models (LLMs), providing an efficient way to transfer the knowledge and capabilities of teacher models into student models. However, teacher guidance on student-generated prefixes is not always reliable. Training should optimize the model to generate responses that are more likely to be correct, or equivalently, to get higher outcome rewards. But during OPD, the teacher model may provide guidance that discourages the student from moving toward correct trajectories or moves the student toward incorrect ones, which is misaligned with outcome reward. Such misaligned guidance is unreliable, as it would mislead the optimization process and ultimately degrade model performance. To mitigate misaligned teacher guidance, we propose Reward-Aligned On-Policy Distillation (RA-OPD). The key insight is to keep only trajectories whose induced updates move the student toward correct trajectories or discourage the student from moving toward incorrect ones. Specifically, for each sampled trajectory, RA-OPD checks whether its trajectory-level distillation return is consistent with its outcome reward and then filters out the misaligned trajectories. RA-OPD selects more reliable trajectories to improve student model performance without requiring additional computational cost. We evaluate RA-OPD on math and code benchmarks using models from the Qwen3 family and the DeepSeek-R1 family. Across seven math benchmarks and three code benchmarks, RA-OPD significantly outperforms standard OPD and other tested OPD variants.

</details>


### [125] [SABER: Stability-Aware Early Exit for LLM Reasoning via Adversarial Branch Probing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27963)
*Wanli Cheng,Haiya Xiang,Juntao Li,Hongling Wang,Wenliang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SABER is a training-free stability-aware early-exit framework for large reasoning models that uses adversarial branch probing to estimate final outcomes of perturbed intermediate reasoning states; exits early when probing results agree, reducing reasoning tokens substantially while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long chain reasoning is costly and often yields diminishing returns; current early-exit methods fail to capture stability and consistency-based methods require sequential rollouts, causing delays.

Method: Construct semantic perturbations around intermediate reasoning states to create adversarial branches; apply lightweight probing to estimate final outcomes without full trajectory rollouts; exit early when probed outcomes are consistent across branches; otherwise continue reasoning; training-free.

Result: Empirical evaluation across multiple benchmarks and architectures shows 30.2%–39.8% reduction in reasoning tokens on average with competitive accuracy compared to full-length reasoning.

Conclusion: SABER offers a practical, cost-effective approach to stability-aware early exit for LRMs, enabling efficient reasoning without sacrificing accuracy by leveraging adversarial probing of intermediate states.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) achieve strong reasoning capabilities, yet long-chain reasoning becomes inefficient once the intermediate answer stabilizes across reasoning steps: additional reasoning yields little marginal benefit while incurring substantial inference cost. Existing early-exit methods based on confidence or entropy poorly capture reasoning stability, while consistency-based approaches rely on multi-step trajectory agreement, requiring sequential evaluations that delay exit. To better balance efficiency and reliability, we propose SABER, a training-free framework for stability-aware early exit via adversarial branch probing. SABER constructs simple yet effective semantic perturbations around intermediate reasoning states to form adversarial branches, and applies lightweight probing to estimate their likely final outcomes without full trajectory rollouts. When the probed outcomes remain consistent across branches, SABER exits early; otherwise, it continues reasoning. Experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks and model architectures show that SABER reduces reasoning token consumption by 30.2\%--39.8\% on average while maintaining competitive accuracy with full-length reasoning.

</details>


### [126] [AERA: Adaptive Evidence Residual Allocation for Efficient Test-Time Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27964)
*Ziming Wang,Ivor Tsang,Hangwei Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Adaptive Evidence Residual Allocation (AERA) allocates computation adaptively in test-time reasoning by estimating the potential future gains from additional steps, achieving near-baseline accuracy with far less computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typical adaptive stopping uses present confidence, but evidence can be non-monotonic and further computation can alter outcomes; thus stopping based on current evidence can be suboptimal.

Method: AERA is a sequential controller that uses cumulative prefixes of responses and a set of features (answer distribution, temporal, re-solving, semantic, compute) to decide whether to stop or generate the next response block. It uses offline supervision from future checkpoint correctness but relies only on observable evidence at inference.

Result: On GSM8K and GPQA Diamond, AERA identifies question-specific residual opportunities and reduces inference cost.
