In a frozen-threshold incremental-generation test on 300 GSM8K questions, AERA achieves 92.61% accuracy with fewer tokens, compared to 93.01% accuracy with 128 responses and a 95.99% reduction in completion tokens.

Conclusion: Adaptive reasoning should estimate the future value of computation rather than equating present confidence with correctness.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves language-model reasoning by generating additional candidate solutions, but allocating the same inference budget to every problem is computationally wasteful. Existing adaptive stopping methods commonly rely on confidence, agreement, or answer stability, implicitly assuming that stronger current evidence indicates that further computation is unnecessary. We show that this assumption can fail: checkpoint-level correctness evolves non-monotonically, and observable evidence may strengthen before an answer collapses or weaken before it recovers. Motivated by this mismatch, we introduce Adaptive Evidence Residual Allocation (AERA), a sequential controller that learns whether additional computation is likely to recover a better answer from checkpoint-observable evidence. AERA characterizes cumulative response prefixes using answer-distribution, temporal, re-solving, semantic, and compute features, and repeatedly decides whether to stop or allocate the next response block. Future checkpoint correctness is used only to construct offline supervision and is never available to the controller at inference time. Across GSM8K and GPQA Diamond, AERA identifies question-specific residual opportunities while substantially reducing inference computation. In a frozen-threshold incremental-generation evaluation on 300 untouched GSM8K questions, AERA achieves 92.61% accuracy versus 93.01% with 128 responses while reducing completion tokens by 95.99%. These results suggest that adaptive reasoning should estimate the future value of computation rather than equating present confidence with correctness.

</details>


### [127] [openJiuwen: Beyond Static Harnesses for Long-Horizon Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27969)
*openJiuwen Team,Tao Yu,Xinyu Zhang,Qianqian Chen,Xiaoneng Xiang,Chia Kwangyang,Xingchen Huang,Ran Chen,Yangkai Ding,Zheng Wang,Yeo Boon Hong,Bingzheng Gan,Enrui Hu,Shuo Cheng,Deyang Li,Ruifeng Shi,Hongbo Wang,Qi Ye,Xuefeng Jin,Zhangchun Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: openJiuwen is an open-source agent harness that enables structural composability and runtime adaptivity for long-horizon coding agents by providing Rail-based capability composition and multi-agent coordination (single agents, delegated sub-agents, Swarm Flow) with a fixed policy-driven runtime decision loop that is influenced by evolving evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies two core challenges: (1) enabling developers to compose capabilities, reconfigure execution logic, and scale agent systems without rebuilding orchestration; (2) continuously updating runtime decisions as new evidence (diagnostics, outcomes, task progress, context relevance) emerges during complex coding tasks.

Method: Introduce openJiuwen as a shared execution substrate with Rail-based capability composition, supporting single agents, delegated sub-agents, and Swarm Flow, to construct sophisticated agent harnesses under common execution semantics. The framework adapts runtime decisions around a fixed model policy so evolving evidence can dynamically affect context, feedback, and task control.

Result: Empirical evaluation on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1 yields 82.6% and 87.19% respectively, outperforming the strongest selected official leaderboard points by 3.4 and 3.39 points.

Conclusion: openJiuwen demonstrates strong performance on complex coding tasks while offering a composable and adaptive harness design.

Abstract: Long-horizon coding agents operate over evolving repository states while increasingly relying on heterogeneous capabilities, delegated agents, and multi-agent coordination. These trends pose two complementary challenges for the agent harness. First, developers need to compose capabilities, reconfigure execution logic, and scale increasingly complex agent systems without repeatedly rebuilding orchestration. Second, complex coding tasks continuously produce new evidence---such as semantic diagnostics, execution outcomes, task progress, and changing context relevance---that should dynamically influence subsequent runtime decisions. We characterize these challenges as Structural Composability and Runtime Adaptivity. We present openJiuwen, an open-source harness designed for both developer composability and adaptive task execution. openJiuwen provides a shared execution substrate and Rail-based capability composition across single agents, delegated sub-agents, and Swarm Flow, enabling developers to construct sophisticated agent harnesses under common execution semantics. It further adapts framework-controlled runtime decisions around a fixed model policy, allowing evolving evidence to dynamically affect context, feedback, and task control toward successful completion. We systematically evaluate openJiuwen on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1, where it achieves 82.6% and 87.19%, respectively, exceeding the strongest selected official-leaderboard point estimates by 3.4 and 3.39 percentage points. These results show that openJiuwen achieves strong performance on complex coding tasks while providing a composable and adaptive harness design.

</details>


### [128] [Learning from Hard Prompts: Difficulty-aware Advantage Amplification in Dynamic Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27982)
*Siyuan Gan,Yuhan Li,Xiran Wang,Linjian Meng,Boyan Wang,Zhen Zhao,Jing Huo,Lei Bai,Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DA3PO combines DAPO's Dynamic Sampling with Direct Advantage Amplification to boost training efficiency on hard prompts while preserving accuracy; it is a lightweight modification (<30 lines of code) that outperforms GRPO and other GRPO variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address training instability and zero-gradient issues in GRPO-based methods and fix the inefficiency of Dynamic Sampling on hard prompts by ensuring hard-to-sample correct responses are amplified.

Method: Dynamic Sampling reduces zero-gradient issues by filtering prompts with entirely correct or incorrect responses, but causes asymmetric amplification that hurts hard prompts. Direct Advantage Amplification (DAA) explicitly amplifies the advantages of hard-to-sample correct responses on hard prompts. DA3PO integrates DAA into DAPO, yielding a concise, training-efficient policy optimization approach.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows DA3PO significantly outperforms GRPO and other GRPO variants, with improved training efficiency and accuracy. The implementation requires fewer than 30 lines of code compared to DAPO.

Conclusion: DA3PO offers a difficulty-aware, lightweight enhancement to GRPO-style policy optimization, delivering higher performance with minimal code changes and improved efficiency on hard prompts.

Abstract: Decoupled Clip and Dynamic Sampling Policy Optimization (DAPO) is a prominent variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). DAPO introduces several improvements over GRPO. Among these, Dynamic Sampling contributes the most to DAPO's accuracy gains relative to GRPO. To improve accuracy, Dynamic Sampling enhances training stability by eliminating zero policy gradients from zero advantages. Specifically, it avoids such zero gradients by filtering out prompts where sampled responses are either entirely correct or incorrect. However, our theoretical analysis shows that Dynamic Sampling decrease training efficiency as it cannot effectively utilize hard-to-sample correct responses on hard prompts. Formally, it asymmetrically amplifies the advantages of distinct responses to the same prompts. On hard prompts, incorrect responses undergo greater amplification than correct ones. This leads the model to avoid generating the observed incorrect responses rather than capitalizing on the hard-to-sample correct ones on hard prompts, resulting in low training efficiency. To improve training efficiency, we propose Direct Advantage Amplification (DAA), which amplifies the advantages of hard-to-sample correct responses on hard prompts, as obtained by Dynamic Sampling. This ensures that, when Dynamic Sampling is used, these hard-to-sample responses can be effectively capitalized on, implying higher training efficiency. By integrating DAA into DAPO, we obtain Difficulty-aware Advantage Amplification Policy Optimization (DA3PO), which is implemented with fewer than 30 lines of code from DAPO. Experiments show that DA3PO significantly outperforms GRPO and other classical GRPO variants.

</details>


### [129] [When Evidence Shapes Collaboration: Knowledge-Conditioned Topology Generation for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27984)
*Yangxiao Jiang,Jiarun Fan,Mingcong Xu,Yanxi Guo,Jiwen Feng,Shanqing Xu,Mengchen Qian,Wei Chen,Xiaojin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: K-GAT is a neuro-symbolic, knowledge-conditioned topology generator for MAS, achieving significant accuracy gains with lower computational cost on GPQA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current topology generation in MAS relies on parametric LLM knowledge and reactive external tools, causing structure-knowledge misalignment, redundant interactions, and insufficient verification in knowledge-intensive tasks. There is a need to explicitly integrate external evidence into topology design.

Method: K-GAT formulates topology design as a knowledge-conditioned structure learning problem within a neuro-symbolic framework. It integrates external evidence into autoregressive graph generation to produce collaboration topologies.

Result: On knowledge-intensive benchmarks, K-GAT demonstrates efficiency and effectiveness. On the GPQA dataset, it outperforms the LLM-Debate baseline by +15.7% in accuracy and uses less than half the computational tokens.

Conclusion: Knowledge-guided topology design via a neuro-symbolic framework reduces misalignment and redundancy while increasing accuracy and efficiency, showing strong potential for knowledge-intensive MAS tasks.

Abstract: Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) have recently moved from static workflows toward dynamically generated collaboration topologies. However, existing topology generation methods rely primarily on the parametric knowledge of large language models, with external search or retrieval used only as a reactive tool rather than an explicit determinant of collaboration structure. This leads to structure-knowledge misalignment, where systems exhibit redundant interactions or insufficient verification in knowledge-intensive tasks. We propose K-GAT (Knowledge-Guided Agent Topology Generator), a neuro-symbolic framework that formulates collaboration topology design as a knowledge-conditioned structure learning problem, integrating external evidence directly into autoregressive graph generation. Extensive experiments on knowledge-intensive benchmarks demonstrate K-GAT's efficiency and effectiveness: notably on the expert-level GPQA dataset, K-GAT outperforms the LLM-Debate baseline by a substantial margin of +15.7% in accuracy, while consuming less than half the computational tokens.

</details>


### [130] [GOD: Govern, Observe, and Direct - A Real-Time Control Room for Agent Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27992)
*Yige Luo,Ran Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GOD provides a local-first control room for generative-agent systems enabling interactive replay, targeted interventions, and packaging, bridging live control and replay evidence via a command-and-artifact loop; evaluation shows high accuracy in navigation and state reporting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the difficulty of inspecting and reproducing large multi-agent runs, which produce raw logs or finished replays, hindering reasoning about agent behavior.

Method: Introduce GOD with components: setup wizard, Agent Studio, Map Studio, spatial replay, Ask/Intervene, and portable packs. Define a command-and-artifact loop that unifies live control and replay evidence; separate scenario/map/profile data from local runtime state via package contracts; release replays and packs; perform evaluation on 15 run slots and 14 interventions, recording high success rates.

Result: Quantitative results: 78/84 target-agent checks recorded commanded destination; 169/182 state answers matched saved location or action string.

Conclusion: The approach improves inspectability and controllability of complex agent runs, enabling reproducible experiments and easier distribution; the design balances live control with replay, but further work may explore scalability, robustness, and generalization to other simulators.

Abstract: Generative-agent systems are easier to start than to inspect. A run can contain many agents, locations, messages, commands, and model calls, yet the operator often gets either a finished replay or raw logs. That makes it hard to ask why an agent moved, test a small intervention, or package a run for another researcher. GOD is a local-first control room for agent societies. From the same browser workflow, an operator can issue targeted questions or interventions and inspect the resulting replay state. The system combines a setup wizard, Agent Studio, Map Studio, a spatial replay interface, Ask and Intervene commands, and portable experiment, map, and agent packs. Its technical contribution is the command and artifact loop: live controls and replay evidence share the same operator command model, while package contracts separate scenario, map, and profile data from local runtime state. The public release includes hosted Smallville-style and PKU replays, the open-source repository, and downloadable packs. We evaluate this path on 15 completed run slots. Across the 14 intervention runs, 78 of 84 target-agent checks recorded the commanded destination, and 169 of 182 state answers matched a saved location or action string.

</details>


### [131] [Should I Use This Synthetic Dataset for Training? How to Test with Minimal Real Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27996)
*Zhenyu Tao,Wei Xu,Xiaohu You,Petar Popovski,Osvaldo Simeone*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces adaptive sign-flip testing (aeSFT) for evaluating whether adding synthetic data improves population performance under a fixed learner, with two formulations (mean-loss test and paired-loss symmetry test); aeSFT is anytime-valid and data-efficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the sim-to-real gap in digital twins and learned world models, where synthetic data may harm or help real-world performance; aims to minimize real data needed for decision-making about augmentation.

Method: Two formulations: (i) direct mean-loss difference test between models trained on real vs augmented data; (ii) symmetry-based paired-loss difference test (sign-flip) with aeSFT. aeSFT adaptively determines number of Monte Carlo sign flips and data usage, providing anytime-valid Type-I error control without fixing test-set size.

Result: Empirical evaluation on synthetic classification, DT-aided wireless scheduling, and radio-map prediction shows aeSFT detects beneficial synthetic data using far fewer real-test samples than mean-based sequential tests, achieving power comparable to fixed-sample sign-flip tests and paired t-test while controlling false positives.

Conclusion: aeSFT offers a robust, data-efficient framework for assessing synthetic-data augmentation in AI for engineering, enabling reliable early stopping and reducing real-data costs.

Abstract: Digital twins (DTs) and learned world models are increasingly used to generate synthetic data that augment the scarce real datasets available for training artificial intelligence (AI) models in engineering systems. Owing to the inevitable simulation-to-reality (sim-to-real) gap, however, augmentation may fail to improve the performance of the trained model on the real data distribution. This paper addresses the resulting decision problem: Given a real dataset, a candidate synthetic dataset, and a fixed learning algorithm, decide whether training on the augmented dataset improves the true, population-level performance, while consuming as few real test data points as possible. Two formulations are considered: a direct test on the mean loss difference between the two trained models, and a symmetry-based test on the paired loss difference, which trades a stronger null assumption for faster evidence accumulation. For the latter, we introduce the {adaptive e-process sign-flip test} (aeSFT), a doubly adaptive procedure that adapts both the number of Monte Carlo sign-flip rounds, and hence the computational cost, and the amount of real test data consumed. aeSFT yields anytime-valid Type-I error control, with no need to pre-specify the test-set size. Experiments on a synthetic-data classification task, a DT-aided wireless packet-scheduling task, and a radio-map prediction task show that aeSFT identifies useful synthetic data using substantially fewer real test samples than mean-based sequential testing, matches the power of fixed-sample sign-flip testing and the paired $t$-test, while keeping the false-positive rate below the target level.

</details>


### [132] [Automated Analysis Framework for Multilingual Climate-Health Literature Based on Multi-Agent Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27998)
*Yuze Sun,Shihui Zhang,Jiancheng Pan,Yunjia Ye,Wentao Luo,Jiahao Li,Quan Zhang,Wenjia Cai,Xiaomeng Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A centralized multi-agent LLM framework for multilingual literature analysis with 3 agents and a coordination module, achieving 0.92 F1 in core information extraction and standardizing 2,012 city–literature pairs on a large bilingual climate–health corpus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome inefficiency, scalability limits, and domain adaptability issues of manual and single-algorithm approaches for interdisciplinary, multilingual science mapping, especially in climate–health.

Method: Central coordinator; three specialized agents (document evaluation, information extraction, analytical review); four-layer hallucination control; human verification; end-to-end automation: screening, extraction, integration; tested on bilingual Chinese-English dataset (32,642 papers, 1993–2023).

Result: F1 = 0.92 for core information extraction; 2,012 city–literature association pairs extracted/standardized; demonstrates scalable, automated evidence mining capabilities.

Conclusion: Validates the feasibility and effectiveness of multi-agent LLM-based literature analysis in climate–health; indicates potential applicability to other interdisciplinary multilingual domains and large-scale evidence synthesis.

Abstract: The rapid proliferation of interdisciplinary and multilingual scientific literature has left traditional manual analysis and single-algorithm methods plagued by low efficiency, poor scalability, and insufficient domain adaptability. Targeting the literature analysis needs of the typical interdisciplinary climate-health field, this study proposes a multi-agent large language model automated analysis framework for multilingual scientific literature, which realizes full-process automation covering literature screening, structured information extraction, and standardized integration. With a central coordination module as the core, the framework deploys three dedicated agents for document evaluation, information extraction, and analytical review to mimic the literature analysis thinking of domain experts, and adopts a four-layer hallucination control strategy together with a manual verification procedure to ensure the accuracy and reliability of analytical outcomes. Validated on a bilingual Chinese-English corpus of 32,642 climate-health papers covering China from 1993 to 2023, the framework achieves an F1 score of 0.92 in core information extraction, and completes the extraction and standardization of 2,012 city-literature association pairs, offering effective technical support for large-scale evidence mining in the climate-health research domain.

</details>


### [133] [PhenoIntel: A Lifecycle-Aligned Multi-Agent Web Application for Verified, Accessible Plant Phenotype Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27999)
*Narendren S,Soumyashree Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhenoIntel is a lifecycle-aligned, multi-agent web platform for reliable, uncertainty-aware plant phenotyping that runs in a browser and adapts with new models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current conversational plant-phenotyping tools are unreliable and hard to use: failed analyses go unflagged, statistical assays lack assumption checks, predictions lack uncertainty, and access is limited by specialized hardware. A reliable, user-friendly, uncertainty-aware, and accessible system with provenance is needed.

Method: PhenoIntel uses nine specialized agents that split the workflow from image collection to reporting, with independent stage checks and a shared fixed-structure record to catch inconsistencies. Uncertainty is matched to model families via conformal prediction, detection-confidence spread, or Monte Carlo Dropout; quality thresholds adapt to crop/task. The system can propose, validate, and integrate new models. A model repository contains ten trained models across five crops and four imaging modalities. It runs in a browser on standard hardware (no GPU) and is validated by a 1,200-test automated suite; outputs include calibrated uncertainty and FAIR-compliant provenance.

Result: Classification models reach Macro F1 of 0.78–0.996; object-detection models reach 0.96 mAP@50 with a 54% reduction in counting error over an unoptimised baseline; temporal model reaches held-out Macro F1 of 0.7050; PhenoIntel runs in a browser without GPU; comprehensive automated testing validates end-to-end pipeline; all results include calibrated uncertainty and provenance.

Conclusion: PhenoIntel provides reliable, uncertainty-aware, and provenance-rich phenotyping across crops and modalities, addressing reliability and accessibility gaps in existing tools, with the ability to expand models and operate without GPUs.

Abstract: Existing conversational plant-phenotyping platforms are difficult for plant scientists to use and lack the reliability scientific research demands: failed analyses are reported as valid measurements rather than flagged as missing, statistical tests run without checking assumptions, predictions carry no uncertainty estimate, and specialised hardware limits accessibility. We present PhenoIntel, a lifecycle-aligned multi-agent web platform that turns the full machine-learning workflow into a reliable, user-friendly phenotyping system. Nine specialised agents divide the analysis into stages, from image collection through model selection, inference, and reporting, rather than handing the whole task to one AI manager. Independent checks separate these stages, and every agent reads from and writes to one shared, fixed-structure record, so an inconsistent output from one stage is caught before it reaches the next. Uncertainty is matched to each model family, conformal prediction, detection-confidence spread, or Monte Carlo Dropout, rather than applied uniformly, and quality thresholds adapt to crop and task instead of one global cutoff. When no suitable model exists, PhenoIntel can propose, validate, and integrate a new one on its own. The model repository spans ten trained models across five crops and four imaging modalities. Classification models reach Macro F1 of 0.78-0.996; object-detection models reach 0.96 mAP@50 with a 54% reduction in counting error over an unoptimised baseline; and a temporal model reaches held-out Macro F1 of 0.7050. PhenoIntel runs in a browser on standard hardware, requiring no GPU, and a 1,200-test automated suite confirms complete pipeline execution. Every result carries calibrated uncertainty, validated statistics, and FAIR-compliant provenance, a combination existing conversational phenotyping tools do not offer.

</details>


### [134] [Coverage, Not Credit: Failure-Credit Routing of Zeroth-Order Perturbation Budgets Does Not Improve On-Pool Sample Efficiency for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28011)
*Yuxu Ge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: No detectable improvement from trajectory-level credit routing for ZO/ES budgets in frozen-LM tool-using agents; only a minor exception on held-out endpoints; multiple failure modes; overall uniform allocation remains robust in most settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether using failure credit to guide fixed zeroth-order/evolution-strategy perturbation budgets improves optimization efficiency in tool-using LLM agents.

Method: Empirical study across a synthetic environment and frozen LLMs (Qwen2.5-1.5B/3B, SmolLM2-1.7B). Three task families, six allocation schemes, credit-noise sweep, paired seeds, exact sign-flip tests. Assess on-pool comparisons (AUC) and end-to-end performance. Compare fixed-budget routing: joint soft-plus-sigma, concentrated budget on credit argmax, and other schemes. Include inverse-propensity debiasing, fixed-step schedules, and held-out BFCL endpoints.

Result: No statistically detectable improvement over uniform allocation in on-pool comparisons. Joint soft-plus-sigma is equivalent to uniform within ±0.02 AUC on 1.5B/3B. Concentrating the full budget on the credit argmax is marginally equivalent on 1.5B and significantly worse on 3B. Inverse-propensity debiasing does not rescue routing; misrouting costs negative AUC in-house and end-to-end. Across six fixed-step schedules, loss scales with bottleneck starvation (R^2=0.94). A preregistered credit-free coverage floor avoids harm. Matched-budget burst and catch-up schedules align with harm from insufficient cumulative parameter movement rather than update frequency. On unseen BFCL functions, soft routing exceeds uniform on held-out endpoints (+0.047 AUC, p=0.031, n=6). Identifies three failure modes that can silently invalidate ZO/ES experiments on frozen LLMs.

Conclusion: Trajectory-level credit routing for ZO/ES perturbations yields little, if any, improvement in optimization efficiency for frozen-LM tool-using agents. Gains are limited to rare held-out endpoints and are not robust across models. The study highlights risks of misrouting and offers mitigations (credit-free floor) but overall suggests avoiding reliance on failure-credit routing in these settings.

Abstract: Trajectory-level credit assignment can localize which module of a tool-using LLM agent causes failures using only verifiable signals. We ask whether such failure credit should route a fixed zeroth-order/evolution-strategies (ZO/ES) perturbation budget. Across a synthetic environment and frozen Qwen2.5-1.5B/3B and SmolLM2-1.7B agents, three task families, six allocation schemes, a credit-noise sweep, paired seeds, and exact sign-flip tests, we find no statistically detectable improvement over uniform allocation in any on-pool comparison (no gain of at least 2 percentage points). The joint soft-plus-sigma scheme is equivalent to uniform within a +/- 0.02 AUC margin on 1.5B and 3B; concentrating the full budget on the credit argmax is marginally equivalent on 1.5B, where that module is the verified bottleneck, and significantly worse on 3B. Inverse-propensity debiasing does not rescue routing, and misrouting costs up to -0.074 AUC in-house and -0.118 end-to-end on the BFCL-derived family. Across six fixed-step schedules, loss is linear in bottleneck starvation rate (R^2 = 0.94, descriptive), and a preregistered credit-free coverage floor removes detected harm. Matched-budget burst and step-compensating catch-up schedules are consistent with harm arising from insufficient cumulative parameter movement rather than update frequency. Our primary estimand is optimization efficiency on a fixed task pool. On unseen BFCL functions, the study's one exception is that soft routing exceeds uniform on held-out endpoints (+0.047, p = 0.031, n = 6). A plausible but untested reading is that routing-favored caller improvements transfer while uniform's on-pool gains reflect a synthesizer behavior specific to our harness. We report this exception explicitly and document three failure modes that can silently invalidate ZO/ES experiments on frozen LLMs.

</details>


### [135] [String: An Agentic OS Where Every App Is a Markdown File](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28027)
*Jookyung Song,Nojun Kwak,Simyung Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An OS-like runtime (String) for LLM agents that unifies tool usage via a single SFMD (String-Flavored Markdown) document, exposing two actions (/open, /act) and rendering views as Markdown, HTML, or raw SFMD. It decouples tool knowledge, enforces provenance-based privileges, and achieves token-efficient, scalable, cross-surface operation with production validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents operate through human-centric surfaces (pages, tool schemas) that incur re-reading and duplication of context. A unified, low-overhead, secure interface is needed to manage tool knowledge, state, and secrets across diverse renderings (web, apps, shells).

Method: Introduce the String runtime and SFMD. A single document declares views, typed actions, navigation, and credentials. The runtime handles discovery, validation, execution, state, and secrets, using two core verbs: /open (see) and /act (do). Web and app renderings are two views of one architecture: SFMD serves styled HTML to humans and raw SFMD to agents. Views are partial; staging is causal to control information disclosure. Privilege follows provenance: remote pages can call HTTP but not the shell; caller text cannot expand stored secrets. Evaluation uses an 87-task benchmark with curated skills, deploying as on-demand String apps across six models (frontier to small).

Result: On average, String-based apps achieve comparable aggregate success across six models (+1.3 percentage points) while using 33.5% fewer tokens in completed episodes. The resident interface remains a constant 53 tokens regardless of catalog size. Causal staging reduces wrong-action selection from 28% to 2% and maintains higher accuracy when partial information is revealed.

Conclusion: The paper presents a practical, portable architecture that unifies agent-facing and human-facing interfaces via SFMD, delivering reduced context overhead, improved safety via provenance-based privileges, and competitive performance in production-like tasks. It also reports learnings from three months of production use.

Abstract: LLM agents have become a new class of software user, but every surface they work through was designed for someone else. Pages are built for human eyes, which can skim and ignore; tool schemas for programs, which pay nothing to carry definitions they never call. An agent has neither luxury: it re-reads, and pays again for, everything it is shown on every turn. We present String, an open-source runtime that gives this user an interface of its own and treats the job as an operating-systems problem. Tool knowledge moves out of the agent's context and into a common layer that renders it back one view at a time as Markdown. A single SFMD (String-Flavored Markdown) document declares an application's views, typed actions, navigation, and credentials, and the runtime handles discovery, validation, execution, state, and secrets behind two core verbs: /open to see and /act to do. Web and app turn out to be two renderings of one architecture: an SFMD site serves styled HTML to browsers and the raw document to agents, so one grammar reaches apps, files, shells, and the web, even legacy HTML, with no per-site integration. Views stay partial by design, and the staging is causal: disclosing one tier of detail a single turn too early costs up to 23 accuracy points, while proper staging drops wrong-action selection from 28% to 2%. Privilege follows provenance: a remote page may call HTTP but never the shell, and caller-supplied text never expands a stored secret. On an 87-task benchmark that pairs each task with curated skills, operationalizing those procedures as on-demand String apps yields comparable aggregate success across six models from frontier to small (+1.3pp) while using 33.5% fewer tokens among completed episodes, and the resident interface stays a constant 53 tokens at any catalog size. We report the design, the evaluation, and what three months of production use taught us.

</details>


### [136] [WeAgent-MMSearch: Native Text-Vision Interaction for Multimodal Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28062)
*Zongkai Liu,Hui Zhang,Liqiang Niu,Zhen Cao,Han Li,Juntao Liu,Wenchao Chen,Chengduo Zhao,Chao Yu,Fandong Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes WeAgent-Harness for multimodal agentic search with persistent image references; builds WeAgent-MMSearch and VisTarget-Bench; shows large gains in post-training and superior performance relative to parameter count on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate text-only evidence usage and runtime failures in long-horizon multimodal agentics; enable native text-vision interaction and salvageable trajectories; provide nuanced evaluation distinguishing image retrieval vs perception failures.

Method: Introduce WeAgent-Harness enabling text-vision, persistent image references; develop WeAgent-MMSearch system across data construction, post-training, and rollout; Data: MLLM leverages harness to discover and synthesize MMSearch tasks and collect expert trajectories; FA-GSPO failure-aware policy optimization to recover salvageable rollouts and filter invalid ones; VisTarget-Bench 150 tasks with held-out target image; evaluation across seven public benchmarks.

Result: Agentic post-training improves average score by 19.22 points; model outperforms similarly sized open-source models and rivals models with ~10x parameters.

Conclusion: Multimodal agentic frameworks with persistent visuals and failure-aware training yield robust planning and search capabilities; the benchmark differentiates retrieval vs perception failures, enabling more precise evaluation.

Abstract: Multimodal search agents extend parametric knowledge with newly emerging and long-tail evidence from the open web. Yet many existing agentic search environments often expose retrieved evidence only as text and omit tool-returned images from subsequent context, reducing visually grounded trajectories to text-only reasoning. Long-horizon interaction also compounds tool-call, response-length, timeout, and budget failures, which can discard salvageable trajectories, waste rollout computation, and disturb policy updates. To address these issues, we introduce WeAgent-Harness, a multimodal agentic harness that supports native text-vision interaction and runtime recovery. Retrieved images receive persistent disk references, allowing the model to inspect, process, and cite them throughout the trajectory. Based on this harness, we develop WeAgent-MMSearch, an integrated system spanning data construction, agentic post-training, and multimodal rollout. For data construction, a strong MLLM uses WeAgent-Harness to discover, synthesize, and verify MMSearch-style tasks and collect expert trajectories. During post-training, our Failure-Aware GSPO (FA-GSPO) recovers salvageable abnormal rollouts and filters invalid ones to improve bounded multimodal planning and search.We also introduce VisTarget-Bench, a 150-task human-verified benchmark that pairs each question with a held-out target image, distinguishing image-retrieval failures from visual-perception failures. Evaluation on VisTarget-Bench and seven public benchmarks shows that agentic post-training improves the average score by 19.22 points, enabling our model to outperform similarly sized open-source models and rival models with roughly ten times its parameter count.

</details>


### [137] [Learning to Allocate Incentives for Incentivized Advertising via Offline Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28065)
*Zilin Zhao,Han Yang,Tianpei Yang,Fangsheng Huang,Yanfei Cui,Kan Peng,Yi Li,Yiming Zong,Hao Zhang,Yinsong Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An offline model-based RL framework for sequential incentive allocation in incentivized ads, using a world model and conservative policy optimization, evaluated with an independent counterfactual scorer; MB-IQL outperforms TD3+BC, while plain IQL underperforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incentives must balance upfront promised rewards against delayed downstream ad revenue; current methods either optimize opportunities or incentives in isolation, but do not address sequential decision-making with delayed feedback and carryover effects.

Method: Formulate as an MDP; learn a world model of user feedback and ad revenue; perform conservative policy optimization in offline RL; use an independent counterfactual scorer to evaluate policies on held-out data; deployment path includes causal inference → offline RL → Offline-MBRL; compare MB-IQL to TD3+BC and plain IQL.

Result: Experiments on large-scale industrial data and online A/B tests show the counterfactual scorer provides a stable offline signal; MB-IQL improves per-user net profit by 7.96% over TD3+BC (p<0.0001); plain IQL reduces profit by 6.56% (p<0.0001).

Conclusion: The proposed offline MB-MBRL framework with a counterfactual scorer yields stable offline evaluation and superior profit performance, validating sequential incentive allocation as an offline RL problem and the MB-IQL approach.

Abstract: Complete your ad view and grab a 5-cent bonus! In incentivized advertising, a platform promises users a bonus before observing downstream ad revenue, encouraging them to click and complete ads. It must balance the incentive promised in advance against the revenue realized afterward: insufficient incentives forfeit monetization opportunities, whereas excessive incentives reduce net profit. Because current incentives may also shape user expectations and future engagement, incentive allocation is a sequential decision problem with delayed revenue, cost sensitivity, and carryover effects.
  Existing work has not studied decision-making algorithms for this setting. Auto-bidding assumes available ad opportunities, while targeted promotion optimizes incentives outside the ad monetization pipeline. We formulate the problem as an MDP and develop an offline model-based RL framework for cost-controllable sequential incentive allocation. It learns a world model of user feedback and ad revenue, then performs conservative policy optimization. An independent counterfactual scorer evaluates each learned policy on held-out logs, enabling pre-launch selection without costly online exposure. Experiments on large-scale industrial data and online A/B tests show that the scorer provides a stable offline signal. The deployment path from causal inference to offline RL and then Offline-MBRL further validates the framework: MB-IQL improves per-user net profit by 7.96\% over TD3+BC, whereas reverting to plain IQL reduces it by 6.56\% (both \(p<0.0001\)).

</details>


### [138] [SEPO: Evidence-Grounded Prompt Optimization via Structural Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28067)
*Xiaoyu Ma,Haoyue Liu,Yiwen Li,Jionghao Zhu,Zhichao Wang,Ye Chen,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEPO introduces a structural, evidence-grounded approach to prompt optimization that tracks local edits and their effects, achieving better accuracy with greater interpretability and efficiency than GEPA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the opacity of API-only prompt optimisers that rewrite whole prompts, SEPO aims for editable, traceable changes to typed prompt units, enabling attribution and actionable guidance in the optimization process.

Method: SEPO uses a two-layer prompt schema with stable, typed units. Edits are local and linked to the examples they affect; an edit-effect lineage records guides for subsequent architect calls on the same search branch. The approach supports multi-trajectory search and results in more interpretable, trackable optimization.

Result: On a 14-task hold-out suite, SEPO outperforms GEPA by 3.1 percentage points on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and 2.2 points on Qwen3-8B (macro accuracy 61.9% and 73.3%). It resides on both the optimization-time and test-time Pareto frontiers, requires 2.9M optimization tokens (vs 4.1M for GEPA), and yields prompts over 5x shorter.

Conclusion: SEPO provides a more addressable, attributable, and actionable prompt optimization paradigm, delivering accuracy gains and efficiency advantages over prior API-only optimisers while maintaining Pareto-optimality across time horizons.

Abstract: Existing API-only prompt optimisers are often described as interpretable, but in practice, this usually means only post-hoc inspectability: each iteration still rewrites the prompt as one opaque string, leaving a trace of full-prompt diffs rather than localisable, machine-readable edits. This paper introduces SEPO (Structural, Evidence-grounded Prompt Optimization), a multi-trajectory prompt optimiser centred on edit-effect lineage feedback. Rather than treating each iteration as an isolated whole-prompt rewrite, SEPO locally edits stable, typed units in a two-layer prompt schema, links the target and realised structural operations of each edit to the examples it newly fixes or breaks, and carries this edit-effect record forward to guide later architect calls on the same search branch. This makes prompt optimisation addressable, attributable, and actionable. Across a 14-task held-out suite, SEPO improves over the strongest baseline, GEPA, by 3.1 pp on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and 2.2 pp on Qwen3-8B, reaching 61.9% and 73.3% macro accuracy. SEPO also lies on both the optimisation-time and test-time Pareto frontiers, spending 2.9M optimisation tokens versus 4.1M for GEPA and producing prompts over 5x shorter.

</details>


### [139] [Speculative Probing: LLM Monitoring at Speculative-Decoding Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28099)
*Collin Zhang,Tingwei Zhang,Vitaly Shmatikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Repurpose speculative decoding as an efficient sequence classifier for real-time LLM safety/monitoring tasks by appending a soft prompt; achieves high accuracy with negligible overhead, outperforming several baselines without running a full LLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time classification during LLM inference is useful for safety filtering and monitoring, but existing hidden-state probes force a trade-off between accuracy and efficiency. There is a gap for context-aware, efficient methods.

Method: Attach a trained soft prompt to the end of the target sequence and use the speculative-decoding module's KV cache as an inference-time classifier within the speculative-decoding pipeline. Evaluated across four tasks and four models (Qwen3.5 4B, 9B, 27B, MiniCPM4.1-8B). Probes are lightweight, context-aware, and do not require full LLM inference.

Result: Small probes consistently exceed zero-shot GPT-5.4-mini performance and, on multilingual prompt safety, match or surpass specialized 8B safety classifiers (e.g., Qwen3Guard-Gen-8B, Llama-Guard-3-8B) without running a full LLM.

Conclusion: Speculative-decoding-based sequence classification offers a highly efficient, high-accuracy alternative to full-model or hidden-state probing approaches for real-time safety classification during LLM inference.

Abstract: Real-time classification during language model inference is valuable for safety filtering, behavioral analysis, and model monitoring, but current approaches force a trade-off between accuracy and efficiency. Hidden-state probes are fast but limited: they are either not context-aware: operating on a single vector and cannot model interactions across positions; or they are very costly: having dedicated classifier models (Llama Guard, Qwen Guard, LLM-as-judge) or performing computation on hidden states for all tokens and then pooling the results (MultiMax). This shows an intrinsic trade-off between efficiency and accuracy.
  However, we find that the speculative-decoding module in recent LLMs can be repurposed for efficient high-quality classification. By appending a trained soft prompt at the end of the target sequence, we can repurpose the speculative-decoding module into a sequence classifier. At inference time in a speculative-decoding pipeline, the KV cache is already in GPU memory, so classification adds negligible overhead. We evaluate on four classification tasks across four models (Qwen3.5-4B, 9B, 27B, MiniCPM4.1-8B). Our small probes consistently outperform zero-shot GPT-5.4-mini and, on multilingual prompt safety, match or beat specialized 8B safety classifiers (Qwen3Guard-Gen-8B, Llama-Guard-3-8B) without running a full LLM.

</details>


### [140] [The Shape of Power: A Multilingual Framework for Social Power Reasoning in Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28144)
*Farah Atif,Sougata Saha,Monojit Choudhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A theory-driven multilingual framework and annotated corpus for cross-cultural social power in dialogue, using French and Egyptian Arabic movie scripts, plus evaluation of LLMs reveals gaps with human reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current computational studies of social power are constrained to narrow linguistic and cultural settings and lack demographic and relational depth for robust cross-cultural analysis; there is a need for cross-lingual, naturalistic dialogue data and scalable annotation pipelines.

Method: Proposes a framework grounded in social science theory, a native-speaker annotation pipeline refined through pilot studies, and a custom interface enabling scalable cross-lingual analysis; builds an initial corpus of 15,836 annotated instances from 100 scenes in French and Egyptian Arabic films.

Result: Demonstrates strong agreement on observable demographic/contextual attributes; socially interpretive aspects like power asymmetry and intention alignment remain contested; evaluation of 6 LLMs and multimodal LLMs shows persistent gaps between human and model reasoning in relational and theory-of-mind tasks.

Conclusion: Introduces the first extensible multilingual framework for studying social power in dialogues and provides an initial evaluation setting for cross-cultural social reasoning.

Abstract: Social power plays a fundamental role in shaping human interaction, yet computational studies of power remain limited to narrow linguistic and cultural settings. Existing datasets further lack the demographic and relational depth needed for robust cross-cultural analysis. To address this gap, we introduce a theoretically grounded framework for studying social power in naturalistic multilingual dialogue through movie screenplays. The framework integrates a schema informed by social science theory, a native speaker annotation pipeline refined through pilot studies, and a custom interface for scalable cross-lingual analysis. Using this framework, we constructed an initial corpus containing 15,836 annotated instances from 100 scenes in French and Egyptian Arabic movies. Our analysis reveals strong agreement on observable demographic and contextual attributes, while socially interpretive aspects, such as power asymmetry and intention alignment, remain more contested, highlighting the complexity of social power across cultures. We evaluated 6 Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal LLMs on cross-cultural social power reasoning, finding persistent gaps between human and model agreement in relational and theory-of-mind reasoning. Our work introduces the first extensible multilingual framework for studying social power in dialogues and provides an initial evaluation setting for studying cross-cultural social reasoning.

</details>


### [141] [Under-Mattress Temporal Sensing for Next-Day Agitation Risk Scoring in Dementia Wards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28152)
*Zhen Liu,Marta Bono,Robbe Decloedt,Ajda Flisar,Maarten Van Den Bossche,Maarten De Vos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Full-night sequence modeling of contactless under-mattress signals yields best discrimination for next-day agitation risk among four paradigms; minute-level data improve over nightly summaries; calibration is limited; external validation required.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agitation risk fluctuates over short horizons in dementia patients and continuous physiological information is scarce. The study tests whether preserving minute-level temporal structure from overnight signals improves next-day agitation risk prediction compared with traditional nightly summaries.

Method: 423 patient-nights from 65 subjects in a specialized hospital dementia unit using two under-mattress sensing systems. Four paradigms: nightly handcrafted summaries; three-period handcrafted features; full-night sequence modeling; sliding-window multiple-instance learning. Source-specific preprocessing; five-fold patient-grouped cross-validation; performance estimated from pooled out-of-fold predictions. Evaluation across discrimination, calibration, fixed-threshold metrics, and period-signal attribution across two temporal models.

Result: Full-night sequence modeling achieved highest discrimination (AUROC 0.692; AUPRC 0.849) and balanced accuracy (0.658). Minute-level pipelines had higher AUROC than nightly summaries; differences from three-period handcrafted features were uncertain. Cross-model attribution emphasized activity, heart rate, and respiratory rate during the core overnight period. Calibration remained limited. Preceding night's signals provided modest next-day risk discrimination, with minute-level temporal modeling outperforming nightly summaries.

Conclusion: Preceding-night signals show modest predictive value for next-day agitation; minute-level temporal modeling is promising. Prospective calibration and external validation are required before clinical deployment. The study positions contactless overnight sensing as a promising direction for agitation-risk research in hospitalized dementia cohorts.

Abstract: Agitation fluctuates over short time horizons in people living with dementia, yet continuous physiological information for anticipating next-day risk is limited. We assessed whether contactless under-mattress signals from the preceding night inform next-day agitation risk and whether preserving minute-level temporal structure improves performance over conventional nightly summaries. We analyzed 423 patient-nights from 65 subjects in a specialized hospital dementia unit using two under-mattress sensing systems. A unified four-paradigm benchmark compared nightly handcrafted summaries, three-period handcrafted features, full-night sequence modeling, and sliding-window multiple-instance learning. Source-specific preprocessing and five-fold patient-grouped cross-validation were used, with performance estimated from pooled out-of-fold predictions. Evaluation included discrimination, calibration, fixed-threshold metrics, and a comparison of period-signal attribution patterns across two temporal models. Full-night sequence modeling achieved the highest discrimination (AUROC, 0.692; AUPRC, 0.849) and balanced accuracy (0.658). Both minute-level pipelines had higher AUROC than nightly summaries, but differences from three-period handcrafted features were uncertain. Cross-model attribution prioritized activity, heart rate, and respiratory rate during the core overnight period. Calibration remained limited. The preceding night's signals supported modest next-day risk discrimination, with minute-level temporal modeling outperforming nightly summaries. Prospective calibration and external validation are needed before use in individual care decisions. This patient-grouped benchmark identifies contactless overnight sensing as a promising biomedical engineering direction for agitation-risk research in hospitalized dementia cohorts.

</details>


### [142] [CrabOS: An Operating System for Human-AI Co-inhabitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28165)
*Qi Yang,Yun Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CrabOS enables seamless handoffs between humans and AI by representing task state as shared natural-language text objects, creating a native operating-system-level environment for co-inhabiting workflows and reducing bridge-dependent handoffs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world tasks often alternate leadership between humans and AI; existing systems use separate workspaces and ad-hoc handoffs (screenshots/text) that are error-prone and non-scalable. A native, auditable, shared work-state interface could improve efficiency and traceability.

Method: Design and implement CrabOS, a work environment where the task state is represented as natural-language-readable text objects shared by humans and AI. These objects are manipulated directly through a common interface, enabling both parties to access and update the same state without bridges.

Result: Case studies show CrabOS supports complex tasks with alternating human/AI leadership more effectively, shifting handoffs from bridge-dependent, application-specific solutions to native OS capabilities and providing a scalable foundation for AI agents.

Conclusion: CrabOS establishes a new, auditable, shared-work-state foundation for human–AI co-inhabitation, enabling robust, long-running AI agents and simpler development of handoff-capable agents across applications.

Abstract: AI agents are evolving into long-running computational entities that can invoke tools, maintain memory, and complete complex tasks across applications. In real-world settings, completing a task often requires humans and AI to take turns leading its execution. Such alternation depends on the seamless handoff of the work state of the task between humans and AI. Existing agent systems, however, provide humans and AI with separate work environments. AI agents must therefore rely on additional bridges to continue work: either developers build task-specific interfaces to access the work state, or users manually transfer relevant parts of it through screenshots or textual descriptions. Both approaches make handoffs costly and scale poorly.
  We propose Human-AI Co-inhabitation, a type of work environment that enables humans and AI to seamlessly take turns continuing work on the same task, and design and implement CrabOS to realize this concept. CrabOS represents the work state as natural-language-readable text objects shared by humans and AI, allowing both to access and manipulate it directly through the same auditable interface without bridges. Case studies show that CrabOS elevates support for complex tasks with alternating human and AI leadership from bridge-dependent application-level solutions to native operating-system capabilities, which provide a new foundation for developing and running AI agents.

</details>


### [143] [Expert Knowledge & Machine Understanding: Bridging Reactome's Ontology with LLM Semantic Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28178)
*Susanna Bravi,Riccardo De Luca,Rosa Sicilia,Christine Nardini,Mario Santoro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Textual metadata from Reactome can recover the global hierarchy of pathways via a Semantic Hierarchy built with SPECTER2 and agglomerative nesting, validated by spectral distance and topology metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether expert-written textual descriptions reflect the high-level, global hierarchical organization of biological pathways, enabling scalable augmentation of manually curated knowledgebases.

Method: Extract Homo sapiens Reactome hierarchy; build a Semantic Hierarchy using a sentence transformer (SPECTER2) and a modified agglomerative nesting algorithm plus a graph reconstruction step; evaluate with Laplacian Spectral Distance, bootstrapping, and global topological metrics.

Result: Quantitative and qualitative analyses support the hypothesis: the global hierarchical structure of pathways can be inferred from experts' textual metadata.

Conclusion: Expert textual metadata can inform and recover the higher-order hierarchical organization of biological pathways, suggesting NLP-driven approaches as scalable adjuncts to manual curation.

Abstract: Biological knowledgebases like Reactome provide high-quality pathways that include biological elements' relationships and textual descriptions (metadata). The quality of such pathways is granted by manual curation, that presents, however, significant scalability challenges. Lately, numerous NLP tools have been proposed to cope with this issue, leveraging textual information to automatically expand biological knowledgebases. However, little exploration has been done so far to assess whether relationships among textual descriptions mirror higher order biological relationships. This study explores whether human-written descriptions in Reactome can be used to infer the experts' defined global hierarchical structure. To test this, we extracted from Reactome the Homo Sapiens hierarchy of pathways and their reactions (Reactome Hierarchy), and used textual metadata to reconstruct a Semantic Hierarchy, combining a sentence transformer model (SPECTER2) with a modified agglomerative nesting algorithm and a graph reconstruction algorithm. Quantitative (Laplacian Spectral Distance and Bootstrapping) and qualitative (global topological metrics) analyses confirm our hypothesis and indicate that the global hierarchical structure of pathways can be inferred by experts textual metadata.

</details>


### [144] [Generative AI Alignment with Hinduism's Theological Plurality and Sacred Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28228)
*Dipto Das,Arpita Kundu,Nusrat Jahan Mim,Shion Guha,Syed Ishtiaque Ahmed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Generative AI can assist Hindu users in Bangladesh with religious inquiry and devotional practices, but raises concerns about misrepresentation and perceived sacred authority; advocates interpretive alignment for AI religious use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fills a gap where AI ethics literature centers Western/Abrahamic frameworks; investigates how AI representations influence Hindu knowledge, belief, and practice.

Method: Qualitative study using 15 semi-structured interviews with Bangladeshi Hindu participants; thematic analysis of how they engage with AI-generated religious content (scriptures, visuals, storytelling).

Result: AI can support scriptural inquiry, devotional visualization, and storytelling; but risks include theological flattening, cultural misrepresentation, devotional manipulation, and simulated sacred presence/authority.

Conclusion: Religious alignment requires interpretive alignment: AI should disclose limits, preserve plurality, and avoid simulating sacred authority or sycophantic personalization.

Abstract: Generative AI systems are increasingly used to answer personal questions and mediate everyday practices, including religion. However, existing discussions around AI alignment and ethics have largely centered secular, Western, and Abrahamic assumptions about religion, offering limited attention to other faith-based traditions. In this paper, we examine how Hindu users engage with generative AI systems in relation to their religious knowledge, belief, and practice. Drawing on 15 semi-structured interviews with Bangladeshi Hindu participants, we analyze how users interpret AI-generated religious representations, scriptural explanations, devotional interactions, and synthetic religious media. We found that AI can be both accessible and ethically troubling. While AI supported scriptural inquiry, devotional visualization, and religious storytelling, our study also identified concerns about theological flattening, cultural misrepresentation, devotional manipulation, and the simulation of sacred presence and authority. We conclude by arguing that religious alignment in generative AI requires interpretive alignment: systems that disclose their limits, preserve plurality, and avoid simulating sacred authority and sycophantic personalization.

</details>


### [145] [Stay Within Your Bounds: Distance-Guided Decoding for Guaranteed Context-Free Grammar Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28229)
*Vincenzo Collura,Karim Tit,Eleonora Giunchiglia,Mike Papadakis,Maxime Cordy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A lookahead-guided, pushdown automata-based decoding framework for context-free grammars to ensure syntactic validity during decoding, with offline summaries and online horizon-aware pruning; improves JSON/SQL/LTL generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grammar-constrained decoding often enforces only local prefix feasibility, which can fail to reach an acceptable completion due to tokenizer-grammar mismatch and finite token budgets; there is a need for stronger, global syntactic guarantees and better quality.

Method: Offline compute bounded pushdown summaries (reachability labels and upper-bound distances to acceptance) for CFGs; online, use these estimates to guide horizon-aware pruning and beam search to ensure syntactically valid outputs.

Result: Outputs are syntactically sound (accepted by the target grammar); experiments on JSON, SQL, and LTL show consistent syntactic validity and improved completion quality over existing baselines.

Conclusion: Pushdown‑aware, lookahead-guided decoding provides a principled, grammar-safe decoding strategy with empirical gains across multiple CFG-based domains; framework is general and applicable to other CFGs.

Abstract: Grammar-constrained decoding helps large language models produce syntactically valid structured outputs, such as code, JSON, and SQL. For context-free grammars, many practical decoders enforce local prefix feasibility: each token must keep the current prefix extendable to some valid completion. Yet, under tokenizer-grammar mismatch and finite token budgets, feasible prefixes may still fail to reach acceptance. We propose a lookahead-guided decoding framework for context-free grammars based on pushdown automata. Offline, we compute bounded pushdown summaries with reachability labels and upper-bound distances to acceptance. Online, these estimates guide horizon-aware pruning and beam search. The resulting decoder is syntactically sound: every output is accepted by the target grammar. Experiments on JSON, SQL, and Linear Temporal Logic (LTL) show both consistent syntactic validity and improved completion quality over existing baselines.

</details>


### [146] [REINS: Refusal-Enhanced Inhibitory Steering with Sparse Autoencoder Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28233)
*Kai-Xuan Ding,Hao-Xiang Xu,Ji-Hua Peng,Zi-Qi Chen,Jiaqi Wang,Zhen-Hua Ling*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework to enhance safety of sparse autoencoder steering for LLMs by combining refusal signals with inhibitory steering, addressing failure modes on complex prompt wrappers; introduces GUISE dataset and REINS method, achieving stronger safety without sacrificing general capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SAE steering with single-direction refusal can fail on harmful prompts wrapped with wrappers. There is a need to suppress harmful features while boosting safe-refusal cues within the same sparse feature space to robustly constrain harmful continuations.

Method: Introduce Refusal-Enhanced Inhibitory Steering (REINS) that jointly suppresses harmful continuation features and enhances safe-refusal features within the SAE feature space. Evaluate on GUISE and other datasets, comparing to prior single-direction steering methods, likely via feature-level masking and optimization.

Result: REINS substantially reduces harmful responses and improves safe refusals, avoiding mere apparent safety through feature collapse and largely preserving general capabilities, whereas prior methods are either too weak or give false safety.

Conclusion: Combining inhibitory suppression with reinforced safe-refusal signals in SAE steering yields stronger, more robust safety control for LLMs without major loss of general capabilities; GUISE reveals failure modes of existing methods and highlights REINS as a promising direction for safety-aware model steering.

Abstract: Steering with Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) offers a lightweight inference-time path for adapting the behavior of large language models without retraining. By exposing sparse and interpretable features, SAE steering provides a promising interface for safety control that guides harmful continuations toward refusal. However, we observe that complex wrappers can still undermine existing SAE steering methods on harmful prompts. To evaluate this failure mode systematically, we construct Generalized Undercover Instruction Safety Evaluation (GUISE), a dataset of harmful prompts with complex wrappers. Existing single direction SAE steering methods do not reliably produce refusals on harmful prompts, suggesting that refusal enhancement alone can be too weak when the harmful continuation path remains active. This motivates us to propose Refusal-Enhanced INhibitory Steering (REINS), which suppresses harmful continuation features and enhances safe refusal features in the same SAE feature space. Experiments on GUISE and other datasets show that prior methods either intervene too weakly or achieve only apparent safety through collapse, while REINS substantially reduces harmful responses, markedly improves safe refusals and largely preserves general capabilities.

</details>


### [147] [Beyond Task-Only Matching: Personalized Skill Routing with Counterfactual Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28241)
*Tianle Wang,Yanghe Zou,Xiang Liu,Ziyao Huang,Chenchen Fu,Weiwei Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Personalized skill routing via profile-conditioned retrieval; introduces a profile-counterfactual benchmark and SkillFeed's progressive retrieve-and-rerank method; achieves strong gains on SkillFeed-Bench and demonstrates the value of user profiles in skill suitability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current skill routing treats relevance as task-only, conflating task relevance with skill suitability when user constraints differ. A profile-aware router is needed to separate task alignment from user constraints and to expose failure modes of task-only routing.

Method: Propose profile-conditioned retrieval with a profile-counterfactual benchmark. Develop paired counterfactual supervision. Introduce SkillFeed, a progressive retrieve-and-rerank framework that first aligns task and skill, then discriminates based on user profile. Retrieve body-level evidence and rerank semantically similar but profile-conflicting candidates to identify skills that satisfy both task and user constraints.

Result: On SkillFeed-Bench, SkillFeed achieves 75.1% top-1 retrieval accuracy, a 23.1-point improvement over the pretrained routing baseline. Adding profile conditioning yields a 35.1-point gain on queries where the user profile changes the reference skill. These results indicate user profiles are most impactful when they alter skill suitability.

Conclusion: Personalized, profile-conditioned routing substantially improves skill selection fidelity for LLM agents. The proposed benchmark, supervision strategy, and SkillFeed framework collectively demonstrate that incorporating user profiles is crucial for reliable skill routing and can mitigate failures of task-only routing. The authors provide a public website for their resources.

Abstract: The rapid expansion of reusable skill repositories makes skill routing a critical capability for large language model (LLM) agents. Existing methods treat routing as task-only semantic matching. However, when users with incompatible constraints issue an identical request, this assumption conflates task relevance with skill suitability: a task-only router can select a semantically plausible skill that is unsuitable for the requesting user. To expose this failure mode, we formulate \textit{personalized skill routing} as profile-conditioned retrieval, in which relevance depends jointly on the task and the user profile. We first introduce a profile-counterfactual benchmark, in which the task is held fixed while changes in the user profile induce changes in the reference skill. We further construct paired counterfactual supervision and propose SkillFeed, a progressive retrieve-and-rerank framework that first establishes task--skill alignment and then learns profile-conditioned discrimination. By retrieving body-level evidence and reranking semantically similar but profile-conflicting candidates, SkillFeed identifies skills that satisfy both task requirements and user constraints. On SkillFeed-Bench, SkillFeed attains 75.1\% top-1 retrieval accuracy, a 23.1-point improvement over the corresponding pretrained routing baseline. Adding profile conditioning yields a 35.1-point gain on queries where user profile changes the reference skill. This contrast shows that user profiles are most consequential precisely when they change skill suitability. Our website is publicly available at http://www.aiskillfeed.com .

</details>


### [148] [Regime-Aware Portfolio Management via Retrieval-Augmented LLM-Guided Expert Switching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28252)
*Ahmad Asadi,Reza Safabakhsh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Retrieval-augmented expert-switching for adaptive portfolio management using a dual-stream VAE and a retrieval-based knowledge base, with an instruction-tuned LLM to select experts; shows improved returns and Sharpe across crypto, stock, and FX; monotonicity property established.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial markets are non-stationary, making fixed portfolio strategies suboptimal. The work aims to dynamically select among multiple experts using retrieved historical evidence to adapt to current market regimes.

Method: A dual-stream variational autoencoder models asset-level and market-wide information. A retrieval-based knowledge base stores historical market situations and expert performance. At inference, an instruction-tuned LLM reasons over retrieved evidence to choose the most appropriate expert, rather than generating portfolio actions. A monotonicity property is proven for the switching mechanism.

Result: Across cryptocurrency, stock, and foreign-exchange markets, the selector achieves the highest cumulative return and Sharpe ratio among evaluated strategies. In stock, cumulative return rises from 26% (best fixed expert) to 34%, and Sharpe from 0.74 to 0.96. Ablations show retrieval and LLM reasoning are important; larger expert pools provide complementary value.

Conclusion: Retrieval-grounded expert switching is an effective approach for adaptive portfolio management in non-stationary financial environments.

Abstract: Financial markets are inherently non-stationary, making the effectiveness of individual portfolio-management strategies highly dependent on changing market conditions. This work proposes a retrieval-augmented expert-switching framework that dynamically selects portfolio management experts based on their historical performance under similar market situations. A dual-stream variational autoencoder represents asset-level and market-wide information, while a retrieval-based knowledge base stores historical situations and expert performance. During inference, an instruction-tuned LLM reasons over the retrieved evidence to identify the most appropriate expert rather than directly generating portfolio actions. We further establish a monotonicity property showing that adding a locally superior expert cannot degrade the switching mechanism's performance. Experiments across cryptocurrency, stock, and foreign-exchange markets show that the proposed selector achieves the highest cumulative return and Sharpe ratio among the evaluated selection strategies in all three markets. In the stock market, for example, cumulative return increases from 26% for the best fixed expert to 34%, while the Sharpe ratio improves from 0.74 to 0.96. Ablation results confirm the importance of both retrieval and LLM reasoning, while experiments with different expert-pool sizes demonstrate the value of complementary expertise. Overall, the findings support retrieval-grounded expert switching as an effective approach to adaptive portfolio management in non-stationary financial environments.

</details>


### [149] [Physics-Guided Flow Matching for CT Image Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28256)
*Davide Evangelista*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Flow Matching provides a more efficient and stable generative prior for high-resolution CT reconstruction, outperforming diffusion-based priors in PSNR/SSIM with fewer sampling steps; validated on 256x256 Mayo Clinic LDCT data using a two-stage, augmentation-driven training; code and model released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models offer strong priors for ill-posed CT inversion but suffer from stochastic sampling, long inference, and fixed noise schedules, hindering efficiency and stability at high resolutions. Flow Matching is explored as a potentially faster, more stable alternative.

Method: Train a high-resolution Rectified Flow Matching model on 256x256 Mayo Clinic Low-Dose CT data. Use a two-stage training strategy: (1) strong anatomically informed data augmentation; (2) fine-tuning with reduced/no augmentation. Evaluate Flow-Matching-based reconstructions using Plug-and-Play Flow, FlowDPS, Flower, and Flow-Priors (ICTM), and compare against diffusion baselines DDRM, DPS, and DiffPIR. Publicly release trained model and code.

Result: Flow Matching-based approaches consistently outperform diffusion-based methods in PSNR, SSIM, and perceptual quality, while requiring fewer sampling steps across multiple CT inverse problem settings.

Conclusion: Flow Matching provides a stable, efficient, and effective alternative to diffusion models for high-resolution CT reconstruction; supports reproducibility via public release of models and code.

Abstract: Deep generative models have recently emerged as powerful priors for solving ill-posed inverse problems in CT, with diffusion-based approaches achieving state-of-the-art reconstruction performance. However, diffusion models typically rely on stochastic sampling procedures, long inference trajectories, and carefully tuned noise schedules, which can limit computational efficiency and numerical stability, especially at high spatial resolutions. In this work, we investigate Flow Matching as an alternative generative prior for CT reconstruction. We train a high-resolution Rectified Flow Matching model on 256x256 chest images from the Mayo Clinic Low-Dose CT dataset. To mitigate overfitting and limited anatomical variability, we employ a two-stage training strategy consisting of an initial phase with strong, anatomically informed data augmentation, followed by a fine-tuning phase with reduced or no augmentation to refine structural fidelity. The resulting model is capable of generating high-quality and anatomically coherent CT-like images, serving as a strong learned prior. We then evaluate multiple reconstruction methods specifically designed for Flow Matching models, including Plug-and-Play Flow, FlowDPS, Flower, and Flow-Priors (ICTM), and compare them against state-of-the-art diffusion-based reconstruction algorithms such as DDRM, DPS, and DiffPIR. Experimental results across several CT inverse problem settings show that Flow Matching-based approaches consistently outperform diffusion-based methods in terms of PSNR, SSIM, and perceptual quality, while requiring fewer sampling steps. Finally, we publicly release the trained Flow Matching model and accompanying code to facilitate reproducibility and future research. Overall, this work demonstrates that Flow Matching provides a stable, efficient, and effective alternative to diffusion models for high-resolution CT image reconstruction.

</details>


### [150] [Finding Where the Buck Stops: An Automated Failure Attribution-Based Reflection Framework for Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28264)
*Xiaoqing Wang,Keman Huang,Bin Liang,Hongyu Li,Xiaoyong Du,Wuqiong Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DoCtOR is a diagnose-then-correct reflection framework for multi-agent systems that identifies the decisive error agent and step, uses counterfactual reasoning to propose a corrected step, and restricts reflection to the decisive agent. It achieves substantial performance gains (22%, 26%, 27%) on HotPotQA, ChartQAPro, and Mind2Web, outperforming Reflexion, Retroformer, and COPPER, and shows generalization in low-resource settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In LLM-powered MAS, failures are often caused by a single decisive agent; traditional reflection methods have agents reflect collectively, which contaminates memory and degrades performance. A targeted reflection strategy could improve task success and memory hygiene.

Method: 1) Automated failure attribution to identify the decisive error step and agent; 2) Counterfactual reasoning to generate a corrected decisive error step; 3) Restrict reflection to the decisive error agent, leveraging PPO-enhanced reflection; evaluate on HotPotQA, ChartQAPro, Mind2Web; compare to Reflexion, Retroformer, COPPER; test low-resource settings for generalization.

Result: Reported improvements of 22%, 26%, and 27% in success rate on HotPotQA, ChartQAPro, and Mind2Web over baseline methods; DoCtOR outperforms Reflexion, Retroformer, and COPPER; demonstrates generalization to different tasks and efficiency in low-resource settings.

Conclusion: Diagnose-then-Correct reflection reduces error propagation by focusing on the root-cause agent and step, enabling targeted reflections and improved coordination in MAS; potential applicability to broader LLM-augmented MAS; future work may address attribution reliability and robustness to misidentification.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) powered by large language models have shown promise for complex tasks but suffer from high failure rates. Current self-reflection methods for MAS require all agents to reflect upon failure, overlooking a critical reality: failures typically stem from a specific agent leading the task astray, namely the decisive error agent, while others merely fulfill their regular duties. Forcing regular-behaving agents to reflect contaminates their memory with wrong insights. Hence, we propose DoCtOR (Diagnose-then-Correct PPO-enhanced Reflection), a novel reflection framework that enhances multi-agent collaboration. DoCtOR first identifies the decisive error step and decisive error agent through automated failure attribution, then employs counterfactual reasoning to generate a corrected decisive error step, and finally engages only the decisive error agent to produce targeted reflections. Experimental results show DoCtOR achieves 22%, 26%, and 27% improvements over initial success rates on HotPotQA, ChartQAPro, and Mind2Web datasets, outperforming Reflexion, Retroformer, and COPPER.
