We further establish the generalizability of our diagnose-then-correct paradigm and demonstrate that in low-resource settings, focusing reflection on reasoning steps after the decisive error step achieves comparable quality to reflecting on the complete failure trajectory.

</details>


### [151] [RECAST: Recent & Context-Aware Sampling for Test-Time Adaptation in Streaming Biosignals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28271)
*Yong-Yeon Jo,Junho Song,Joon-myoung Kwon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RECAST: a sampling module for test-time adaptation in streaming biosignals that selects adaptation samples based on recency, contextual similarity, and predictive reliability, improving online personalization without changing the model or objective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Population-trained models for streaming biosignals drift over time; online personalization requires effective sample selection to keep updates relevant and stable.

Method: Introduce RECAST within buffered TTA frameworks. Build each adaptation batch from three signals—temporal recency, contextual similarity, and predictive reliability—while leaving the model architecture and training objective unchanged; only the sampling strategy changes.

Result: On two blood-pressure datasets, RECAST improves estimation accuracy and trend tracking over baselines and ablations. Per-patient gains are statistically significant, with stronger improvements on harder patients in an emergency-department setting; the method adds sub-second latency per segment on a single GPU and CPU core.

Conclusion: RECAST is a lightweight, practical sampling strategy for stream-based TTA that enhances personalization by selecting temporally relevant, context-aligned, and reliable samples without modifying the learning objective.

Abstract: Streaming biosignals vary across subjects and drift over time, so population-trained models lose accuracy during long-term monitoring. Test-time adaptation (TTA) enables online personalization by updating the model on incoming samples. But in a stream, a basic question is left open: \emph{which samples should drive each update?} Using all buffered samples blurs the update with irrelevant segments. Using only the latest segment makes the update noisy and unstable. The most useful samples are recent, aligned with the current physiological state, and reliable enough to learn from. We propose \textbf{RECAST} (REcent \& Context-Aware Sampling for TTA), a lightweight sampling module for buffered TTA frameworks. RECAST builds each adaptation batch from three signals: temporal recency, contextual similarity, and predictive reliability. It changes only which samples are used, leaving the model and the training objective unchanged. On two blood-pressure datasets, RECAST improves estimation accuracy and trend tracking over baselines and ablations. The per-patient gains are statistically significant on both datasets, with broad improvement on the regular benchmark and gains concentrated on the hardest patients in the emergency-department setting. RECAST stays practical, adding only sub-second latency per segment on a single GPU and CPU core.

</details>


### [152] [LoopArena: Benchmarking Models as Runtime Controllers for Loop Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28281)
*Yi Wang,Haopeng Zhang,Chengxiang Huang,Rui Dai,Kaikui Liu,Piotr Koniusz,Xiangxiang Chu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoopArena is a benchmark that evaluates how well a Controller can guide a fixed coding agent (Worker) through long-running tasks, using three evaluation settings (Type I-III). It reveals low strict success (best 24.69%) but substantial inference-cost savings (≈64%), with Type II showing strong rank correlation (ρ ≈ 0.975). The authors release data and code for reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify the reliability and efficiency of long-horizon loop control, where a model guides a separate agent via iterative rounds, mitigating issues from stale progress, verification gaps, and misallocation of budget.

Method: Controller-Agent setup with a fixed Worker. Three complementary evaluation types: Type I (execution-validated questions without Worker execution), Type II (control over a task slice via multiple rounds), Type III (paired full-task evaluation). Metrics include Strict Success Rate (per task), inference-cost reduction, and rank correlations (Spearman ρ).

Result: Full-task Strict Success Rate peaks at 24.69%, indicating considerable room for improvement in long-horizon loop control. Across Controllers, estimated inference cost is reduced by 64.4%. Type II preserves a strong ordinal agreement with the Core criterion (Spearman ρ = 0.9747).

Conclusion: LoopArena highlights the challenges of reliable long-horizon loop guidance and confirms meaningful efficiency gains from controller-led control. The benchmark and accompanying data/code enable systematic evaluation and comparison of future loop-control methods, guiding further research into more robust, cost-effective loop architectures.

Abstract: Loop Engineering is emerging as a practice for organizing development work around coding agents. Instead of writing each prompt by hand, practitioners design loops that monitor progress, assign work, run checks, and decide what the agent should do next. Even with a capable coding agent, a loop may trust a stale progress note, skip needed verification, spend its budget in the wrong direction, or stop before the task is safe to submit. Yet the final outcome of one end-to-end run cannot tell whether success or failure reflects the loop's guidance or the coding agent's ability to carry out the task. We introduce LoopArena, a benchmark for evaluating how well one model can guide a separate coding agent through a long-running task. The model under evaluation is the \textbf{Controller}: after each coding round, it receives a structured summary of the run and instructs a separate, fixed coding agent, the \textbf{Worker}, on what to do or verify next, or decides whether to stop. LoopArena evaluates this ability in three complementary settings that differ in execution scope and cost. Type I scores next-step Loop Contract selection through execution-validated questions without running the Worker at evaluation time. Type II executes repeated control over a selected slice of a full task, while Type III evaluates the paired full task from its original state. On full tasks, the best observed Strict Success Rate is \textbf{24.69\%}, leaving substantial room for improvement in long-horizon loop control. Across Controllers, the paired reduction in estimated inference cost averages \textbf{64.4\%}, and Type II produces a similar ordering under the main Core criterion (Spearman's \(ρ=\textbf{0.9747}\)). We release the benchmark data and evaluation code at https://github.com/AMAP-ML/LoopArena .

</details>


### [153] [Memristive-Friendly Hadamard Reservoir Computing: Structured, Multiplier-Free Recurrences at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28295)
*Andrea Ceni,Gianluca Milano,Carlo Ricciardi,Claudio Gallicchio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multiplier-free, orthogonal reservoir computing operator replaces dense recurrent matrices with a structured blend of sign diagonals, permutation, and a fast Walsh-Hadamard transform, enabling O(N) parameters and O(N log N) per-step compute. It is implemented in standard and memristive-friendly ESNs, achieves hardware-friendly speedups, and matches or surpasses dense orthogonal reservoirs on classification/regression with large reservoirs (up to N=8192).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable hardware-efficient recurrent neural networks for neuromorphic hardware by removing dense recurrent matrices. The goal is a multiplier-free, structured operator that preserves echo-state properties and provides full-state mixing, with small memory footprint for real-time hardware implementation.

Method: Construct an orthogonal operator from sign diagonals, a permutation, and a fast Walsh–Hadamard transform (FWHT). The operator is not materialized as a matrix; it is applied implicitly. It is instantiated in both a standard ESN and a memristive-friendly ESN with one binary input per unit. Theoretical analysis shows exact orthogonality yields a tight echo-state constraint and predictable noise response; experiments across 20 classification and 7 regression benchmarks with reservoir sizes up to N=8192; hardware timing on three platforms; ablation studies on noise, quantization, device mismatch, and discrete faults.

Result: Structured reservoirs match dense orthogonal reservoirs in performance and outperform the cycle reservoir on average, with performance gaps widening as reservoir size increases. Hardware timing shows up to 50× faster recurrent steps than dense matrix products and memory footprint reduced by up to 10^4×. The operator ablation demonstrates robustness to noise, quantization, and device faults.

Conclusion: The proposed operator yields a scalable, hardware-friendly RC benchmark: multiplier-free, O(N) parameter count, O(N log N) compute, no dense matrix, with strong theoretical guarantees and empirical parity or superiority over existing dense/cycle reservoirs, plus substantial hardware efficiency and robustness evidence.

Abstract: Reservoir Computing (RC) designs Recurrent Neural Networks around a fixed, i.e., untrained, recurrent layer, and is a natural candidate for neuromorphic hardware. Memristive-friendly reservoirs derive the neuron dynamics from memristive-device kinetics, but still rely on dense recurrent matrices, which are expensive to realize physically. In this paper, we replace the dense matrix with a structured orthogonal operator, built from sign diagonals, a permutation, and a fast Walsh-Hadamard transform. The operator is multiplier-free, requires $O(N)$ parameters and $O(N\log N)$ operations per step, and is never materialized as a matrix. We instantiate it in a standard and in a memristive-friendly Echo State Network, with one binary input connection per unit.
  Our mathematical analysis shows that exact orthogonality yields an echo state condition that is tight in the recurrent scaling, and a noise response that is predictable at design time. Moreover, the operator mixes the whole state in a single application. Experiments on twenty classification and seven regression benchmarks, at reservoir sizes up to $N = 8192$, show that the structured models match dense orthogonal reservoirs, and achieve better mean performance than the cycle reservoir by a margin that widens with size. Furthermore, we time the recurrent step on three hardware platforms, where it is up to $50\times$ faster than a dense product and $10^4\times$ smaller in memory. Finally, we ablate the operator and measure the response to noise, quantization, device mismatch and discrete faults.

</details>


### [154] [MAIL: Memory-driven, Adaptive, Incremental, and Literature-grounded Framework for Hypothesis Generation in Chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28315)
*Mahdi Babaei,Xueshen Li,Yutao Kuang,Jolene P. Reid,Yu Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes MAIL, a memory-augmented, adaptive, incremental, and literature-grounded framework for autonomous hypothesis generation in chemistry using LLMs; demonstrated on TOMATO-Chem and a high-novelty nature/science challenge, achieving top MIOS/MPOS and expert-quality scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth of chemical literature creates a bottleneck for generating high-quality, experimentally useful hypotheses. Existing approaches rely on static corpora, predefined heuristics, or labor-intensive human-in-the-loop pipelines, limiting scalability and novelty.

Method: MAIL employs memory-driven, temporally grounded reasoning where hypotheses emerge from an evolving conceptual path that accumulates and reinterprets prior knowledge. It automates hypothesis generation and continuously updates its reasoning with literature-derived information, evaluated on TOMATO-Chem and a newly curated high-novelty dataset (HN-NS).

Result: Across both datasets, MAIL produces structurally coherent and mechanistically plausible hypotheses, achieves the highest MIOS and MPOS scores by better recovering central ideas and methodological elements of historical target hypotheses, and obtains the highest expert-evaluation scores for scientific quality.

Conclusion:  MEMORY-augmented, adaptive, incremental, and literature-grounded reasoning enables LLMs to autonomously explore chemical domains, yielding innovative and chemically plausible hypotheses; MAIL demonstrates strong potential for scalable, high-quality hypothesis generation in chemistry.

Abstract: The ever-expanding volume of the chemical literature offers unprecedented opportunities to generate novel and impactful hypotheses. However, the bottleneck lies in efficiently navigating this vast knowledge base to formulate high-quality, experimentally meaningful insights.
  While Large Language Models (LLMs) show promise for this task, existing methods often rely on static inspiration corpora, predefined heuristics, or laborious human-in-the-loop pipelines and decision-support frameworks that limit scalability and novelty. In this work, we propose an automated approach, a Memory-augmented, Adaptive, Incremental, and Literature-grounded (MAIL) framework for hypothesis generation in chemistry. Our MAIL method formulates hypothesis generation as a temporally grounded, memory-driven reasoning process, where hypotheses emerge from an evolving conceptual path that continuously accumulates and reinterprets prior knowledge. We evaluated the MAIL framework on a public TOMATO-Chem dataset and a newly curated and disseminated high-novelty nature/science challenge (HN-NS) dataset. Across both datasets, MAIL generates structurally coherent and mechanistically plausible hypotheses, achieves the highest MIOS and MPOS by more effectively recovering the central ideas and methodological elements of the historical target hypotheses, and obtains the highest overall expert-evaluation scores for scientific quality. These results demonstrate the potential of LLMs to autonomously explore chemical domains and generate hypotheses that are both innovative and chemically plausible.

</details>


### [155] [Real-Valued Hyperdimensional Sequence Representations with Hadamard Product Binding and Shift Equivariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28334)
*Kenny Schlegel,Dmitri A. Rachkovskij,Denis Kleyko,Amy Loutfi,Stefan Streif,Evgeny Osipov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Real-valued position encodings inspired by Random Fourier Features enable Hadamard-product binding in Hyperdimensional Computing, with a Sinusoid variant offering exact shift-equivariance and strong performance on time-series tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To encode temporal order in HD computing with real-valued vectors compatible with Hadamard binding, overcoming limitations of traditional Fractional Power Encoding that favor circular convolution or complex multiplication.

Method: Introduce three real-valued variants: an inverse Fourier transform baseline, and Sinusoid and Cosine-only representations derived from Random Fourier Features; derive an explicit shift operator for the Sinusoid variant; evaluate on time-series classification datasets.

Result: All three variants achieve performance comparable to standard Fractional Power Encoding; the Sinusoid variant provides the best trade-off, offering efficient real-valued implementation and exact shift-equivariant transformations, enabling Hadamard product binding.

Conclusion: Real-valued position encodings grounded in Random Fourier Features are viable for sequence representations in hyperdimensional computing, with Sinusoid offering the most favorable balance between efficiency and exact shift handling.

Abstract: Encoding temporal order is a fundamental requirement for sequence representations in Hyperdimensional Computing. Fractional Power Encoding provides similarity-preserving position vectors whose inner products approximate shift-invariant kernels, and it supports shift-equivariant transformations of encoded sequence representations. However, standard formulations of Fractional Power Encoding are primarily designed for binding operations such as circular convolution or complex-valued multiplication, which limits their compatibility with Hadamard product binding of real-valued vectors. This paper develops real-valued position encodings motivated by Random Fourier Features, aiming to retain the desirable properties of Fractional Power Encoding while supporting Hadamard-based operations. We propose three real-valued position-encoding variants: a real-valued baseline based on the inverse Fourier transform, and Sinusoid and Cosine-only representations derived from Random Fourier Features. Among them, the Sinusoid variant provides an explicit algebraic shift operator, allowing temporal shifts to be applied directly to the vector-encoded sequence representation without re-encoding the shifted sequence. Experiments on time-series classification datasets show that the proposed real-valued representations achieve performance comparable to standard Fractional Power Encoding while enabling computationally efficient Hadamard product binding. The Sinusoid variant offers the most favorable trade-off, combining efficient real-valued implementation with exact shift-equivariant transformations.

</details>


### [156] [AGENT-O: A Semantic Agent Card Framework for Interoperable and Governed Healthcare AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28345)
*Pengze Li,Cui Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AGENT-O: an OWL 2/RDF ontology and semantic Agent Card for health AI agents; enables automated reporting-completeness assessment across five dimensions; applied to 279 papers; reveals gaps in runtime/governance/reproducibility; provides reusable ontology and workflow but not agent quality or deployment readiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for structured representation of health AI agents and objective assessment of reporting completeness to improve reproducibility, governance, and trust.

Method: Developed AGENT-O ontology; covers runtime, models, workflow, tools, clinical use, evaluation, provenance, governance, and reporting assessment; used OWL 2/RDF, SHACL suites, SPARQL queries; three case studies; evaluated 279 papers; model-assisted scoring; generated a reporting-completeness workflow.

Result: Ontology captured 1,962 RDF triples and 1,922 Protege axioms; 252 active classes; 198 active object properties; 51 datatype properties; all SHACL suites conformed; all competency queries returned prespecified evidence; 279 papers scored; incomplete reporting highest for runtime/architecture (84.6%), governance/safety (82.8%), provenance/reproducibility (78.1%), with evaluation (25.8%) and benchmark-process alignment (29.8%); identified evaluation-spec gap where evaluation and benchmark reporting was more consistent than runtime architecture, governance, reproducibility.

Conclusion: AGENT-O provides a reusable ontology, semantic Agent Card profile, and reporting-completeness workflow for structured reporting and gap identification, but it does not assess agent quality or deployment readiness.

Abstract: AGENT-O is a modular ontology framework that defines a semantic Agent Card for representing health-oriented AI agent systems and supports assessment of reporting completeness in scientific publications. AGENT-O was developed as an OWL 2/RDF ontology covering runtime, models, workflow, tools, clinical use, evaluation, provenance, governance, and reporting assessment. Evaluation included ontology inventory, OWL-RL reasoning, three SHACL suites, 12 SPARQL competency queries, three cases, and model-assisted reporting-completeness assessment of 279 papers across five dimensions. The ontology contained 1,962 RDF triples and 1,922 Protege axioms, with 252 active classes, 198 active object properties, and 51 datatype properties. All SHACL suites conformed on example graphs, all competency queries returned prespecified evidence, and all 279 papers were scored. Incomplete reporting was highest for runtime/architecture (84.6%), governance/safety (82.8%), and provenance/reproducibility (78.1%), compared with evaluation (25.8%) and benchmark-process alignment (29.8%). AGENT-O supported semantic Agent Card representation and reporting assessment while revealing an evaluation-specification gap: evaluation and benchmark procedures were reported more consistently than runtime architecture, governance, and reproducibility. AGENT-O provides a reusable ontology, semantic Agent Card profile, and reporting-completeness workflow for structured reporting and gap identification, but does not assess agent quality or deployment readiness.

</details>


### [157] [Propagating construction-time knowledge quality into medical question answering: A framework grounded in clinical guidelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28360)
*Jie Hu,Junjie Wang,Shan Lu,Yifang Hu,Gong Cheng,Yun Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A quality-aware framework that fuses construction-time triple quality signals (SchemaConf and EvidScore) into a per-triple Q(t) to guide graph retrieval and evidence prompting, improving QA on clinical guidelines under domain and model shifts; yields better triple quality discrimination and reduces omissions/conflicts with high evidence precision and clinician support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a disconnect between KG construction quality control and inference-time evidence use; reusing construction-time quality as a persistent signal can improve downstream QA performance and trustworthiness.

Method: Model two quality dimensions: SchemaConf (structural conformance) and EvidScore (evidential support). Fuse into Q(t); propagate as graph attributes; perform quality-weighted subgraph retrieval and tier-conditioned prompting; preserve passage-level provenance; evaluate on Chinese diabetes guidelines with cross-version/model shifts; compare against baselines; collect metrics including AUC, omission/conflict rates, evidence precision, clinician ratings.

Result: Q(t) fusion outperforms single components in discriminating triple quality (AUC 0.748 vs 0.703/EvidScore, 0.645 SchemaConf). In QA, reduces omissions from 16.3% to 5.3% and conflicts from 16.3% to 2.7%. Evidence-grounded precision 81.6%; near-zero invalid citations. Clinician ratings: 4.68 vs 4.21 (no retrieval) approaching oracle 4.80; cross-generator trends hold.

Conclusion: Construction-time quality signals can serve as persistent evidence for retrieval and prompting, improving reliability and reducing erroneous outputs; suggests broader applicability of quality-aware KG-toA pipeline.

Abstract: Large language models have facilitated knowledge graph (KG) construction from clinical guidelines, but extracted triples vary in structural validity and evidential support. Meanwhile, graph-augmented question answering (QA) systems typically optimize query relevance during retrieval, with limited reuse of quality information produced during KG construction. This creates a disconnect between construction-time quality control and inference-time evidence use. We investigate whether construction-time triple quality can serve as a persistent signal for downstream evidence selection and presentation. We propose a quality-aware framework that models structural conformance (SchemaConf) and evidential support (EvidScore) as complementary dimensions and fuses them into a per-triple quality signal, Q(t). Rather than using quality solely for filtering, the framework retains Q(t) and derived quality tiers as graph attributes and propagates them into quality-weighted subgraph retrieval and tier-conditioned evidence prompting, while preserving passage-level provenance. Experiments on Chinese diabetes clinical guidelines show that the utility of the quality signal is distribution dependent. Under cross-version and cross-model shift, the fused Q(t) provides stronger triple-quality discrimination than either component alone (AUC 0.748 vs. 0.703 for EvidScore and 0.645 for SchemaConf). In guideline-grounded QA, propagating construction-time quality reduces required-knowledge omission from 16.3% to 5.3% and conflicting outputs from 16.3% to 2.7%, with an evidence-grounded precision of 81.6% and near-zero invalid citations. Blinded clinician ratings favor the full framework over no retrieval (4.68 vs. 4.21 on a five-point scale) and approach the oracle condition (4.80), while cross-generator experiments show consistent trends.

</details>


### [158] [GRACE:Gradient-guided Coreset Selection for LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28361)
*Praveen Bushipaka,Andrea D'Angelo,Lucia Passaro,Tommaso Cucinotta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Gradient-guided coreset selection (GRACE) for LLM unlearning that constructs forget and retain sets from data using gradient directions and orthogonal matching pursuit, improving utility with comparable forget quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In realistic settings, forget and retain sets must be inferred from heterogeneous corpora rather than pre-specified; current methods assume seed/predefined sets, limiting applicability for unlearning requests with few examples.

Method: GRACE computes a forget direction from seed examples eliciting the undesired behavior, then selects a compact forget coreset whose gradients approximate this direction via non-negative orthogonal matching pursuit. To preserve utility, it selects retain examples after projecting out the forget direction and applying clustered orthogonal matching pursuit in the remaining gradient space.

Result: GRACE improves model utility while maintaining comparable forget quality across two target domains, two model families, and four unlearning algorithms, with consistent gains over prior gradient-based selection methods.

Conclusion: Gradient-guided coreset selection provides effective, data-driven forget/retain set inference for LLM unlearning, yielding utility gains without sacrificing forgetting performance and demonstrating robustness across domains and model families.

Abstract: Machine Unlearning methods for Large Language Models typically assume pre-specified forget and retain sets. In realistic settings, however, requests may provide only a few examples of undesired behavior, requiring forget and retain sets to be inferred from heterogeneous corpora. We study this data-selection problem and propose GRACE , a gradient-guided coreset selection method that constructs both forget and retain sets for LLM unlearning. GRACE first computes a forget direction from seed examples that elicit the undesired behavior, then selects a compact forget coreset whose gradients approximate this direction using non-negative orthogonal matching pursuit. To preserve model utility, it selects retain examples after projecting out the forget direction and applying clustered orthogonal matching pursuit in the remaining gradient space. Across two target domains, two model families, and four unlearning algorithms, GRACE improves model utility while maintaining comparable forget quality, with particularly consistent gains over prior gradient-based selection methods.

</details>


### [159] [EvoUndo: Recoverability-Constrained Self-Evolution for LLM Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28363)
*Tanmay Sah,Dolly Sah,Harshul Jain,Tanya Sah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoUndo framework for self-modifying LLM agents; represents, synthesizes, diagnoses, and verifies recoverability of model-generated self-modifications across counterfactual states; shows many mutations fail recoverability and that enhanced recovery-language and grounding are needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolution can enhance capabilities but may cause irreversible, hard-to-reverse mutations; need robust verification across counterfactual states to ensure safe, recoverable evolution.

Method: Define EvoUndo, formal recovery representations (L0 and extended calculus), evaluate 600 unseen one-shot self-evolution tasks; perform deterministic oracle analysis; introduce protocol-locked grounding-by-expressivity intervention; test across GPT-OSS-120B and Qwen3.8-27B backbones; assess exact-address grounding and expressivity effects.

Result: Outcomes: 0/197 recoveries with conventional repair under original representation; 48/197 with deterministic oracle under L0; 191/197 with extended recovery calculus; with grounding-by-expressivity, 38/48 when original language suffices; 142/143 in S1 stratum; on primary backbone, exact-address diagnostics reduce recovery to 133/143; replication preserves grounding/expressivity effects but not this negative interaction; indicates significant gains from improved verification and grounding.

Conclusion: To achieve reliable agent self-evolution, co-design recovery verification, state grounding, witness semantics, and recovery-language expressivity rather than relying on iterative prompting alone.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly modify their own prompts, tools, middleware, resources, and execution harnesses at runtime. Such self-evolution can improve capability, but a successful mutation may leave persistent effects that cannot be safely reversed in states different from the one in which it was created. We introduce EvoUndo, a framework for representing, synthesizing, diagnosing, and independently verifying recoverability of model-generated self-modifications across counterfactual states. Across 600 unseen one-shot self-evolution tasks, we identify 197 capability-improving mutations that fail recoverability verification. Under the original recovery representation, conventional repair strategies recover 0/197 of these natural failures. Deterministic oracle analysis recovers 48/197 under the original recovery language L0, while the extended recovery calculus increases empirical oracle recovery to 191/197. A protocol-locked 2x2 grounding-by-expressivity intervention then separates two bottlenecks: exact state-address grounding increases successful recovery from 0/48 to 38/48 (79.2%) when the original language is sufficient, while extending the recovery language enables recovery on 142/143 (99.3%) failures in the oracle-defined S1 stratum. On the primary gpt-oss-120b backbone, adding exact-address diagnostics to the richer language reduces recovery to 133/143 (93.0%); a Qwen3.8-27B replication preserves the grounding and expressivity effects but not this negative interaction, indicating that the latter is model-dependent. These results indicate that reliable agent self-evolution requires co-designing verification, state grounding, witness semantics, and recovery-language expressivity rather than relying on iterative prompting alone.

</details>


### [160] [MAP: A Benchmark on Multimodal Accessibility Planning for Real World Places](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28384)
*Jason Armitage,Ioannis Tsochantaridis,Linda Mazzone,Chuqiao Yan,Srini Narayanan,Sarah Ebling*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAP: a benchmark to evaluate multimodal AI assistants for accessibility planning in the real world; combines claim verification and visual evidence retrieval; supports time-evolving data with automatic and human ratings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need to assess how multimodal AI systems assist users with accessibility requirements when planning visits to real-world places, accounting for changes in place information and accessibility features over time.

Method: Two novel assessments: (1) Claim verification for accessibility planning—verify that place information and accessibility features are supported and identify places meeting requested features; (2) Visual evidence retrieval for accessibility planning—select visual evidence for the requested place and feature. Ground truth is refreshed periodically to reflect changes; evaluation combines automatic ratings with human ratings for a subset of responses.

Result: Not specified in the abstract. The paper proposes the framework and evaluation protocol.

Conclusion: MAP provides a benchmark for evaluating multimodal AI assistants in accessibility planning with time-evolving data, using two complementary assessments and mixed automatic/human scoring to enable system comparison.

Abstract: We introduce MAP, the first benchmark to evaluate multimodal AI systems as assistants for users with accessibility requirements when planning visits to places in the real world. In our evaluation, systems are presented with requests to verify or recommend a point of interest meeting an accessibility requirement. MAP contains two novel assessments: Claim verification for accessibility planning assesses if information on places and stated accessibility features is supported and identifies places that satisfy requested accessibility features. Visual evidence retrieval for accessibility planning checks if a multimodal AI system can select visual evidence for the requested place and accessibility feature. Our methodology supports comparison of AI systems in a setting where place information and accessibility information can change over time by evaluating systems and refreshing ground truth data at scheduled times. The benchmark is based on automatic rating and human rating for a proportion of responses.

</details>


### [161] [Timing-Aware Repurchase Prediction for Web-Scale E-Commerce: Survival Models for Multi-Surface Grocery Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28393)
*Akshay Kekuda,Shreeranjani Srirangamsridharan,Ishan Bhatt,Yanan Cao,Sinduja Subramaniam,Evren Korpeoglu,Kaushiki Nag,Kannan Achan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survival models for time-to-repurchase replace horizon-specific binary classifiers; Log-Normal best marginal fit, Weibull best conditional residual, Exponential AFT offers best calibration; a single AFT model reduces complexity ~3x; calibration-ranking trade-off within AFT family.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the inefficiency and fragility of training a separate horizon-specific binary classifier for repurchase; leverage time-to-event modeling to predict when a customer will repurchase items.

Method: Apply survival analysis to repurchase data across millions of customers and 30+ ablations. Compare hazard patterns, fit (marginal vs conditional), and calibration. Evaluate using R^2, ranking metrics, and calibration errors. Introduce a 4-parameter parametric calibration mapping survival CDFs to horizon probabilities. Compare distributions within AFT family (Exponential, Weibull, Log-Normal, etc.).

Result: Hazard analysis shows slightly decreasing marginal hazard (k ~ 0.9). Log-Normal provides best marginal fit (R^2 ≈ 0.998) and best ranking; Weibull yields best conditional residual fit. A single Accelerated Failure Time (AFT) model matches/exceeds per-horizon classifiers while using ~3x fewer trees. Feature importance shifts toward channel-cadence and recency; aggregate frequency counts drop. A four-parameter calibration maps raw survival CDFs to horizon probabilities; Exponential AFT achieves very low calibration error (ECE ≈ 1e-4), much better than Log-Normal, while ranking metrics are similar. Conclusion: adopt Exponential AFT for probability surfaces and Log-Normal for ranking, highlighting a calibration-ranking trade-off within the AFT family.

Conclusion: Survival modeling offers a unified, efficient framework for repurchase prediction, revealing nuanced hazard behavior and enabling calibrated probability surfaces alongside ranking performance. The study recommends using Exponential AFT for calibrated probability estimates and Log-Normal for ranking, acknowledging calibration-ranking trade-offs across the AFT family.

Abstract: Repurchase recommenders in e-commerce are commonly framed as a binary question asking "will this customer buy this item within W days", a formulation that requires a separately trained model for every horizon of interest. We replace this stack with survival models that predict time-to-repurchase directly, and evaluate them on millions of customers from a major grocery e-commerce platform across more than thirty ablation configurations. Our study makes three contributions. First, an empirical hazard analysis reveals a slightly decreasing marginal hazard (k ~ 0.9), differing from the common intuition that grocery items become more likely to be repurchased the longer since the last purchase (increasing hazard, k > 1). Log-Normal achieves the best marginal fit (R^2 = 0.998) and the best ranking, despite Weibull providing the best conditional residual fit, revealing an apparent discrepancy we analyze in detail. Second, a single Accelerated Failure Time (AFT) model replaces three per-horizon binary classifiers, matching or exceeding each at its own horizon while using roughly 3x fewer total trees. Feature importance reshuffles under the survival objective: channel-cadence and recency signals rise while aggregate frequency counts fall. Third, a 4-parameter parametric calibration maps raw survival CDFs to per-horizon probabilities with zero cross-horizon monotonicity violations. Calibration quality varies by an order of magnitude across the AFT family: Exponential AFT (Weibull k=1) achieves expected calibration error (ECE) ~1e-4, roughly 10x lower than Log-Normal, while ranking metrics agree within 0.3% relative. We adopt Exponential AFT for probability-consuming surfaces and Log-Normal for pure ranking, exposing a principled calibration-ranking trade-off within a single AFT family.

</details>


### [162] [RetailAgent: Structured Adverse Timing in Self-Conditioned Multimodal LLM Trading Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28399)
*Yupeng Zhang,Liuyuan Jiang,Hongyi Huang,Bingheng Li,Lisha Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based trading agents exhibit a recoverable, directional timing signal in intraday decisions, with negative timing that persists across modalities, horizons, states, and model families. The effect weakens when action sequences are shuffled, and is amplified by self-authored memories, indicating a predictable policy alignment with future returns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether sequential LLM trading agents develop predictable directional timing in intraday decision policies and determine if such structure is recoverable and exploitable for modeling interactions between participants.

Method: RetailAgent framework: an LLM observes anonymized intraday price histories and permitted state, then repeatedly chooses long (hold) or flat (stay out) before the next interval return is revealed. Performance is assessed by comparing long vs flat returns along the same stock’s intraday path after removing the overall fraction of long decisions (exposure-matched). Analyses span modalities, horizons, states, and model families; controls include shuffling action sequences and introducing self-authored memories of decisions to test persistence; additional checks assess co-occurrence of actions within stock-days.

Result: Exhibits persistent negative timing across modalities, horizons, states, and model families. Shuffling actions substantially attenuates the effect, indicating that alignment between actions and subsequent returns drives the negative score. Self-authored memories amplify policy persistence; timing becomes more negative on stock-days where the agent uses both actions.

Conclusion: A stable, recoverable directional structure exists in sequential LLM financial decisions, providing a behavioral signal for studying how another participant could respond to a predictable policy.

Abstract: In financial markets, a sequential policy that reacts systematically to price movements may become predictable to other market participants. This paper studies whether large language model (LLM) agents exhibit such directional structure through RetailAgent, an experimental framework in which an LLM observes anonymized intraday equity price histories and permitted state, then repeatedly chooses long (hold the stock) or flat (stay out) before the subsequent interval return is revealed. We compare returns during long and flat intervals along the same stock's intraday path after removing the overall fraction of long decisions. This exposure-matched measure reveals persistent negative timing across modality, horizon, state, and model family. Shuffling saved action sequences substantially attenuates the effect, showing that alignment between actions and subsequent returns drives the negative score. Feeding self-authored memories into decisions further increases policy persistence, while timing becomes more negative among stock-days on which the agent uses both actions. These results reveal stable, recoverable directional structure in sequential LLM financial decisions and a behavioral signal for studying how another participant could respond to a predictable policy.

</details>


### [163] [VERA-8B: Evidence-Grounded Audit Risk Reasoning from SEC Filings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28402)
*Menghan Liu,Elynn Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VERA-8B: an end-to-end audit reasoning system that unifies SFT and GRPO for evidence-grounded audit reasoning, with abstention/uncertainty handling and an AuditBridge to ground filings into reviewer-ready reports, yielding auditable outputs and improved risk identification before enforcement actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard financial language models prioritize fluency over evidence, creating a grounding gap for audit work; no prior ML work targeted pre-enforcement audit prediction; need for a unified evidence standard and auditable outputs.

Method: Develop VERA-8B as an end-to-end system; combine supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and GRPO for evidence-grounded reasoning; incorporate abstention and uncertainty qualification; introduce AuditBridge to convert raw filings into verified records and reviewer-ready reports.

Result: Performance surpasses all evaluated baselines.

Conclusion: The approach yields auditable, review-ready outputs suitable for practical audit work and closes the grounding gap by identifying risks pre-enforcement and grounding reasoning in verified records.

Abstract: Across audit applications, judgments must be supported by reasonable evidence. However, standard financial language models prioritize fluency over evidence. They are built for general financial reasoning and may produce plausible but ambiguous answers, creating a grounding gap that makes them unsuitable for audit work. We address this gap with VERA-8B, a new end-to-end audit reasoning system that identifies audit risks before enforcement actions occur. Constructing such a model raises several challenges, as no prior machine learning work targets pre-enforcement audit prediction. To our knowledge, we are the first to unify SFT and GRPO for evidence-grounded audit reasoning under one evidence standard, achieving performance that surpasses all evaluated baselines. Because auditing cannot tolerate unsupported claims, we introduce abstention and uncertainty qualification to defer uncertain or evidence-incomplete cases. Finally, we design an AuditBridge to ground model reasoning for practical audit work. It transforms raw filings into verified records and then into reviewer-ready reports, bridging finance and computation with broad generality. Together, these components produce auditable, review-ready outputs suitable for practical audit work.

</details>


### [164] [Program Learning with Verifiable Rewards: Symbolic Backpropagation for Post-Training LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28421)
*Vishvesh Bhat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PLVR learns external, verifiable programs for reasoning via symbolic backpropagation, avoiding weight updates, and outperforming RL-based approaches on benchmarks with reusable primitives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the opaqueness and fragility of reasoning learned inside model weights after fine-tuning or RL; for tasks with verifiable intermediate steps, externalizing reasoning as an explicit program enables inspection, portability, and potentially better sample efficiency.

Method: Post-training method that induces programs from input-output examples. Uses symbolic backpropagation where each program layer has a typed ontology; a loss is computed at the output and required input ontologies are propagated back by type inference over primitive signatures (a derivation-based credit assignment). Per-step contract-based rewards, unlike terminal-only rewards in RLVR. A single primitive library supports multiple benchmarks; evaluation on LiveCodeBench v6 and Tau2Bench with 30B base models shows improvements; at equal budgets, PLVR outperforms RLVR; 100 examples per new task; ablation shows backward pass (not type system) drives advantage. Release includes symbolic backpropagation library and a conformance checker to enable applying the method to other primitive libraries.

Result: Empirical results show PLVR-based 30B base models outperform RL by 27.8 points on average on LiveCodeBench v6 and Tau2Bench; frontier models show a larger advantage (~13.6 points). A unitary primitive library enables two benchmarks with 100 examples per new task; replacing loss-guided search with uniform sampling collapses median program from 65.6 to 17.5, highlighting the role of the backward pass. The approach reduces reliance on finetuning data and preserves inspectability of the reasoning steps.

Conclusion: Externalizing reasoning as verifiable programs via symbolic backpropagation yields substantial performance gains over RL-based approaches, encourages reuse of primitive libraries, and provides tooling (library and conformance checker) to generalize the method to other tasks.

Abstract: Post training a language model to reason means updating its weights. Supervised finetuning and reinforcement learning both place the acquired capability inside the model where it cannot be inspected cannot be checked step by step and cannot be moved to another model. We argue that for tasks whose intermediate steps admit verification, reasoning is better placed outside the base models weights as an explicit program composed from deterministic and neural primitives. We introduce PLVR (Program Learning with Verifiable Rewards): a post training method that learns such programs directly from input-output examples. Its mechanism is symbolic backpropagation: each program layer carries a typed ontology a loss is computed at the output against ground truth and required input ontologies are propagated backward by type inference over primitive signatures: an analogue of the chain rule in which credit assignment is a derivation rather than an estimate. Where RLVR verifies a terminal outcome, PLVRs reward is a per step contract verdict dense over program structure. On LiveCodeBench v6 and Tau2Bench, 30B base models with PLVR outperform RL at matched budget by 27.8 points on average and frontier models an order of magnitude larger by 13.6 points. A single primitive library serves two benchmarks, so the marginal cost of a new task is 100 examples of program search and no new finetuning data. Replacing the loss guided search with uniform sampling over the same type admissible space at equal budget collapses the median program from 65.6 to 17.5, identifying the backward pass rather than the type system as the source of the advantage. We release the symbolic backpropagation library and a conformance checker so the method can be applied to primitive libraries other than our own.

</details>


### [165] [Prove2Me: An Open Collaborative Platform for Scaling Math Formalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28433)
*Shuze Chen,Kunal Marwaha,Xiaoyang Lu,Henry Yuen,Tianyi Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prove2Me is an open, AI-assisted platform enabling crowd-sourced formalization of mathematics via mission-based collaboration and proof reuse, aiming for scalable, machine-checked collaboration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce barriers to formal verification by lowering the expertise/time required and enabling internet-scale collaboration through AI agents that can write and reuse proofs.

Method: Design and implement Prove2Me with formalization missions, AI agents contributing formal proofs, and a specialized harness that supports building on others’ work and reusing existing results for large-scale collaboration.

Result: The paper presents the design and intended workflow of Prove2Me, detailing platform architecture and collaboration mechanisms for AI-assisted formalization, but does not report empirical results.

Conclusion: Prove2Me aims to democratize formal verification by making math formalization scalable and accessible to anyone with an agent, enabling crowd-sourced, machine-checked collaboration.

Abstract: Proof assistants such as Lean 4 promise the paradigm of formally verified mathematics, but large-scale formalization projects have faced major barriers to entry, including the need for expertise in formal verification (as well as the underlying mathematics) and the significant time required for writing formal proofs. AI coding agents have dramatically reduced these barriers; human users can now use natural language to prompt agents to write complex proofs in Lean. This opens up the intriguing possibility of internet-scale mathematical collaboration involving both humans and AI agents, where correctness is machine-checked.
  To realize this possibility, we introduce Prove2Me (https://prove2.me), an open collaborative platform for formalizing mathematics. Users launch formalization "missions", to which AI agents contribute formal proofs toward completion. We designed mechanisms and a specialized harness in Prove2Me that enable large-scale collaboration so that agents can build on one another's work and freely reuse existing results. In doing so, Prove2Me aims to turn math formalization into a scalable, crowd-sourced effort open to anyone with an agent.

</details>


### [166] [Learning to Use Tools: Reinforcement Learning for Tool-Integrated Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28447)
*Minghui Xu,Zi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tool-integrated LLMs with a calculator for Countdown; SFT + RL with tool-use policies; 1,024-problem held-out benchmark; Tool-DAPO strongest; ~10-point gains; tool reduces arithmetic errors; RL improves reasoning traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Arithmetic errors are a major failure mode; external calculators and RL-guided tool use can improve reliability and verification of solutions.

Method: Create tool-use formatted data; supervise fine-tuning; apply several on-policy RL methods (RLOO, RLOO++, GRPO, DAPO); use automatically verifiable final-answer rewards; evaluate on held-out Countdown benchmark of 1,024 problems.

Result: Consistent improvements with tool integration across SFT and RL baselines; about 10 percentage points in pass@k; Tool-DAPO raises pass@1 from 35.8% to 66.0%; RL encourages better tool use even with final-answer rewards.

Conclusion: Integrating a calculator tool reduces arithmetic and verification errors; RL enhances correct reasoning traces; combining tool use with RL yields strong performance gains on mathematical reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Current large language models (LLMs) increasingly benefit from external tool integration, especially for tasks requiring reliable computation and verification. Motivated by this, we study calculator tool calling for improving mathematical reasoning on the Countdown task. We first analyze reasoning failures and find that calculation errors account for a substantial portion of incorrect responses. We then construct supervised fine-tuning datasets to teach the model useful tool-use patterns and how to interpret returned outputs. Building on this tool-formatted policy, we apply several on-policy reinforcement learning methods, including RLOO, RLOO++, GRPO, and DAPO, using automatically verifiable final-answer rewards. To enable a more reliable evaluation, we construct a fresh 1,024-problem held-out Countdown benchmark with no exact overlap with the training data. Our results show that calculator tool integration consistently improves both SFT and RL baselines, yielding roughly 10 percentage-point gains across pass@k. Among the RL methods, Tool-DAPO achieves the strongest performance, improving pass@1 from 35.8% for Tool-SFT to 66.0%. Further analysis shows that RL encourages more effective tool use even when only final-answer rewards are provided. These findings suggest that tool integration reduces arithmetic and verification errors, while RL increases the probability of correct reasoning traces.

</details>


### [167] [COVER: Identifiable Evaluation of Coalition Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28475)
*Raghul Sugumar,Amrit Gopinath*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COVER provides an auditable framework to quantify routing effects in multi-agent systems by fixing public information boundaries and team families, enabling exact finite-benchmark oracle regret; empirical results show selective improvements in regret across multiple datasets without implying universal routing superiority.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To distinguish routing-induced effects from end-to-end accuracy changes in dynamic team compositions; address the difficulty that routing changes can confound measurement of agent performance.

Method: Define an evaluation contract with fixed boundary G and legal team family; complete coverage to identify exact finite-benchmark oracle regret conditional on G; union of frozen policies yields minimal assumption-free support for pairwise policy contrasts; two controlled tests; tests across MuSiQue-12 and HotpotQA-4; fixed-stack Llama; ToolSandbox with five families; crossed-stack diagnostic; pre-specified controls and retrospective comparators to validate measurement.

Result: Regret improvements observed: MuSiQue-12: baseline 0.532 to 0.402 with privileged control; later public-interface control 0.424 vs 0.554 (retrospective); HotpotQA-4: 0.313 to 0.110; fixed-stack Llama: route regret improved by 0.190, raw-answer gain 0.010 (interval crosses zero); ToolSandbox: declared-family oracle 0.768 safe-evidence completion; prospective router 0.637 (regret 0.131), failing the 0.10 criterion; retrospective comparator 0.655, workers 4.57 vs 5.00; cross-stack: absolute scores depend on G, no detectable router-by-finalizer interaction.

Conclusion: COVER is an auditable measurement methodology that reveals selection headroom without claiming stack-invariant or universal agent-routing superiority; emphasizes boundary-conditioned, stack-aware evaluation and reproducibility.

Abstract: When a multi-agent system changes its team, it also changes the messages and final answer it produces, so an end-to-end accuracy gap does not by itself identify a routing effect. We introduce method, an evaluation contract that fixes a public information boundary, downstream stack G, and finite legal team family before outcomes are generated. Complete coverage identifies exact finite-benchmark oracle regret conditional on that stack. For any finite collection of frozen policies, executing the union of their distinct selected teams is the minimal assumption-free support for every pairwise policy contrast, though not for absolute oracle regret. Two controlled tables with source-ID-disjoint splits test the instrument. On MuSiQue-12, a pre-specified privileged positive control improves regret from 0.532 to 0.402; a later public-interface control reaches 0.424 versus 0.554 but is retrospective. On HotpotQA-4, a pre-specified public direct scorer improves regret from 0.313 to 0.110. In fixed-stack Llama execution, verified route regret improves by 0.190, while the raw-answer gain is 0.010 with an interval crossing zero. A five-family ToolSandbox variant-shift validation exhaustively evaluates 16 declared teams on 14 untouched task variants (224/224 valid rows): the declared-family oracle reaches 0.768 safe-evidence completion, while the prospectively frozen router gets 0.637 (regret 0.131), failing the predeclared 0.10 criterion. A later retrospective comparator reaches 0.655, matching all-workers with 4.57 versus 5.00 workers on average. Thus COVER exposes selection headroom without manufacturing a routing win. A crossed-stack diagnostic shows absolute scores depend on G but finds no detectable router-by-finalizer interaction. COVER is an auditable measurement methodology, not a claim of stack-invariant or universal agent-routing superiority.

</details>


### [168] [Training Communication-Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Language Models with Layer Re-Configuration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28511)
*Simeng Sun,Roger Waleffe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CE-MoE: a heterogeneous MoE design that decouples token-mixing and channel-mixing to reduce communication overhead; concentrates expert capacity in a subset of routed MoE layers while using additional token-mixing/dense-FFN layers to preserve depth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the substantial end-to-end training time caused by all-to-all token dispatch/collectives in MoE models by reducing communication while preserving performance.

Method: Introduce a heterogeneous layer pattern that decouples token-mixing depth from channel-mixing (expert) depth. Concentrate expert capacity in a few routed MoE layers and add non-expert layers for token-mixing and dense FFN to maintain depth. Compare against conventional interleaved MoE architectures across a scale from 2B to 31.5B parameters, under matched total and activated parameter budgets.

Result: CE-MoE consistently reduces training cost while matching validation loss and downstream benchmarks with full-MoE baselines. At 31.5B scale, it uses 33.3% fewer GPU-hours and achieves improved average downstream score and inference throughput.

Conclusion: A communication-efficient MoE design can achieve substantial training-time savings without degrading accuracy or downstream performance by reordering and partitioning responsibilities across layers and concentrating routing capacity in fewer MoE layers.

Abstract: When training Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models with expert parallelism, all-to-all token dispatch and combine collectives can consume a substantial fraction of end-to-end training time. In this work, we study communication-efficient MoE models (CE-MoE), in which we adopt a heterogeneous layer pattern that decouples token-mixing and channel-mixing depth. Compared to conventional models which interleave MoE layers after each token-mixing layer (e.g., attention, Mamba-2), CE-MoE models concentrate expert capacity in a select few routed MoE layers, while maintaining depth by adding additional token-mixing and dense-FFN layers. Across a scaling ladder from 2B to 31.5B total parameters, under matched total and activated parameters, CE-MoE models consistently reduce training cost while matching validation loss and downstream benchmarks with full-MoE baselines. At the 31.5B scale, CE-MoE uses 33.3\% fewer GPU-hours while improving average downstream score and inference throughput.

</details>


### [169] [InstructMesh: Selective Refinement of Generative 3D Models for Fabrication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28534)
*Faraz Faruqi,Ahmed Katary,Demircan Tas,Theresa Hradilak,Ning Zhang,Jiaji Li,Fabian Manhardt,Martin Nisser,Vrushank Phadnis,Ruofei Du,Federico Tombari,Megan Hofmann,Stefanie Mueller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InstructMesh is a post-generation refinement tool for generative 3D models, enabling region-based repairs in latent space via region selection and operations (opening/sealing voids, adjusting thickness) with natural language prompts or sliders; two user studies show novices can perform fabrication-relevant repairs and prefer hybrid NL+slider interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the mismatch between visual plausibility and geometric accuracy in generative 3D models, which leads to fabrication-flawed outputs; provide accessible, robust geometric corrections for non-expert users.

Method: An interactive tool (InstructMesh) that edits intermediate latent representations through region-based operations (open/close voids, thickness adjustments); supports natural language prompts and sliders for control; analyzes common fabrication-related failure modes; conducts two user studies to evaluate usability and repair capability, focusing on novices and interface preferences.

Result: Demonstrates that novices can identify fabrication-relevant flaws and perform repairs; users prefer hybrid interfaces that combine sliders with natural language input.

Conclusion: InstructMesh enables robust geometric corrections of generative 3D outputs without expert modeling, improving fabrication viability and accessibility; future work could expand operation types, automate flaw diagnosis, and evaluate impact on downstream fabrication outcomes.

Abstract: Recent advances in generative AI allow users to create 3D models from text or images. However, these models prioritize visual plausibility over geometric accuracy, often generating results with flaws that compromise their intended use post-fabrication. We present InstructMesh, an interactive post-generation refinement tool that enables selective repair of generative 3D models through region selection and targeted operations, such as opening or sealing voids, or adjusting local thickness. Users can invoke edit operations via natural language prompts or slider controls. By operating directly on the intermediate latent representation, InstructMesh allows users to apply robust geometric corrections without requiring expert modeling skills. To inform our design, we first analyze common fabrication-related failure modes in outputs from state-of-the-art generative tools. We then conduct two user studies, demonstrating that novices can identify and perform fabrication-relevant repairs on generative outputs using InstructMesh, and revealing user preference for hybrid interfaces that combine slider controls with natural language input.

</details>


### [170] [Logos: An Agent Harness on a Cross-Process Bus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28553)
*Hanzhang Jia,Liheng Zeng,Hao Cheng,Yi Gao,Bo Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study of spatiotemporal-composability calculus for dynamic agent composition; introduces Logos, a ROS-like cross-process agent framework where plugins are processes and shared state is an append-only transcript; evaluates fault-tolerance across process boundaries with multi-process vs single-process configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address fragility of single-process shared-context models and enable safe cross-process agent composition under stateless language-model inference.

Method: Derives four lemmas from calculus premises and language-model statelessness; constructs Logos with plugins as processes and an append-only transcript as the sole shared state; evaluates fault-resilience by running eight sessions and comparing fault isolation between peer-process and single-process configurations.

Result: Eight sessions resume with no repeated effects after kills at tool-call cycle boundaries; in single-process config, a single fault interrupts all co-resident sessions; in the peer-process design, a fault affects only the node it occurs on.

Conclusion: Stateless inference and cross-process plugin architecture can enhance fault isolation and resilience; the Logos framework demonstrates practical viability for cross-process agent harnessing, though deeper formalization and performance analysis are needed.

Abstract: Modern agent systems assemble capabilities at runtime, and this dynamic composition has recently received a complete formal treat ment in the spatiotemporal-composability calculus, in which a capability is a component carrying a tracked inverse, and agents are assembled as plugins. This plugin form is carried by a single process sharing one context, a carrier that places all components in one physical failure domain, a fault suspends every component at once, and process death interrupts every session the process hosts. This paper shows that neither the modeling nor the calculus binds an agent to one process, the statelessness of the language model keeps all cross-step state outside the model, and the soundness invariant is defined on the state space alone. These observations condense into four lemmas whose premises are the hypotheses of the calculus and the statelessness of language-model inference. On these lemmas this paper constructs Logos, a ROS-like cross process agent harness in which a plugin is a process and the only shared state is an append-only transcript. Eighty sessions resume with no repeated effect after kills placed at the four boundaries of the tool-call cycle, and a same-fault comparison with a single process reference configuration shows one fault interrupting every co-resident session while under the peer-process construction one fault ends at one node.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [171] [Marginal Coverage Credit Reduces Redundant Exploration in Parallel State-Entropy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27507)
*Junhao Cao,Hongyi Xia,Jianian Wu,Xiaopeng Yi,Lixia Huang,Ping Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MCC-PGPSE introduces policy-specific marginal credits to distribute auxiliary rewards in parallel state-entropy maximization, preserving the original pooled objective while reducing redundancy and promoting complementary coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PGPSE expands coverage via parallel policies but its pooled team-entropy cannot identify non-redundant contributions; there is a need for interpretable, policy-specific credit to encourage diverse coverage.

Method: Compute leave-one-policy-out coverage to estimate policy-specific credit, combine with state-owner specialization, and redistribute non-negative auxiliary intrinsic rewards according to these credits without changing total reward mass, preserving the pooled objective.

Result: MCC-PGPSE yields positive final window gains in normalized team state entropy and state support over an entropy baseline across controlled tasks, seven public discrete-state benchmarks, and Room/Maze settings; several comparisons are statistically significant; ablations indicate gains mainly arise from leave-one-policy-out coverage rather than credit weightings or neural novelty.

Conclusion: Contribution-conditioned auxiliary rewards provide an interpretable mechanism to improve complementary coverage among parallel policies in discrete state spaces, without altering the overall reward budget.

Abstract: Policy Gradient for Parallel State Entropy maximization (PGPSE) expands state-space coverage by training independently parameterized policies in replicated copies of the same environment. However, its pooled team-entropy score measures only collective exploration and cannot identify policies that contribute non-redundant coverage. We introduce Marginal Coverage Credit for PGPSE (MCC-PGPSE), which combines leave-one-policy-out coverage with state-owner specialization to estimate policy-specific credit. MCC-PGPSE preserves PGPSE's pooled objective and redistributes non-negative auxiliary intrinsic rewards according to these credits without changing their total mass. This redistribution is designed to discourage redundant visitation and promote complementary coverage. We evaluated MCC-PGPSE in controlled environments, seven public discrete-state benchmarks, and representative Room and Maze settings from the original PGPSE protocol. Across all tested settings, MCC-PGPSE produced positive final window gains in normalized team state entropy and state support over the Entropy baseline. Controlled-task comparisons and the fixed-suite public aggregate were significant, whereas five-seed original-protocol comparisons were directionally consistent. Ablations and credit alignment controls indicate that most gains arise from leave-one-policy-out coverage rather than non-uniform weighting, mismatched credit, or neural novelty alone. These results support contribution-conditioned auxiliary reward allocation as an interpretable approach to improving complementary coverage among parallel policies in discrete state spaces.

</details>


### [172] [Quantization-Triggered Backdoors in Language Models: Cross-Quantizer Transferability and the Validation--Deployment Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27512)
*Jacopo Dardini,Claudio Stanzione,Giordano Colò,Giuseppe Fenza*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quantization can change model behavior; QBECs show source-precision checks do not guarantee deployed behavior; introduces quantization-triggered backdoors; empirical threats in multilingual MT and political content analysis; calls for end-to-end behavioral certification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between source-precision validation and deployed quantized models; establish a formal framework (QBECs) and a practical adversarial attack to expose quantization-induced behavioral changes and risks.

Method: Formalize Quantization Behavioral Equivalence Classes (QBECs); prove non-implication to behavioral equivalence; three-stage adversarial fine-tuning to embed payloads that evade FP16 checks; evaluate on encoder-decoder LMs in MT and stance classification; perform cross-quantizer transferability across schemes and architectures.

Result: Backdoors activate after INT8 or 4-bit quantization; translation corruption up to 85.02% whereas FP16 showed near-zero; stance classifier bias shift ΔBias=0.33; transferability varies by quantization scheme and model architecture; demonstrates risk of relying on source-precision auditing.

Conclusion: Auditing should include the final deployed quantized configuration; establish behavioral certification for edge AI; mitigate quantization-induced backdoors; consider cross-quantizer risk.

Abstract: Post-training quantization is often treated as a semantically neutral optimization for edge deployment of Large Language Models. When a full-precision source checkpoint is evaluated and quantization is applied downstream without equivalent re-evaluation, this workflow creates a structural validation--deployment gap: because quantization is a many-to-one mapping over parameter space, source-precision certification does not guarantee behavioral equivalence in the deployed configuration. We formalize this gap through Quantization Behavioral Equivalence Classes (QBECs) and prove that QBEC membership does not imply behavioral equivalence, providing a theoretical basis for quantization-triggered backdoor attacks. Building on a three-stage adversarial fine-tuning framework, we embed latent malicious payloads into models that satisfy the source-precision checks used in our evaluation, yet activate targeted adversarial behavior upon INT8 or 4-bit compression. We evaluate this threat in two operationally motivated scenarios, tactical machine translation and political content analysis, extending prior work from decoder-only causal LMs to multilingual encoder-decoder sequence-to-sequence models. Results show that backdoored translation models move from zero measured friend--foe corruption at repaired FP16 to up to 85.02% inversion after quantization, and that a paired stance classifier measures an ideological shift of up to $Δ\mathrm{Bias}=0.33$ upon compression. A cross-quantizer transferability analysis further shows that attack persistence varies across quantization schemes and model architectures, rather than being determined by nominal bit-width alone. These findings demonstrate that source-precision auditing alone does not rule out quantization-triggered behavior and that the final deployed configuration must be included in behavioral certification for trustworthy edge AI.

</details>


### [173] [DAMP: Decay-Aware Mixed-Precision Recurrent-State Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27513)
*Tao Zhang,Jianchao Tan,Pingwei Sun,Yanqi Yu,Zixu Jiang,Yuchen Xie,Xunliang Cai,Ziqian Zeng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DAMP applies post-training mixed-precision quantization to recurrent states in GDN/KDA models, achieving large memory/storage reductions with minimal accuracy loss on reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Softmax attention-based KV caches grow with sequence length and FP32 recurrent states are memory- and bandwidth-bound during decoding; prior work lacks robust post-training quantization for these states.

Method: Offline calibration identifies high-risk channels via quantization-error energy and decay-based persistence; store high-risk channels at higher precision (beyond INT8) and remaining channels in INT8, achieving about 9.9 bits per state value; evaluate on Qwen3.6-35B and Kimi-Linear-48B across six benchmarks.

Result: INT8/FP8 quantization degrades accuracy on complex tasks; INT4/NVFP4 nearly collapses performance. DAMP improves memory savings by 69.1%, accelerates recurrent-state updates up to 2.01x, and reduces full-model TPOT by up to 10.9%, with accuracy close to FP32 baseline at 9.9 bits per state.

Conclusion: Channel-aware mixed-precision quantization guided by energy and persistence is effective for post-training quantization of recurrent states in GDN/KDA models, enabling substantial memory and latency reductions while preserving accuracy on diverse reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Softmax attention stores key and value vectors for every preceding token, causing inference memory to grow with sequence length. Recent language models incorporating Gated DeltaNet (GDN) or Kimi Delta Attention (KDA) reduce this cost by replacing the KV cache in most layers with fixed-size recurrent states. However, these recurrent states are commonly stored in FP32 and consume substantial GPU memory; their updates are memory-bandwidth bound and contribute significantly to decoding latency. To our knowledge, we are the first to study post-training quantization of recurrent states in GDN and KDA based language models. We find that uniform quantization provides a poor accuracy--storage trade-off: INT8 and FP8 already degrade accuracy on complex reasoning tasks, while INT4 and NVFP4 reduce it to near zero. We further find that most quantization-error energy is concentrated in a small subset of channels and that the relative decay strength of state channels remains stable across prompts and tasks. Motivated by these findings, DAMP uses both quantization-error energy and decay-based persistence to identify high-risk channels during offline calibration. It stores these channels at higher precision and the remainder in INT8. We evaluate DAMP on Qwen3.6-35B and Kimi-Linear-48B across six benchmarks covering mathematical reasoning, general reasoning, and code generation. At 9.9 bits per state value, DAMP maintains average accuracy close to the FP32 baseline. DAMP reduces recurrent-state storage by 69.1%, accelerates the recurrent-state update kernel by up to 2.01x, and lowers full-model TPOT by up to 10.9%.

</details>


### [174] [A Deeper Analysis of Block-Sparse Featurizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27515)
*Alexandru-Iulius Jerpelea,Amith Ananthram*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BSF generalizes sparse autoencoders by using block subspaces as atoms, suitable for low-dimensional manifolds common in vision; it still exhibits SAE-like failure modes (feature splitting, composition); introduces Tournament Top-K to mitigate feature splitting and extends the block idea to the crosscoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand the strengths and weaknesses of block-sparse featurizers (BSF) relative to SAEs on vision-like data, identify failure modes, and propose architectural improvements to enhance representation quality.

Method: Empirical/analytical examination of BSF’s behavior on low-dimensional manifold features; propose architectural changes including a Tournament Top-K selection rule; extend the block paradigm to a crosscoder; assess impact on feature splitting and composition.

Result: Tournament Top-K significantly reduces feature splitting; the BSF’s fundamental strengths are preserved while mitigating some SAE-like failure modes; the block paradigm is extended to the crosscoder.

Conclusion: BSF remains a promising approach for manifold-valued features in vision, but still experiences SAE-like failures; architectural enhancements like Tournament Top-K and crosscoder extension can alleviate key weaknesses and broaden applicability.

Abstract: The recently introduced block-sparse featurizer (BSF; Fel et al., 2026) is similar to a sparse autoencoder (SAE), but its atomic unit is a small subspace (a block of directions) rather than a single direction. It is designed for features that live on low-dimensional manifolds, which are especially frequent in vision. This work studies the BSF's strengths and weaknesses, finding how it still somewhat suffers from classic SAE failure modes, like feature splitting and composition. We propose several architectural changes to the BSF, including a Tournament Top-K selection rule that significantly reduces feature splitting, and we also extend the block paradigm to the crosscoder.

</details>


### [175] [When Muon Meets Task Interference: A Spectral Perspective on Continual Learning and Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27518)
*Shangge Liu,Yuehan Yin,Yinghuan Shi,Lei Wang,Wenbin Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Task interference is the unifying cause of performance degradation in continual learning (CL) and model merging (MM); a layer-wise update that benefits one task can harm another, captured by a shared Frobenius inner product. The spectral norm of updates is a key optimizer-controllable factor, and the Muon optimizer mitigates interference by constraining this norm, yielding strong empirical gains across CL and MM benchmarks and backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CL and MM address related but distinct failure modes (catastrophic forgetting vs. weight-disentanglement). The authors argue these are instances of a single phenomenon—task interference—driven by parameter updates that shift outputs across tasks. Understanding this commonality through an optimizer-sensitive bound on interference clarifies how optimizers influence multi-task performance.

Method: Formalize interference as the layer-wise Frobenius inner product ⟨ΔW_ℓ, J_ℓ(x)⟩_F. Derive an upper bound isolating the spectral norm ‖ΔW_ℓ‖_2 as an optimizer-controllable factor. Perform per-mode analysis to show the bound tracks the dominant interference component. Identify Muon as a mechanism that constrains this spectral norm and thus reduces interference. Validate theoretically with a derived bound and empirically across multiple CL and MM benchmarks.

Result: Replacing AdamW with Muon yields up to +5.02 accuracy points on an eight-task model-merging benchmark across three CLIP backbones. In continual learning, Muon provides uniformly positive gains across ten class-incremental, three task-incremental protocols, and the 11-task MTIL benchmark.

Conclusion: Task interference underlies both CL and MM and can be mitigated by optimizer design.
