By constraining the spectral norm of parameter updates, Muon tightens the interference bound and improves multi-task performance, making it a principled, optimizer-centric complement to existing strategies.

Abstract: Continual learning (CL) and model merging (MM) both aim to obtain a single model that performs well across multiple tasks, challenged respectively by catastrophic forgetting and weight-disentanglement error. In the literature, these difficulties are merely treated separately and mitigated through a variety of solutions, while the geometry induced by the base optimizer is treated as an implementation detail. In this work, we show that the two difficulties are in fact two instances of the same phenomenon: a parameter update useful for one task shifts the model's outputs on another. We formalize this shared phenomenon as \textit{task interference} and reduce it to a common layer-wise Frobenius inner product $\langle ΔW_\ell, J_\ell(x)\rangle_F$. This quantity, in turn, is utilized to expose the role of the optimizer. We theoretically derive an upper bound that isolates the spectral norm $\|ΔW_\ell\|_2$ as an optimizer-controllable factor of task interference, and a per-mode analysis shows that this bound tracks the dominant part of the empirical interference. Specifically, we then identify the recent Muon optimizer as a mechanism that regulates this factor by construction. Our work reveals that its elegant control on spectral norm tightens the interference bound for both CL and MM, positioning Muon as a principled optimizer-centric approach complementary to existing solutions. Our theoretcal analysis is well validated by experimental results. Replacing the AdamW optimizer with Muon improves accuracy by up to +5.02 points on the eight-task model-merging benchmark across three CLIP backbones. For continual learning, Muon also delivers uniformly positive gains across ten class-incremental protocols, three task-incremental protocols, and the 11-task MTIL benchmark.

</details>


### [176] [Dandelion: A Spherical Flower for Neural Simulation of Planetary Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27521)
*Till Muser,Giovanni Abati,Ivan Dokmanić*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dandelion is a warp-based spherical neural PDE solver that avoids convolutions by predicting tangent-plane displacements and transporting features along great circles; it uses hierarchical pooling in the spherical-harmonic domain to form a U-Net-like architecture; evaluated on a new native-sphere PDE benchmark where it ranks top or near top, with stronger performance at higher resolutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address fundamental limitations of Euclidean neural architectures for spherical dynamics (grid-distortions at high latitudes, misassumptions about periodicity in Fourier operators, geodesic distance distortions in Cartesian encodings) by developing native spherical primitives for PDEs and providing a challenging, scalable benchmark.

Method: Warp-based architecture (no convolutions): predict tangent-plane displacements and transport features along great circles. Hierarchical pooling is implemented entirely in the spherical-harmonic domain to yield a U-Net-like structure. Spatial mixing occurs via spherical coordinate changes (warps). Evaluation against existing spherical architectures using a new benchmark suite of native spherical PDE tasks.

Result: Dandelion achieves top-2 performance on all benchmark datasets across single-step prediction and rollouts; at 256×512 resolution, it and Flower2D occupy the top two slots, with the performance gap to non-warp baselines widening with resolution.

Conclusion: Warp-based, non-convolutional spherical solvers with operations in the spherical-harmonic domain can outperform convolution-based spherical models. The paper introduces a challenging, native-sphere PDE benchmark to drive progress and demonstrates strong scalability and accuracy, particularly at higher resolutions.

Abstract: Many dynamical processes unfold on the sphere but the default scientific machine learning architectures are Euclidean. Applying these architectures on a regular lat-lon grid causes problems: Cartesian convolutions become distorted at high latitude; 2D FFTs in Fourier neural operators incorrectly assume double periodicity; Cartesian positional encodings in ViTs distort spherical geodesic distances. Recent work moves towards natively spherical primitives, including spherical convolutions (e.g., DeepSphere or DISCO), Spherical Fourier Neural Operators (SFNOs), and geodesic attention. Here we propose Dandelion, a spherical version of Flower, a warp-based neural PDE solver. Layers of Dandelion predict a tangent-plane displacement and transport features along great circles. We obtain a U-Net-like structure by implementing hierarchical pooling entirely in the spherical-harmonic domain. There are thus no convolutions: spatial mixing is achieved only through spherical coordinate changes, or warps. To compare Dandelion with existing spherical architectures, we release an evolving benchmark suite of challenging, natively-spherical PDE datasets including a modified Galewsky jet, anomalous chained turbulence, Cahn-Hilliard decomposition, spherical Riemann shocks, Held-Suarez dry atmospheric transport and global ocean dynamics. This new benchmark fills the gap in existing spherical datasets which are either too small and stylized, or much too large (ERA5) for model iteration. Dandelion is best or second-best on every dataset, and the gap to non-warp baselines widens with resolution: at $256\times 512$, Dandelion and Flower2D occupy the top two slots in both single-step prediction and rollout.

</details>


### [177] [Self-Explainable Multi-Label Graph Neural Network for Correlated Evidence Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27574)
*Yingqi Feng,Yufei Tang,Min Shi,Xingquan Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unifies multi-label graph learning with end-to-end self-explanation by jointly training a multi-label GNN and a sparse edge-mask explainer, leveraging label correlations to improve predictions and provide label-conditioned explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-label graph learners lack training-time interpretability; post-hoc explanations do not capture label-dependent evidence sharing, especially for weak or negative label associations.

Method: Proposes SEMGNN, an end-to-end model that simultaneously performs multi-label node classification and learns a sparse edge-mask that highlights edges contributing to each target label, with joint optimization. Uses label-label correlations to guide both predictions and explanations.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real-world multi-label networks (social, entertainment, life sciences) show competitive or improved predictive performance and more faithful, compact explanations compared to baselines.

Conclusion: SEMGNN provides a faithful, compact, label-conditioned explanation mechanism within an end-to-end framework, addressing limitations of post-hoc explainers and enabling better understanding of label dependencies.

Abstract: Multi-label graph learning intends to capture the intrinsic complexity of real-world applications, where one sample is often related to multiple groups or consists of multiple objects. To date, a handful of multi-label graph learning methods exist, but none of them integrate training-time interpretation capability. While post-hoc graph explainers have been developed, they do not explicitly model label-dependent evidence sharing in multi-label graph learners, especially when label pairs are weakly or negatively associated. As a result, post-hoc approaches may miss how evidence should be shared or separated across different labels. This paper advances a new end-to-end self-explainable multi-label graph neural network (SEMGNN), which aims to simultaneously classify multi-labeled nodes and identify edges significantly contributing to each target node w.r.t. predicted labels. Different from post-hoc methods, SEMGNN jointly learns a predictor and a sparse edge-mask explainer within a unified framework and training objective. Label-label correlations are used to improve multi-label node classification and enhance individual label explanations, so that different labels of a node can be supported by distinct yet coherent structural and/or correlated evidence. Experiments and comparisons on synthetic and real-world multi-label networks, in social networking, entertainment, and life sciences, show that SEMGNN achieves competitive or improved predictive performance while providing more faithful and compact label-conditioned explanations.

</details>


### [178] [Curvature-Aware Radius Shrinkage for Adaptive Nearest Neighbor Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27634)
*Alexandre L. M. Levada*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CARSANN adaptively shrinks neighborhood radii based on local curvature to improve nearest-neighbor classification across manifolds, using TwoNN for intrinsic dimensionality, PCA for intrinsic representation, and a shape-operator-based mean-curvature estimate; it shows consistent gains over standard k-NN and competitive with adaptive NN methods on 70 OpenML datasets, with notable gains in balanced accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitation of fixed neighborhood size in k-NN across heterogeneous local geometries, by introducing a curvature-informed radius adaptation mechanism.

Method: Estimate intrinsic dimensionality with TwoNN; construct an intrinsic representation via PCA; estimate local mean curvature using a shape-operator formulation; adapt each point's neighborhood radius inversely with curvature (stronger shrinkage in highly curved regions); evaluate on real-world data against standard k-NN and adaptive NN baselines; use Friedman and Nemenyi tests for statistical significance.

Result: CARSANN consistently improves over standard k-NN and competes with adaptive NN methods across 70 OpenML datasets; in a controlled comparison with the same base neighborhood size, it achieves higher balanced accuracy on 40 of 45 datasets, increasing mean balanced accuracy from 0.6506 to 0.7528; also outperforms fixed-k=5 k-NN; results are statistically significant per Friedman and Nemenyi tests.

Conclusion: Local manifold curvature can serve as an effective geometric control variable for adapting neighborhood support, providing a complementary paradigm to cardinality-based approaches in nearest-neighbor classification.

Abstract: Nearest neighbor classification relies fundamentally on how locality is defined, yet conventional $k$-NN imposes the same neighborhood cardinality throughout the feature space. This assumption can be inadequate for data whose local geometry varies substantially across the underlying manifold. We introduce Curvature-Aware Radius Shrinkage for Adaptive Nearest Neighbor Classification (CARSANN), a geometry-driven framework that adapts the spatial support of each neighborhood according to local geometric complexity. CARSANN first estimates intrinsic dimensionality using TwoNN and constructs an intrinsic representation through principal component analysis. Local mean curvature is then estimated using a shape-operator-based formulation and controls neighborhood scale: highly curved regions receive stronger radius shrinkage, whereas approximately flat regions retain broader spatial support. Unlike methods that modify only the number of neighbors or the local metric, CARSANN explicitly adapts the spatial extent of local evidence. Experiments on more than 70 real-world OpenML datasets show that CARSANN consistently improves upon standard $k$-NN and is competitive with adaptive nearest-neighbor methods. In a controlled comparison using the same base neighborhood size, CARSANN achieves higher balanced accuracy on 40 of 45 datasets, increasing mean balanced accuracy from 0.6506 to 0.7528. The advantage also persists against $k$-NN with fixed $k=5$. Friedman and Nemenyi tests confirm that the improvements are statistically significant. These results indicate that local manifold curvature can serve as an effective geometric control variable for adapting neighborhood support, providing a complementary paradigm to cardinality-based nearest-neighbor adaptation.

</details>


### [179] [More Data Cannot Break a Symmetry: Identifiability by Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27651)
*Jing Xu,Christopher Kanan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unsupervised representational alignment is bounded by the automorphism group of the stimulus geometry; a design-time diagnostic based on relabeling shows that symmetric designs can lock the alignment, while asymmetric color-geometry choices unlock it. Selecting nine colors (geometry-aware) dramatically reduces catastrophic alignment failures (75% → 2%) across models, layers, N, and solver settings. Discrimination between representations and recovering a correspondence are nearly uncorrelated (r ≈ -0.02 over 3,000 subsets). The diagnostic costs a single function call and generalizes to other regular designs and isometry groups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To prevent degeneracies in unsupervised representational alignment caused by stimulus-geometry symmetries (automorphism groups) and to provide a simple, design-time diagnostic that guides data collection and model evaluation without relying on learned representations.

Method: Analyze the impact of geometry-induced symmetries by comparing symmetric vs. asymmetric color-geometry designs. Use a closed-form color geometry to illustrate degeneracy, quantify how relabelings (isometries) affect alignment, and evaluate across multiple model subsets. Measure recovery of stimulus-by-stimulus correspondence, the correlation between discriminating representations and alignment, and the effect of limiting restart budget. Identify a minimal color set (nine colors) that avoids the degenerate point.

Result: Symmetric designs can remain stationary under large restart budgets, while asymmetric designs at the same sample size recover the intended correspondence. The discriminability of models and the recovered alignment are essentially uncorrelated (r ≈ -0.02 over 3,000 subsets). Selecting nine colors moves representations away from the degenerate point and reduces catastrophic alignment failures from ~75% to ~2% with fixed model configurations.

Conclusion: Discrimination and alignment are largely uncorrelated; a lightweight, design-time color-geometry diagnostic can avert degeneracies by steering data collection away from the stimulus geometry’s isometry group. The check costs one function evaluation and applies broadly to any regular design interacting with isometry groups (orientations, tones, motion directions).

Abstract: Unsupervised representational alignment recovers a stimulus-by-stimulus correspondence from geometry alone, but the automorphism group of the stimulus geometry bounds what any such alignment can identify, before data exist. The obvious diagnostic for this degeneracy, the cheapest non-identity relabelling, ranks two published designs in the wrong order, because dense sampling creates near-duplicates whose transposition is nearly free. We turn this known invariance (Demetci et al., 2024) into a design-time diagnostic and intervention. In colour, where candidate geometries have closed form, we show that the failure is structural: sixty-four times the restart budget leaves a symmetric design unmoved while an asymmetric set at the same N recovers every time. Discriminating representational models and recovering a correspondence are essentially uncorrelated objectives (r = -0.02 over 3,000 subsets). Choosing nine colours by this diagnostic alone, without consulting any learned representation, moves all 93 model representations away from the degenerate point and cuts catastrophic alignment failures from 75% to 2% with the models, the layers, N and the solver all held fixed. The same risk arises wherever a regular design meets its candidate geometry's isometry group, including evenly spaced orientations, tones, or motion directions, and the check costs one function call before data collection.

</details>


### [180] [Unsupervised Continual Learning with Growing Self-Organizing Maps and Synthetic Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27662)
*Pujan Thapa,Alexander Ororbia,Travis Desell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fully unsupervised exemplar-free continual learning framework using growing self-organizing maps (GSOMs) augmented with learned distributional statistics and encoder-decoder models. It generates synthetic replay samples from per-unit statistics (via ancestral sampling) and decodes them when available, enabling class-incremental learning without storing raw data. The method achieves competitive performance with memory-based methods, outperforms memory-free baselines, and provides strong baselines for single-class incremental tasks, including TinyImageNet and MiniImageNet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address catastrophic forgetting in continual learning without raw data storage or explicit task boundaries, by leveraging distributional statistics stored per model unit and exemplar-free replay, enabling privacy-preserving, scalable, and unsupervised continual learning.

Method: Grow­ing Self-Organizing Maps (GSOMs) that, per unit, maintain mean, variance, and covariance estimates. These statistics form a distributional memory used to sample synthetic data for replay. In encoder-decoder configurations, synthetic samples are decoded back into input space via ancestral sampling. The training is fully unsupervised, with no reliance on task boundaries or class labels, and targets class-incremental learning with exemplar-free replay.

Result: Empirical results show competitive performance relative to supervised state-of-the-art memory-based methods, while consistently outperforming memory-free baselines. The approach matches or surpasses existing baselines in challenging single-class incremental scenarios and provides baseline results for single-class incremental TinyImageNet and MiniImageNet.

Conclusion: Unsupervised, adaptive, topology-driven neural statistics replay via GSOMs offers a scalable and flexible mechanism for continual learning, enabling effective replay without raw data storage and performing well across benchmarks, especially in single-class incremental settings.

Abstract: This work presents a generative continual learning framework based on growing self-organizing maps (GSOMs) that are augmented with learned distributional statistics as well as encoder-decoder models for class-incremental learning. The proposed approach enables exemplar-free replay using distributional statistical memory, which eliminates the need to store raw data. Each GSOM unit maintains its own mean, variance, and covariance estimates, which are subsequently used to generate synthetic samples for replay; in encoder-decoder configurations, these samples are then decoded back into the input space (via ancestral sampling) for subsequent training. Our method is fully unsupervised, as it does not rely on explicit task boundaries or class labels during training. Results across multiple benchmarks show that the proposed approach achieves performance competitive even with supervised state-of-the-art memory-based methods while consistently outperforming memory-free approaches. In several settings, our framework matches or exceeds existing baselines, particularly in challenging single-class incremental scenarios. We also provide baseline results for single-class incremental TinyImageNet and MiniImageNet, offering a useful reference for future work. This work highlights the effectiveness of an unsupervised, adaptive, topology-driven neural form of statistical replay as a scalable, flexible approach to continual learning.

</details>


### [181] [SegBench-GC: Testing Segmentation Invariance in Multi-Step Offline Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27678)
*Musa Shams*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SegBench-GC introduces a controlled stress test to study segmentation invariance in offline GCRL, using continuation-valid targets (CVT) to preserve continuation value across artificial trajectory cuts. Results show segmentation can materially affect learning outcomes across seeds and benchmarks; CVT mitigates to near-uncut performance but does not fully equal uncut results. The work provides a benchmark, isolation of failure modes, and cross-learner evidence that administrative segmentation influences multi-step offline GCRL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline GCRL relies on trajectory structure for multi-step targets; in practice, logged data may be segmented for administrative reasons that do not align with termination, potentially biasing learning. There is a need to quantify how segmentation boundaries affect learning and to develop benchmarks that isolate this failure mode.

Method: SegBench-GC constructs a controlled stress test with fixed transitions, source trajectories, goals, optimization settings, and evaluation, while varying only artificial backup boundaries and whether those boundaries retain continuation value. It uses Continuation-Valid Targets (CVT) to bootstrap from stored successors after an artificial cut, rather than stopping reward accumulation. The study varies segmentation realizations (three realizations), applies 35,000 artificial cuts, and evaluates with multiple optimization seeds across 50-episode tasks (PointMaze and Puzzle-4x5). It also compares an independent n-step baseline (n=25) from the Decoupled Q-Chunking codebase. Diagnostics include a target-level check of analytic vs numerical target difference and learned-critic analysis for optimism.

Result: Final 50-episode-per-task success under three segmentation conditions: 50.5% (uncut), 39.1% (CVT), 19.1% (absorbing). Across segmentation realizations, naive mean success ranges from 4.8% to 31.9%. Independent n-step baseline showed: 47.2% (uncut), 58.5% (CVT), 0.27% (naive). Target difference diagnostics align numerically; learned-critic diagnostics show substantial optimistic bias with naive handling, while CVT remains near-aligned with the uncut critic. CVT relies on standard continuation bootstrapping rather than a new Bellman rule. The contributions are the controlled benchmark, failure isolation, and cross-learner evidence that administrative segmentation materially affects multi-step offline GCRL.

Conclusion: SegBench-GC demonstrates that administrative segmentation of logged trajectories can significantly alter offline GCRL performance, with standard CVT-based continuation mitigating this effect but not eliminating it. The work provides a benchmark and diagnostic evidence that segmentation invariance is a real, actionable failure mode in offline GCRL across learners and tasks.

Abstract: Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning (GCRL) often uses trajectory structure for future-goal sampling and multi-step targets, yet logged trajectories may be partitioned for administrative reasons that do not correspond to termination. We introduce SegBench-GC, a controlled stress test of segmentation invariance that holds transitions, source trajectories, goal sampling, optimization settings, and evaluation fixed while varying only artificial backup boundaries and whether those boundaries retain continuation value. Continuation-valid targets (CVT) provide the segmentation-consistent control: reward accumulation stops at an artificial cut, but the target bootstraps from its stored successor. In a matched-count PointMaze study with 35,000 artificial cuts, three segmentation realizations, and three optimization seeds, final 50-episode-per-task success is 50.5% uncut, 39.1% with CVT, and 19.1% when the same cuts are treated as absorbing; across segmentation realizations, naive mean success ranges from 4.8% to 31.9%. An independent published n-step baseline (n=25) from the Decoupled Q-Chunking codebase shows the same failure on Puzzle-4x5: 47.2% uncut, 58.5% CVT, and 0.27% naive across three optimization seeds. A target-level diagnostic verifies the analytic target difference to numerical precision, and learned-critic diagnostics show a large optimistic shift under naive handling while CVT remains approximately aligned with the uncut critic. CVT applies standard continuation bootstrapping rather than a new Bellman rule; the contribution is the controlled benchmark, failure isolation, and cross-learner evidence that administrative segmentation can materially change multi-step offline GCRL.

</details>


### [182] [SafeStep: An Interactive Demonstration of Semantic Communication for Pedestrian Safety Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27688)
*Christian McDowell,Andrea Panebianco,Jeremiah Yang,Sirin Chakraborty,Samuel Chamoun,Travis Ross,Yin Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SafeStep is a browser-based semantic communication platform for live pedestrian safety monitoring; it benchmarks Meta-VIB against baselines, showing strong task-loss reductions and real-time AoI-aware behavior, with scalable performance across users and configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable real-time, configurable semantic communications for safety-critical vision tasks and study how Age of Information (AoI) affects downstream performance in live monitoring.

Method: Build SafeStep to extract pedestrian data from four live camera feeds, transmit via a semantic transceiver over AWGN with selectable transceiver, SNR, codelength, and AoI; render per-browser positions, trajectories, and risk labels; compare Meta-VIB (4.16M parameters, no online retraining) to five baselines across varying SNR/codelength/AoI; evaluate on live streaming to browsers.

Result: Meta-VIB achieves up to 92.1% mean task-loss reduction over baselines. On a high-end GPU server, the system sustains 5 frames/s for 20 concurrent users; with 100 users, no request failures and mean app response time < 1 s, though per-browser frame rate drops to ~1 fps. SafeStep uniquely demonstrates AoI-induced downstream degradation in real-time monitoring.

Conclusion: SafeStep is the first real-time semantic communication platform that explicitly observes AoI-induced performance degradation in live monitoring and demonstrates scalable, configurable semantic communication for safety-critical applications.

Abstract: In this paper, we develop SafeStep, an interactive browser-based semantic communication platform for live pedestrian safety monitoring. SafeStep extracts pedestrian information from four live traffic-camera feeds, transmits it through a semantic communication transceiver over an Additive White Gaussian Noise (AWGN) channel, and renders user-specific positions, trajectories, and risk labels. The platform allows to independently select the transceiver, Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR), codelength, and Age of Information (AoI), and demonstrates the transceiver performance of the selected configuration through live pedestrian safety monitoring to each browser. SafeStep compares a recently proposed semantic communication design called Meta-VIB with five baseline transceivers. Meta-VIB uses a compact neural model with only $4.16$ million parameters to generalize across varying SNR, codelength, and AoI values without online retraining. Experimental results show that Meta-VIB achieves mean task-loss reductions of up to $92.1\%$. On one high-end GPU server, the integrated concurrent-access workload maintains the target $5$ frames/s through $20$ users. At $100$ users, each requesting a distinct configuration, SafeStep records no request failures and a mean application response time below $1$ s, but its mean per-browser frame rate falls to approximately $1$ frame/s. To our knowledge, SafeStep is the first real-time semantic communication platform to make AoI-induced downstream degradation directly observable in live monitoring applications.

</details>


### [183] [RiskBlend: A Multi-Signal Framework for Test Input Prioritization in Machine Learning Regression Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27704)
*Madhusudan Srinivasan,Namith Nishal Raphae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RiskBlend combines four cross-version risk signals to prioritize regression faults after model updates, achieving higher APFD than baselines across diverse settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrained ML models can introduce regression faults that are costly to detect; traditional single-model confidence approaches miss cross-version dynamics, making efficient verification challenging.

Method: Classifier-agnostic framework that fuses four signals—historical failure patterns, prediction shift, decision-boundary shift, and neighborhood change—using validation-learned APFD-squared weighting. Evaluated across 4 datasets, 5 classifiers, 4 regression-update scenarios, and 15 seeds (1,200 configurations).

Result: RiskBlend yields the highest average APFD in all 80 dataset-classifier-scenario combinations, with up to 0.32 APFD improvement over the strongest baseline; confidence-based methods remain competitive mainly for linear models on sparse features; cross-version signals provide complementary information for regression fault prioritization.

Conclusion: Cross-version behavioral signals enhance regression fault prioritization; RiskBlend offers a classifier-agnostic, effective approach across diverse update scenarios, outperforming baselines on average.

Abstract: When machine learning classifiers are retrained, inputs correctly classified by the previous model version may be misclassified by the updated version, creating regression faults that are costly to detect because verifying predictions against ground truth may require human annotation, expert review, or expensive simulation rather than inexpensive model inference. Test input prioritization addresses this problem by ranking inputs so that a limited verification budget reveals as many regression faults as possible. Existing approaches rely predominantly on single-model confidence scores and do not exploit how predictions, decision boundaries, and local neighborhoods change between model versions. We propose RiskBlend, a classifier-agnostic prioritization framework that combines four complementary risk signals: historical failure patterns, prediction shift, decision-boundary shift, and neighborhood change. These signals are combined using validation-learned APFD-squared weighting. Across four datasets, five classifiers, four regression-update scenarios, and 15 random seeds, totaling 1,200 experimental configurations, RiskBlend achieves the highest average APFD in all 80 dataset-classifier-scenario combinations, with improvements of up to 0.32 APFD over the strongest baseline. Confidence-based methods remain competitive primarily for linear classifiers on sparse categorical features, which we attribute to feature-space geometry. The results show that cross-version behavioral signals provide important complementary information for prioritizing regression faults in machine learning systems.

</details>


### [184] [DART-FL: Burst-Aware Multitask Federated Learning under Dynamic Inference Demand at the Edge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27713)
*Yiming Xie,Pinrui Yu,Geng Yuan,Xue Lin,Ningfang Mi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DART-FL is an SLO-aware, demand-driven multitask federated learning framework that dynamically allocates inference and training resources and adjusts task loss weights to prioritize high-demand tasks, improving accuracy for frequently requested tasks while preserving overall multitask performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge intelligence on resource-constrained devices must satisfy inference SLAs while training across multiple tasks; static resource splits fail under time-varying demand, necessitating a dynamic, SLA-aware scheduling approach.

Method: At each scheduling interval, DART-FL computes the minimum inference-resource requirement from the inference backlog and profiled service capacity, then allocates the remaining training capacity across tasks using a queue-aware DPP-inspired scheduler; these allocations are mapped to dynamic loss weights. Clients train a shared backbone with task-specific heads, and FedAvg aggregates the multitask model. Evaluation uses Stanford Cars and Oxford Flowers 102 under synthetic and Alibaba trace-derived workloads.

Result: DART-FL dynamically adapts the inference-training resource split to time-varying demand and shifts learning progress of high-demand tasks toward their burst periods, improving accuracy for frequently requested tasks while maintaining comparable long-term multitask performance.

Conclusion: DART-FL effectively balances inference demands and training emphasis in edge multitask learning, demonstrating practical viability on real datasets and workload traces and enabling responsive resource management without sacrificing multitask performance.

Abstract: Edge intelligence systems increasingly require model training and online inference to coexist on resource-constrained devices, while inference demand can vary substantially across tasks over time. This creates two coupled challenges: sufficient computation must be reserved for inference to maintain service-level objectives (SLOs), while the remaining training capacity should adapt to task-specific demand so that frequently requested tasks can improve earlier during training.
  We propose an SLO-aware, demand-driven multitask federated learning framework (DART-FL) that jointly adapts the inference-training resource split and task-level training emphasis. At each scheduling interval, DART-FL uses the inference backlog and profiled service capacity to determine the minimum resource allocation required for inference. The remaining training capacity is then distributed across tasks using a queue-aware DPP-inspired scheduler, and the resulting task allocations are mapped to dynamic loss weights. This allows tasks experiencing higher inference demand to receive greater training emphasis in earlier communication rounds. Clients train a shared backbone with task-specific heads, and the complete multitask model is aggregated through FedAvg.
  We evaluate DART-FL using Stanford Cars and Oxford Flowers 102 under both synthetic and real Alibaba trace-derived workloads. Results show that DART-FL dynamically adapts the inference-training resource split to time-varying inference demand and shifts the learning progress of high-demand tasks toward their burst periods, improving model accuracy when those tasks are frequently requested while maintaining comparable long-term multitask performance.

</details>


### [185] [Beyond Non-IID: Learner--Client Distribution Mismatch in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27715)
*Yiming Xie,Lili Su,Ningfang Mi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A dynamic, influence-aware client selection method for federated learning uses a learner-specific proxy dataset to estimate each client’s utility via leave-one-out influence signals, addressing learner–client distribution misalignment and heterogeneity to improve convergence and accuracy on CIFAR-10.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In FL, the learner and client data distributions are often misaligned, and standard client selection assumes uniform benefit from all clients. This misalignment with multi-source transfer limits performance and convergence.

Method: Maintain a small proxy dataset at the learner. Use leave-one-out evaluations to estimate each client’s influence on the learner’s objective via proxy influence signals computed from the learner-specific proxy set. Dynamically select the most informative clients across rounds to maximize beneficial transfer while suppressing noise and heterogeneity effects.

Result: Empirical evaluation on CIFAR-10 with heterogeneous data partitions shows faster convergence and higher accuracy compared to static and dynamic baselines.

Conclusion: Influence-aware, proxy-informed client selection can mitigate learner–client population misalignment in FL, improving efficiency and performance in multi-source transfer scenarios; the approach is validated on CIFAR-10 and can generalize to other heterogeneous FL settings.

Abstract: Federated learning systems are increasingly deployed to facilitate collaborative model training across a heterogeneous client population. Existing practice mostly implicitly assumes that the aggregated client data distribution is representative of the learner's target distribution or that learning from all available clients is uniformly beneficial for the learner distribution. However, such an assumption often does not hold in reality. Traditional client selection strategies in FL literature largely overlook such misalignment, while most existing work on multi-source transfer learning either requires direct access to local data or uses one-shot model/feature aggregation.
  In this paper, we take the initiative to understand and mitigate the impacts of such learner-client population misalignment. In particular, we consider the practical setting where the learner keeps a small proxy dataset. We observe that client contributions vary significantly across training rounds, and traditional technology is insufficient to identify beneficial sources under multi-source transfer diversity. Then, we propose a dynamic, influence-aware client selection framework that estimates each client's potential utility to the learner's optimization objective using proxy influence signals on a learner-specific proxy set. Via using leave-one-out evaluations, we prioritize the most informative sources of knowledge while controlling the negative impacts of statistical noise and data heterogeneity. Experiments on CIFAR-10 under heterogeneous data partitions demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms static and dynamic baselines, achieving faster convergence and higher accuracy.

</details>


### [186] [Leveraging a Foundation Model for the EEG-Based Diagnosis of Alzheimer's Disease](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27719)
*Maggie Lin,Chung-Lin Hou,Tzyy-Ping Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep latent EEG features from LaBraM combined with a non-linear classifier enable non-linear, data-efficient AD diagnosis from 8-second EEGs, achieving near-90% ROC-AUC and outperforming spectral baselines, with biomarker and severity correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biological heterogeneity in Alzheimer's Disease hinders diagnosis with traditional linear methods. Non-linear neural dynamics in EEG demand advanced representations that are data-efficient and robust to noise.

Method: LaBraM pretrained on ~2,500 hours of EEG to obtain high-dimensional latent embeddings. These embeddings are fed into a non-linear Random Forest classifier. Evaluation uses subject-independent 5-fold cross-validation on 8-second EEG segments. Post-hoc occlusion identifies key biomarkers; additional neurophysiological alignment assesses correlations with cognition and clinical severity.

Result: ROC-AUC: 89.36% ± 3.49; PR AUC: 81.45% ± 4.43; Balanced accuracy: 82.44% ± 4.34. Outperforms traditional spectral baselines (band-power, FOOOF). Occlusion highlights occipital-frontal Alpha/Theta degradation as biomarkers. Higher model-predicted dementia probability correlates with worse cognition, greater severity, increased theta/alpha relative power, and higher aperiodic exponent.

Conclusion: Deep latent EEG representations capture clinically relevant signatures in noisy data, enabling precise, rapid, and data-efficient AD diagnosis.

Abstract: Biological heterogeneity in Alzheimer's Disease (AD) poses a critical diagnostic challenge, particularly for traditional linear methods that fail to capture non-linear neural dynamics. To address this, we propose a diagnostic framework utilizing the Large Brain Model (LaBraM), pretrained on over 2,500 hours of EEG data. By integrating these high-dimensional latent embeddings with a non-linear Random Forest classifier, our approach effectively isolates robust disease markers. Under a rigorous subject-independent 5-fold cross-validation protocol, the method achieves an ROC-AUC of 89.36% +/- 3.49%, PR AUC of 81.45% +/- 4.43%, and Balanced Accuracy of 82.44% +/- 4.34% in distinguishing dementia patients from healthy controls. Notably, this performance uses only 8-second EEG segments, surpassing traditional spectral baselines, including band-power and parameterized oscillatory features (FOOOF). Post-hoc occlusion analysis confirms the model captures clinically validated biomarkers, specifically occipital-frontal Alpha and Theta rhythm degradation. Additional neurophysiological alignment analysis demonstrated that higher LaBraM-predicted dementia probability significantly correlated with worse cognitive performance, greater clinical severity, increased theta and alpha relative power, and higher aperiodic exponent. These findings demonstrate that deep latent representations extract clinically relevant signatures from noisy signals, enabling precise, rapid, and data-efficient diagnosis.

</details>


### [187] [Diffusion Distillation for Efficient Weather Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27728)
*Yiming Yang,Valentin Brekke,James Briant,Serge Guillas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A supervised energy-distance distillation method compresses a multi-step diffusion weather model into a single-step student, aligning with teacher samples and ground-truth, achieving teacher-like performance with one neural evaluation per autoregressive step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models provide accurate weather ensembles but incur high computational cost due to iterative sampling; a single-step predictor is desired for efficiency while retaining accuracy, especially for extreme events.

Method: Train a student network to match the distribution of the teacher's samples and the ground-truth observations using an energy-distance based objective, effectively distilling a multi-step diffusion process into a single-step predictor, requiring only one neural function evaluation per autoregressive step.

Result: The student outperforms existing distillation baselines and preserves skill for extreme events, matching or surpassing the teacher on key metrics while using only one neural evaluation per autoregressive step, demonstrated on global forecasting and typhoon-track prediction.

Conclusion: Energy-distance distillation enables efficient, single-step weather forecasting that retains high skill andExtreme-event performance, offering a broadly applicable approach to distill diffusion-based ensembles into fast, reliable predictors.

Abstract: Diffusion models generate skillful weather ensembles but require costly iterative sampling. We introduce a supervised energy-distance distillation method that compresses a multi-step diffusion teacher into a single-step student by aligning student forecasts with teacher samples and ground-truth observations. Experiments on global forecasting and typhoon-track prediction show that our student outperforms existing distillation methods and preserves skill for extreme events. It matches or surpasses the teacher across key metrics using only one neural function evaluation per autoregressive step.

</details>


### [188] [The Calls are Coming from Inside the Model: Investigating Probe-based Detection of Tool-Calling Errors in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27750)
*Eric Yeats,Brendan Kennedy,Loc Truong,John Buckheit,Jung Lee,Jesse Friedbaum,John Emanuello,Henry Kvinge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Linear probes can detect incorrect tool calls in LLMs by querying hidden states; effective across 18 tool-calling LLMs; generalizes to novel errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable reliable tool usage in LLM-based systems by detecting and diagnosing tool-calling mistakes through lightweight linear probes on internal representations.

Method: Apply linear probes to hidden representations from 18 tool-calling LLMs evaluated on the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard; assess ability to detect various tool-calling errors (including value-type mismatches); analyze factors like model size, probe layer, and post-training type; test generalization to unseen error types.

Result: Probing effectively identifies a spectrum of tool-calling errors, including cases where the argument value is wrong but the type is correct; performance improves with larger models, appropriate probe layers, and certain post-training regimes; probes generalize to novel errors, aiding deployment in real-world settings.

Conclusion: Linear probes on LLM hidden states offer a viable, generalizable approach to detecting tool-calling errors, with practical implications for model size, probing strategy, and training regime.

Abstract: The hidden states of large language models (LLMs) are known to capture rich information relating to model knowledge and behavior that can be hard to extract from examination of input and output alone. As LLM-based systems increasingly interface with the external world, one area of concern is detecting incorrect or improper use of tools. Motivated by this, we study the effectiveness of using linear probes to detect incorrect tool-calls, measuring probe efficacy across 18 tool-calling LLMs evaluated on the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard. Overall, we find that probing is an effective means to catch a range of different tool-calling errors, including errors arising from using an argument that has the wrong value but the correct type, which might not be recorded by standard logging frameworks. Important factors in success include model size, probing layer, and model post-training type. We also show that probes are capable of generalizing to novel types of errors, which is critical in real world deployments.

</details>


### [189] [Beyond Search-Imitation: Prior-Directed Exploration for Searchless Chess](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27757)
*Szymon Miłosz,Piotr Duch,Szymon Grabowski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A searchless chess network is built by distilling an AlphaZero-style MCTS visit counts into Chessformer, then fine-tuned with self-play reinforcement learning to achieve single-pass strength. The authors replace the standard entropy bonus with forward, mass-covering KL toward the network’s own MCTS prior (prior-directed exploration) and pair it with an entropy-adaptive sampling temperature guided by the value head’s uncertainty. In ~2000 steps this yields modest puzzle and mating accuracy gains while preserving base-level searchless strength. Across evaluations, accuracy gains dissociate from playing strength: small gains in tactical/puzzle metrics do not translate to large Elo gains; puzzle-only tuning improves tactical metrics but reduces Elo. Regularization and the choice of KL objective affect policy diversity and solution hardness; forward KL vs reverse KL anchors yield different distributions and performance.Overall the work shows that imitation of search alone is insufficient, and exploration guided by the network’s prior with adaptive temperature can yield competitive searchless strength while exposing trade-offs between tactical accuracy and overall play.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve human-master strength in a searchless chess model by distilling a stronger teacher’s search behavior and then overcoming the limitations of imitation through self-play RL. The work seeks to improve tactical accuracy and puzzle-solving without sacrificing (or even while maintaining) single-pass playing strength, and to understand how exploration objectives (forward KL toward the prior vs traditional entropy) shape outcomes.

Method: 1) Distill visit counts from an AlphaZero-style MCTS (as in Leela Chess Zero Chessformer) into a Chessformer model. 2) Fine-tune with self-play RL to optimize playing strength without search. 3) Replace the standard entropy bonus (reverse KL to uniform) with prior-directed exploration using forward KL toward the network’s MCTS prior (mass-covering). 4) Add entropy-adaptive sampling temperature tied to the value head’s outcome uncertainty. 5) Train for ~2k steps and evaluate puzzle accuracy, mate-in-four accuracy, and Elo-like ratings, including distribution-level analyses and comparisons of forward KL vs reverse KL anchors. 6) Analyze regularization effects on policy diversity and solution difficulty.

Result: Puzzle accuracy improved from 93.9% to 94.9%; mate-in-four accuracy rose from 77% to 81%; searchless strength remained at or slightly above the base. Across a matched-compute sweep, tactical (puzzle) gains largely did not translate into large Elo gains; some models tuned for puzzles alone increased tactical performance but lost ~260 Elo, indicating a dissociation between tactics and overall play. Distribution-level results show that without regularization self-play collapses to a narrow line of play; newly solved puzzles were often near-misses the prior kept alive. The forward KL prior achieved top ratings, statistically tied with a strong reverse-KL anchor that concentrates harder and excludes some difficult solutions favored by the mass-covering prior.

Conclusion: Forward KL-directed exploration toward the model’s own MCTS prior, coupled with an entropy-adaptive temperature, can modestly improve puzzle/tactical metrics without sacrificing base searchless strength, but improving tactical accuracy does not automatically yield higher overall strength. The KL objective and regularization critically shape policy diversity and solution exploration; the method’s strength resides in its ability to maintain broad searchless play while selectively enhancing moves favored by the prior. Comparisons show forward KL can rival or outperform reverse KL anchors in rating, with trade-offs in solution hardness and distributional behavior.

Abstract: Searchless chess networks reach human master strength from a single forward pass by imitating a stronger teacher: the strongest, Leela Chess Zero's (Lc0) released Chessformer, distills the visit counts of an AlphaZero-style Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). Imitating a search is a poor proxy for playing without one, so we fine-tune for single-pass strength with self-play reinforcement learning (RL). Its exploration is usually supplied by an entropy bonus, the reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence to uniform. We replace it with a forward, mass-covering KL toward the network's own MCTS prior (prior-directed exploration), so exploration covers the moves the prior judges promising, and pair it with an entropy-adaptive sampling temperature, set by the value head's outcome uncertainty, that sharpens once a position is decided. In about two thousand steps it raises puzzle accuracy from 93.9% to 94.9% on a 100,000-puzzle suite and mate-in-four accuracy from 77% to 81% while holding searchless strength at or slightly above the base. Measuring tactical accuracy and playing strength together across a matched-compute sweep, we find the two dissociate: accuracy gains fall in a one-point band while ratings straddle the base, and a control fine-tuned on puzzles alone posts the study's largest tactical gains while shedding roughly 260 Elo; a better puzzle-solver is not thereby a stronger player. Distribution-level measurements show what anchoring buys: without a regularizer self-play collapses onto a single line of play, and the puzzles newly solved are the near misses whose winning move the prior kept alive. The forward-KL prior tops the rating ladder, statistically tied with a reverse-KL anchor that concentrates twice as hard and drops the hardest solutions the mass-covering prior keeps in support.

</details>


### [190] [Fast Weight Attention for Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27763)
*Yifan Zhang,Steve Ta,Jasper Zhang,Jichen Feng,Shuzhen Li,Yongxin Zhang,Yifeng Liu,Huizhuo Yuan,Mengdi Wang,Quanquan Gu,Andrew Chi-Chih Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes RFWM and selective state-space models to compress expanding context into a fixed-size recurrent state, derives normalized online updates (Falcon-1/2/3 and A variants), and demonstrates competitive language modeling and longer-horizon extrapolation; emphasizes separating temporal alignment, plasticity, forgetting, and rehearsal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable online learning and stable long-horizon sequence modeling by compressing long contexts into fixed-size recurrent states and to formulate a unified yet flexible update rule family for read-after-write autoregressive settings.

Method: Derive normalized first-order updates for squared-error regression and negative inner-product objectives; define Falcon-1/2/3 (and A variants), with per-column, sliding-window, and inner-product forms; provide recurrent, masked-parallel, chunk-parallel implementations and a renormalization scheme; test on language modeling and variable-digit addition tasks.

Result: Several Falcon variants competitive in language modeling; improved length extrapolation on variable-digit addition; framework successfully separates temporal alignment, plasticity, forgetting, and bounded rehearsal.

Conclusion: RFWM and selective state-space models offer a scalable online learning paradigm for recurrent sequence models, enabling stable learning with compressed context and adaptable update dynamics.

Abstract: Recurrent fast-weight memories and selective state-space models compress an expanding context into a fixed-size recurrent state, making the state transition an online learning rule. We study this rule under read-after-write autoregressive semantics. For the prefix-prediction objective considered here, the local fast-memory example revealed at step $t$ is the prefix-aligned pair $(\mathbf{x}_t,\mathbf{y}_t)=(φ(\mathbf{k}_{t-1}),\mathbf{v}_t)$. The common same-step association $(φ(\mathbf{k}_t),\mathbf{v}_t)$ remains causal, but optimizes a different internal objective. We derive normalized first-order updates for squared-error regression and negative inner-product objectives. The regression family comprises Falcon-1 (a scalar NLMS update), Falcon-2 (its per-column extension), and Falcon-3 (a sliding-window mini-batch update); Falcon-1A/Falcon-2A/Falcon-3A are the corresponding inner-product variants. We provide recurrent, masked-parallel, and chunk-parallel forms, together with numerically stable positive-decay renormalization. Representative variants remain competitive in language modeling and improve length extrapolation on variable-digit addition. This framework separates temporal alignment, plasticity, forgetting, and bounded rehearsal in recurrent sequence models.

</details>


### [191] [Initialization Is Critical: Advancing Federated Short-Term Load Forecasting under Load Heterogeneity via Model Initialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27791)
*Jianing Chen,Vajiheh Farhadi,Yan Li,Thomas La Porta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two initialization strategies (global pretrained and sequential local initialization, SLIAvg) mitigate client drift in federated short-term load forecasting, improving accuracy and convergence under structured data heterogeneity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: STLF relies on fine-grained data, raising privacy concerns. Federated learning (FL) helps preserve privacy but structured heterogeneity across clients degrades forecasting performance. Initialization strategies aim to reduce client drift without changing FL frameworks.

Method: Global initialization uses pretrained initialization with auxiliary public load data to initialize the global model before federated training. Local initialization introduces SLIAvg, a sequential local initialization where participating clients start from progressively adapted models within each communication round. The approaches are designed to be compatible with existing FL frameworks and privacy techniques.

Result: Experiments on real smart-meter data with two representative forecasting architectures show reduced client drift, improved convergence, and lower forecasting errors, indicating robustness of the proposed initialization strategies across architectures and data settings.

Conclusion: Initialization strategies can enhance FL-based STLF under structured heterogeneity, providing lightweight integration and privacy-preserving improvements, with potential applicability to other FL tasks.

Abstract: Short-term load forecasting (STLF) provides essential information for numerous applications in modern power systems. However, accurate STLF often relies on fine-grained smart-meter data from distributed users, raising increasing concerns about data privacy. Federated learning (FL) has therefore emerged as a promising privacy-preserving paradigm for STLF. Nevertheless, this paper reveals structured heterogeneity in clients' load data. Specifically, clients exhibit different responses to exogenous factors and distinct temporal load profiles, which can degrade forecasting performance in FL. To mitigate these issues, this paper studies the role of model initialization in federated STLF, and proposes two initialization strategies from global and local perspectives. For global model initialization, when auxiliary public load data are available, a pretrained initialization strategy is developed to initialize the global model before federated training, thereby reducing client drift during the training process. For local model initialization, we propose SLIAvg, a sequential local initialization strategy that promotes a more consistent training process by allowing participating clients to start from progressively adapted models within each communication round. Since the proposed strategies only modify the initialization process, they are compatible with most existing FL frameworks and privacy-enhancing techniques. Experiments on real smart-meter data with two representative forecasting architectures demonstrate that the proposed strategies effectively improve forecasting performance, as evidenced by reduced client drift, improved convergence behavior, and lower forecasting errors.

</details>


### [192] [Node-wise Feature Encoding for Neural Performance Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27794)
*Matthew Grenier,William Hammer,Andrew Heuer,Nikhil Krishna,Yi Wang,Ramtin Zand*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FeatureFormer introduces node-wise encodings of FLOPs, parameter counts, and memory proxies inside a gated graph attention predictor, paired with the NNEQ dataset, to deliver state-of-the-art latency and energy predictions with strong out-of-domain generalization and negligible overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate latency and energy prediction is crucial for efficient neural architecture search on edge devices; existing GNN/Transformer predictors overlook per-node compute costs, hindering modeling of critical operations.

Method: FeatureFormer embeds explicit node-wise cost encodings (FLOPs, parameter counts, memory proxies) into a gated graph attention network. It is evaluated using the new NNEQ dataset for unified latency/energy prediction and includes broader applicability assessment.

Result: Extensive experiments show state-of-the-art performance for both latency and energy prediction, including robust out-of-domain generalization. The proposed encoding improves existing predictors with negligible overhead.

Conclusion: Node-wise cost encodings are broadly applicable and offer a practical, low-overhead enhancement to neural performance predictors across latency and energy tasks.

Abstract: As neural networks are increasingly deployed on resource constrained edge devices, accurate prediction of latency and energy is critical for efficient neural architecture search. Existing GNN and transformer based predictors achieve strong results but largely ignore node-level computational cost, limiting their ability to model performance critical operations. To address this, we introduce FeatureFormer, a neural performance predictor that incorporates explicit node-wise encodings of FLOPs, parameter counts, and memory proxies within a gated graph attention architecture. We also present NNEQ, a new large-scale energy consumption dataset that enables unified evaluation of latency and energy prediction. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FeatureFormer achieves state-of-the-art performance across both metrics, including challenging out-of-domain settings. Finally, we show that the proposed encoding is broadly applicable and consistently improves existing predictors with negligible overhead.

</details>


### [193] [Actionable CBFI: Integrating Structural Decomposition and Causal Counterfactual Recourse for Tabular Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27821)
*Sejong Oh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A-CBFI: a diagnosis-prescription framework for tabular ML that uses SCMs to identify causal bottlenecks and provide targeted, actionable recourse with high causal validity, reducing human intervention while maintaining recourse efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current XAI recourse methods struggle with causal validity, cognitive burden, and predictive failure. Exhaustive causal search is costly; additive attributions (e.g., SHAP) miss higher-order interactions. A-CBFI aims to diagnose bottlenecks and prescribe targeted interventions within an SCM framework to improve recourse efficiency in nonlinear models like XGBoost.

Method: Grounded in structural causal models, A-CBFI isolates synergistic interaction bottlenecks and releases suppressive structural locks. It separates the active intervention space L_active from downstream effects and concentrates over 98.3% of intervention effort on diagnosed root causes, enabling targeted interventions. Demonstrated on tabular data (financial and healthcare domains) with models such as XGBoost.

Result: A-CBFI reduces active human intervention burden by 76.9% while maintaining comparable global recourse cost to exhaustive causal baselines. It achieves targeted, causal-valid recourse with full relative convergence across causally feasible instances by prioritizing diagnosed bottlenecks.

Conclusion: A-CBFI delivers actionable, causally valid, and efficient recourse for tabular ML by focusing interventions on diagnosed bottlenecks, reducing human effort, and achieving convergence across feasible cases; the framework bridges the gap between comprehensive causal search and additive attribution methods.

Abstract: Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) increasingly calls for actionable counterfactual recourse, yet current methodologies face challenges related to causal invalidity, excessive cognitive burden, and predictive failure. Exhaustive causal search algorithms often require modifications to multiple attributes, whereas additive attribution-guided methods, such as SHAP, ignore higher-order feature synergies, leading to suboptimal predictive momentum and diffuse intervention effort in complex nonlinear models, such as XGBoost. To bridge this gap, we introduce actionable case-based feature importance (A-CBFI), a diagnosis-prescription integrated framework for tabular machine learning. Grounded in structural causal models (SCMs), A-CBFI isolates synergistic interaction bottlenecks and releases suppressive structural locks, translating them into targeted interventions. By mathematically separating the active user intervention space (L_{\mathrm{active}}) from downstream effects and concentrating over 98.3% of the intervention effort on diagnosed root causes, A-CBFI enables highly targeted interventions. Empirical evaluations across the financial and healthcare domains demonstrate that A-CBFI reduces the active human intervention burden by 76.9% while maintaining comparable global recourse cost to exhaustive causal baselines. By prioritizing the diagnosed causal bottlenecks, A-CBFI provides targeted and actionable recourse while maintaining causal validity and achieving full relative convergence across all causally feasible instances.

</details>


### [194] [FedEHR-Agents: Federated Agentic Optimization for Automated EHR Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27856)
*Jun Bai,Ruilin Wang,Yue Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An experience-centric federated agent framework (FedEHR-Agents) for automated EHR modeling where hospital-local autonomous agents share modeling experience (memory, evaluations, TextGrad prompts) via a federated server to produce global meta-prompts, aiming to outperform traditional FL by leveraging experience rather than only model parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address privacy/silo constraints in multi-hospital EHR modeling by moving beyond parameter-centric FL. Exploit richer modeling experience (memory, evaluation, prompts) accumulated by autonomous agents to improve cross-hospital collaboration and performance.

Method: Each hospital deploys an autonomous EHR agent for data preprocessing and model development. Agents refine local modeling experience via historical memory, task-specific evaluation, and TextGrad-based prompt refinement. A federated server aggregates evidence-guided experiences across heterogeneous hospitals and distills them into global meta-prompts for local refinement.

Result: FedEHR-Agents consistently outperforms local and federated baselines across diverse clinical prediction tasks, is robust across different federation scales and LLM backbones, demonstrated on real-world multi-hospital EHR benchmarks.

Conclusion: Clinical modeling experience can serve as a collaborative object beyond parameters, enabling federated autonomous clinical intelligence and broader cross-hospital collaboration.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models are enabling autonomous clinical agents to perform increasingly complex electronic health record (EHR) modeling workflows. However, agents deployed at individual hospitals remain constrained by institution-specific data and modeling environments, while direct cross-hospital collaboration is restricted by the sensitivity of patient-level EHR data. Although federated learning (FL) provides a natural foundation for privacy-preserving collaboration, existing approaches remain predominantly model-centric, limiting federation to prediction models or their updates while overlooking the richer modeling experience accumulated by autonomous agents. To address this limitation, we propose FedEHR-Agents, an experience-centric federated agentic optimization framework for automated EHR modeling. Each hospital deploys an autonomous clinical EHR agent that performs data preprocessing and model development while refining local clinical modeling experience through historical memory, task-specific evaluation, and TextGrad-based prompt refinement. The federated server performs evidence-guided experience aggregation to integrate reliable and complementary modeling experience across heterogeneous hospitals and distills the aggregated experience into global meta-prompts for subsequent local refinement. Extensive experiments on real-world multi-hospital EHR benchmarks demonstrate that FedEHR-Agents consistently outperforms local and federated baselines across diverse clinical prediction tasks and remains robust across different federation scales and LLM backbones. These results establish clinical modeling experience as a promising collaborative object beyond conventional parameter-centric FL and point toward federated autonomous clinical intelligence.

</details>


### [195] [SOMTab: Set-Order Mamba for Efficient Tabular In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27882)
*Hao Wang,Siyu Zhang,Wei Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract investigates whether attention is required at every stage of tabular in-context learning and proposes a more efficient architecture, SOMTab, that decouples representation construction from query-conditioned retrieval. It questions the dominance of attention-heavy transformers and offers a pathway to reduce compute while maintaining accuracy.

Method: SOMTab constructs row and column representations by mapping unordered table tokens into stable latent slots and applying Mamba-based state-space mixing to create compact representations. It separates representation construction from the final, query-conditioned retrieval, where attention-based in-context learning is still used for the final prediction. The approach is complemented by DCH-TailMix, a synthetic prior combining degree-corrected graph heterogeneity with mixed heavy-tailed dependencies to diversify dependency structures. Experimental evaluation compares SOMTab to strong Transformer-based tabular foundation models on tabular benchmarks, focusing on efficiency metrics.

Result: SOMTab approaches the performance of strong Transformer-based tabular models while offering faster inference and lower GPU memory usage, yielding a favorable efficiency–accuracy trade-off across benchmarks.

Conclusion: Attention is not strictly necessary at every stage of tabular in-context learning; separating representation construction (via Set-Order Mamba) from query-conditioned retrieval (attention-based final prediction) can achieve competitive accuracy with improved efficiency. The synthetic prior DCH-TailMix contributes to diversifying dependencies to support robust performance.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models based on in-context learning have recently emerged as strong alternatives to task-specific model fitting. However, the current performance frontier remains dominated by attention-heavy architectures, where attention is used throughout the modeling pipeline. This raises a natural question: is attention necessary at every stage of tabular in-context learning? We introduce SOMTab, a Set-Order Mamba architecture for efficient tabular in-context learning. SOMTab separates representation construction from query-conditioned retrieval. For row and column representations, it maps unordered table tokens into stable latent slots and applies Mamba-based state-space mixing to construct compact representations. For final prediction, it retains attention-based in-context learning to preserve query-conditioned retrieval from labeled context examples. We further introduce DCH-TailMix, a synthetic prior that combines degree-corrected graph heterogeneity with mixed heavy-tailed regimes to diversify synthetic dependency structures. Across tabular benchmarks, SOMTab approaches the performance of strong Transformer-based tabular foundation models while achieving faster inference and lower GPU memory usage, yielding a favorable efficiency--accuracy trade-off.

</details>


### [196] [Beyond Pairwise Graphs in Science: Hypergraph Adaptive Wavelet Operators for Parametric PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27883)
*Rajat Sarkar,Venkataramana Runkana,Souvik Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HALO introduces a hypergraph-based spectral wavelet operator for learning PDE solution maps on unstructured domains, using Chebyshev wavelet filters to avoid eigendecomposition, enabling stable, scalable multi-step rollouts with competitive accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To learn solution operators for time-dependent PDEs on unstructured meshes where grid-based methods struggle and standard graph operators fail to capture group-wise couplings and conservation volumes; the goal is accuracy, stability, and scalability across varying discretizations.

Method: Lift the domain to a hypergraph and learn in its spectral wavelet domain. Use Chebyshev polynomial wavelet filters to avoid explicit hypergraph-Laplacian eigendecomposition, yielding localized spectral kernels with linear sparse-matrix cost. Employ dyadic wavelet scales regularized toward a tight-frame to adapt frequency response per PDE while ensuring stable multi-scale coverage.

Result: Across 2D/3D benchmarks on structured/unstructured discretizations, HALO achieves best or near-best accuracy among baselines (frequency-, transformer-, DeepONet-, state-space-, and graph-based). It sustains stable multi-step rollouts and scales to industrial geometries (meshes with hundreds of thousands of points), performing on par with or better than strong fixed-discretization transformers and remaining resolution-equivariant.

Conclusion: HALO provides a scalable, stable, and adaptable operator for PDEs on unstructured domains, outperforming several baselines and maintaining resolution equivariance, with practical relevance for large-scale industrial geometries.

Abstract: Physical systems are often modeled by solution operators that map input fields, parameters, geometries, or past states to steady or future physical states. Learning these maps is difficult, especially for time-dependent systems that must assimilate history and remain stable under autoregressive rollout. Many neural operators work best on regular, structured grids, while realistic simulations often require unstructured meshes or point clouds to resolve complex geometries; in such settings, grid-centric representations can lose accuracy. Graph neural operators handle these domains through message passing or spectral graph filtering, but pairwise edges do not directly capture group-wise couplings among mesh cells, local neighborhoods, or conservation volumes. We introduce the Hypergraph Adaptive waveLet Operator (HALO), which lifts the domain to a hypergraph and learns in its spectral wavelet domain. HALO avoids explicit hypergraph-Laplacian eigendecomposition through Chebyshev polynomial wavelet filters, giving localized spectral kernels at linear sparse-matrix cost. Its trainable dyadic wavelet scales are regularized toward tight-frame coverage, allowing the frequency response to adapt to each PDE while encouraging stable multi-scale spectral coverage. Across 2D and 3D benchmarks on structured and unstructured discretizations, HALO achieves best or near-best accuracy among frequency-, transformer-, DeepONet-, state-space-, and graph-based baselines and sustains stable multi-step rollouts. The same model scales to industrial aerodynamic geometries: on meshes of a few hundred thousand points it is on par with, or better than, the strongest fixed-discretization transformers, while remaining resolution-equivariant.

</details>


### [197] [There and Back Again: Bidirectional Diffusion Bridges for Multimodality Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27885)
*Gabe Guo,Elon Litman,Thanawat Sornwanee,Jose Blanchet,Stefano Ermon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BIT enables bidirectional text–image generation by starting from text and diffusing to images, with an endpoint-conditioned pathway enabling image-to-text inversion; derived via SDEs for scalable high-dimensional diffusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of existing multimodal diffusion methods: (i) non-source-aware generative paths that limit sampling flexibility, and (ii) unidirectionality that prevents inversion between image and text.

Method: Derive BIT through stochastic calculus to obtain SDE formulations; initialize diffusion from text and interpolate to images; design endpoint-conditioning to allow traversal from image back to text; provide tractable loss functions scalable to high dimensions.

Result: BIT is competitive with denoising-diffusion and deterministic-flow baselines, and outperforms them on several vision-language and natural-science evaluations.

Conclusion: BIT provides a unified, bidirectional generative framework for text–image translation with scalable SDE-based training and flexible sampling.

Abstract: Multimodality translation (e.g., text-to-image) is a core generative AI task. However, existing approaches (1) follow generative paths that do not directly represent the source modality, limiting the flexibility of some sampling algorithms; and (2) are unidirectional, preventing inversion (e.g., image-to-text). We propose BIT: Bidirectional Image-Text Diffusion Bridges. In contrast to previous approaches, BIT starts directly from text and interpolates into images, providing (1) a source-aware generative path that enables diverse and flexible sampling algorithms; and (2) an endpoint-conditioned process that can be traversed from image to text, providing a unified, bidirectional generative framework. BIT is derived through stochastic calculus, yielding SDE forms amenable to simulation and tractable loss functions that scale to high dimensions. Our experiments show that BIT is competitive with denoising-diffusion and deterministic-flow baselines, and outperforms them on several vision--language and natural-science evaluations.

</details>


### [198] [TACIT-Switch: Cost-Aware Model Escalation for LLM Agents from Censored Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27911)
*Ji'an Lei,Jian Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learned handoff policy TACIT-SWITCH for dynamically routing between LM backbones by modeling cumulative trajectory risk with interval-censored TACIT annotations; achieves 7.4–11.1pp gains in a mechanism-based multi-step simulation and strong held-out performance on ALFWorld and DABench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cost-reliability trade-off in language-model backbones. Smaller backbones are cheaper but less reliable; larger backbones are more reliable but costly. Need routing strategies to decide when to invoke a stronger model.

Method: Accumulate trajectory evidence and represent Teacher-Annotated Censored Intervention Times (TACIT) as interval-censored observations on a cumulative-risk scale. Use a mixture-cure threshold model to estimate (i) the probability that the strong rollout will succeed, and (ii) conditional on success, the handoff threshold. Learn from accumulated trajectory data; deployment requires no teacher.

Result: In a mechanism-based multi-step simulation, TACIT-SWITCH improves success by 7.4–11.1 percentage points over task-level, step-level, and fixed-prefix routing baselines at comparable cost. Ablations show task features and cumulative trajectory risk provide complementary information. On held-out data, TACIT-SWITCH yields the highest observed success: ALFWorld 48.5% (4B Cheap) and 45.5% (9B Cheap); DABench 73.1%.

Conclusion: TACIT-SWITCH enables higher success with learned routing policies that use accumulated trajectory evidence and interval-censored TACIT signals, without requiring teachers at deployment. Demonstrates complementary information from task features and cumulative risk, and strong generalization in controlled simulations.

Abstract: Agents with smaller language-model backbones are less expensive but can drift into persistent failure modes, whereas those with larger backbones are generally more reliable but more costly. This reliability-cost trade-off motivates routing methods that decide when to invoke an agent with a larger backbone: before execution, after a fixed trajectory prefix, or locally at individual steps. Our method, TACIT-SWITCH, learns permanent handoff policies from accumulated trajectory evidence and Teacher-Annotated Censored Intervention Times (TACIT). It represents each annotation as an interval-censored observation on a cumulative-risk scale. The resulting mixture-cure threshold model estimates the probability that the paired Strong rollout succeeds and, conditional on success, the handoff threshold; no teacher is required at deployment.
