In a mechanism-based multi-step simulation, TACIT-SWITCH improves success by 7.4-11.1 percentage points over task-level, step-level, and fixed-prefix routing baselines at comparable cost. Within that controlled simulation, ablations show that task features and cumulative trajectory risk provide complementary information. With operating points selected on development data, TACIT-SWITCH achieves the highest held-out success among learned policies on both ALFWorld (48.5% with 4B Cheap; 45.5% with 9B Cheap) and DABench (73.1%).

</details>


### [199] [TI$^2$PS: A Topology-Informed Inverse Design Framework for Stochastic Multicellular Pattern Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27931)
*Kenji Komiya,Andrew Kailiang Jin,Ryo Nishikimi,Kunio Kashino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework combining Betti vectors from topological data analysis with inverse surrogate modeling to infer ABM parameters from target multicellular patterns; validated on zebrafish pigment patterns and outperforming PointNet++, even with limited data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome two challenges in multicellular ABMs: estimating cell-level parameters under stochastic proliferation/death, and quantitatively evaluating topology of arrangements; current representations are weak for topology and parameter inference.

Method: Integrate Betti vectors as a topology-based feature representation with inverse surrogate modeling to directly infer ABM parameters from target patterns; apply to zebrafish pigment pattern formation.

Result: Accurate ABM parameter estimation; better performance than PointNet++; high data efficiency (10% training data) relative to PointNet++ using full data.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a robust, data-efficient framework for ABM parameter inference leveraging TDA features; Betti vectors provide stable representations across configurations, enabling effective inverse modeling and potential generalization to other pattern-forming systems.

Abstract: This study proposes a novel framework to estimate parameters for reproducing target multicellular patterns using an agent-based model (ABM). Two major challenges in multicellular ABMs are estimating cell-level parameters (agent-specific variables) and quantitatively evaluating the topological characteristics of multicellular arrangements under stochastic cell proliferation and death. To address these challenges, we integrate two approaches: Betti vectors and inverse surrogate modeling. The Betti vectors obtained through topological data analysis can consistently represent features of a wide range of multicellular spatial configurations. The inverse surrogate modeling enables direct inference of the corresponding ABM parameters from the target patterns. We validated the proposed framework using zebrafish pigment pattern formation, a representative model of pattern formation driven by multicellular interactions. The results demonstrate that our framework successfully estimates ABM parameters and outperforms conventional methods such as PointNet++. Notably, the proposed method, which used only 10% of the training data, outperformed PointNet++, which used 100% of the data, across all evaluation metrics.

</details>


### [200] [Temporal Memory-Aware Online Test-Time Adaptation on Dynamic Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27948)
*Bo Li,Xin Zheng,Ming Jin,Can Wang,Shirui Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DGOTTA is a three-module framework for test-time adaptation on dynamic graphs, combining temporal-aware augmentation, memory-aware prediction, and consistency-guided online adaptation to improve generalization across shifts and backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic graphs and DGNNs exhibit evolving structure and semantics at test time, causing distribution shifts and degrading generalization; there is a gap in test-time adaptation for dynamic graphs.

Method: DGOTTA consists of (1) temporal-aware augmentation to diversify test dynamics, (2) memory-aware model prediction to mitigate catastrophic forgetting, and (3) consistency-guided online adaptation enforcing temporal alignment and memory smoothness during test-time updates.

Result: Empirical evaluation on three real-world datasets and four DGNN backbones demonstrates significant improvements in generalization under diverse distribution shifts and architectures.

Conclusion: DGOTTA effectively enables robust test-time adaptation for DGNNs across varying shifts and architectures, with potential for further work on efficiency, theoretical guarantees, and broader benchmarks.

Abstract: Test-time adaptation (TTA) on graphs aims to adapt a graph neural network (GNN) that is well-trained on the training graph to the test graph, which involves potential distribution shifts that may harm model generalization and test-time inference. While recent efforts have investigated TTA on static graphs, there is still a research gap on dynamic graphs learned with dynamic GNN (DGNN) models, where both structural connectivity and node semantics evolve continuously over time. This makes adapting a DGNN model for reliable test-time performance substantially challenging. To fill this gap, in this work, we propose a novel framework of temporal memory-aware Online Test-Time Adaptation on Dynamic Graphs, named DGOTTA, to effectively adapt well-trained DGNNs during test time. Specifically, the proposed DGOTTA contains three modules: (1) temporal-aware augmentation, to extend the diversity of test dynamic graphs for addressing complex temporal and spatial shifts; (2) memory-aware model prediction, to alleviate catastrophic forgetting; (3) consistency-guided online adaptation, to enforce temporal alignment and memory smoothness. Extensive experiments on three real-world datasets and four DGNN backbones demonstrate that DGOTTA significantly improves generalization under diverse distribution shifts and multiple model architectures.

</details>


### [201] [PhyMamba: Physics-Modulated Mamba for Robust Battery Health Prognostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27978)
*Sara Sameer,Yunyi Zhao,Wei Zhang,Minggang Zeng,Wenqing Li,Man-Fai Ng,Yonggang Wen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PhyMamba is a two-stage physics-modulated Mamba framework for battery health prognostics, integrating aging physics into sequence modeling to achieve improved accuracy and efficiency over baselines across datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon health forecasting from BMS signals is hindered by operating-condition dependency and sensor noise; existing methods often rely on intrusive aging parameter identification. A non-intrusive, physics-informed approach is needed.

Method: Stage-1: a lightweight Mamba encoder processes BMS signals to produce a latent representation that is transformed via an aging parameterization module into physics-informed aging features. Stage-2: a customized Mamba forecasting backbone performs multi-cycle prediction with physics tightly integrated to regulate temporal updates toward degradation-consistent evolution.

Result: On three public datasets and multiple forecast horizons, PhyMamba achieves the best aggregated performance with an overall mean error reduction of 31.8% compared with a diverse range of baselines, and offers an optimized accuracy-efficiency trade-off suitable for practical deployment.

Conclusion: A physics-informed, two-stage architecture effectively improves long-horizon battery health prognostics non-intrusively and efficiently.

Abstract: Battery health prognostics is a core function in battery management systems (BMSs), yet long-horizon health forecasting from BMS signals remains challenging due to operating-condition dependency and sensor noise. In this paper, we propose PhyMamba, a two-stage physics-modulated Mamba framework that integrates electrochemical aging into sequence modelling. PhyMamba does not require explicit identification of internal aging parameters, which often relies on intrusive measurements. In stage-1, a lightweight Mamba encoder first processes BMS signals and produces a latent representation that is transformed via an aging parameterization module, into physics-informed aging features. In stage-2, a customized Mamba forecasting backbone performs multi-cycle prediction, where physics is tightly integrated to regulate the model's internal temporal updates toward degradation-consistent evolution. Experiments on three public datasets under multiple forecast horizons show that PhyMamba achieves the best aggregated performance, with an overall mean error reduction of 31.8% compared with a diverse range of baselines. PhyMamba also offers an optimized accuracy-efficiency trade-off, which supports practical deployment for robust battery health prognostics.

</details>


### [202] [Is Monte Carlo Tree Search Just Every-Visit Monte Carlo Control?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27985)
*Xianyi Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper claims MCTS and every-visit Monte Carlo control are fundamentally the same when viewed through trajectory generation and action-value updates, reducing MCTS to two operations: trajectory sampling under the current policy and MC updating, thus presenting MCTS as MC control expressed in search terms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify and unify two prominent planning/learning frameworks (MCTS and MC control) by showing their deep underlying equivalence, to aid cross-application and understanding.

Method: Reinterpret the four stages of MCTS (selection, expansion, simulation, backup) as two processes within a single evolving policy: trajectory sampling under the current policy and every-visit MC updates. Map expansion to first-visit initialization and backup to standard MC update, thereby equating the procedures.

Result: Demonstrates an explicit equivalence between MCTS and every-visit MC control, reducing MCTS to trajectory sampling under the current policy plus MC updates; frame MCTS as MC control implemented using search-style data structures.

Conclusion: MCTS can be viewed as MC control embedded in search terminology and data structures; the note aims to provide an expository clarification of this equivalence.

Abstract: Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) and every-visit Monte Carlo (MC) control are usually presented as different methods. MCTS is described in the language of search (selection, expansion, simulation, and backup), whereas MC control is described in the language of reinforcement learning (trajectory sampling, return estimation, action-value updating, and policy improvement). This note argues that, at the level of trajectory generation and action-value updating, the distinction is largely terminological. The tree policy and rollout policy can be viewed as the learned and not-yet-learned parts of a single evolving policy; expansion corresponds to first visit and initialization; and backup is the ordinary every-visit Monte Carlo update. Under this interpretation, the four stages of MCTS reduce to two basic operations: trajectory sampling under the current policy and every-visit Monte Carlo updating. In this sense, MCTS is simply every-visit Monte Carlo control expressed in the language and data structure of search. The purpose of this note is expository: to make this equivalence explicit and easier to recognize.

</details>


### [203] [A Method for Layer Bit-Width Allocation in LLM Quantization via Performance Maximization Under a Quality-Degradation Constraint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28003)
*Artem Safronov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Layer-wise mixed-precision quantization for Gemma-3-1B aims to maximize latency reduction under a degradation budget, using SA-PTQ sensitivity and TensorRT-LLM. It reports up to ~19% speedup (FFN+lm_head) with modest quality loss; best single configuration FFN 5+5 + lm_head yields ~11% latency reduction with negligible quality loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce LLM latency without indiscriminate, uniform quantization. Leverage layer-wise sensitivity to target speedups while constraining acceptable generation quality loss.

Method: Determine per-layer precision in blocks based on prior SA-PTQ sensitivity, using grouping 5+5, 10+10, all26; apply activation pass-through in TensorRT-LLM; evaluate FFN, Attention, and lm_head contributions; implement manual SmoothQuant for TensorRT-LLM due to export failures; test 13 W8A8 variants on RTX 5090; explore optimizations: fused int8 kernels, KV-cache quantization, FP8, partial Attention quantization.

Result: Best configuration: FFN 5+5 with lm_head yields 11.0% latency reduction with negligible quality loss (Top-1 98.90%, perplexity +0.85%). Acceptable quality loss for FFN with all26 + lm_head enables up to 19.1% speedup.

Conclusion: Layer-wise, selective quantization guided by sensitivity profiles can yield meaningful latency gains with controlled quality degradation. Manual solutions (SmoothQuant) and targeted optimization (fused kernels, FP8, KV-cache) are promising paths; further work should address Attention quantization trade-offs and generalize to other models.

Abstract: This paper proposes a layer bit allocation method for Gemma-3-1B, formulating the problem as performance maximization (latency decrease) given a degradation budget constraint (allowable level of generation quality loss). This approach is different from time- and resource-consuming uniform layer quantization methods that are used in the literature (like GPTQ or AWQ) or allocation methods without proven performance-accelerating effect (like MixLLM or TorchAO). The layer sensitivity profile resulting from our prior work SA-PTQ is applied using the activation pass-through mode inside TensorRT-LLM. For each layer precision is determined individually in blocks, according to a grouping introduced in the prior step (5+5, 10+10, all26), differentiating the contribution of FFN, Attention, and lm_head to the overall speedup. The clock speed was measured for 13 W8A8 variants on an RTX 5090. We find that for FFN and lm_head the time cost of quantization/dequantization is compensated for by the use of integer arithmetic, while for short context lengths, the opposite holds true for Attention: an additional step of quantization slows execution down. We propose a manual implementation of SmoothQuant for TensorRT-LLM which was necessary due to export failures, unavailable for lm_head. The best solution found under joint consideration of all three criteria with minimal degradation was FFN 5+5 with lm_head, providing an 11.0% reduction in latency with negligible quality loss (98.90% Top-1 agreement, +0.85% perplexity degradation). With acceptable quality loss for FFN all26 + lm_head, a speedup up to 19.1% was found possible. We suggest further optimizations: fused attention kernels in INT8, KV-cache quantization, using FP8 instead of INT8 and partial Attention quantization analogous to FFN.

</details>


### [204] [Exact Risk Ratios for Weighted Data Selection in Linear Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28007)
*Guangjian Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Advances understanding of a minimax data-selection problem for weighted least-squares ERM. Determines exact minimax loss ratios in several open-regime cases (n between d and 2d): F_w(d,2d-1)=1+1/d; F_w(3,4)=5/3; F_w(4,5)=2; provides general lower bound F_w(d,d+k)≥1+Γ_{d,k} and proves tightness in datasets with an orthogonal circuit-block structure; conjectures equality for all d<n<2d; develops rigidity/geometry methods (positive spanning configurations, small bases in R^3/R^4) and dimension-free extremal-basis arguments; yields counterexamples to simpler routes; offers constructive polynomial-time selection algorithms for all cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resolve the open problem on the exact minimax loss ratio for selective training data under a weighted least-squares objective and minimum-norm ERM, bridging geometry, convex analysis, and learning theory.

Method: Lower bounds via harmonic quantities Γ_{d,k} over balanced partitions; upper bounds via a rigidity theorem for positive spanning configurations of loss gradients, classifications/reductions of small positive bases in R^3 and R^4, and a dimension-free extremal-basis argument that translates sign-cone geometry into five-point selections; analysis within a structured dataset class (orthogonal circuit-block structure).; construction of counterexamples and polynomial-time selection algorithms.

Result: Exact values established for several open cases: F_w(d,2d-1)=1+1/d for all d; F_w(3,4)=5/3; F_w(4,5)=2. For general n=d+k, a universal lower bound F_w(d,d+k)≥1+Γ_{d,k} is shown to be tight in the considered class, with conjecture that it is tight in general. The results match the bound in all proven instances; provides frameworks and algorithms.

Conclusion: The paper closes part of the open regime by exactly characterizing the minimax loss for several cases and offers a strong conjecture and methodological toolkit (rigidity, positive spanning geometry) that plausibly extends to all d<n<2d; also demonstrates algorithmic feasibility and highlights limitations of shorter approaches.

Abstract: Hanneke, Moran, Shlimovich and Yehudayoff (COLT 2025) posed the following open problem. A selector sees a finite dataset $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^d \times \mathbb{R}$, picks at most $n$ examples together with nonnegative weights, and hands the weighted least squares objective to the minimum-norm ERM. Writing $F_w(d,n)$ for the worst-case ratio between the loss of the returned predictor on all of $D$ and the optimal loss, they proved $F_w(d,n)=\infty$ for $n<d$, $F_w(d,d)=d+1$ and $F_w(d,n)=1$ for $n \ge 2d$, and asked for the value in the open regime $d<n<2d$. We determine this value in several cases. For every $d$ we prove $F_w(d,2d-1)=1+1/d$, which confirms a claim stated without proof in the original note. We further prove $F_w(3,4)=5/3$ and $F_w(4,5)=2$, the two smallest cells not covered by the endpoint formula. For every intermediate budget $n=d+k$ we prove the lower bound $F_w(d,d+k) \ge 1+Γ_{d,k}$, where $Γ_{d,k}$ is an explicit harmonic quantity over balanced partitions, and we show that this bound is the exact minimax value over the class of datasets whose whitened gradient systems carry an orthogonal circuit-block structure. All three exact values match $1+Γ_{d,k}$, and we conjecture that equality holds throughout the open regime. The upper bound proofs run on a common geometric spine: a rigidity theorem for positive spanning configurations of loss gradients, classifications and structural reductions of small positive bases in $\mathbb{R}^3$ and $\mathbb{R}^4$, and a dimension-free extremal-basis argument that converts sign-cone geometry into five-point selections. We also give explicit counterexamples showing that several shorter routes fail, and constructive polynomial-time selection algorithms for all proved cases.

</details>


### [205] [When Can Conditional Flow Matching Replace Pointwise Negative Log-Likelihood?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28010)
*Yansen Han,Hongxin Sun,Tao Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For linear Gaussian paths, endpoint NLL decomposes into entropy, a weighted CFM objective, an interior velocity–score residual, and a boundary residual. CFM-only estimates are exact only when those residuals cancel; at off-policy optima, w_sc(t) = (1−t)/t removes the interior residual but this does not generally hold for training or on-policy alignment. On-policy log-ratios can be biased even with identical endpoint laws or surrogate optimization. Experiments across dims/distributions/geometries support these claims. The work provides a theoretical basis for adapting likelihood-based methods to flow matching, while highlighting when substitutions are exact versus surrogate.



<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify when conditional flow matching (CFM)–based substitutions for endpoint NLLs and log-likelihood ratios are valid, and to connect flow matching with likelihood-based training in large models.

Method: Theoretical decomposition for linear Gaussian paths of endpoint NLL into entropy, weighted CFM objective, interior velocity–score residual, and boundary residual; analysis of off-policy vs on-policy settings; evaluation of the special weight w_sc(t) = (1−t)/t; empirical validation across dimensions and geometries.

Result: Exact decomposition shows the conditions under which CFM-only estimates are NLL-equivalent; off-policy optimum can make w_sc(t) remove interior residual but not guarantee general training/on-policy validity; on-policy log-ratios can remain biased; experiments corroborate the theoretical claims.

Conclusion: The decomposition provides a theoretical basis for adapting likelihood-based LLM methods to flow matching, distinguishing exact substitutions from controlled surrogates and guiding when substitutions are valid.

Abstract: Flow matching enables likelihood-free training, yet alignment methods increasingly reuse conditional flow matching (CFM) losses as endpoint negative log-likelihoods (NLLs) and their old/new differences as log-likelihood ratios. We characterize when these substitutions are valid. For linear Gaussian paths, we exactly decompose endpoint NLL into entropy, a weighted CFM objective, an interior velocity--score residual, and a boundary residual. Thus CFM-only estimates and differences are exact only when the corresponding residuals cancel. At the off-policy population optimum, ordinary CFM is not generally a pointwise NLL estimator, whereas \(w_{\mathrm{sc}}(t)=(1-t)/t\) removes the interior residual; this positive result does not extend generally to training or on-policy alignment. On-policy log-ratios can remain biased even for identical endpoint laws or after surrogate optimization. Experiments across dimensions, distributions, and geometries support these conclusions and the mechanisms that make inexact ratios useful. **More broadly, the decomposition provides a theoretical basis for adapting likelihood-based LLM methods to flow matching, while distinguishing exact substitutions from controlled surrogates.**

</details>


### [206] [Explainable Uncertainty Estimation for Reliable Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28052)
*Li Rong Wang,Jamie Duell,Xinran Xu,Thomas C. Henderson,Yu Yue Hew,Pik Wan Erica Chiang,Xiao Wei Alstar Ang,Bingwen Eugene Fan,Xiuyi Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces egRUE, a unified method that merges uncertainty estimation with feature-level explanations to quantify and explain uncertainty, offering theoretical guarantees, empirical validation, and a user study showing improved calibrated trust in healthcare decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the gap where uncertainty estimation and explainable AI (XAI) are treated separately, lacking feature-level insight into why a prediction is uncertain and which tests to prioritize to reduce it; aims to improve reliability and interpretability in safety-critical healthcare decision-making.

Method: Proposes Expected Gradients Reconstruction Uncertainty Estimate (egRUE) that integrates prediction explanations into its uncertainty computation and decomposes uncertainty into feature-wise contributions. Provides theoretical properties, conducts empirical evaluations comparing against existing methods, and includes a user study with medical experts.

Result: egRUE improves reliability and interpretability relative to existing methods. The user study shows explanations boost calibrated trust, increasing confidence in correct predictions and reducing confidence in incorrect ones.

Conclusion: Combining prediction uncertainty with feature-level explanations strengthens decision-making in safety-critical healthcare by signaling when predictions may be unreliable and clarifying which features drive that uncertainty.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence has strong potential to support clinical decision-making, yet its adoption in healthcare remains limited due to a lack of trust. Uncertainty estimation can signal unreliable predictions, and explainable AI (XAI) can clarify how predictions are made but existing methods treat them separately, providing no feature-level insight into why a prediction is uncertain or which tests to prioritize to reduce it. To address this gap, we propose explainable uncertainty estimation, which unifies uncertainty estimation and XAI to both quantify uncertainty and explain feature-level contributions. We introduce the Expected Gradients Reconstruction Uncertainty Estimate (egRUE), which incorporates prediction explanations into its uncertainty computation and decomposes uncertainty into feature-wise contributions. We prove theoretical properties of egRUE and show through experiments that it improves reliability and interpretability compared to existing methods. A user study with medical experts further demonstrates that egRUE's explanations improve calibrated trust over uncertainty scores alone, increasing confidence in correct predictions and reducing confidence in incorrect ones. By combining prediction uncertainty with feature-level explanations, egRUE strengthens decision-making support in safety-critical healthcare settings, clarifying both when predictions may be unreliable and which features drive that uncertainty.

</details>


### [207] [Comparing Classical and Quantum Machine Learning for Regression in High Energy Physics Collision Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28084)
*Tariq Mahmood,Zain ul Abidin,Itzel Luviano Soto,Alfredo Raya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quantum models offer parameter-efficient alternatives to classical deep learning for regression in HEP event data; QCNN matches deep CNN performance using only 4 qubits, suggesting near-term quantum advantages despite classical models performing slightly better overall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate the trade-offs between classical and quantum ML architectures for regression on high-energy physics event data to determine efficacy and resource efficiency under practical constraints.

Method: Systematic comparison of four classical ML models (SVM, ANN, CNN, LSTM) and four quantum counterparts (QSVM, QNN, QCNN, QLSTM) trained on simulated proton-proton collisions with e+e- and μ+μ- final states from CERN Open Data; inputs are transverse momentum components; task is regression to p_T magnitude.

Result: Classical CNN and LSTM offer marginally better quantitative performance under current hardware/dataset constraints; quantum models achieve competitive accuracy with substantially fewer trainable parameters; QCNN reproduces CNN performance with only four qubits and circuit depth three, indicating potential parameter efficiency on near-term devices; regression is non-trivial for shallow polynomial fits, supporting the value of architectural comparisons.

Conclusion: The study clarifies trade-offs between classical and quantum ML for HEP regression under resource constraints, providing a benchmark for future hardware-enabled investigations and emphasizing parameter efficiency of quantum architectures like QCNN.

Abstract: The classification and regression of particle collision events constitute a persistent computational challenge in experimental high energy physics, where large volumes of simulated data must be processed with both speed and precision. This work carries out a systematic comparison of four classical machine learning architectures, support vector machines (SVM), artificial neural networks (ANN), convolutional neural networks (CNN), and long short-term memory (LSTM) networks against their quantum counterparts: quantum SVM (QSVM), quantum neural networks (QNN), quantum CNN (QCNN), and quantum LSTM (QLSTM). All models are trained on simulated proton-proton collision events with electron-positron and muon-antimuon final states from the CERN Open Data portal, using transverse-momentum components as input features and transverse-momentum magnitude as the regression target. Classical architectures, and in particular the CNN and LSTM, achieve marginally better quantitative performance under current hardware and dataset constraints. Quantum models, however, reach competitive accuracy with substantially fewer trainable parameters: the QCNN reproduces the performance of the deep classical CNN using only four qubits and a circuit of depth three, pointing to a genuine parameter-efficiency advantage on near-term quantum devices. A baseline analysis confirms that the regression problem is non-trivial for shallow polynomial fits, supporting the relevance of the architectural comparison. These results characterize the trade-offs between classical and quantum approaches under realistic, resource-constrained conditions and provide a benchmark for future studies on actual quantum hardware.

</details>


### [208] [Generalized Gibbs Ensemble Weighting for Forecast Combination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28116)
*Prasen R. Nuthanakaluva,Nava K. Gaddam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Generalized Gibbs Ensemble Weighting (GGEW) for forecast combination with online adaptation; yields stable Gibbs variants and a Local-UCB mechanism; demonstrates competitiveness across diverse benchmarks but no universal superiority.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecast combination using multiple base forecasters is sensitive to horizon, data, and disagreement; need an adaptive, probabilistic weighting framework that can adjust to settings and maintain robust performance.

Method: Introduce GGEW: exponential Gibbs-style weighting of normalized predictive loss; develop stability and diversity-aware score corrections; define variants (Stable Gibbs, Directional Gibbs-NCL, Symmetric Gibbs-NCL); apply online Local-UCB to adapt learning rate, diversity strength, and variant without full hyperparameter grids.

Result: Empirical evaluation on M4 submissions and external rolling-origin experiments across Monash Traffic Hourly, Electricity Hourly, and Solar Weekly datasets shows Gibbs-style adaptive weighting is competitive across settings; performance varies by dataset, horizon, deployment protocol, and disagreement groups.

Conclusion: Frames a general, adaptable framework and empirical study suggesting Gibbs-style forecast combination can be useful; highlights the need for further investigation into when and how adaptive Gibbs-style weighting yields gains.

Abstract: Forecast combination is a reliable way to improve predictive performance when several forecasting models are available. Simple aggregation rules such as the mean, median, trimmed mean, inverse-loss weighting, and exponential weighting are often strong baselines, but their relative performance can vary across datasets, forecast horizons, deployment settings, and levels of disagreement among base forecasters. We develop Generalized Gibbs Ensemble Weighting (GGEW), a probabilistic framework that treats forecasting models as experts and assigns ensemble weights using a Gibbs-style exponential transformation of normalized predictive loss. The framework extends this basic weighting rule through numerical stabilization, diversity-aware score corrections, and online hyperparameter adaptation. GGEW produces a family of related methods, including Stable Gibbs weighting, Directional Gibbs-NCL, and Symmetric Gibbs-NCL. These variants share one core algorithm and differ only in the score used inside the exponential weighting rule. For sequential deployment, we adopt a UCB-style bandit mechanism, called online Local-UCB, to adapt the learning rate, diversity strength, and Gibbs variant without evaluating the full hyperparameter grid at every prediction step. We evaluate GGEW on official M4 competition forecast submissions and external rolling-origin deployment experiments using Monash Traffic Hourly, Electricity Hourly, and Solar Weekly datasets. Results suggest that Gibbs-style adaptive weighting is a useful and competitive tool across several benchmark settings, although its relative performance varies across datasets, forecast horizons, deployment protocols, and forecast disagreement groups. The contribution is not a universal dominance claim, but a framework and empirical study motivating further investigation of when adaptive Gibbs-style forecast combination is useful.

</details>


### [209] [VICT: Verifier-Instrumented Credit Tracing for Long-Horizon LLM Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28128)
*Pengcheng Li,Zhengyang Zhang,Dongxu Zhang,Sui Huang,Shaohua Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VICT proposes a training-time, verifier-side credit tracing method for long-horizon agents. It exposes verifiable atoms and dependency-valid proof edges to trace back credit from a terminal verifier to actions, shifting credit assignment from rollout-based inference to verifier-side tracing while preserving the original terminal reward.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL methods for long-horizon, executable tasks rely on scalar, terminal rewards broadcast to all actions, and often add auxiliary rollout signals. These approaches neglect the internal structure of verifiers. The work aims to leverage the verifier's internal checks to enable finer-grained credit assignment.

Method: Introduce VICT (VerifierInstrumented Credit Tracing), a training-time interface that exposes executable or evidence-backed atoms and traces them back to actions via dependency-valid proof edges. It redistributes group-relative advantage along verifier edges, preserves the terminal reward, abstains when evidence is incomplete or ambiguous, and updates only the training-time advantage tensor. No learned critics, process labels, branch rollouts, or inference-time verifier access are required.

Result: VICT achieves substantial improvements over outcome-only training on ALFWorld and WebShop and matches strong performance of recent fine-grained credit methods. Ablations indicate that dense atom rewards, final-commit credit, temporal proximity, and sparsity alone do not explain the gains.

Conclusion: Verifier-side tracing can enable fine-grained credit assignment without additional supervision or inference-time access, preserving terminal rewards and reducing reliance on rollout-based inference. The approach shows promise for long-horizon LLM agents and suggests avenues for applying verifier structure in credit assignment.

Abstract: Fine-grained credit assignment is a central challenge in reinforcement learning for long horizon LLM agents. Standard objectives often train from programmatically verifiable terminal rewards by broadcasting each sparse outcome to every action in a trajectory. Existing methods typically seek finer credit from the rollout side, constructing auxiliary trajectory signals or additional comparisons to estimate action importance. Although useful, these approaches still treat the verifier that judged success as a scalar reward, discarding its internal task structure. Our key insight is that many verifiable tasks already encode the relevant checks inside their terminal verifier. We propose VICT (VerifierInstrumented Credit Tracing), a training-time interface that exposes executable or evidence backed atoms and traces them back to actions through dependency-valid proof edges. VICT redistributes group-relative advantage only along those edges, shifting credit assignment from rollout-side inference to verifierside tracing. It preserves the original terminal reward, abstains when evidence is incomplete or ambiguous, and changes only the training-time advantage tensor, requiring no learned critic, process labels, branch rollouts, or inference-time verifier access. On ALFWorld and WebShop, VICT improves substantially over outcome-only training and achieves strong performance alongside recent fine-grained credit methods; ablations rule out dense atom rewards, final-commit credit, temporal proximity, and sparsity as sufficient explanations.

</details>


### [210] [Learning to Difference: Adaptive Reversible Differencing (AdaRDiff) for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28134)
*Morad Laglil,Younes Hlal,Marouane El Hadari,Emilie Devijver,Eric Gaussier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaRDiff provides a learnable, reversible differencing mechanism that stabilizes time-series residuals for long-horizon forecasting, enabling fast, GPU-friendly reconstruction and serving as a plug-in to diverse models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional differencing relies on hand-picked orders, limiting integration with deep learning. A differentiable, learnable differencing operator can capture trend/seasonality while enabling efficient training and inference.

Method: AdaRDiff introduces adaptive reversible differencing with learnable weights over previous time instants to produce stabilized residuals. Forecasting is performed on these residuals, and the removed components are restored autoregressively via a closed-form convolutional expression, enabling GPU parallelism. Training uses a two-phase schedule: (1) discovery of temporal structure, (2) reconstruction learning, supported by a gradient analysis in the linear forecasting setting. The method is designed as a plug-in to various backbones.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art forecast accuracy across eight benchmarks (electricity, weather, traffic, energy) with negligible parameter cost. Provides up to 33.7× speedup over naive recurrence due to the closed-form reconstruction. When used as a module, AdaRDiff improves eight backbones (e.g., up to 25.9% for linear backbones and 18.3% for iTransformer).

Conclusion: AdaRDiff effectively captures trend and seasonality within a single differentiable operator, delivering accurate, fast forecasts and broad plug-in compatibility with existing models.

Abstract: Reliable long-horizon time series forecasting is an important yet difficult problem. Trends and seasonality introduce complex temporal structure that challenges learning-based forecasting models. Differencing, which subtracts nearby past values to remove such structure, is the classical remedy, but its reliance on hand-picked orders and periods has kept it largely absent from recent deep architectures. We propose \textbf{\underline{Ada}}ptive \textbf{\underline{R}}eversible \textbf{\underline{Diff}}erencing \textbf{(AdaRDiff)}, a generalized differencing approach that uses learnable weights to simplify the series through weighted differencing with previous time instants. This yields stabilized residuals on which forecasting is performed, after which the removed components are restored autoregressively to reconstruct the forecast, capturing trend and seasonality jointly through a single operator. This reconstruction admits a closed-form convolutional expression, which parallelizes on GPU and yields up to $33.7\times$ speedup over the naive recurrence. We furthermore rely on a two-phase training schedule that separates temporal structure discovery from reconstruction learning, as suggested by a theoretical analysis of the gradient when using a linear forecasting model. AdaRDiff attains state-of-the-art forecast accuracy across eight benchmarks spanning electricity, weather, traffic, and energy, at negligible parameter cost. Furthermore, it is designed as a plug-and-play module: integrating AdaRDiff improves eight diverse backbones, from linear models to Transformers, in the large majority of cases, by up to $25.9\%$ with a linear backbone and $18.3\%$ with iTransformer.

</details>


### [211] [Conditional Diffusion Models for Energy-Efficient Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28142)
*Hemanth Neelgund Ramesh,André Snoeck,Chyi-Fu Hong,Shijing Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent-conditioned diffusion model for EV energy trajectories conditioned on route features; achieves close alignment with real data and substantial improvements over direct conditioning, enabling uncertainty-aware fleet planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sequence models yield deterministic estimates or limited uncertainty, insufficient for operational decisions requiring energy-consumption trajectories with plausible variability under real-world conditions.

Method: A conditional diffusion framework with a latent conditioning encoder and a temporal 1D U-Net denoising backbone. Conditions include route features (e.g., vehicle velocity, ambient temperature). Trained on 12k trips from 9 EVs; compares latent conditioning to direct condition injection; evaluated via Wasserstein distance and MAE.

Result: Generates realistic current trajectories capturing both dominant temporal envelope and sharp transients. Wasserstein distance to measured distributions = 0.0029, below real-vs-real reference distance 0.0085, indicating samples lie within empirical variability. Latent conditioning reduces Wasserstein distance by 89.1% and MAE by 52.8% compared to direct conditioning.

Conclusion: Provides a generative modeling framework for characterizing EV energy consumption under real-world operating conditions, laying the foundation for uncertainty-aware fleet planning in large-scale, data-driven settings.

Abstract: Electrification of commercial delivery fleets is shifting fleet routing from distance- and time-based optimization toward energy-aware decision-making. Existing sequence models primarily provide deterministic point estimates or limited uncertainty summaries, which do not capture the range of plausible energy-consumption trajectories required for operational decision-making. In this work, we introduce a conditional diffusion framework that generates EV battery-current profiles conditioned on route features such as vehicle velocity and ambient temperature. The model combines a latent conditioning encoder with a temporal 1D U-Net denoising backbone that enables trip-related conditions to be mapped into a shared representation and guides the reverse diffusion process. We evaluate the framework on an open-access commercial EV telemetry dataset containing 12k trips from 9 vehicles. The proposed latent-conditioned diffusion model generates realistic cur- rent trajectories that capture both the dominant temporal envelope and sharp transient events. The model achieves a Wasserstein distance of 0.0029 between generated and measured current distributions below the real vs real reference distance of 0.0085 indicating that generated samples lie within the empirical variability of the test set. We further demonstrate that learned latent conditioning substantially improves performance over direct condition injection, reducing the Wasserstein distance by 89.1% and MAE by 52.8%. This work demonstrates a generative modeling framework for characterizing EV energy consumption under real-world operating conditions, providing an essential foundation for uncertainty-aware fleet planning in large-scale operational settings.

</details>


### [212] [The Approximation Rank of Softmax Attention: Sharp Geometric Laws and Robust Interaction Dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28150)
*Yuhe Sui,Jianing Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that rank complexity in normalized softmax attention is governed by two geometries: support geometry (affecting worst-case temperature scaling) and softmax-visible interaction geometry (affecting per-head approximation). It provides sharp worst-case bounds for two geometries (spherical self-attention and full-ball), analyzes per-head interaction dimension r with a r/2 bound, and demonstrates explicit residual error and SVD-dimension notions. Empirically, a calibration on 84-head BERT-base reveals modest effective-dimension reductions and links to rank-certificates, indicating a separation between geometry control of scaling and geometry control of approximation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle how the geometric structure of vector supports and the geometry of visible interactions in normalized softmax attention govern the rank complexity, separating the factors that set worst-case scaling from those that bound per-head approximation, and to validate the theory with empirical evidence from large pretrained models.

Method: Theoretical worst-case analysis of maximum-row-L1 approximation rank under two geometries: spherical self-attention and full-ball geometry, deriving sharp asymptotic bounds Θ_{d,ε}(min{n,(1+β)^{(d-1)/2}}) and Θ_{d,ε}(β^{d/2}) respectively, for fixed dimension d and error ε, with β and n conditions. For a fixed head, analysis of row-softmax reveals the visible interaction is confined to an r-dimensional subspace, yielding an upper bound scaling as r/2 per instance; constructive lower bounds show minimax sharpness. Additional results provide residual output error for approximate interaction subspaces and a tolerance-indexed SVD dimension. An empirical calibration on an 84-head BERT-base setup explores effective-dimension reductions across head-temperature settings and their relation to rank upper certificates.

Result: The work isolates two geometry-controlled mechanisms for rank complexity: support geometry governs worst-case temperature scaling, while softmax-visible interaction geometry governs per-head approximation complexity. It offers sharp Θ-laws for spherical self-attention and full-ball geometries, confirms a per-head r/2 bound for the interaction subspace, and characterizes residual error and SVD-dimension in approximate settings. Empirically, modest effective-dimension reductions are observed across many head–temperature settings, with positive association to finite constructive rank upper certificates.

Conclusion: The analysis demonstrates a clean separation: support geometry sets the temperature-scaling limits of the rank, whereas the geometry of the visible interaction sets the per-head approximation complexity. The combination of tight theoretical bounds and empirical calibration indicates practical regimes where attention can be efficiently approximated without sacrificing accuracy, as evidenced by observed dimension reductions and certificates in a BERT-base calibration.

Abstract: Which geometry controls the rank complexity of normalized softmax attention? We study maximum-row-$\ell_1$ approximation rank, exactly the least unrestricted rank preserving every bounded vector-valued output. Two sharp worst-case laws isolate support geometry: for fixed $d$ and error $\varepsilon$, spherical self-attention has rank $Θ_{d,\varepsilon}(\min\{n,(1+β)^{(d-1)/2}\})$, while full-ball geometry adds one radial degree and, for $β\geβ_0(d,\varepsilon)$ and $n\ge C_d e^{β/8}$, gives $Θ_{d,\varepsilon}(β^{d/2})$. For a fixed head, row-softmax quotients out row-scalar logit directions: the remaining visible query--key interaction dimension $r$ yields an $r/2$ per-instance upper law, and bounded constructions show this exponent is minimax sharp. Approximate interaction subspaces incur an explicit residual output error and yield a tolerance-indexed SVD dimension. On an 84-head BERT-base calibration set, we observe modest effective-dimension reductions across many head--temperature settings, together with positive associations with finite constructive rank upper certificates. Together, these results separate support geometry, which sets worst-case temperature scaling, from softmax-visible interaction geometry, which controls per-head approximation complexity.

</details>


### [213] [HARTS: Efficient Agentic Reinforcement Learning for Hybrid-Attention Models over Arbitrary Rollout Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28158)
*Boyuan Meng,Peihua Bao,Hong Liu,Xiaowei Zhu,Chao Wang,Gen Li,Zhenxuan Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HARTS is a hybrid-attention RL system for agentic, tree-structured rollouts that packs microbatches and state into a differentiable, activation-recomputable execution path, yielding about 4.8x speedups without changing RL objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic RL creates irregular rollout trees with shared prefixes that are inefficient to recompute if trained per root-to-leaf trajectory. Existing full-attention systems lack dense, differentiable hybrid-attention with activation recomputation compatible with prefix-sharing and incremental state replay.

Method: HARTS co-plans microbatches, DP replica assignments, and microbatch-slot schedules using non-replay compact-token work after compressing prefixes. It uses a chunkwise linear attention with a linear-time algorithm to coordinate chunk-boundary state recovery and replay, preserving chunkwise state partitioning to avoid repeating projections/MLP/MoE and performing only bounded state replay for numerical alignment. Each training round packs all branches into a single call, propagates gradients through differentiable state handoffs, supports activation recomputation, and restores per-token log-probabilities. For deterministic top-k MoE routing, semantic multiplicities restore MoE token weights and load statistics. RL objectives retain their interfaces.

Result: On SWE-bench Agentic RL tasks, HARTS achieves 4.81–4.87x forward/backward/gradient speedup with activation recomputation across several parallel configurations. Numerical differences are within baseline self-rerun variation, and reward trend matches the baseline during the first 120 steps of τ^3-Bench training.

Conclusion: HARTS is the first system to demonstrate arbitrary-rollout-tree prefix-sharing speedups on a real hybrid-attention model, delivering substantial runtime speedups while preserving RL objective interfaces and comparable early performance.

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) often produces irregular rollout trees with shared histories. Training root-to-leaf trajectories independently recomputes these shared prefixes. Existing systems primarily target full-attention models and lack dense, differentiable hybrid-attention execution compatible with activation recomputation. We present HARTS (Hybrid-Attention RL over Tree Structures). HARTS jointly plans microbatches, data-parallel (DP) replica assignments, and microbatch-slot schedules using non-replay compact-token work after prefix compression. For chunkwise linear attention, a linear-time algorithm coordinates chunk-boundary state recovery and replay and produces the minimum number of sequential linear-attention calls under our packed execution model. HARTS preserves the chunkwise state partitioning of trajectory-wise training: it does not repeat projections, MLP/MoE computation, or final outputs, and performs only bounded state replay for numerical alignment. Per round, HARTS batches all branches into one packed call, propagates gradients through differentiable state handoffs, supports activation recomputation, and restores per-token log-probabilities. For deterministic, no-token-drop top-$k$ MoE routing, semantic multiplicities restore MoE-objective token weights and load statistics. Existing RL objectives retain their interface. To our knowledge, HARTS is the first system to demonstrate arbitrary-rollout-tree prefix-sharing speedups on a real hybrid-attention model. On an Agentic RL workload generated from SWE-bench tasks, HARTS achieves $4.81$--$4.87\times$ forward/backward/gradient speedup with activation recomputation across multiple parallel configurations. Its numerical differences are comparable to baseline self-rerun variation, and its reward trend is similar to the baseline over the first 120 steps of $τ^3$-Bench training.

</details>


### [214] [Biologically Inspired Mechanisms for Facilitating Grokking in Multilayer Perceptrons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28184)
*Florin Leon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Biologically inspired activity regulation mechanisms can aid grokking in MLPs; homeostasis yields the strongest generalization benefit, followed by sparsification; other mechanisms have smaller, inconsistent effects; suggests explicit neuron utilization and connectivity regulation can promote generalizable representations, with potential for scaling to large models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grokking involves a delayed shift from memorization to generalization. The study asks whether biologically plausible mechanisms that regulate hidden-layer computation can actively facilitate this transition.

Method: Augment a multilayer perceptron with input gating, structural plasticity, gain modulation, threshold modulation, homeostasis, lateral inhibition, and activation decorrelation. Evaluate via systematic ablations on two grokking benchmarks: sparse parity and noisy XOR classification.

Result: The mechanisms contribute unevenly to generalization. Homeostasis provides the strongest and most consistent benefit, while structural sparsification is the second major mechanism. Other mechanisms show smaller or less consistent effects. Overall, explicit regulation of neuron utilization and effective connectivity improves emergence of generalizable internal computation.

Conclusion: Explicit regulation of neuron utilization and effective connectivity can promote generalizable representations during grokking. This motivates broader investigation of biologically inspired activity regulation and adaptive sparsification, including application to large language models to accelerate generalizable representations and reduce optimization time for robust generalization.

Abstract: Grokking is a delayed transition from memorization to generalization that is often accompanied by substantial reorganization of internal representations. This paper studies whether biologically inspired mechanisms, many of which are not commonly incorporated into artificial neural networks, can actively promote this transition by regulating hidden-layer computation at the levels of neuronal activity, response, and effective connectivity. We augment a multilayer perceptron with input gating, structural plasticity, gain modulation, threshold modulation, homeostasis, lateral inhibition, and activation decorrelation, and evaluate these mechanisms through systematic ablations on two established grokking benchmarks: sparse parity and noisy XOR classification. The results show that the mechanisms contribute unequally to generalization. Homeostasis provides the strongest and most consistent benefit, while structural sparsification emerges as the second major mechanism. The remaining biologically inspired mechanisms have smaller or less consistent effects in the present experiments. For both problems, the results support the common principle that explicit regulation of neuron utilization and effective connectivity can improve the emergence of generalizable internal computation. These findings motivate broader investigation of biologically inspired activity regulation and adaptive sparsification, including in large language models, where they may accelerate the development of generalizable representations and reduce the optimization time required for robust generalization.

</details>


### [215] [Beyond Flat Netlist: Hierarchical Graph Representation Learning for Scalable Analysis of Sequential Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28188)
*Jingyi Zhou,Zhengyuan Shi,Jiaying Zhu,Ziyang Zheng,Qiang Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepSeq3 introduces a hierarchical circuit representation learning framework with a two-level GNN architecture and a state-centric pre-training objective, achieving 18% reduction in BMC solving time on large-scale benchmarks while ensuring correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CRL struggles with industrial-scale netlists and lacks explicit modeling of register-level temporal dynamics; there is a need to scale CRL to large circuits and incorporate state transitions.

Method: Represent circuits as fine-grained combinational subgraphs partitioned by flip-flops plus a high-level Super-Node Graph; employ dual GNNs to learn local and global representations; pre-train with a state-centric objective predicting reachability between FF states to encode temporal behavior.

Result: On large-scale benchmarks, the approach yields superior scalability and richer representations; achieves 18% reduction in bounded model checking time with correctness guarantees.

Conclusion: Hierarchical CRL with temporal-aware pre-training improves scalability and efficacy for EDA tasks like BMC, suggesting a practical pathway for industrial deployment.

Abstract: Circuit Representation Learning (CRL) offers a powerful paradigm to guide and optimize core Electronic Design Automation (EDA) tasks, but its practical adoption is hindered by the immense scale of industrial netlists and a failure to explicitly model register-level temporal dynamics. To overcome these barriers, we introduce DeepSeq3, a novel hierarchical framework that abstracts circuits into a two-level representation: fine-grained combinational subgraphs partitioned by flip-flops (FFs), and a high-level Super-Node Graph (SNG) that models the register-transfer structure. A dual Graph Neural Network (GNN) architecture learns representations at both levels, capturing local Boolean logic and global state transitions. Crucially, we introduce a state-centric pre-training scheme that predicts the reachability between FF states, endowing the model with a deep understanding of temporal behavior. Demonstrated on large-scale benchmarks, DeepSeq3's approach yields superior scalability and richer representations, reducing bounded model checking (BMC) solving time by 18% while guaranteeing correctness.

</details>


### [216] [Performative Privacy: When Differential Privacy Maximizes Utility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28198)
*Uddalak Mukherjee,Edwige Cyffers,Yann Chevaleyre*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Finite DP can outperform non-private estimation in the long run due to feedback between privacy leakage and participation in a performative learning setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Protecting user data can influence participation and thereby long-term utility. The paper formalizes this by coupling differential privacy with performative effects on data collection.

Method: A simple repeated mean-estimation model where agents may leave if their data leaks; privacy via DP mechanisms; analysis of dynamics plus numerical experiments to compare private vs non-private regimes.

Result: There exists a finite privacy budget under which private, DP-based learning yields higher long-term utility than non-private learning when leakage-to-participation feedback is strong.

Conclusion: Differential privacy can be optimal for long-term utility in presence of performative feedback, suggesting DP's value beyond protection to include system-level performance under data leakage dynamics.

Abstract: Privacy-preserving learning is often motivated by the idea that protecting users' data can preserve trust and thus participation, improving utility in the long term. However, this claim has not been formalized so far. In parallel, performative learning provides a framework for studying learning systems whose deployment affects the data they later observe. In this work, we bring these two perspectives together and introduce \emph{performative privacy}, where data leakage reduces future participation. We study a simple model where agents repeatedly contribute data for mean estimation but may leave the system when their data is leaked. Privacy is implemented through differentially private mechanisms, creating a trade-off between estimation noise and future participation. We show, through a theoretical study of the dynamics and numerical experiments, that a finite privacy budget can outperform non-private estimation in the long term when the feedback loop between leakage and participation is sufficiently strong. This provides first evidence that differential privacy can be optimal not only as a protection mechanism, but also from the perspective of long-term utility.

</details>


### [217] [Generalized Context in Cross Attention for Transfer Learning of Disjoint Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28209)
*Kazi F. Akhter,Ibna Kowsar,Manar D. Samad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Cross-domain Attention Transfer Learning (CATTLE) to enable transfer learning for tabular data without shared features using generalized context via transformer projection weights; shows superiority over baselines across ten data pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transfer learning for tabular data is hindered by heterogeneity of feature types, structures, and semantics; existing methods assume shared features across data tables, which is often impractical.

Method: Introduce generalized context learning using transformer key/value/query projection weights. Cross-domain Attention Transfer Learning (CATTLE) operates in a data-agnostic manner by having key weights from the source interact with target query weights to transfer context without requiring shared features. Evaluated on ten disjoint source-target dataset pairs.

Result: CATTLE is rank-wise and statistically superior to nine state-of-the-art baselines, including ML, DL, and transfer-learning methods with large pre-trained models; best average rank 2.9; ~3.7% AUROC gain over baselines; code released.

Conclusion: Generalized context learning via cross-domain attention transfer enables effective knowledge transfer for heterogeneous tabular data without shared features, outperforming strong baselines on multiple dataset pairs.

Abstract: Unlike images and text, applying transfer learning to tabular data is challenging due to heterogeneity in feature types, structures, and semantics across disparate domains. Existing methods assume shared features across data tables to enable knowledge transfer between domains, which is unrealistic in practice. \mds{This paper introduces generalized context learning to remove the requirement of shared features across domains. The generalized context captured by transformer projection weights for $key$, $value$, and $query$ provides rule-based generalization rather than the domain-specific context conventionally learned from transformer activations. Projection weights for $key$ from the source domain interact with the weight for $query$ in the target domain to achieve Cross-domain Attention Transfer Learning (CATTLE) in a data-agnostic manner. Our experiments on ten pairs of disjoint source-target data sets show that CATTLE can learn generalized context from a single source data set and is rank-wise and statistically superior to nine state-of-the-art baselines, including machine learning, deep learning, and transfer learning methods using large-scale pre-trained models. CATTLE achieves the best average rank (2.9) and delivers a 3.7% average AUROC gain over the baseline methods.} The CATTLE source code is available at https://tinyurl.com/pr5s8ywn.

</details>


### [218] [D-TAIA: Domain-Aware LLM Adaptation for Multi-Task Predictive Process Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28236)
*Sjoerd van Straten,Christine Jacob,Marwan Hassani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: D-TAIA enables joint next activity and remaining-time prediction in predictive process monitoring using a compact FM backbone with domain-aware pre-training and TAIA inference, achieving state-of-the-art or competitive results with a 10M-parameter model across four real-world logs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To tackle data scarcity, high process entropy, and distributional shift that degrade existing methods, and to address limitations of FM-based approaches that struggle with distributional shift or use regression heads misaligned with continuous time tasks.

Method: Joint next activity and remaining-time prediction via parameter-efficient fine-tuning of a foundation model backbone. Key components: domain-aware triplet loss (DATL) pre-training, FAISS-based nearest-neighbor retrieval for remaining time, and TAIA inference to preserve pre-trained sequential reasoning during fine-tuning.

Result: D-TAIA achieves state-of-the-art or competitive performance compared to a fine-tuned LLM and an RNN baseline across four real-world event logs. Ablation shows transferability of NLP/vision techniques to PPM with a 10M-parameter backbone, though the contribution of each component varies with dataset entropy.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the feasibility of adapting foundation models to multi-task predictive process monitoring under data scarcity, entropy, and distributional shift. Introduces DATL, FAISS-based time retrieval, and TAIA as effective components, with results suggesting further gains from larger models and entropy-aware designs.

Abstract: Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM) enables organizations to forecast future process behavior, such as the next activity and remaining time of ongoing cases. In practice, three conditions cause existing methods to degrade, namely data scarcity, high process entropy and distributional shift. While Foundation Models (FMs), especially Large Language Models (LLMs), offer a new paradigm through broad sequential reasoning, adapting them to multi-task PPM under these conditions remains an open challenge. Existing FM-based approaches either lack mechanisms for handling distributional shift or rely on direct regression heads that can be structurally misaligned with continuous time prediction tasks. This paper introduces D-TAIA (Domain-aware Training and Attention-based Inference Architecture), a framework for a joint next activity and remaining time prediction task via parameter-efficient fine-tuning of an FM backbone. Our approach combines domain-aware triplet loss (DATL) pre-training with FAISS-based nearest neighbor retrieval for remaining time prediction, and adopts the TAIA inference strategy to preserve pre-trained sequential reasoning during fine-tuning. Evaluated across four real-world event logs, D-TAIA consistently shows SOTA or competitive performance compared to a fine-tuned LLM and a recurrent neural network baseline. Ablation studies confirm that techniques from NLP and computer vision can be transferred effectively to PPM with only a 10M-parameter backbone, though component contributions vary by dataset entropy.

</details>


### [219] [Efficient Online Continual Foundation Model Fine-Tuning for Predictive Process Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28237)
*Sjoerd van Straten,Marwan Hassani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: COMPASS introduces online continual fine-tuning of Foundation Models for Predictive Process Monitoring, using adaptive task boundary detection and a unified knowledge subspace; achieves improvements over non-FM baselines across nine streams with manageable overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address cold-start and drift adaptation in Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM); leverage Foundation Models in an online continual setting, which is underexplored in process mining.

Method: Loss-plateau drift detection is adapted to autonomously identify task boundaries in event streams. COMPASS maintains a unified knowledge subspace that combines pre-trained and task-specific directions and performs online fine-tuning of foundation models within this subspace (adaptive subspaces). Evaluation on nine event streams with synthetic and real-world drift, across task-free and task-aware settings, multiple backbones, and consistent hyperparameters.

Result: COMPASS outperforms three state-of-the-art non-foundation-model competitors and two update-strategy baselines. Gains are especially strong on streams with recurrent drift and long-running, complex cases. Computational overhead is acceptable compared with non-FM competitors.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility and advantages of online continual fine-tuning of foundation models for PPM under concept drift, via adaptive drift detection and subspace knowledge consolidation. Indicates robustness to recurrent and long-running drift scenarios; suggests future work on scalability and broader drift patterns.

Abstract: Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM) models are increasingly deployed in dynamic environments where concept drift causes the underlying process distribution to shift over time. While recent work has moved toward online continual learning, existing methods train compact, task-specific networks entirely from scratch, leaving a persistent cold-start problem. Foundation Models (FMs) offer a compelling solution to this problem, but their continual fine-tuning in the process mining domain remains unexplored. We propose COMPASS (Continual Online foundation Model-based PPM with Adaptive SubSpaces), the first framework for online continual fine-tuning of FMs for PPM. COMPASS adapts loss-plateau drift detection to autonomously identify task boundaries in event streams and maintains a unified knowledge subspace including both pre-trained and task-specific directions. We evaluate our approach on nine event streams covering synthetic and real-world concept drift scenarios, across task-free and task-aware settings with multiple backbones and with consistent hyperparameter tuning across all methods. Our approach outperforms three SOTA non-FM competitors and two update strategy baselines, with particularly strong gains on streams exhibiting recurrent drift and complex, long-running cases, while incurring acceptable computational overhead compared to the non-FM competitors.

</details>


### [220] [Spectral Features Dominate BCG Respiratory-Event Detection: A Large-Scale Patient-Independent Comparison of Feature Groups in Sleep Apnea Patients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28242)
*Israel Campero Jurado,Zoe Bousraou,Lara Benning,Sara Padilla Neira,Alexander Breuss,Robert Riener,Esther Irene Schwarz,Elisabeth Wilhelm*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Frequency-domain features (0.1–0.4 Hz breathing-band power) and time-domain features provide the majority of discriminative information for respiratory-event detection in unobtrusive ballistocardiography (BCG); a compact feature subset yields high, patient-independent performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify which BCG feature groups most effectively discriminate respiratory events for long-term, patient-independent sleep-apnea monitoring using unobtrusive sensing.

Method: 512-sensor capacitive pressure mat recorded with respiratory polygraphy in 155 patients (in-hospital evaluation for obstructive sleep apnea). Six spatial channels yielded a 191-dimensional feature vector spanning general statistics, time-domain, frequency-domain, wavelet, frame-energy, and nonlinear complexity descriptors. Leave-one-patient-out cross-validation for binary classification of respiratory-event windows vs. reference windows. Random Forest and Histogram Gradient Boosting used.

Result: AUC-ROC: ~0.967–0.969; AUC-PR: ~0.977–0.979 for both methods. Frequency-domain features dominated: 0.1–0.4 Hz breathing-band power contributed 30.3% of discriminative information across channels; FFT spectral-shape features added 15.1%. Time-domain features (AUC and curve-length) contributed 21.5%; wavelet/nonlinear features together added 10.4% across 59 features. Frequency-domain and time-domain features together accounted for 67% of total discriminative information; a compact subset suffices for clinically relevant performance and guidelines future feature selection in BCG systems.

Conclusion: A compact, interpretable feature subset—heavily weighted toward frequency-domain and time-domain descriptors—achieves strong, patient-independent detection of respiratory events in BCG-based sleep monitoring, supporting focused feature selection for practical BCG systems.

Abstract: Unobtrusive ballistocardiographic (BCG) sensing is a promising modality for long-term sleep-apnea monitoring, yet it remains unclear which signal features are most discriminative for respiratory-event detection. We present a literature-guided, patient-independent comparison of ten BCG feature groups using a 512-sensor capacitive pressure mat recorded simultaneously with respiratory polygraphy in 155 patients (52 female, 103 male) undergoing in-hospital evaluation for obstructive sleep apnea. Features were extracted from six spatially distinct signal channels, yielding a 191-dimensional feature vector spanning general statistical, time-domain, frequency-domain, wavelet, frame-energy, and nonlinear complexity descriptors. Under strict leave-one-patient-out cross-validation for binary classification of respiratory-event windows versus event-free reference windows, Random Forest and Histogram Gradient Boosting achieved AUC-ROC of 0.967 and 0.969 and AUC-PR of 0.977 and 0.979, respectively. Feature-importance analysis revealed that frequency-domain features dominate discrimination: breathing-band power in the 0.1-0.4 Hz range accounted for 30.3% of total discriminative information across all spatial channels, and Fast Fourier Transform spectral-shape descriptors of the adaptively preprocessed channel contributed a further 15.1%. AUC and curve-length features provided the main complementary time-domain evidence (21.5%), whereas wavelet-derived and nonlinear features contributed smaller secondary effects (10.4% combined across 59 features). Frequency-domain and time-domain features together accounted for 67% of total discriminative information, demonstrating that a compact, interpretable subset of the full feature library achieves clinically relevant performance under patient-independent validation and providing an empirical basis for feature selection in future BCG systems.

</details>


### [221] [SinkSLOT: Sinkhorn via Sparse Lifted Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28262)
*Ian Hsieh,Soumya Snigdha Kundu,Tom Vercauteren,Reuben Dorent*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SinkSLOT introduces a sparsified entropic OT scheme by using an expected sliced lifted transport plan with a non-independent prior, achieving provable convergence and per-iteration cost O(LN) (L slices). It yields speedups over state-of-the-art EOT methods and provides a debiasing-free divergence for gradient flows; code is released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Sinkhorn-Knopp for entropic OT has O(N^2) per-iteration cost on discrete measures and relies on an independent prior, which biases mass toward high-cost edges at moderate regularization. There is a need for faster, more faithful regularization and scalable OT solvers.

Method: Propose expected sliced lifted transport plan to sparsify the Gibbs kernel, using L random slices to form a sparse coupling. Prove convergence of SinkSLOT; show that each Sinkhorn iteration costs O(LN); the objective is a divergence that requires no debiasing. Demonstrate on synthetic benchmarks and in a gradient-flow context; release code publicly.

Result: Theoretical guarantees: convergence established; computational complexity reduced to O(LN) per iteration; empirical results show substantial speedups over dense and sparse EOT methods; utility in gradient-flow experiments demonstrated.

Conclusion: SinkSLOT is a principled, scalable alternative to standard EOT solvers, offering convergence guarantees and practical speedups by leveraging a non-independent prior and sliced lifting to induce sparsity without debiasing. Code availability enables replication and broader adoption.

Abstract: Entropic optimal transport (EOT) has been shown to offer a computationally tractable approximation to exact optimal transport. However, the standard Sinkhorn-Knopp algorithm has two main limitations. First, given discrete measures with $N$ points, each iteration requires $O(N^2)$ operations, which restricts its use on large-scale datasets (e.g. $N\geq10^4$). Second, it uses the independent coupling as a reference measure for regularisation. This assigns mass to high-cost transport edges at moderate regularisation strengths. We propose SinkSLOT, which addresses both limitations by putting forth the expected sliced lifted transport plan as a natural way to sparsify the Gibbs kernel with a non-independent prior coupling. We prove that: 1) SinkSLOT converges; 2) with $L$ slices, each resulting sparse Sinkhorn iteration costs $O(LN)$; and 3) the resulting objective is a divergence requiring no debiasing. Experiments on synthetic benchmarks show that SinkSLOT delivers substantial speedups over state-of-the-art dense and sparse EOT methods. We also demonstrate the applicability of the proposed divergence in a gradient flow experiment. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/cai4cai/SinkSLOT.

</details>


### [222] [Residual-Guided Randomized Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28267)
*Mushir Akhtar,M. Tanveer,Mohd. Arshad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Residual-guided greedy construction for randomized neural networks improves performance by progressively selecting hidden units via a closed-form residual decrease criterion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Randomized neural networks rely on a single random fixed feature layer, which can yield redundant representations and underutilize model capacity. A principled, data-driven method to construct hidden units could improve accuracy and stability while preserving analytic tractability.

Method: Iteratively build the hidden layer by: (i) sampling a pool of random candidate units, (ii) scoring each candidate by the exact decrease in the ridge-regularized objective they induce, (iii) selecting the top k candidates, and (iv) refitting the readout in closed form using direct input links. This creates a progressive training process with guaranteed monotonic decrease of the training objective. The approach is model-agnostic for the scoring/refitting loop; only candidate generation is architecture-specific.

Result: Empirically, the method consistently outperforms baselines on 71 UCI datasets across binary and multiclass classification, in terms of accuracy, stability, and overall ranking.

Conclusion: A simple, broadly applicable residual-guided construction method yields progressive, monotonic training improvements for randomized neural networks, without sacrificing closed-form learnability of the readout; performance gains are demonstrated across a wide benchmark suite.

Abstract: Randomized neural networks enable fast and analytically tractable training by fixing the input to hidden layer parameters at random and learning the output weights in closed form; however, their performance critically depends on a single uninformed draw of hidden units. This one shot and task uninformed feature construction often leads to redundant representations and suboptimal utilization of model capacity. To address this limitation, we propose a simple and broadly applicable residual guided procedure that greedily constructs the hidden layer using a closed form residual decrease criterion. At each stage, we (i) generate a pool of random candidate units, (ii) score each candidate by the exact reduction it induces in the ridge regularized objective, (iii) select the top k units, and (iv) refit the readout in closed form using the standard design with direct input links. This procedure yields a progressive training process with a guaranteed monotonic decrease of the training objective. The method is model agnostic: only the candidate generation is architecture specific, while the scoring selection refitting loop is shared across models.
