Extensive experiments on 71 benchmark datasets from the UCI repository, covering both binary and multiclass classification tasks, demonstrate that the proposed residual-guided models consistently outperform their baseline counterparts in terms of accuracy, stability, and overall ranking performance.

</details>


### [223] [Learning to Transfer Across Modes: Towards Unified Urban Mobility Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28273)
*Yixuan Zhao,Man Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TransMod is a unified cross-modal urban mobility forecasting framework that aligns heterogeneous spatial structures and transfers learned spatio-temporal patterns from data-rich modes to data-scarce ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting demand jointly across multiple mobility modes is hard due to heterogeneity, spatial misalignment, and limited data for newer modes; existing methods mainly handle single modes and assume compatible spatial structures.

Method: Construct a shared zone-level spatial representation to align different spatial granularities; learn transferable spatio-temporal patterns from source modes; adapt to target modes with limited histories.

Result: Empirical results on real-world datasets show TransMod consistently outperforms existing approaches and is robust under limited target data.

Conclusion: TransMod enables effective knowledge transfer across heterogeneous mobility modes by reducing structural mismatch, with broad applicability to multi-modal urban mobility forecasting.

Abstract: Urban transportation systems consist of multiple mobility modes that coexist within the same city and exhibit complex interdependencies, leading to correlated demand dynamics across modes. However, forecasting demand jointly across different modes remains challenging due to substantial heterogeneity in space and the limited availability of historical data for emerging modes. Existing forecasting methods are largely developed for individual mobility modes and implicitly assume compatible spatial structures between source and target systems, which severely restricts their applicability in multi-modal settings. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{TransMod}, a unified framework for urban mobility demand forecasting that enables effective knowledge transfer across heterogeneous mobility modes. TransMod constructs a shared zone-level spatial representation that aligns mobility systems with different spatial granularities into a common space, thereby reducing structural mismatch and distributional shift. Built on this unified representation, TransMod further learns transferable spatio-temporal patterns from data-rich source modes and adapts them to data-scarce target modes, alleviating the dependence on extensive target-domain histories. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that TransMod consistently outperforms existing approaches and provides robust forecasting performance under limited target data.

</details>


### [224] [An algebraic proof of Colombo's difference-power determinant conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28274)
*Kun Li,Li Tie,Peng Wang,Zihan Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper completes Colombo's conjecture by proving det A_d(λ) ≠ 0 for all d ≥ n-1 when n is even and λ has distinct coordinates, hence rank A_d(λ) = min{n, d+1}.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand the rank/determinant behavior of distance-power matrices A_d(λ) and extend Colombo's early results; connect matrix theory with real algebraic geometry.

Method: Assume a nontrivial kernel vector for A_d(λ). Transform into a real binary form and analyze its projective real linear factors. Compare with the real Waring length to derive a contradiction for odd d ≥ n+1; deduce nonsingularity. Combine with even-d cases from prior results.

Result: Proved nonsingularity for all odd exponents d ≥ n+1, completing Colombo's conjecture; hence rank A_d(λ) = min{n, d+1} for all d.

Conclusion: This work closes Colombo's conjecture for even n and distinct λ, using a novel link between distance-power matrices and real binary forms/Waring theory; suggests further geometric interpretation and potential extensions.

Abstract: Let $n\ge2$ be even, let $λ=(λ_1,\ldots,λ_n)\in\mathbb{R}^n$ have pairwise distinct coordinates, and define the difference-power matrix \[ A_d(λ) := \bigl[(λ_r-λ_s)^d\bigr]_{r,s=1}^n, \qquad d\in\mathbb{N}. \] In 1928, Colombo proved that $\det A_{n-1}(λ)\ne0$---and hence $\det A_{n-1}(λ)>0$---and that $\operatorname{rank} A_d(λ)=d+1$ for $0\le d<n-1$. He conjectured that \[ \det A_d(λ)\ne0 \qquad\text{for every } d\ge n-1. \] For even $d$, the conjectured nonsingularity follows from previously published results on distance-power matrices. The remaining open cases were therefore the supercritical odd exponents $d\ge n+1$. We prove nonsingularity for all these odd exponents, thereby completing Colombo's conjecture. Consequently, \[ \operatorname{rank} A_d(λ)=\min\{n,d+1\} \qquad(d\in\mathbb{N}). \] Our proof converts a hypothetical kernel vector into a real binary form having more projective real linear factors, counted with multiplicity, than its real Waring length permits.

</details>


### [225] [Parser States Already Know: Structure-Conditioned KV Persistence for Structured Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28276)
*Linze Wu,Xinrui Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Parser-aware KV persistence (PASK) uses parser-derived structure to guide layer-specific KV storage, aligning persistence with task-level structural risk under constrained decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured generation relies on correct schema decisions, but existing KV compression ignores the structural signals used by constrained decoding; there is a mismatch between model-side KV sensitivity and task-level risk.

Method: Introduce PASK: (1) offline calibration derives a persistence policy from task-error sensitivity and attention-output distortion; (2) use parser-derived structure to drive layer-group-specific KV persistence decisions; (3) lightweight structure-conditioned lookup online; (4) evaluate on Qwen3-4B with constrained decoding Budget~0.33 KV.

Result: With a total KV budget of 0.33, PASK outperforms the strongest compressed baseline by 17.39 percentage points across eight BFCL non-live and Live subcategories on Qwen3-4B. In end-to-end serving, PASK achieves up to 2.2x higher throughput and 3.3x lower TPOT, while using 0.53x the peak GPU memory of Full KV.

Conclusion: PASK provides a structure-aware KV persistence mechanism that aligns KV allocation with task-level risk, yielding significant accuracy gains and efficiency improvements under constrained KV budgets.

Abstract: Structured generation underpins large language model (LLM) agents that produce JSON, SQL, and function calls, where a single wrong field can cause the downstream action to fail. Constrained decoding already tracks parser transitions to enforce formal validity, and these transitions expose how generated tokens participate in schema-critical decisions such as required fields, arguments, and structural boundaries under the active grammar. Existing KV compression largely leaves this task-relevant structural signal unused. We introduce PASK (Parser-Aware Structural KV Persistence), which turns parser-derived structure into layer-group-specific KV persistence decisions. PASK addresses the mismatch between model-side KV sensitivity and task-level structured risk by using task-error sensitivity to set minimum protection floors and attention-output distortion to allocate residual KV capacity. An offline calibration stage compiles these signals into a persistence policy, leaving only lightweight structure-conditioned lookup online. At a targe total KV budget of 0.33, PASK outperforms the strongest compressed baseline by 17.39 percentage points on average across eight BFCL non-live and Live subcategories on Qwen3-4B. In end-to-end serving, PASK achieves up to 2.2x higher throughput and 3.3x lower TPOT, while using 0.53x the peak GPU memory of Full KV.

</details>


### [226] [VISTA: Verifier-Informed Student-to-Teacher Adaptation for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28306)
*Zewen Ding,Zezhong Wu,Zhou Tao,Shida Wang,Shizhuo Hou,YongXiang Hua,Haoyu Cao,Linli Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VISTA enhances OPSD by aligning teacher outputs to verified outcomes, adapting only the top-k divergence positions; yields consistent performance gains over OPSD across multiple tasks and model scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard OPSD uses a fixed teacher target during the student rollout, which can misalign with valid problem-only reasoning when privileged conditioning is imperfect. Outcome-verified rollouts offer a way to adjust the teacher to better support student reasoning.

Method: Introduce Verifier-Informed Student-to-Teacher Adaptation (VISTA). Retain the OPSD student update and rollout/loss; within each verified rollout, adapt the teacher toward the student distribution, restricted to the top-k positions with the largest teacher–student KL divergence. No additional sampling or separate reward objective is introduced.

Result: Across AIME24, AIME25, and HMMT25 using Qwen3 models at 1.7B, 4B, and 8B, VISTA achieves the highest Avg@12 at every scale, improving over OPSD by 0.6, 0.7, and 2.1 points respectively.

Conclusion: Outcome-verified supervision and student-to-teacher adaptation are promising directions for OPSD; VISTA demonstrates the value of adapting the teacher to the student via outcome-verified signals without extra sampling.

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) improves reasoning by training a problem-only student on its own rollouts using dense token-level supervision from a privileged teacher that also sees a reference solution. However, standard OPSD treats the teacher distribution as a fixed target along the student's rollout and updates only the student %, although -- even though privileged conditioning does not guarantee that the teacher always provides the most appropriate target for problem-only reasoning. This one-way supervision can therefore misdirect the student when the teacher distribution is misaligned with valid student reasoning. We therefore introduce Verifier-Informed Student-to-Teacher Adaptation (VISTA), which preserves the standard OPSD student update while using outcome-verified rollouts to adapt the teacher toward the student distribution. Within each verified rollout, VISTA further restricts this adaptation to the top-$k$ positions with the largest teacher--student KL divergence. Notably, VISTA reuses the rollout and loss function from standard OPSD, introducing no additional sampling or separate reward objective. Across AIME24, AIME25, and HMMT25 with Qwen3 models at 1.7B, 4B, and 8B, VISTA achieves the highest Avg@12 at every scale, improving over OPSD by $0.6$, $0.7$, and $2.1$ points, respectively. These results demonstrate the value of student supervision from outcome-verified rollouts and highlight student-to-teacher adaptation as a promising direction for OPSD.

</details>


### [227] [Deriving Scaling Laws for OpenEuroLLM Models: Learning Rate, Batch Size and Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28308)
*Niccolò Ajroldi,Diana Alexandra Onutu,Haider Al-Tahan,Jörg Franke,Sampo Pyysalo,Jenia Jitsev,Aaron Klein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes scaling laws for learning rate and batch size in pretraining dense LLMs, with a marginal-evolution model across capacity and data scale, plus a Warmup-Stable-Decay LR schedule, LR annealing, and loss–capacity/data interactions; delivers a baseline and open-sourced pretraining runs for OpenEuroLLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and predict how training hyperparameters interact with model size and data scale to optimize efficiency/performance, enabling principled hyperparameter transfer and reproducibility for OpenEuroLLM.

Method: Extensive pretraining experiments across varying model capacities and data budgets; develop a marginal-scaling model tying LR and batch size to capacity/data; evaluate Warmup-Stable-Decay LR schedules and the gains from annealing across hyperparameters; assess transfer of optimal LR/batch between phases; test scaling forms modeling loss as a function of capacity and dataset size; open-source the full set of pretraining runs.

Result: The marginal model captures undertraining and overtraining regimes and supports both joint and marginal scaling insights; annealing yields gains over a wide hyperparameter range; partial transfer between stable and decay phases is observed; scaling forms effectively describe interactions between capacity and data size; establishes a practical baseline/scaling procedure for OpenEuroLLM and provides openly available pretraining runs.

Conclusion: Provides actionable scaling guidelines for future OpenEuroLLM development, contributes to scaling-law literature by incorporating LR/batch size marginals and phase-transfer considerations, and enhances reproducibility through open-source data. Potential limitations include generalization beyond English-prevalent corpora and the need for broader validation across tasks and architectures.

Abstract: We study the scaling behavior of learning rate and batch size in pretraining dense large language models on English-prevalent corpora. Beyond scaling \textit{jointly optimal} learning rates and batch sizes, we investigate their \textit{marginal} evolution with model capacity and data scale and develop a model that captures these relationships. As we employ a Warmup-Stable-Decay learning rate schedule, we further investigate the gains from learning rate annealing over a broad range of hyperparameters settings, models and data budgets, and whether the optimal learning rate and batch size \textit{transfer} between the stable and decay phases. Finally, we characterize the dependence of loss on model capacity and dataset size, evaluating recently proposed scaling forms that explicitly model their interaction. We find these approaches particularly effective at capturing both undertraining and overtraining regimes across our experiments. This study establishes a first baseline and scaling procedure for the development of future OpenEuroLLM models. We open-source the complete collection of pretraining runs used in this study.

</details>


### [228] [SymboLLM-FE: LLM-Accelerated Symbolic Regression for Automated Feature Engineering on Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28408)
*Zi-Jian Cheng,Zi-Yi Jia,Zhi Zhou,Yu-Feng Li,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SymboLLM-FE integrates symbolic regression with LLM-driven refinement to generate interpretable, high-utility features for tabular data, achieving state-of-the-art AutoFE performance with reduced iterations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the two main drawbacks of existing AutoFE: poor interpretability in traditional AutoFE and high iterative cost (and bias risk) in LLM-based AutoFE.

Method: Leverage symbolic regression to extract expressive, target-correlated formulas; then refine these formulas with LLMs guided by prior knowledge to ensure interpretability. Introduce a prior-grounded refinement mechanism that minimizes LLM calls (single-digit calls) and mitigates bias/hallucination.

Result: Empirical results on six real-world datasets and four Kaggle competitions show that SymboLLM-FE outperforms existing AutoFE baselines.

Conclusion: The approach effectively combines symbolic regression with LLMs to produce interpretable, high-utility features for tabular data, addressing interpretability and efficiency challenges in AutoFE.

Abstract: Tabular data, as a core data format in machine learning, often lacks the discriminative power needed for high-performance modeling due to insufficient feature informativeness. Automated Feature Engineering (AutoFE) overcomes this by automating feature generation and selection, ensuring both model performance and operational efficiency. However, traditional AutoFE often yield features with poor interpretability because they rely on blind mathematical transformations, while large language models (LLM)-based AutoFE faces challenges in requiring costly multi-round iterations to generate high-utility features to effectively enhance model performance, compounded by inherent risks of bias and hallucination. In this paper, we combine symbolic regression with LLMs for feature engineering (SymboLLM-FE) to solve these challenges. We extract mathematically expressive formulas strongly correlated with the target via symbolic regression, which can enhance model performance, then refine them by LLMs with rich prior knowledge to ensure interpretability. Empirical results on six real-world datasets and four Kaggle competitions demonstrate that SymboLLM-FE outperforms existing AutoFE. SymboLLM-FE also addresses the dual challenges of poor interpretability and numerous iterations by employing a statistical prior-grounded LLM refinement mechanism and single-digit LLM calls.

</details>


### [229] [Euclidean Fourier Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28425)
*Nathanael Bosch,Niklas Frederik Schmitz,Michael F. Herbst*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Domain-agnostic extension of Fourier neural operators (EFNO) that parameterizes the spectral kernel as a continuous function of the wavevector, enabling consistent operator learning across periodic domains of varying shape and size. Demonstrated on a heat equation and learning exchange-correlation potentials across different crystal structures, with generalization to unseen grid sizes and domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fourier neural operators (FNOs) are grid- and domain-agnostic in theory but in practice the discrete spectral weights are tied to integer Fourier modes that depend on the domain. When applied to a different domain, the same trained weights act at different wavevectors, effectively changing the operator. This limits cross-domain transfer, which is important for applications requiring domain generalization.

Method: Introduce Euclidean Fourier neural operators (EFNOs) by parameterizing the spectral kernel as a continuous function of the physical wavevector k, rather than discretized mode indices. This yields a domain-independent operator that can be evaluated consistently on periodic domains of varying shape and size. The kernel K(k) is learned as a function over the continuous wavevector, enabling transfer across domains without re-training for each domain.

Result: EFNO learns operators that act consistently across periodic domains of varying shape and size, demonstrated by generalization to unseen grid sizes and domains. Empirical evaluation on a simple heat equation and a materials science task (learning exchange-correlation potentials across crystal structures) shows improved cross-domain transfer compared to standard FNOs.

Conclusion: EFNO provides a domain-independent extension of Fourier neural operators, enabling robust transfer across periodic domains of different sizes and shapes. This broadens the applicability of operator learning to multi-domain PDE problems, with potential benefits for physics-informed ML tasks and materials science simulations. Future work could explore performance on higher-dimensional problems, theoretical guarantees of domain invariance, and more diverse PDE classes.

Abstract: Fourier neural operators (FNOs) provide an efficient framework for learning mappings between function spaces as they are, by construction, independent of the grid resolution at which they are trained and evaluated. However, FNOs are not independent of the periodic domain they are applied to: their discrete spectral weights are indexed by integer Fourier mode numbers, which correspond to physical wavevectors. When applied to a different domain, the same trained weights act at different wavevectors, and the FNO silently represents a different operator. This makes FNOs unsuitable for tasks where transfer across domains is crucial. We propose Euclidean Fourier neural operators~(EFNOs) as a domain-independent alternative to FNOs. By parameterizing the spectral kernel as a continuous function of the physical wavevector, the EFNO can learn operators that act consistently across periodic domains of varying shape and size. We evaluate the EFNO on a simple heat equation and on a practically relevant materials science task of learning exchange-correlation potentials across different crystal structures, and demonstrate that the EFNO is able to generalize to unseen grid sizes and domains.

</details>


### [230] [Curvature-Conditioned Multiscale Momentum with Sphere Constraints for LLM Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28442)
*Shuchen Zhu,Yuxin Fang,Mingze Wang,Kun Yuan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Curvature-conditioned multiscale momentum with sphere constraints accelerates training along flat directions in LLM pretraining, improving Muon optimizer performance across architectures and model sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretraining incurs high compute costs; gradients are noise-dominated and the loss landscape is ill-conditioned, hindering convergence. Existing adaptive optimizers (e.g., AdamW, Muon) rely on gradient normalization and do not sufficiently address curvature along flat directions, which dominate final loss reduction.

Method: Propose curvature-conditioned multiscale momentum (CCMSM) applied only along flat directions to boost progress where small eigenvalues slow learning. The method combines a slow-decay component for noise reduction with a fast-decay component for rapid curvature adaptation. A sphere constraint is imposed to prevent parameter inflation and excessive effective learning-rate decay that can arise from naive combinations.

Result: Extensive experiments show substantial acceleration of Muon across diverse architectures (dense, MoE) and model sizes (0.12B–2.3B parameters). Theoretical analysis validates the acceleration mechanism and sheds light on the design principles for flat-direction multiscale momentum.

Conclusion: The curvature-conditioned multiscale momentum with sphere constraints enhances optimization along flat directions in LLM pretraining, delivering steady acceleration and providing theoretical and practical insights into curvature-aware optimization. Sphere constraints are crucial to prevent parameter blow-up and unstable learning-rate behavior.

Abstract: Pretraining accounts for a large fraction of the total computational cost in LLM training. However, noise-dominant gradients and the highly ill-conditioned loss landscape bring severe challenges. Although modern adaptive optimizers such as AdamW and Muon have achieved great success in large-scale pretraining, their reliance on gradient normalization offers limited mitigation of the ill-conditioned curvature. The progress along flat directions (eigen-directions of small eigenvalues), which dominates the final loss reduction, remains relatively slow. To enhance training dynamics along flat directions, we propose a curvature-conditioned multiscale momentum method with sphere constraints, delivering steady acceleration in LLM pretraining. This multiscale momentum, applied only along flat directions, pairs a slow-decay component for noise reduction with a fast-decay component for rapid curvature adaptation, harnessing their complementary strengths. Crucially, we employ a sphere constraint technique to prevent parameter inflation and excessively rapid effective learning rate decay that would otherwise arise from a naive combination. Extensive experiments show that the proposed method significantly accelerates Muon across diverse architectures (dense, MoE) and model sizes (0.12B--2.3B parameters). Theoretically, we verify the acceleration effect and provide insight into the design principles underlying the flat-direction multiscale momentum.

</details>


### [231] [How Proper Scoring Rules Shape LLM Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28482)
*Benjamin Turtel,Paul Wilczewski,Kris Skotheim,Ville A. Satopää,Philip E. Tetlock*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training LLM forecasters with five proper scoring rules yields differing calibration and error structures, with modest aggregate accuracy differences; rules are not interchangeable as training objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although all scoring rules incentivize truthful probability reporting, their practical effects on forecasts and error decomposition warrant investigation.

Method: Compare five proper scoring rules as training objectives for binary forecasts of resolved real-world events; evaluate calibration, probability use, and decomposed bias/information/noise; single seed per condition; report metrics including Brier score, log score, AUC-ROC, calibration error.

Result: Brier-trained model achieves lowest Brier score and highest AUC-ROC; log-trained model achieves highest log score and lowest calibration error; models with similar aggregate performance use different combinations of bias, information, and noise; results may be affected by training stochasticity due to a single seed.

Conclusion: Reward choice shapes not only forecast accuracy but the structure of forecasting errors; proper scoring rules are not interchangeable as training objectives; forecast-error profiles can be influenced by training objective; seed-related variability may contribute to observed differences.

Abstract: This paper evaluates how reward function choice shapes the performance and behavior of LLM forecasters. We compare five proper scoring rules as training objectives for binary forecasts of resolved real-world events. Although the rules share the same theoretical incentive for truthful probability reporting, the resulting models differ in calibration, probability use, and estimated profiles of bias, information, and noise, with smaller differences in aggregate accuracy and discrimination. The Brier-trained model has the lowest observed Brier score and highest AUC-ROC, while the log-trained model has the highest observed log score and lowest calibration error. Models with similar aggregate performance also reach that performance through different combinations of bias, information, and noise. Proper scoring rules therefore need not behave interchangeably as training objectives. Reward choice may shape not only how well an LLM forecasts, but how its forecasting errors are structured. Each condition uses a single seed, so some differences may reflect training stochasticity.

</details>


### [232] [REPLICANT: Learning Policies for Evading and Hardening Malware Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28499)
*Shae McFadden,Ilias Tsingenopoulos,Mario D'Onghia,Alexander Herzog,Myles Foley,Chris Hicks,Lorenzo Cavallaro,Fabio Pierazzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Replicant uses deep reinforcement learning under a label-only black-box threat model to learn evasion policies for malware detectors. It transfers across samples, detectors, and feature spaces, achieving high attack success and improving robustness when used for adversarial training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the realism gap in adversarial malware attacks by modelling an attacker with limited access (no training data, feature space, or confidence scores) and with only label queries, aiming to evaluate and improve real-world detector robustness.

Method: Replicant trains a reusable policy that learns how to modify a malware sample and when to query the detector, within a strict label-only black-box setting. The policy transfers across samples, detectors, and feature spaces, and is evaluated on seven Android detectors across three feature spaces.

Result: Mean attack success rate of 78.8%, representing a relative improvement of 20.9%-39.2% over prior state-of-the-art methods. Also, using Replicant for adversarial training yields detectors with improved generalizable robustness.

Conclusion: Learning the task of evasion itself yields stronger attacks and provides a meaningful signal for hardening malware detectors through adversarial training; Replicant demonstrates both effective attacks and utility for detector hardening.

Abstract: To determine the real-world effectiveness of machine learning based malware detection, it is vital to evaluate its robustness against highly capable adversaries. However, state-of-the-art attacks do not effectively model realistic adversaries, as they often assume access to privileged information such as the training data, feature space, or confidence scores of the target. In this work, we present Replicant, a deep reinforcement learning framework that learns the realistic task of evasion under a strict label-only black-box threat model. Replicant learns a reusable policy on how to modify a malware sample and when to query the target, which transfers across samples, detectors, and feature spaces. Across seven Android malware detectors and three feature spaces, Replicant is the strongest and most query-efficient approach achieving a mean attack success rate of 78.8%, a relative improvement of 20.9%-39.2% over the state-of-the-art. Furthermore, when used for adversarial training, Replicant also outperforms the state-of-the art by producing detectors with more generalizable robustness. With Replicant we demonstrate that learning the task of evasion not only results in stronger attack performance but, crucially, provides a better signal for hardening malware detectors.

</details>


### [233] [An Enclosed Mode Is a Gauge Choice: Topology Relative to Reach in Certified Code World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28541)
*Javier Aguilar Martín*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A formal analysis of a code-world model with a sampling gate shows that acceptance-with-certainty yields exact knowledge on the reachable query set, while errors beyond are governed by topology; an annular freeze mode with a wrong-topology artifact (filled-disc) is studied via LLM synthesis across three model families, revealing a gamma-controlled progression through unfalsifiable-harmless, falsifiable-costly, and instantly falsified regimes. Three organizing principles are: topology-relative danger, bounded repair, and dimension-matched mitigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify the limits of certified models under partial observability and topology-induced reachability constraints, and to understand how topology, repair, and mitigation affect the cost and falsifiability of errors.

Method: Define a gate quotient to formalize acceptance; analyze an annular freeze mode enclosing an unreachable interior; study a minimal ring instrument and an extreme wrong-topology artifact (filled-disc); use LLM synthesis across three model families; vary a channel width gamma to propagate the artifact through three regimes; quantify with topological tools (Betti numbers, persistent homology); test three mitigation strategies and articulate three organizing principles.

Result: Acceptance-with-certainty fixes the model exactly on the reachable query set; beyond reach its state is gauge. The extreme artifact is unfalsifiable by any sampling gate and harmless at play; gamma induces three regimes: unfalsifiable-and-harmless, falsifiable-and-costly, instantly-falsified. Danger is topology-relative to reach; repair is parameter- and sensor-bound and cannot recover exterior evidence, while inside the model aligns topology with the persistent-homology summary (beta1). Mitigation must match the error’s dimension and direction: point fences, dimension-matched persisted fences, and dual-freedom certificates. In n-dimensions the shell yields near-certain misidentification while danger remains exploitable; the two axes are independent.

Conclusion: Topology-aware certification reveals fundamental trade-offs: exact knowledge is confined to the reachable region, while outside, errors persist in a topology-driven manner. Repair alone cannot fully recover missed structure; mitigation must be dimension- and direction-aware. The framework highlights distinct regimes and the necessity of aligning mitigation with the error geometry.

Abstract: A code world model accepted by a sampling gate can be exactly right on everything the gate can see and arbitrarily wrong beyond it. We characterize what a certified model can know, and what its errors can cost, when the omission is an annular freeze mode enclosing an unreachable interior. The gate quotient makes the question precise: acceptance-with-certainty determines the model exactly on the reachable query set; beyond reach is gauge. On a minimal ring instrument we prove the extreme case (a wrong-topology filled-disc artifact unfalsifiable by any sampling gate and bitwise harmless at play) and measure, with LLM synthesis across three model families, how one knob (a channel of width gamma) walks the same artifact through three regimes: unfalsifiable-and-harmless, falsifiable-and-costly, and instantly falsified. Three principles organize the empirics. First, danger is topology relative to reach: a channel the planner can use collapses the blind model's exploitation (play cost 1.09 to ~0 over a knee at gamma ~ 0.1), while a hidden channel with the same first Betti number keeps it at full strength (1.12). Second, repair is parameter-bound and sensor-bound: no family recovers the region from outside evidence; from inside, models pose the right topology but cannot pin its parameters, and the posed topology tracks the guiding persistent-homology summary's wrong beta_1 (a sensor with a measured geometric resolution limit), not the truth. Third, mitigation must match the error's dimension and direction: point fences fail against the one-dimensional boundary, a dimension-matched persisted fence collapses exploitation to a two-lesson transient (0.999 to 0.058), and the dual freedom certificate collapses the invented-mode failure symmetrically (1.769 to 0.029). In n dimensions the shell makes misidentification near-certain while the danger stays fully exploitable: the two axes are independent.

</details>


### [234] [DARTS: Decoder-Aware Representation Tuning via Surgery for Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28547)
*Aaryan Ajay Sharma,Sai Nishanth Padala,Seganrasan Subramanian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Model merging combines multiple task-specific fine-tuned LLMs into a single multi-task model without additional training. However, merged models are known to suffer from representation bias: systematic drift between the merged model's hidden states and those of each individual source model. Prior work (Yang et al., 2024a) study and mitigate this bias for encoder-based vision models using a lightweight correction module trained with L1 loss. However, such bias is not studied for decoder models due to their autoregressive nature. We analyze the problem of representation bias in decoder models, and show two challenges absent in encoders: (1) the causal attention mask causes bias to accumulate across token positions, requiring position-dependent correction; and (2) not all token positions are equally important, i.e., high-entropy (decision-critical) positions matter far more than low-entropy ones. To address these challenges, we propose Decoder-Aware Representation Tuning via Surgery (DARTS). DARTS employs a novel entropy-weighted L1 loss to upweight correction at high-entropy positions where errors most affect generation quality, and a per-position additive bias that captures position-dependent error without overparameterization. We perform extensive evaluation on three domains: code generation (HumanEval), mathematical reasoning (GSM8K), and instruction following (AlpacaEval) on Llama-2-7B models, and show DARTS achieves significant improvement over the standard surgery approach while adding negligible parameters ($0.1\%$ of total parameters).

</details>


### [235] [Advancing Interaction-Sensitive Feature Selection: Novel Relief-Based Algorithms, Expanded Comparisons, and Recommendations for Biomedical Data Mining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28552)
*Kia Kazemi-Nia,Harsh Bandhey,Philip J. Freda,Ryan J. Urbanowicz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Refactors and expands scikit-rebate with new Relief-based variants; conducts comprehensive benchmark on simulated genomic data; achieves 10–35x runtime reduction; identifies strong performance in detecting main effects and 2-way/3-way interactions, with far-score variants excelling in interaction detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional biomedical data require effective, scalable feature selection capable of capturing feature interactions without prohibitive computational costs.

Method: Refactor and optimize scikit-rebate; add SWRF*, mu-Relief, and five novel RBA variants with alternative neighbor selection and scoring strategies; benchmark across simulated genomic datasets varying sample size, feature count, heritability, and association types; compare predictive feature ranking and runtime.

Result: RBAs (except mu-Relief) detect 2-way interactions in noisy data. Far-scoring RBAs excel at 2-way interactions (MultiSWRFDB* top). SWRF, MultiSWRF, MultiSURF, and MultiSWRFDB perform best across main effects and 2-way interactions; MultiSWRFDB performs best when 3-way interactions considered. Refactoring yields 10–35x runtime reductions. The new RBAs are among the strongest performers, preserving predictive signals for downstream modeling.

Conclusion: New RBAs provide robust detection of both main effects and low-order epistasis, delivering strong predictive signal preservation and substantial runtime gains—beneficial for scalable, interpretable genomic modeling.

Abstract: As a precursor to high-dimensional biomedical data modeling, reliable feature selection can reduce computational expense, improve modeling performance, and yield simpler, more interpretable models. However, most filter-based feature selection methods struggle to detect feature interactions, while wrapper or embedded feature selection methods are computationally expensive. Relief-based algorithms (RBAs) are filter methods that are sensitive to feature interactions while mitigating these other limitations. This study (1) refactors, optimizes, and expands the scikit-rebate Python package with existing and newly proposed RBA variants and (2) conducts rigorous RBA benchmark comparisons across diverse genomic simulations. We expand scikit-rebate to include SWRF*, mu-Relief, and 5 novel RBA variants implementing alternative strategies for neighbor selection and feature scoring. All RBAs were evaluated to compare predictive feature ranking and runtime across simulated genomic datasets varying in sample size, number of features, heritability, and underlying association type (e.g. main effects and interactions). All RBAs, except mu-Relief, were proficient in detecting 2-way interactions in noisy data. RBAs utilizing 'far' scoring were best at detecting 2-way interactions - with MultiSWRFDB* top-performing - but were far less sensitive to main effects. SWRF, MultiSWRF, MultiSURF, and MultiSWRFDB yielded top performance across main effect and 2-way interaction datasets with MultiSWRFDB performing best when also considering 3-way interactions. Refactoring of scikit-rebate resulted in 10 to 35-fold reductions in RBA runtimes. The newly introduced RBAs were among the strongest performing, and by robustly retaining both main effects and 2-way epistatic interactions, these algorithms preserve predictive signals for downstream modeling.

</details>


### [236] [Blog: Survey of Optimizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28557)
*Ruoran Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Broad survey of neural optimizer design beyond Adam variants, emphasizing matrix-aware methods and a compositional evaluation framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organize an expanding optimizer design space across four axes (temporal estimation, update geometry, horizon management, representation and systems) and assess empirical gains in context.

Method: Survey of recent optimizers and training methods mapped along four axes; connect methods such as spectral normalization (Muon), Shampoo/SOAP statistics, adaptive/hybrid matrix approaches, memory-efficient optimizers, schedule-free training, small-batch corrections, and quantized optimizer states; synthesize practical implications.

Result: Matrix-aware methods offer genuine advances but cannot universally replace AdamW; rankings depend on model scale, data-to-parameter ratio, batch size, schedule, parameter partition, tuning budget, and the chosen metric (tokens, FLOPs, wall-clock time, memory).

Conclusion: Advocate a compositional view of optimizer design and establish stricter evaluation protocols for optimizer claims, emphasizing context-aware, scalable, and hardware-conscious design.

Abstract: Neural-network optimization in 2025-2026 is no longer well described as a succession of new Adam variants. The design space has expanded from coordinates to matrices and layers, from fixed training horizons to policies over time, and from mathematical update rules to state representations that must survive sharding and low-precision computation. This survey organizes recent optimizers and training optimization methods along four largely independent axes: temporal estimation, update geometry, horizon management, and representation and systems. It connects the spectral normalization of Muon, the historical matrix statistics of Shampoo and SOAP, adaptive and hybrid matrix methods, memory-efficient optimizers, schedule-free training, small-batch corrections, and quantized optimizer states. The central empirical conclusion is deliberately non-triumphal: matrix-aware methods represent a genuine advance, but there is no context-independent replacement for AdamW. Rankings change with model scale, data-to-parameter ratio, batch size, schedule, parameter partition, tuning budget, and whether the target metric is tokens, FLOPs, wall-clock time, or memory. The practical consequence is a compositional view of optimizer design and a stricter protocol for evaluating optimizer claims.

</details>


### [237] [QGPINNs: A Physics-Informed Neural Network Framework for Nonlocal Differential Equations on Quantum Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28589)
*Vaibhav Mehandiratta,Saket Ramchandra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A PyTorch-based physics-informed neural network framework (QGPINNs) for nonlocal PDEs on quantum graphs, using edge-wise nets and a unified graph loss to enforce vertex conditions, enabling forward and inverse problems for fractional elliptic and time-fractional models; validated on IEEE 14-bus and open-channel drainage networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of solving nonlocal differential equations on networks (quantum graphs) by coupling edge-local neural approximations through vertex conditions, and by enabling parameter/order identification from data.

Method: Introduce QGPINNs: neural nets on each edge with a graph-based loss that enforces governing equations, initial/boundary, and vertex transmission (continuity and Kirchhoff-Neumann). Apply to multi-order fractional elliptic and time-fractional evolution problems. Enhance training with graph-adapted strategies: soft/hard constraints, dynamic loss balancing, Fourier feature embeddings, and a learnable singularity-capturing feature. Extend to inverse problems for identifying fractional orders and physical parameters. Validation on benchmark graphs and real networks (IEEE 14-bus, open-channel drainage).

Result: Demonstrates improved accuracy and training stability, computational efficiency, and physical consistency; capable of identifying operator orders and parameters from noisy data; scalable to realistic network topologies.

Conclusion: QGPINNs provide a general, scalable framework for forward and inverse nonlocal PDE problems on quantum graphs, integrating physics-informed learning with graph structure and applicable to real-world networks.

Abstract: We propose QGPINNs, a physics-informed neural network framework developed in PyTorch for the numerical solution of nonlocal differential equations on quantum graphs. The framework is designed as a general computational implementation in which the solution on each edge of the graph is approximated by a neural network, while a unified graph-based loss function enforces the governing equations together with initial, boundary, and vertex transmission conditions. In particular, the formulation incorporates standard continuity and Kirchhoff-Neumann vertex conditions and Dirichlet boundary conditions into the learning process to couple the local edge-wise neural approximations into a global solution on the graph. The framework is developed for two representative classes of nonlinear models: multi-order fractional elliptic problems and time-fractional evolution equations on quantum graphs. To improve accuracy and training stability, QGPINNs integrates several graph-adapted learning strategies, including soft and hard constraint enforcement, dynamic loss balancing, Fourier feature embeddings, and a learnable singularity-capturing feature for weakly singular solutions arising in the considered problems. The framework also extends naturally to inverse problems, including the identification of the orders of fractional operators and physical parameters from noisy observational data. We validate the accuracy, computational efficiency, and physical consistency of the proposed framework through numerical experiments on benchmark graph structures and real-world networks, including the IEEE 14-bus system and an open-channel agricultural drainage network.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [238] [Beyond Relative Geometry: Metric-Aware Geometry Perception for Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27497)
*Fengjun Zhong,Congjia Chen,Zhaoxu Liu,Jinyang Du,Yuchen Gong,Enqi Mao,Ruihao Gong,ShuJie Wang,Xianglong Liu,Zhongliang Qiao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MAGP is an end-to-end, plug-and-play framework that enforces metric-scale consistency in geometry reconstruction for robotics, enabling robust object dimensions and distances across scenes and sensing setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing geometry reconstruction only yields relative geometry with arbitrary scale, misaligning perception with real-world robotic actions. A metric-scale, geometry-aware reconstruction is needed for reliable manipulation.

Method: Introduces Metric Scale Equivariant Augmentation to enforce metric-scale reconstructions from camera/depth data and Flexible Metric Conditioning to handle varying numbers and types of sensors, forming a metric-aware, robust reconstruction pipeline integrated into robotic policies.

Result: MAGP preserves strong relative geometry accuracy while dramatically reducing absolute scale error (2.01 m → 0.07 m). When integrated into policies (LIBERO, RoboTwin, LIBERO-Plus), it yields up to 6.26% improvement on RoboTwin across tasks and datasets (ETH3D, MegaDepth, ScanNet++).

Conclusion: Metric geometry provides robust, scalable improvements for robotic manipulation; MAGP's design achieves cross-scene consistency and policy gains, demonstrating generalizability across sensing configurations.

Abstract: Recent embodied models increasingly leverage geometric representations to improve spatial reasoning and robotic manipulation. However, existing reconstruction methods only reconstruct relative geometry with arbitrary scales, causing predicted object dimensions and spatial distances to vary across scenes, viewpoints, and input configurations. This inconsistency prevents geometric perception from being directly aligned with robotic actions defined on the real-world scale. To address this limitation, we propose Metric-Aware Geometry Perception (MAGP), an end-to-end, plug-and-play framework for metric geometry reconstruction that can be seamlessly integrated into robotic policies. At its core, Metric Scale Equivariant Augmentation encourages the model to reconstruct metric geometry from camera parameters and depth observations, ensuring that the reconstructed geometry follows the metric scale specified by observations. Flexible Metric Conditioning further enables MAGP to support arbitrary view counts and combinations of camera and depth inputs, improving robustness to heterogeneous robotic sensing configurations. Together, these designs produce geometrically consistent reconstructions with stable object dimensions and spatial distances across scenes and sensing conditions. Experiments on ETH3D, MegaDepth, and ScanNet++ demonstrate that MAGP maintains strong relative geometry accuracy while reducing the absolute error by over an order of magnitude, from 2.01m to 0.07m. When integrated into multiple robotic policies, MAGP consistently improves performance on LIBERO, RoboTwin, and zero-shot LIBERO-Plus, with gains of up to 6.26% on RoboTwin. These results demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of metric geometry for robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [239] [Remote Human and Robot Interaction for Greenhouse Gardening Using Virtual Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27545)
*Daniel Udekwe,Hasan Seyyedhasani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VR-guided remote leaf inspection and soil-moisture assessment in a greenhouse using an unmanned ground vehicle and robotic manipulator with cameras. Leaf-disease detection reached up to 88% accuracy; soil-moisture success up to 64% (9/14) with canopy morphology strongly predicting reliability. Occlusion is the dominant sensing bottleneck; adapting sensor viewpoint to canopy density is recommended to improve robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess the viability and limits of remote human–robot interaction via VR teleoperation for precision horticulture, focusing on sensing performance and factors limiting accuracy.

Method: Two experiments (leaf inspection and soil-moisture assessment) with 14 plants in a greenhouse. A UGV with a robotic manipulator equipped with cameras, controlled by kinematic models, used under VR teleoperation guided by predefined questions/hypotheses. Measured metrics included leaf-disease detection accuracy and cycle times (leaf inspection) and success of watering-need determination (soil moisture). Post hoc and secondary analyses evaluated the influence of plant canopy morphology and operator adaptation, with statistical tests (p-values reported).

Result: Leaf inspection: cycle times 3.3–8.0 s; disease detection up to 88%; disease-spot detection improvement in Experiment 2 not statistically significant (p=0.378). Soil moisture: successful watering-need determination in 9/14 plants (64.3%), with some plants showing consistent success; p=0.50 for the overall improvement. Post hoc: canopy morphology strongly predicts reliability (p<0.01); broad, single-leaf canopies reached 100% by the second experiment vs 16.7% for dense, compound canopies. Secondary analysis: operators' speed increased for dense-canopy plants without corresponding increase in success, indicating occlusion—not skill—limits sensing accuracy.

Conclusion: Sensing performance is bottlenecked by camera occlusion due to canopy density. VR teleoperation alone cannot overcome this; adaptive sensing strategies and camera viewpoints tailored to canopy morphology are needed to improve leaf-disease and soil-moisture assessment robustness.

Abstract: This study evaluates the effectiveness of remote human-robot interaction using virtual reality for leaf inspection and soil moisture assessment in a greenhouse environment. The robotic system comprised an unmanned ground vehicle and a robotic manipulator equipped with cameras, governed by kinematic models for navigation and manipulator control. Fourteen distinct plants were inspected across two experiments utilizing VR teleoperation, guided by a set of pre-specified research questions and hypotheses. In the leaf inspection experiments, cycle completion times varied from 3.3 to 8.0 s, and plant-based disease detection was achieved up to 88% accuracy; diseased-spot detection improved numerically in the second experiment, though this change was not statistically significant (p=0.378). For soil moisture assessment, the experiments achieved successful determination of watering needs in up to 64.3% of plants (9 of 14), with consistent success observed for plants 1, 2, 3, 8, 9, 10, and 13; however, this improvement was likewise not statistically significant (p=0.50). A post hoc analysis instead revealed that soil moisture assessment reliability was strongly and significantly predicted by plant canopy morphology (p<0.01): plants with broad, single-leaf canopies reached 100% success by the second experiment, versus only 16.7% for dense, compound canopies. A secondary analysis showed operators became measurably faster at attempting dense-canopy plants without a corresponding gain in success, indicating that camera occlusion, not operator skill or effort, is the dominant limiting factor. These findings show occlusion imposes a sensing limitation rather than a control or training deficiency, and that adapting camera viewpoint and sensing strategy to canopy density is needed to improve the system's accuracy and robustness.

</details>


### [240] [Beyond Data Scaling: Representation-Centric Continued Pre-training for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27550)
*Senqiao Yang,Chengyao Wang,Yuxin Chen,Zixuan Wang,Longxiang Tang,Haokun Gui,Jinhui Ye,Changsheng Lu,Xiaoyang Wu,Mingkang Zhu,Pengguang Chen,Shu Liu,Zhuotao Tian,Hengshuang Zhao,Bei Yu,Jiaya Jia*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLAct is a VLA-oriented VLM backbone trained on broad, multi-embodiment robot data to enable efficient transfer under limited downstream trajectories; it preserves VLM priors, aligns action semantics across embodiments via multi-head co-supervision and a partially unified cross-embodiment action layout, and uses task-specific heads during fine-tuning. It achieves strong results across simulation, real-world, and unseen embodiments, outperforming industrial baselines on LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin-2.0 and ranking competitively on RoboDojo and RoboCasa-GR1 with modest compute and open data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot data collection is costly and sparsely covers the physical world, making representation quality the main bottleneck. Under a fixed data budget, continued pre-training should extract transferable visual-action knowledge rather than merely fit observed actions.

Method: Train VLAct as a VLA-oriented VLM backbone, preserving the broad VLM prior; enforce shared action semantics across embodiments with multi-head continuous action co-supervision; introduce a partially unified cross-embodiment action layout; allow task-specific action heads during fine-tuning; train on broad, heterogeneous, multi-embodiment robot data using open-source datasets with a modest 16-GPU budget.

Result: VLAct consistently improves downstream performance under fixed fine-tuning protocols across simulation, real-world, and unseen-embodiment transfer. It surpasses ABot-M0 and LingBot-VLA on LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin-2.0; ranks sixth among all policies on RoboDojo and outperforms all explicitly designated WAM entries on both metrics; on RoboCasa-GR1 (unseen humanoid), using only 20% of downstream trajectories, it outperforms the full-data GR00T-N1.6 baseline.

Conclusion: Representation-centric continued pre-training is a powerful axis for VLA progress beyond data scaling. With fully open-source data and modest compute (16 GPUs), VLAct demonstrates competitive performance and strong cross-embodiment transfer, supporting broader applicability of VLA models.

Abstract: Scaling robot data is crucial for building generalist Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, yet robot trajectories are harder to scale than web-scale image-text data because embodied collection is costly and sparsely covers the physical world. This makes representation quality a central bottleneck: under a fixed robot-data budget, continued pre-training must turn limited trajectories into transferable visual-action knowledge rather than merely fit actions. We propose VLAct, a VLA-oriented VLM backbone trained on broad, heterogeneous, multi-embodiment robot data before task-specific fine-tuning. VLAct preserves the broad VLM prior and encourages shared action semantics across embodiments through VLM-prior preservation, multi-head continuous action co-supervision, and a partially unified cross-embodiment action layout, while allowing task-specific action heads during fine-tuning. Across simulation, real-world, and unseen-embodiment transfer, VLAct consistently improves downstream performance under fixed fine-tuning protocols. On LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin 2.0, VLAct surpasses industrial VLA systems including ABot-M0 and LingBot-VLA, achieving success rates of 82.6% and 92.5%. On RoboDojo, VLAct ranks sixth among all policies by success rate and outperforms all explicitly designated world-action model (WAM) entries on both metrics. Most notably, on RoboCasa-GR1, an unseen humanoid embodiment, VLAct using only 20% of downstream trajectories outperforms the full-data GR00T-N1.6 baseline. These results are obtained using fully open-source data and only a 16-GPU training setup, showing that representation-centric continued pre-training can deliver highly competitive performance under a modest compute budget and is an important independent axis of VLA progress beyond data scaling.

</details>


### [241] [PHR-VLA: Planning Horizon Reasoning for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27609)
*Davood Soleymanzadeh,Kaidi Zhang,Zhiyuan Zhang,Bihao Zhang,Xiao Liang,Yu She,Minghui Zheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PHR-VLA adds planning-horizon reasoning to vision-language-action models via a lightweight auxiliary future head trained with privileged latent dynamics, improving anticipatory manipulation and achieving notable gains on LIBERO, real-world disassembly, and Meta-World.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLAs largely condition action on current observations and lack explicit future dynamics reasoning, which is critical for fine-grained, contact-rich manipulation tasks.

Method: Introduce a lightweight auxiliary future head that, during training, aligns the VLA's internal representations with latent dynamics extracted from future observations. Supervises with local contact-centric patch-level dynamics from the wrist camera and, for another ablation, patch-level supervision from a third-person camera.

Result: LIBERO success rate improved from 84.1% to 88.4%; real-world disassembly from 63.3% to 82.5%; Meta-World improved from 56.70% to 57.8% with third-person patch supervision.

Conclusion: Privileged latent dynamics alignment provides an effective training signal for improving anticipatory reasoning in VLA policies and demonstrates the benefit of planning-horizon supervision for contact-rich robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) have shown strong promise for general-purpose robotic manipulation by mapping language instructions and vision observations directly to actions. However, most VLAs primarily condition action prediction on current observations and lack an explicit mechanism for reasoning over future task dynamics, which is particularly important for fine-grained, contact-rich manipulation. We present PHR-VLA, a framework that enables planning-horizon reasoning in VLAs through privileged latent representations of future dynamics. PHR-VLA introduces a lightweight auxiliary future head that, during training, aligns the VLA's internal representations with latent dynamics extracted from future observations. Evaluation results demonstrate that local, contact-centric, patch-level latent dynamics supervision from the wrist camera improves success rate on LIBERO from 84.1% to 88.4% and on real-world disassembly tasks from 63.3% to 82.5%. Patch-level supervision from a third-person camera also improves performance on Meta-World from 56.70% to 57.8%. These results demonstrate that privileged latent dynamics alignment provides an effective training signal for improving anticipatory reasoning in VLA policies. Project website: \href{https://davoodsz.github.io/PHR-VLA.github.io/}{https://davoodsz.github.io/PHR-VLA.github.io/}

</details>


### [242] [One year in a forest: Analyzing the challenges of autonomous navigation in subarctic environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27628)
*Matěj Boxan,Nicolas Lauzon,Veronica Vannini,Mathis Turgeon-Roy,François Pomerleau*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Year-long field test in subarctic boreal forest evaluating 9 odometry/localization/mapping methods over 64 km; lidar robust for cross-season localization; vision and radar less robust; complex SLAM offers limited gains and higher fragility; insights on feature-based drift and confidence weights; lessons and Teach-and-Repeat evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNSS and cloud computing are unreliable in dense canopies and subarctic conditions; need onboard sensing robust to seasonal variations; assess how standard exteroceptive SLAM methods perform under real-world seasonal changes.

Method: Field deployment with 64 km of data; nine odometry/localization/mapping methods; evaluation across seasonal changes; correlation of position drift with features and confidence distributions; cross-season localization on prior maps; year-long Teach and Repeat (T&R) with radar and lidar pipelines.

Result: Seasonal changes significantly hinder performance of state-of-the-art SLAM; fragility increases in self-similar scenes or tall snowbanks; complex SLAM offers limited accuracy gains over proprioceptive baseline; visual SLAM heavily affected by seasonality; lidar enables cross-season localization; radar and visual methods fail due to sparse matching features; multi-season T&R demonstrates practical challenges.

Conclusion: Subarctic deployment exposes fragility of current exteroceptive SLAM pipelines; lidar-based localization demonstrates robustness across seasons; need for robust, season-aware SLAM methods; this field report provides practical lessons and guidance for deploying autonomous systems in boreal environments.

Abstract: Subarctic regions have the potential to see increased deployment of autonomous robots in applications including forestry, mining, and environmental monitoring. In these conditions, an autonomous system's reliance on GNSS or cloud computing is precarious due to dense tree canopies and atmospheric attenuation, necessitating onboard sensing and data processing. However, established exteroceptive modalities, including cameras, lidars, and radars, are typically evaluated in structured urban settings or in environments that lack significant seasonal variations. To address this, we present a field report on a year-long deployment of a mobile robot in a subarctic boreal forest. We evaluate 64 km of data using nine odometry, localization, and mapping methods and assess their performance across seasonal changes. The performed experiments suggest that the environment changes significantly hinder the performance of state-of-the-art techniques, which show increased fragility when subject to conditions characterized by self-similar scenes or tall snowbanks. Additionally, complex Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM) algorithms offer limited accuracy gains over a proprioceptive baseline while significantly increasing system fragility. Furthermore, by correlating the position drift with features and confidence weight distribution, we show that visual-based SLAM methods are particularly affected by the seasonal changes. Additionally, we investigate the task of cross-season localization in a prior map. While lidar-based methods successfully completed localization runs between seasons, radar and visual methods are prone to failure due to a few matching features between runs, even within the same season. Finally, we detail the challenges and lessons learned from this year-long trial, including a multi-season Teach and Repeat (T&R) evaluation using both radar and lidar-based pipelines.

</details>


### [243] [Distributed Model-Based Diffusion: Finite Horizon Contraction under Bounded Delay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27685)
*Seth Golembeski,Keith L. Gibson,Alexander Gross,Shreyas Kousik,Anirban Mazumdar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Distributed Model-Based Diffusion (D-MBD) is a sampling-based MPC method for highly nonlinear, nonconvex, multi-agent systems that remains robust to communication latency. The paper proves contraction properties and latency robustness and demonstrates improvements under latency in driving (circleswap) and aerial combat tasks compared with a centralized baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenges of coordinating many interacting agents with nonlinear dynamics and latency, aiming for scalable, reliable multi-agent trajectory optimization.

Method: Develop a distributed diffusion-based MPC framework using sampling-based optimization. Prove contraction and robustness to latency for multi-agent, nonconvex problems. Validate on circleswap, a cooperative driving task, and aerial combat simulations.

Result: Under latency, the approach improves circleswap makespan by 31% and increases aerial combat win rate by 25% compared to centralized Model-Based Diffusion, indicating robustness and practical gains in real-world-like scenarios.

Conclusion: The distributed diffusion MPC framework provides contraction guarantees and latency robustness for nonconvex, multi-agent control, delivering meaningful performance benefits in complex tasks and suggesting applicability to real-world multi-agent systems.

Abstract: Simultaneously optimizing the trajectories of multiple agents is a challenging problem plagued by nonlinearity, nonconvexity, and the curse of dimensionality. A collection of interacting aerial vehicles or self-driving cars in an intersection are examples of complex multi-agent systems that remain difficult to solve without many simplifying assumptions. The presence of communication latency between agents further increases the difficulty. In this paper, we analyze Distributed Model-Based Diffusion: a sampling-based Model-Predictive Control method suitable for highly nonlinear, nonconvex, nonsmooth, multi-agent systems. We prove contraction and robustness to latency for multi-agent, nonconvex problems, showing applicability to real-world constraints. We test the algorithm on a circleswap task, a cooperative medium-fidelity driving task, and in an aerial combat scenario. Despite the addition of latency, our algorithm improves circleswap makespan by 31% and increases aerial combat win rate by 25% compared to centralized Model-Based Diffusion.

</details>


### [244] [Coordinated Motion Planning for Multi-Arm Systems via Iterative LQ Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27726)
*Junyoung Kim,Hanwen Ren,Lei Zhang,Ahmed H. Qureshi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An iterative differential game framework using local LQ games for multi-arm motion planning, with differentiable collision penalties and Riccati-based feedback Nash strategies, yielding safe, smooth trajectories in high-dimensional settings and outperforming traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses scalability and robustness gaps in centralized vs. decentralized planners for multi-arm manipulation in shared workspaces; leverages game-theoretic interactions to model agent coupling and collision constraints.

Method: Model each manipulator as an independent agent optimizing its own objective; linearize dynamics around a nominal trajectory; solve a sequence of local LQ games; apply Riccati backward recursions to obtain feedback Nash strategies; incorporate differentiable penalties for self-collision and inter-arm collision within the optimization; operate with shared global states and collision constraints to enable coordinated trajectory generation.

Result: Experiments show smooth, safe, and efficient trajectories in high-dimensional settings; collision penalties enable coordinated collision avoidance; the approach outperforms traditional methods, highlighting the efficacy of differential game formulations for multi-robot manipulation.

Conclusion: An iterative LQ game framework with differentiable collision penalties effectively handles multi-arm manipulation in shared workspaces, offering scalable, safe, and efficient coordination over conventional planners.

Abstract: Multi-agent motion planning for high-degree-of-freedom robotics manipulators in shared workspaces remains a fundamental yet challenging problem. Centralized planners often suffer from poor scalability, while decentralized approaches face robustness and safety concerns. Game-theoretic formulations offer a promising approach for modeling agent interactions, potentially overcoming these limitations. However, their application to articulated multi-arm systems remains limited. This paper presents an iterative Linear Quadratic (LQ) game framework for multi-manipulator motion planning, where each manipulator is modeled as an independent agent optimizing its own objective while interacting with other agents based on shared global states and collision constraints. The method solves a series of local LQ games by linearizing the dynamics and approximating the cost around a nominal trajectory, with Riccati backward recursions yielding feedback Nash strategies. To address the challenges of articulated systems, we incorporate differentiable penalties for self-collision and inter-arm collision into the optimization pipeline, enabling coordinated, collision-aware trajectory generation. Experiments demonstrate that our framework produces smooth, safe, and efficient trajectories in high-dimensional settings, outperforming traditional methods. This highlights the effectiveness of differential game formulations for multi-robot manipulation.

</details>


### [245] [CAVE-NAV: VLM-Based Autonomous 3D Navigation in Underwater Cave Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27793)
*Zhenqi Wu,Yuanjie Lu,Yisheng Zhang,Miao Yu,Xuesu Xiao,Jaejeong Shin,Xiaomin Lin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A framework for autonomous underwater cave navigation using a vision-language model with Chain-of-Thought reasoning to infer navigable directions from multimodal cues (RGB, depth, sonar) for safe 3D traversal; validated in high-fidelity simulations showing collision-free end-to-end traversals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater cave environments degrade visual features, reducing feature-based localization; sonar mapping can be overly conservative; communication constraints impede real-time human guidance. A reasoning-enabled, multimodal approach aims to robustly infer safe paths without dense feature dependence.

Method: Integrates RGB imagery, depth maps, and sonar vertical-clearance measurements as inputs to a vision-language model augmented with Chain-of-Thought reasoning to infer navigable directions and generate end-to-end navigation commands for 3D cave passages. Evaluated in high-fidelity simulations across multiple cave topologies.

Result: All evaluated end-to-end traversals completed without collisions and with safe clearance from cave boundaries, demonstrating robust navigation under varied cave geometries in simulation.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates feasibility of VLM+CoT-based autonomous navigation in challenging subterranean environments and highlights potential advantages over traditional feature-based methods. Future work should address real-world deployment, perception noise robustness, safety guarantees, and runtime efficiency.

Abstract: Autonomous navigation in underwater cave environments is essential for search-and-rescue operations, scientific exploration, and emergency egress. Traditional navigation systems commonly depend on dense visual features for localization and mapping. In underwater caves, however, visual degradation can undermine feature-based localization, sonar-based mapping may yield overly conservative obstacle representations, and communication constraints preclude real-time human guidance. To address these limitations, we propose an autonomous underwater cave navigation framework that leverages a vision-language model (VLM) with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning to infer navigable directions from environmental cues, including light intensity gradients, passage morphology, and geometric complexity, captured through multimodal inputs comprising RGB imagery, depth maps, and sonar-based vertical-clearance measurements, thereby supporting safe 3D navigation through confined cave passages. High-fidelity simulations across multiple cave topologies demonstrate that the proposed framework completes all evaluated end-to-end traversals without collisions while maintaining safe clearance from cave boundaries.

</details>


### [246] [Plan Along the Way: Event-Triggered Foundation-Model Planning for TAMP Execution in Partially Observable Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28075)
*Puru Ojha,Narendhiran Vijayakumar,Nav Singhal,Girish Varma,Antony Thomas*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A modular LLM/VLM-guided planning framework (RO-BUST TAMP) for reactive Task and Motion Planning under partial observability; restricts planning to the currently visible scene, validates actions against a strict executable interface, and routes accepted actions to scene-specific execution adapters; treats object discovery as a distinct replanning event and reconstructs the visible state after a stable horizon to replan using history and context; evaluated on six RLBench/CoppeliaSim kitchen and grill variants with hidden/non-target objects; compares text-only LLM and vision-language models across sizes under a uniform pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address planning under partial observability where unseen task-relevant or non-target objects may appear during execution, rendering a plan that was previously valid may become insufficient; enable robust, reactive replanning without assuming a fully specified scene.

Method: A modular framework that confines the planner to the currently visible relational state, validates generated task-level actions against a strict executable interface, and forwards accepted actions to scene-specific execution adapters. Object discovery is treated as a separate replanning event. After a stable execution horizon, the system reconstructs the visible state from action history and replanning context, and re-plans. Evaluations compare text-only LLM and VLM planners of varying sizes under the same pipeline (validation, execution, monitoring, replanning).

Result: Assessed on six RLBench/CoppeliaSim kitchen/grill variants with hidden/non-target objects, articulated-container interactions, and temporal manipulation procedures. Metrics include task success, partial goal completion, discovery- and failure-triggered replanning behavior, implicit non-target-object handling, and planner inference cost; comparisons across LLM vs VLM planners of different sizes.

Conclusion: RO-BUST TAMP enables robust, reactive planning under partial observability by constraining planning to visible information, validating actions, and explicitly treating object discovery as replanning, with performance contingent on planner type and size; demonstrates practical efficacy in simulated kitchen/grill domains and provides a modular integration approach for TAMP under partial information.

Abstract: Manipulation in partially observable environments requires planning under incomplete scene information. In such settings, an initially valid plan may execute successfully yet remain insufficient for task completion. Existing foundation-model-guided task and motion planning (TAMP) systems can generate useful long-horizon task decompositions, subgoals, or constraints, but they often assume having access to a fully specified scene state or invoke model-level replanning after a subgoal, refinement, or execution attempt fails. We present ROBUST TAMP, a modular LLM/VLM-guided planning framework for reactive TAMP where unseen task-relevant and non-target objects may become visible during execution. The framework restricts the foundation-model planner to the currently visible relational scene state, validates generated task-level actions against a strict executable interface, and routes the accepted actions to scene-specific execution adapters. Object discovery is treated as a distinct replanning event and, after a stable execution horizon, the system reconstructs the visible scene state and replans using completed-action history and structured replanning event context. Evaluations are performed on six RLBench/CoppeliaSim kitchen and grill variants involving hidden objects, non-target object discovery, articulated-container interaction, and temporal manipulation procedures. We compare text-only LLM and VLM planners of different sizes under the same validation, execution, monitoring, and replanning pipeline, reporting task success, partial goal completion, discovery- and failure-triggered replanning behavior, implicit non-target-object handling, and planner inference cost.

</details>


### [247] [Stay Seated: Learning Omnidirectional Humanoid Locomotion on a Passive Mobile Chair with Casters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28090)
*Kango Yanagida,Kazuki Miyazawa,Takato Horii*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Study of omnidirectional seated locomotion on a passive chair using learned policies in an extended standing velocity-tracking environment; shows zero-shot sim-to-real transfer to a Unitree G1 and analyzes effects of symmetry regularization, foot-slip regularization, and command curriculum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Move toward seated loco-manipulation by enabling a robot-chair system to perform omnidirectional locomotion with unfixed pelvis-seat contact and intermittent propulsion, bridging the gap between standing torque generation and seated desk-work scenarios.

Method: Extend a standard standing velocity-tracking environment with a passive-chair model, seated-state rewards, critic-only chair observations, and task-tailored contact settings. Learn policies without motion-imitation rewards; actor uses only proprioception and velocity commands; no contact sensing or chair states. Evaluate with random command rollouts (~20 s); perform a 3-factor full-factorial study on symmetry regularization (SY), foot-slip regularization (FS), and command curriculum (CC).

Result: Policies tracked omnidirectional commands in most 20-s rollouts; best seated policies outperformed standing policy in velocity tracking. FS reduced coefficient of time (CoT) but increased tracking error and sometimes led to stationary optima. Combining FS with SY or CC mitigated this. SY improved bilateral leg symmetry during longitudinal motion. Direction-resolved analysis showed CoT ordering backward < lateral << forward, with specific joint patterns.
