Achieved zero-shot sim-to-real transfer to a Unitree G1.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates feasible omnidirectional seated locomotion on a passive chair using learned policies, highlights interactions between training regularizers and curricula, and confirms sim-to-real transfer potential; design choices influence energy and tracking performance and symmetry.

Abstract: Humanoid robots with quasi-direct-drive actuators continuously generate joint torque while standing, whereas seated humans delegate weight support to chairs during desk work. As a first step toward seated loco-manipulation, we study omnidirectional seated locomotion on a passive mobile chair, requiring unfixed pelvis-seat contact and intermittent foot-floor propulsion of the robot-chair system. We extend a standard standing velocity-tracking environment with a passive-chair model, seated-state rewards, critic-only chair observations, and task-tailored contact settings. The policy is learned without motion-imitation rewards; its actor uses only proprioception and velocity commands, without contact sensing or chair states. In random-command evaluation, the policies tracked omnidirectional commands through nearly all 20-s rollouts, and the best seated policies could outperform the Standing policy in velocity tracking. Across four training seeds, a $2^3$ full-factorial comparison of symmetry regularization (SY), foot-slip regularization (FS), and command curriculum (CC) showed that FS reduced CoT but increased tracking error and that some FS-only policies converged to stationary local optima. Combining FS with either SY or CC avoided this failure without retuning FS, while SY improved bilateral leg symmetry during longitudinal motion. Direction-resolved analysis showed CoT ordered backward $<$ lateral $\ll$ forward, with planted-leg extension in backward and lateral motion and knee flexion following heel contact in forward motion. The learned policy achieved zero-shot sim-to-real transfer to a Unitree G1 and generated omnidirectional seated locomotion.

</details>


### [248] [DeicticVLA: Unifying Instruction Modes Based on Language and Deictic Gestures in a Single VLA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28108)
*Kango Yanagida,Tatsuya Aoki,Yuichiro Yoshikawa,Takato Horii*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DeicticVLA unifies LI, VLI, and VI into a single VLA interface using deictic prompts/masks, enabling robust zero-shot and few-shot generalization across instruction modes; two-stage training with deictic masks improves generalization while preserving LI performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLAs struggle to distinguish targets or placements among similar objects with natural-language alone; current models require mode-specific inputs, limiting flexibility and robustness. A unified three-mode interface could improve generalization to unseen layouts, expressions, and objects.

Method: Canonicalize LI, VLI, and VI into a text prompt plus deictic mask predictions derived from text-prompt completion and deictic gesture grounding; use a shared backbone with demonstrations and matched training steps; compare two RGB visual prompting methods, two separate-channel mask prompting methods, and three training strategies in simulation; evaluate in two-stage training and real-world tasks.

Result: Under two-stage training, all prompting methods show high in-distribution success; methods differ in using deictic masks on unseen layouts; two-stage training improves deictic-mask usage; keeping second-stage LI data reduces forgetting without hurting VLI/VI; in real tasks, one policy handles all modes; VLI/VI outperform LI under unseen expressions/appearance changes/novel objects; unseen categories reach 100% success (vs 16.7% for LI); supporting a unified three-mode interface.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a unified three-mode interface for LI, VLI, and VI and offers design guidelines for DeicticVLA, highlighting improved generalization to unseen scenarios through staged training and deictic masking.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) allow users to specify manipulation tasks in natural language, but distinguishing a target or placement goal among objects of the same category or similar appearance requires detailed expressions that VLAs may not use reliably. We propose DeicticVLA, which canonicalizes Language Instruction (LI), Vision-Language Instruction (VLI), and Visual Instruction (VI) into a text prompt and deictic masks through text-prompt completion and deictic gesture grounding, enabling a single pretrained VLA to handle all three instruction modes. With a shared backbone, demonstrations, and matched training steps, we compare two RGB visual prompting methods, two separate-channel mask prompting methods, and three training strategies in simulation. Under two-stage training, the four prompting methods achieve high in-distribution success but differ in their ability to use deictic masks in unseen layouts. Across methods, training-strategy ablations show that two-stage training improves such use, while retaining second-stage LI data mitigates forgetting without reducing VLI and VI performance. In three real-world tasks, one policy supports all modes. VLI and VI outperform LI under unseen expressions, appearance changes, and novel objects. For unseen categories, both achieve 100% success, compared with 16.7% for jointly trained LI. These results demonstrate the unified three-mode interface and guide DeicticVLA design.

</details>


### [249] [Contact-Guided Exploration for Non-Prehensile Locomanipulation with Multi-Critic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28140)
*Simone Tolomei,Mayank Mittal,Franco Angelini,Manolo Garabini,Paolo Salaris,Marco Hutter*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a contact-guided exploration strategy within a Multi-Critic RL framework for non-prehensile manipulation, using a dense contact-seeking exploration critic influenced by grasping-based candidate contact points, decaying to a task-optimal policy; validated on box pushing, chair transportation, and dishwasher opening, including real-world deployment on a quadrupedal mobile manipulator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-prehensile manipulation on mobile platforms exhibits highly hybrid dynamics and sparse contact, making both model-based and model-free approaches data-inefficient and brittle. A guided exploration mechanism is needed to reliably discover meaningful contact interactions across diverse objects.

Method: Train an exploration critic with a dense contact-seeking reward to steer the end-effector toward useful contact points; its influence decays over time to recover a policy optimized for the task. Use candidate interaction points derived from a general-purpose grasping algorithm to generalize across object geometries within a Multi-Critic RL setup.

Result: Empirical evaluation across box pushing, chair transportation, and dishwasher opening tasks; extensive real-world validation of chair transportation on a quadrupedal mobile manipulator, demonstrating deployable non-prehensile manipulation.

Conclusion: A contact-guided exploration strategy within a Multi-Critic RL framework can effectively address sparse-contact challenges in non-prehensile manipulation and generalize over object shapes, with successful real-world deployment.

Abstract: Non-prehensile manipulation offers versatile skills for moving and rearranging heavy or bulky objects, particularly when combined with a mobile manipulation platform. However, both model-based and model-free approaches struggle with the complex hybrid dynamics and the sparsity of the contact in these tasks. To address these challenges, we propose a contact-guided exploration strategy implemented within a Multi-Critic Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework. A dedicated exploration critic is trained with a dense contact-seeking reward that guides the end-effector toward meaningful contact points; its influence is progressively decayed to recover a task-optimal policy. We obtain candidate interaction points from a general-purpose grasping algorithm, enabling the exploration mechanism to generalise across various object geometries. We evaluate the approach on multiple tasks, including box pushing, chair transportation, and a dishwasher opening task. Finally, we validate the chair transportation policy through extensive experiments on a quadrupedal mobile manipulator, demonstrating deployable non-prehensile manipulation in the real world.

</details>


### [250] [From Small Talk to Rapport: Exploring Robot Self-Disclosure in Collaborative Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28154)
*Kaitlynn Taylor Pineda,Anvii Mishra,Brian Chien,Angela Guo,Toluwani Williams,Ziang Xiao,Chien-Ming Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LLM-driven robot small talk with low- vs high-disclosure; unexpectedly, low-disclosure boosted self-disclosure by users and improved teaming/coordination, especially for users with prior HRI experience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate how self-disclosure in robot small talk affects human–robot collaboration, focusing on a non-anthropomorphic industrial manipulator that performs physical work.

Method: Implemented an LLM-driven manipulator capable of small talk under two disclosure regimes (low- and high-disclosure). Conducted a between-subject user study (N=50) to assess self-disclosure, teaming, and coordination, examining the moderating role of prior HRI experience.

Result: In the low-disclosure condition, participants disclosed more than in the high-disclosure condition and reported stronger teaming and coordination. The effects were more pronounced for participants with prior robot HRI experience.

Conclusion: Simply increasing robot self-disclosure does not automatically enhance rapport or reciprocal disclosure. Designers should account for user experience with HRI and contextual factors when integrating small talk into robot behavior.

Abstract: People naturally chat while collaborating and share personal information (i.e., self-disclose) to build rapport and maintain social connections. As robots are increasingly developed to work with people, the effective use of these social behaviors to enhance engagement and support teamwork becomes ever more important. While prior work has shown that robot-initiated small talk can benefit human-robot collaboration, less is known about how best to design such small talk. In this work, we explore how self-disclosure may be designed to support small talk within a human-robot team---especially when the robot is an industrial manipulator that lacks anthropomorphic cues and performs physical work. We first developed an LLM-driven manipulator capable of partaking in small talk, adopting either a low-disclosure or high-disclosure strategy. We then conducted a user study (N = 50) to investigate how self-disclosure in small talk influences human-robot dynamics. Unexpectedly, participants disclosed more in the low-disclosure condition and reported stronger teaming and coordination than those in the high-disclosure condition. This effect was more pronounced among users with prior experience teaming with robots. These results suggest that increasing robot self-disclosure does not necessarily foster rapport, social connection, or reciprocal disclosure; other factors, such as prior HRI experience, should be considered.

</details>


### [251] [Picking Bins Empty: A Hierarchical Hybrid Approach with Online Self-Learning of Grasp Points for Reliable Industrial Bin-Picking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28175)
*Florian Töper,Samarth Kishor Yelvande,Jan Niklas Ewertz,Rudolph Triebel,Peter Ohlhausen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A four-tier hierarchical hybrid approach for auto bin-picking that integrates a model-based backbone with a model-free exploration agent and online self-learning, achieving full bin clearance and outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome deadlocks and occlusion-induced grasp failures in model-based bin-picking, reduce labor-intensive grasp tuning, and enable robust, autonomous production capable of operating without human intervention.

Method: A four-tier architecture: (1) a model-based robust backbone; (2) a model-free exploration agent to resolve deadlocks and discover new grasps; (3) online self-learning using gripper-stroke feedback; (4) autonomous ranking of grasp candidates using Wilson score intervals.

Result: Validation on three automotive parts shows the method outperforms a model-free baseline in grasp success rate and increases the bin-clearing rate of the model-based baseline from 50.9% to 100% across all experiments.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a practical pathway to truly autonomous, intervention-free industrial bin-picking by marrying model-based reliability with model-free flexibility and autonomous self-learning.

Abstract: Bin-picking is a cornerstone of modern manufacturing, yet achieving complete bin clearance without manual intervention remains a critical challenge. While model-based methods provide high precision, they frequently suffer from deadlocks when predefined grasps are occluded or perception fails. Labor-intensive fine-tuning of grasp points is commonly required to reach a satisfactory performance for new parts. Model-free algorithms offer a more flexible alternative with "out-of-the-box" versatility but lack the reliability and repeatability required for production. Unlike existing work, which treats the two techniques in isolation, we propose a fourtiered hierarchical hybrid approach to combine the best of both worlds. A model-based pipeline serves as a robust backbone, while a model-free "exploration agent" resolves deadlock situations and discovers new grasp points. This is supported by an online self-learning mechanism that uses gripper-stroke feedback and Wilson score intervals to autonomously rank grasp candidates, reducing manual commissioning effort. Validation on three automotive parts demonstrates that our method significantly outperforms a model-free baseline in grasp success rate while improving the bin clearance rate of the model-based baseline from 50.9% to 100% across all experiments. This transition to full bin clearance marks a significant step towards truly autonomous, intervention-free industrial operation.

</details>


### [252] [PAMoR: Parameterized Affective Motion Generation in Real Time for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28213)
*Yan Pan,Lingfan Bao,Tianhu Peng,Chengxu Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PAMoR converts affect into a quantifiable valence-arousal control parameter from robot kinematics for real-time, controllable motion; uses action and affect priors in a shared latent space; perceptual validation shows emotion recognition above baselines but below acted humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable quantitatively parameterizable, annotation-free affect-driven robot motion in social settings, addressing the limitation of prior methods that rely on non-parametric references or words.

Method: Compute VA coordinates in closed form from postural expansion and movement energy; integrate an action prior and two affect priors in a shared latent space; combine priors at each denoising step; autoregressive generation on a 29-DoF Unitree G1 in real time; both action and affect editable.

Result: Motion tracks commanded VA across its range; text-to-motion fidelity comparable to text-only baselines; perceptual study shows commanded emotion detected in 0.38 of trials, beating baselines and approaching 0.44 of acted human bodies.

Conclusion: PAMoR provides an annotation-free, quantitative mechanism to control affect in robot motion in real time, yielding perceptually recognizable emotions and flexible control, with performance nearing human-acted benchmarks.

Abstract: People read a humanoid robot's motion in social settings not only for the action performed but for the affect conveyed. Motion carrying that affect has so far been generated for human avatars, where style is taken from a reference clip or an emotion word, neither of which can be quantitatively parameterized. We present PAMoR, which turns affect into a measured control parameter: a valence-arousal (V-A) coordinate computed natively on robot kinematics. It is obtained in closed form from postural expansion and movement energy, and these measurements serve directly as generation conditions, with no human annotation. An action prior and two affect priors, trained in a shared latent space, are composed at each denoising step: the action prior fixes what is performed, the affect priors modulate how. Whole-body motion rolls out autoregressively on a 29-DoF Unitree G1 in real time, with action and affect both editable. Generated motion tracks the commanded V-A over its full range while text-to-motion fidelity still matches text-only baselines. In a perceptual study, raters identify the commanded emotion on 0.38 of trials, above both baselines and approaching the 0.44 reported for acted human bodies.

</details>


### [253] [Probabilistic Multi-Robot Gas Source Localization with Uncalibrated Sensors: A Distributed Estimation Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28214)
*Wanting Jin,Marc Zoel Arias Mitjà,Alcherio Martinoli*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Calibration-free distributed source localization for heterogeneous sensors using rank-based features fused with a product of experts; adds informative region allocation and planning; demonstrated in gas-sensor simulations with superior accuracy over simple aggregation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable reliable environmental state estimation with multi-robot teams employing uncalibrated, heterogeneous sensors whose nonlinear responses hinder traditional data fusion.

Method: Each robot computes a local belief from a rank-based feature that captures relative evolution of observations and is invariant to sensor scaling/nonlinearities. Local beliefs are fused via a product of experts to yield a global estimate. An informative region allocation and planning strategy reduces redundant exploration and balances exploration/exploitation.

Result: The approach achieves reliable source localization despite strong sensor heterogeneity and outperforms a benchmark based on standard measurement aggregation in high-fidelity gas-sensor simulations.

Conclusion: Calibration-free sensing representations can be effectively extended to distributed robotic systems, broadening their applicability to other estimation tasks involving heterogeneous sensors.

Abstract: Estimating environmental states with multi-robot systems becomes particularly challenging when robots are equipped with uncalibrated and therefore heterogeneous sensors, whose nonlinear and inconsistent responses prevent reliable information fusion. In this paper, we propose a distributed probabilistic framework for source localization tasks that enables calibration-free estimation in the presence of sensor heterogeneity. The key idea is that each robot independently estimates a local belief using a rank-based feature that captures the relative evolution of observations and is invariant to sensor scaling and nonlinearities. These local beliefs are then fused through a product of experts formulation to obtain a consistent global estimate across the team. To further improve the efficiency of team coordination, we introduce an informative region allocation and path planning strategy that reduces redundant exploration while balancing exploration and exploitation. We validate the proposed framework using high-fidelity simulations with realistic gas sensor models. Results demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms a benchmark method based on standard measurement aggregation, achieving reliable source localization accuracy despite strong sensor heterogeneity. More broadly, this work demonstrates how calibration-free sensing representations can be effectively extended to distributed robotic systems, paving the way for their application to other estimation tasks involving heterogeneous sensors.

</details>


### [254] [Training-free Suction Grasp Detection for Deformed Aseptic Cartons Using Vision-Language Models and Geometric Surface Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28246)
*Marin Maletic,Goran Vasiljevic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Training-free suction grasping for sorting deformed aseptic cartons; combines open-vocabulary detection, SAM2 mask refinement, and geometry-based suction-point scoring; compares kNN PCA, Sobel cross-product, and RANSAC plane fitting; achieves 88.2% single-object grasp and 72.6% cluttered end-to-end retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic sorting of deformable, geometrically inconsistent objects is challenging; a robust, data-free approach leveraging vision-language perception and geometric cues is sought.

Method: Pipeline: detect cartons via an open-vocabulary vision-language model using text prompts; refine detections to instance masks with SAM2; select suction point by scoring surface flatness and normal alignment; compare three geometric strategies (kNN PCA, Sobel cross-product, RANSAC plane fitting); evaluate on a real robot across three deformation levels and 35 cluttered scenes.

Result: Single-object grasp success: 88.2%; end-to-end retrieval in clutter: 72.6%; demonstrated across deformation levels and clutter in real-world tests.

Conclusion: A training-free approach that separates target identification from grasp-point selection and uses simple geometric cues yields robust suction grasping for deformable cartons, performing well in real-world clutter and indicating the viability of combining open-world perception with geometric scoring.

Abstract: Robotic sorting of recyclable waste is challenging due to the deformable and geometrically inconsistent nature of target objects. We present a training-free suction grasping system for sorting deformed aseptic beverage cartons, decoupling target identification from grasp-point selection. An open-vocabulary vision-language model detects cartons from a text prompt, SAM2 refines each detection into an instance mask, and a geometric scoring method selects the suction point by combining surface flatness with normal alignment. Three geometric methods are compared: k-nearest-neighbour PCA, Sobel cross-product, and RANSAC plane fitting. Evaluated on a real robot across three deformation levels and 35 cluttered scenes, single-object grasp success reaches 88.2% and end-to-end retrieval in clutter is 72.6%.

</details>


### [255] [CoCoBench: A Cooperative Coordination Benchmark for Embodied Multi-Agent Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28266)
*Yang Chen,Ye-Xin Xie,Lirong Che,Danyang Peng,Yuzhe Yang,Peiwen Lin,Xu Cao,Chuang Wang,Lei Yuan,Jian Su,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CoCoBench—a construct-level benchmark for evaluating multi-agent embodied coordination in executable household tasks, featuring 897 oracle-validated instances across four coordination constructs and construct-level scores in addition to task success; evaluated 11 leading MLLMs across coordination modes, inputs, and agent counts, revealing that coordination ability is highly construct-specific.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing embodied-agent benchmarks emphasize single-agent task completion or aggregate success, masking coordination failures such as duplicated work, ordering violations, resource contention, and desynchronized handoffs; there is a need for fine-grained diagnostics to evaluate multi-agent coordination.

Method: Introduce CoCoBench with 897 oracle-validated instances organized around four constructs: task allocation, sequential ordering, mutual exclusion, and handoff coordination. Provide construct-level coordination scores alongside task success. Evaluate 11 leading MLLMs across different coordination modes, observation inputs, and numbers of agents.

Result: Coordination ability is highly construct-specific; strong overall performance does not imply balanced competence across different coordination types. This finding suggests that model architectures should be designed or trained to target specific coordination capabilities, and that benchmarks should include construct-level diagnostics to guide improvements in multi-agent coordination.

Conclusion: CoCoBench enables targeted assessment of embodied multi-agent coordination and provides a pathway to design architectures and training methods that address specific coordination constructs, uncovering gaps not visible through aggregate task-success metrics.

Abstract: Agent systems powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have advanced rapidly in recent years, yet existing embodied-agent benchmarks still lack fine-grained diagnostics for multi-agent coordination. Most benchmarks either focus on single-agent task completion or summarize multi-agent behavior with overall task success rates, which can obscure coordination failures such as duplicated work, violations of ordering constraints, resource contention, and desynchronized handoffs. In this paper, we introduce CoCoBench, a construct-level benchmark for evaluating multi-agent embodied coordination in executable household tasks. CoCoBench contains 897 oracle-validated instances organized around four recurring coordination constructs: task allocation, sequential ordering, mutual exclusion, and handoff coordination. In addition to task success rate, CoCoBench provides construct-level scores that measure whether agents coordinate effectively. We evaluate 11 leading MLLMs across different coordination modes, observation inputs, and numbers of agents. The results show that coordination ability is highly construct-specific: strong overall performance does not imply balanced competence across different coordination types. These findings point to new directions for designing targeted model architectures and improving multi-agent coordination ability.

</details>


### [256] [Spatial-Semantic Reasoning using Large Language Models for Efficient UAV Search Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28270)
*Marin Maletic,Marijana Peti,Tamara Petrovic,Stjepan Bogdan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Real-time LLM-guided semantic navigation for UAV ObjectNav using online 3D mapping, object detection, and spline trajectory planning to reduce mission duration while maintaining high search accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve time efficiency in ObjectGoal Navigation for UAVs and address limitations of offline reasoning and simulator-constrained action spaces.

Method: Integrates an LLM to interpret natural language instructions and perform semantic reasoning over detected objects and spatial context to prioritize high-probability search regions. Combines real-time object detection, 3D mapping, and polynomial spline interpolation for smooth, feasible UAV trajectories. Updates semantic relevance online as new observations arrive, enabling real-time planning.

Result: Simulated and real-world experiments show reduced mission duration with maintained high search accuracy, demonstrating the effectiveness of online, LLM-guided semantic reasoning for time-efficient UAV ObjectNav.

Conclusion: Online, LLM-guided semantic reasoning can significantly improve time efficiency in UAV-based ObjectNav without sacrificing search accuracy, validating the feasibility of real-time semantic planning in practical UAV deployments.

Abstract: We present a real-time semantic navigation framework for Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) focused on improving time efficiency in the Object Goal Navigation (ObjectNav) task. Central to our approach is a Large Language Model (LLM) that interprets user-provided natural language instructions and performs semantic reasoning over detected objects and spatial context to prioritize high-probability search regions. The system combines real-time object detection, 3D spatial mapping, and polynomial spline interpolation for smooth and feasible UAV trajectory planning. Unlike prior methods that rely on offline reasoning or simulator-constrained action spaces, our framework can operate in real time, continuously updating semantic relevance based on new observations. Experiments in both simulated and real-world settings demonstrate reductions in mission duration while maintaining high search accuracy, underscoring the effectiveness of LLM-guided reasoning for time- efficient UAV-based ObjectNav.

</details>


### [257] [STEGNav: Spatio-Temporal Event Graph Reasoning for Multimodal Lifelong Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28279)
*Yang Chen,Zhenyu Huang,Wenbo Fu,Danyang Peng,Shi-Yu Tian,Kun-Yang Yu,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: STEGNav extends scene graphs to spatio-temporal event graphs for lifelong multimodal navigation, introducing a spatial axis for instance grounding and frontier representation and a temporal axis for trajectory-aware memory. A VLM-based agent reasons over the graph to set next goals, achieving strong SR and SPL across GOAT-Bench and HM3D datasets in a training-free framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current state-centric scene graphs struggle to distinguish similar instances, jointly representing semantic targets with exploration frontiers, and exploiting navigation memory across tasks. There is a need for a persistent, expressive, and reusable representation that couples spatial semantics with temporal trajectory experience.

Method: Propose STEGNav, which extends conventional scene graphs into spatio-temporal event graphs along two axes: (1) spatial axis for query-conditioned instance grounding and occupancy-aware frontiers characterized by reachability, path cost, and exploration utility; (2) temporal axis with trajectory-aware dual-window memory to store recent decision-trajectory events and verified cross-subtask outcomes. A VLM-based navigation agent reasons over the graph to select either a target instance or an exploration frontier as the next goal. Training-free framework. 

Result: Empirical evaluations report 66.3% SR and 39.7% SPL on GOAT-Bench, and 64.0% SR / 69.4% SPL on HM3Dv1/v2, indicating robust cross-task performance. Ablations show the two axes provide complementary benefits, improving navigation reliability and cross-subtask experience reuse.

Conclusion: STEGNav offers a training-free, two-axis spatio-temporal graph framework that enhances lifelong multimodal navigation by integrating occupancy-aware frontiers with semantic grounding and by preserving recent and cross-task navigation experiences, leading to improved SR/SPL and cross-task reuse.

Abstract: Multimodal lifelong navigation requires an agent to autonomously explore unseen environments while sequentially completing navigation tasks specified by object categories, language descriptions, or reference images. Existing methods primarily accomplish these tasks by constructing state-centric semantic scene graphs. By treating scene graphs as persistent repositories of semantic observations, these methods struggle to distinguish similar instances, jointly represent semantic targets and exploration frontiers, and effectively exploit navigation memory and trajectory experience. To address these limitations, we propose Spatio-Temporal Event Graph Navigation (STEGNav), a training-free framework that extends conventional scene graphs into spatio-temporal event graphs along complementary spatial and temporal axes. The spatial axis performs query-conditioned instance grounding and jointly represents semantic targets and occupancy-aware exploration frontiers characterized by reachability, path cost, and exploration utility. The temporal axis employs trajectory-aware dual-window memory to retain recent decision--trajectory events and verified cross-subtask navigation outcomes. A VLM-based navigation agent reasons over the resulting spatio-temporal event graph and selects either a target instance or an exploration frontier as its next navigation goal. STEGNav achieves 66.3% SR and 39.7 SPL on GOAT-Bench, as well as SR scores of 64.0% and 69.4% on HM3Dv1 and HM3Dv2, respectively. Ablation studies and error analyses validate the complementary effects of the two axes, demonstrating that event-driven spatio-temporal representations improve navigation reliability and cross-subtask experience reuse.

</details>


### [258] [MaCoPlanner: LLM-Assisted Manual-Compiled Task Planning with Proactive Safety Verification for Robotic Industrial Panel Operation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28300)
*Guipeng Xin,Jiahe Xua,Mohammad Deghat,Chenhui Wan,Jie Liu,Youmin Hu,Zhongxu Hu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MaCoPlanner converts equipment manuals into a typed intermediate representation, retrieves task- and state-relevant evidence, and uses symbolic planning with constraint checking to generate and repair plans for robotic panel operation. It localizes violations for repair, grounds verified symbolic actions to execution, and shows improved task success and controlled violation rates in simulation, without claiming deployment readiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensure compliant, safe, and state-consistent robotic panel manipulation by leveraging heterogeneous manuals to constrain task planning and execution.

Method: Compile manuals into a typed intermediate representation; evidence retrieval for tasks and states; symbolic plan generation; pre-actuation symbolic rollout with constraint checking; violation localization and targeted repair; reject unresolved plans; separate execution interface grounds actions to physical controls and updates device state; evaluated with an independent oracle; comparison to Raw-Manual; experiments on a controller-panel simulator.

Result: Final violation rate of 2.7%; 26.3% of repair-analysis runs rejected after refinement budget; task success on Level-2 increased from 62.8% to 84.4%; Level-3 from 25.9% to 43.2%.

Conclusion: Demonstrates integrated execution feasibility on a controller-panel simulator under representative conditions, but does not claim industrial deployment readiness.

Abstract: Robotic industrial panel operation requires not only accurate control localization but also compliance with operating procedures, safety rules, and device-state constraints distributed across heterogeneous manuals. This study presents MaCoPlanner, a task-planning framework built on knowledge compiled from equipment manuals that converts equipment manuals into a typed intermediate representation, retrieves task- and state-relevant evidence, and uses it to support plan generation. Before actuation, candidate plans are symbolically rolled out and checked against procedural and state-transition constraints; detected violations are localized and returned for targeted repair, while unresolved plans are rejected. A separate execution interface grounds verified symbolic actions to physical controls and updates the device state. Under an independent evaluation oracle, MaCoPlanner achieves a final violation rate of 2.7%, and 26.3% of the runs in the repair analysis are rejected after exhausting the refinement budget. Compared with Raw-Manual, task success increases from 62.8% to 84.4% on Level-2 tasks and from 25.9% to 43.2% on Level-3 tasks. Experiments on a controller-panel simulator without an attached industrial load further demonstrate integrated execution feasibility under representative interaction conditions, without claiming industrial deployment readiness.

</details>


### [259] [PanelShield: Verifiable Closed-Loop Safe Planning for Robotic Industrial Panel Operation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28305)
*Guipeng Xin,Jiahe Xu,Chenhui Wan,Jie Liu,Youmin Hu,Zhongxu Hu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PanelShield presents a verifiable, closed-loop safety planning framework for manual-guided industrial panel operation, combining parameterized action primitives with dual verification (LTL and Safety FSM) to enforce cross-step correctness and local legality; it yields a notable reduction in violations (to 2.7%) with 4.1 s latency and is validated in simulation and real-world experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial panel operation is safety-critical and knowledge-intensive. Existing foundation-model planners lack computable, localizable, and reproducible mechanisms for violation detection and repair, creating safety and auditability gaps.

Method: Generate parameterized action primitive sequences from task-relevant manual evidence. Apply dual formal verification using Linear Temporal Logic (LTL) and a Safety Finite State Machine (FSM) to enforce cross-step temporal correctness and local transition legality. When violations occur, produce a structured counterexample highlighting the earliest violating step and its cause for targeted repair and re-verification. Build a multi-level long-horizon planning benchmark across three industrial panels and evaluate in simulation and real-world robotics experiments.

Result: PanelShield improves safety-constrained task performance over foundation-model-only baselines, reducing violation rates to 2.7% with an overall latency of 4.1 seconds. Real-world experiments demonstrate end-to-end feasibility.

Conclusion: A verifiable approach to robotic panel operation that maintains flexibility, safety, and auditability by coupling goal-driven planners with formal verification and structured repair feedback.

Abstract: Industrial panel operation is knowledge-intensive and safety-critical. Beyond control recognition and action generation, execution must satisfy constraints in operation manuals and safety regulations. While foundation-model-based planners show strong semantic capability, they typically lack computable, localizable, and reproducible mechanisms for violation detection and repair. To address this, we propose PanelShield, a verifiable closed-loop safety planning framework for manual-guided industrial panel operation. The framework generates parameterized action primitive sequences from task-relevant manual evidence and applies dual formal verification with LTL and a Safety FSM to enforce cross-step temporal correctness and local transition legality. When violations occur, it outputs a structured counterexample with the earliest violating step and cause, enabling targeted repair and re-verification. We build a multi-level long-horizon planning benchmark covering three representative industrial device panels, and evaluate the framework in simulation and real-world robotic experiments. Results show that PanelShield improves complex safety-constrained task performance over foundation-model-only planning baselines while reducing the violation rate to 2.7%, with 4.1 s total latency. Real-world experiments demonstrate end-toend feasibility. Overall, PanelShield offers a verifiable approach to robotic panel operation that balances flexibility, safety, and auditability.

</details>


### [260] [Cooperative Risk-Aware Exploration in Heterogeneous Multi-Robot Systems Using Algorithmic Altruism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28409)
*Brooks A. Butler,Jair Certório,João P. Hespanha,Magnus Egerstedt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Game-theoretic, altruism-inspired risk-aware trajectory planning for heterogeneous multi-robot exploration; introduces Social Nash Equilibrium with Hamilton-like relatedness to bias risk-sharing; validated in simulations and hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient exploration in hazardous environments requires balancing information gain, redundancy avoidance, and heterogeneous risk tolerance; inspired by ecological altruism to reallocate risk among teammates.

Method: Define a finite-horizon trajectory planning problem as a Social Nash Equilibrium; agent utility includes information gain, area coverage, uncertainty reduction; penalties for redundancy and exposure; agent-specific relatedness weights shape cross-agent impact; receding-horizon planner with projected gradient optimization; hardware experiments with single-integrator controllers and barrier certificates.

Result: Altruistic planning reduces redundant exploration, improves inter-robot separation, reallocates risk toward lower-valued agents, while preserving map coverage; simulations and hardware experiments validate approach.

Conclusion: Socially grounded utility shaping via relatedness leads to better team performance in risk-aware exploration; feasible to implement with gradient-based optimization and barrier certificates.

Abstract: Multi-robot systems are well-positioned for exploration in hazardous environments, but effective deployment requires deciding not only where robots should gather information, but also how risk should be distributed across heterogeneous team members. This paper develops a game-theoretic framework for cooperative risk-aware exploration based on ecologically inspired altruistic behavior. Each robot selects a finite-horizon trajectory to maximize information gain while penalizing redundant exploration and expected hazard exposure. Heterogeneity is introduced through agent-specific value parameters for encoding altruistic coupling, which is modeled through relatedness weights inspired by Hamilton's rule. We introduce a game-theoretic structure for trajectory planning that defines a Social Nash Equilibrium, which modifies the utility of agent actions according to agent relatedness. This utility shaping causes agents to internalize the effect of their trajectory choices on teammates, encouraging lower-valued robots to accept risk when doing so benefits higher-valued agents and improves team performance. We define an exploration utility for agents that rewards area coverage and uncertainty reduction, while also penalizing redundancy and risk, enabling projected gradient-based waypoint optimization in a receding-horizon planner. Simulations show that altruistic planning reduces redundant exploration, improves inter-robot separation, and reallocates risk according to agent value while maintaining comparable map coverage. We further demonstrate the approach in hardware experiments, where planned waypoints are tracked by wheeled robots using single-integrator controllers and barrier certificates.

</details>


### [261] [Linear Temporal Logic Translation via Human-Inspired Self-Constrained Reasoning for Robot Task Specification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28435)
*Haofei Hou,Fanxu Meng,Shunyi Zhao,Kairui Yang,Mengchen Cai,Lecheng Ruan,Qining Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SCR internalizes structural constraints within the model and uses hierarchical decision-making to translate natural language instructions into LTL, balancing domain-constraint adherence with adaptability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human natural-language task descriptions are ambiguous and context-dependent; existing LLM-based translators either risk violating domain constraints with open-ended reasoning or disrupt reasoning with post-hoc enforcement.

Method: Embed a structural constraint representation inside the model and couple it with a hierarchical decision-making process to guide reasoning within a formally grounded space, avoiding external filters.

Result: SCR improves domain-constraint satisfaction and generalization in translating human intent to verifiable robotic specifications, with improved interpretability.

Conclusion: Internalizing structural constraints within the model offers an effective, adaptable, and interpretable approach for translating natural-language intents into verifiable LTL specifications for robotic execution.

Abstract: Many robotic tasks are temporally extended and demand precise specifications of subgoals, constraints, and their temporal ordering. Yet human operators typically communicate such tasks in natural language, which is inherently ambiguous, underspecified, and context dependent. Translating human instructions into formal task specifications, such as Linear Temporal Logic (LTL), is therefore essential for verifiable and safe robotic execution. Existing LLM-based translators attempt to bridge this gap through open-ended reasoning or post-hoc constraint enforcement, but the former may violate domain constraints, whereas the latter can disrupt the reasoning needed for novel instructions. This paper proposes Self-Constrained Reasoning (SCR), a framework that mitigates this trade-off by internalizing structural knowledge into the model's decision-making process rather than imposing it as an external filter. By combining a structural constraint representation with a hierarchical decision-making formulation, SCR guides reasoning within a formally grounded space while preserving adaptability to unseen instructions. Experiments show that SCR improves both domain-constraint satisfaction and generalization, providing an effective and interpretable approach for translating human intent into verifiable specifications for robotic execution.

</details>


### [262] [ChainSplat: A Physics-Inspired Screw-Theoretic Model for Learning Deformable Linear Object Dynamics from Multi-View RGB Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28570)
*Seungyeon Kim,Noémie Jaquier*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ChainSplat learns a compact, physics-inspired model of deformable linear objects (DLOs) from multi-view RGB that jointly recovers 3D geometry, appearance, kinematics, and dynamics, enabling real-time state/force estimation and model-based control with high-fidelity rendering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DLOs exhibit high-dimensional configuration spaces and material-dependent dynamics; prior pipelines rely on depth inputs or multi-stage processes prone to error; aim to unify geometry, dynamics, and appearance from RGB for robust manipulation.

Method: Represent a DLO as an open chain of rigid links connected by revolute joints; provide an analytic screw-theoretic dynamics model; couple with Gaussian splatting as a differentiable renderer to recover geometry, appearance, and dynamics from multi-view RGB; enable real-time state/force estimation and trajectory optimization.

Result: Demonstrates state-of-the-art performance in dynamics prediction, 3D geometry reconstruction, and RGB rendering under dynamic interactions; enables real-time state and force estimation and accurate model-based trajectory optimization; source code and video released.

Conclusion: Offers a compact, physics-inspired, end-to-end framework that maps RGB observations to a controllable DLO model suitable for robotic manipulation and rendering, with practical implications for real-time control and planning.

Abstract: Identifying the underlying dynamics and 3D geometry of deformable linear objects (DLOs), such as cables, ropes, and hoses, is essential for accurate robotic manipulation, but remains challenging due to their high-dimensional configuration spaces and diverse behaviors arising from varying material properties. Existing methods often rely on multi-stage pipelines and auxiliary depth inputs, which are prone to errors under dynamic interactions, while their high-dimensional state representations make model-based control computationally expensive. In this paper, we introduce ChainSplat, a physics-inspired framework that jointly learns the 3D geometry, appearance, kinematics, and dynamics of DLOs solely from multi-view RGB videos. ChainSplat represents a DLO as an open-chain structure of rigid links connected by revolute joints, yielding an analytic, screw-theoretic model with a compact state representation parameterized by joint configurations. By integrating this formulation with Gaussian splatting, ChainSplat jointly recovers DLO dynamics, kinematics-aware 3D geometry, and appearance, while enabling high-fidelity RGB rendering from arbitrary states. Through real-world experiments, we demonstrate that ChainSplat achieves state-of-the-art performance in dynamics predictions, 3D geometry reconstruction, and RGB rendering across dynamic interactions. ChainSplat further enables real-time state and force estimation, as well as accurate model-based trajectory optimization, highlighting its practical utility for real-world robotic manipulation of DLOs. Accompanying source code and video are available at: https://chainsplat.github.io.

</details>


### [263] [Aero Hand Open: A Simulation-Ready Tendon-Driven Hand for Dexterous Manipulation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28578)
*Nan Wang,Mohit Yadav,Jonathan Wulff,Aidan Rosenbaum,Kezhou Chen,Yuvan Sharma,Xu Dong,Yiwei Tao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A simulation-ready tendon-driven anthropomorphic hand (Aero Hand Open) with a cable-transmission model, an identified bidirectional actuation map including thumb coupling, and a reinforcement-learning pipeline that trains policies in simulation and transfers them to hardware, with complete release of design, model, mapping, training env, and deployment stack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tendon-driven hands save motor size and cost by routing actuation through cables and by using single cables to drive multiple joints, but the underactuated transmission is hard to model and the joints are not independently controllable. A complete, simulatable pipeline is needed for efficient design, learning, and sim-to-real transfer.

Method: (1) A simulation model of the cable transmission; (2) An identified actuation map that connects the model to motor commands in both directions, including the three-way coupling of the thumb; (3) A reinforcement learning package to train policies in simulation; and (4) release of the mechanical design, simulation model, mapping, training environment, and deployment stack for end-to-end use.

Result: A policy can be trained entirely in simulation and run on the real hand without fine-tuning and without state estimation, demonstrating successful sim-to-real transfer within the released pipeline.

Conclusion: The work delivers a complete, simulation-to-realizable framework for tendon-driven hands, enabling rapid development, training, and deployment by providing the design, models, mapping, training tools, and deployment stack in a unified release.

Abstract: Tendon-driven hands are anthropomorphic, and moving the actuators off the joints is what makes a hand of this capability affordable to build. Two effects produce that saving. Routing force through a cable removes the requirement that a motor fit inside the joint it drives, so smaller and cheaper motors suffice, and one motor can drive several joints through a single cable, so fewer motors are needed. They are also harder to learn on than a direct-drive hand. The underactuated transmission that produces the saving is itself difficult to represent in a simulator, and the joints one cable drives are not independently commandable. We present Aero Hand Open, a tendon-driven anthropomorphic hand that is released simulation-ready. Three things ship with it. A simulation model reproduces the cable transmission itself. An identified actuation map connects that model to the motor commands in both directions, including the three-way coupling of the thumb. A reinforcement learning package trains policies for the hand. Together they let a policy be trained entirely in simulation and run on the hand with no fine-tuning and no state estimation. We release the mechanical design, the simulation model, the identified mapping, the training environment and the deployment stack.

</details>
